lỗi Tolerant Tennis

Comprehensive guide to lỗi tolerance in tennis — what it is, why it matters, and how to apply it. Covers the lỗi Tolerance In Action series, technical breakdowns, mental strategies, and training methods for becoming a more reliable player.

Source: lỗi Tolerant Tennis DOCX archive (extracted from "lỗi TOLERANT TENNIS.docx").

What is lỗi Tolerance?

“lỗi tolerance” is a term from computing – it refers to a machine’s ability to remain functional even in the face of a lỗi, a small problem. For example, imagine that one of the transistors in your processor fails. Is your entire computer going to crash? No, because your computer is lỗi tolerant; it can tolerate the lỗi, the transistor failure, and still function. Elite forehands are also lỗi tolerant.

They can tolerate small inaccuracies in chuẩn bị, timing, or execution without failing. This lỗi tolerance is an essential ingredient of a high level thuận tay. To gain a maximal lợi thế in tennis, our goal isn’t just to produce the best possible result. Rather, it’s to develop a cú đánh which produces that best possible result with the highest margin for error out of the widest array of different situations.

Success Under Imperfect Conditions

If a particular cú đánh only produces a fast, heavy, accurate shot when struck perfectly, but when struck imperfectly, frequently results in an outright miss, that shot is useless in an actual trận đấu. The shot is not lỗi tolerant, because it can’t cán vợt a lỗi, a small mistake chuẩn bị, timing or execution, and still succeed.

Roger Federer hitting a swinging bắt lưới – a shot for which it’s very difficult to establish a consistent điểm tiếp xúc, and thus lỗi tolerance is essential to success. On the other hand, a cú đánh which succeeds even in the face of a small chuẩn bị or execution mistake is lỗi tolerant and is competitively useful. This idea is critical to the rest of our discussion. Only lỗi tolerant strokes grant a competitive lợi thế, because during cú đánh production, conditions will never be perfect.

We’d all like to be in perfect vị trí for every shot, and to practice enough that we never mistime our swings, but in practice that’s impossible, especially if the other player is elite. Instead, we’re going to hit many of our shots while slightly out of vị trí, and make many small timing and execution mistakes throughout the trận đấu. This takes some humility to admit, but, as usual, humility is a useful virtue. To win, our cú đánh needs to succeed in spite of these small mistakes.

There are two basic classes of skills, two critical steps in your cú đánh development, that are required when learning an elite thuận tay. The first is developing quick feet and proper di chuyển so that you can avoid ever having to hit out of vị trí, and avoid ever mistiming the ball. The second is building in enough lỗi tolerance that you can hit out of vị trí and mistime the ball.

Tennis is Often Difficult and Awkward

Roger Federer hitting a thuận tay on which he didn’t quite get around the ball, and it’s too close to him. The shot succeeds, and he still wins the point. When two players are competing, each is doing everything in their power to make the other’s shots as difficult and awkward as possible. Therefore, the most advantageous competitive strokes are the strokes that still function at a high level out of difficult and awkward positions. We see this concept at play all the time on the tour.

Professional players constantly slightly mistime their strokes, and they constantly hit shots while slightly out of vị trí. Everyone is moving so fast and hitting so hard that it’s humanly impossible to vị trí for and strike every shot perfectly.

Of course, every player tries to hit every ball perfectly, and they move so well that they actually do hit a lot of them pretty perfectly, but in the end, even the ever graceful Roger Federer hits the occasional mistimed thuận tay, or gets caught by a solid, sâu trả bóng and has to hit out of vị trí. Of course, because he’s Roger Federer, that out of vị trí or mistimed shot almost always still goes in.

Rafa’s thuận tay – A lỗi Tolerant Masterpiece

Rafael Nadal possesses what is probably the best example of a lỗi tolerant thuận tay in all of modern tennis. Like every player, Nadal often slightly mistimes his thuận tay. Rafa’s cú đánh, though, is so lỗi tolerant that you can barely even tell these shots were mistimed. Sometimes, his ball will drop a little shorter in the sân than normal. Other times, it’ll drift a little wider than average.

But as a spectator, you barely notice, because even when Nadal slightly misses, the resulting shot still keeps the point neutral. Rafael Nadal’s lỗi tolerant thuận tay on display on the red clay – hông rotated, lõi stable, cổ tay relaxed. On offense, when Rafa slightly mistimes or mishits the ball, half the time it just lands on the line for a điểm thắng instead of landing inside the line where he was aiming.

Sometimes, sure, he hits it back towards his opponent and the đánh qua lại continues in neutral, but even that isn’t all that bad of a miss. Where other players mishit the ball out and lose the point immediately, Rafa mishits the ball in and just has to start back over from neutral, so he still wins about the half the points he plays after those tấn công “misses.” For Rafael Nadal to miss to the point where his opponent actually gains a significant lợi thế from that miss, he has to really miss.

He is so strong, his hông are so engaged, and his cổ tay is so loose that is his thuận tay is the poster child of lỗi tolerance – he makes a small mistake in timing or chuẩn bị, and the most common outcome is that the đánh qua lại continues like nothing happened.

Why lỗi Tolerance is so Essential

Rafael Nadal is also amazing at digging out balls that land right at his feet. Heck, sometimes he even rips an trong-ra ngoài điểm thắng off of that shot. No player goes into a đánh qua lại wanting to play a shot off of their shoestrings, but sometimes, you’re forced to. This is why lỗi tolerance is so essential to high level tennis. If your thuận tay only works when in perfect vị trí, you’ll never be able to compete with players that can frequently put you in imperfect positions.

At the Gerry Weber Open in Halle, Germany, the shadows from the stadium obscure the sân during the final between eventual champion Roger Federer and runner-up David Goffin. Imperfect positions are inevitable. Here’s another example – what about when the lighting on sân gets weird? At Halle every year, the shadows on the sân get ridiculously distracting.

Every player’s ability for precision declines, at least a little bit, playing in conditions under which their brain is simply receiving less quality information from their eyes. Yet, as we watch these spectacular rallies taking place across the criss-crossing shadows in Halle, the players… still barely miss. How is that possible?

It’s possible because a tour level player’s cú đánh is so lỗi tolerant that this small lỗi – the fact that they aren’t getting perfect visual information – is completely tolerated by the cú đánh. Their cú đánh is resilient enough that the small amount of stress placed on the system by the imperfect lighting isn’t enough to have a significant effect on its results; when they slightly mistime their vung vợt or slightly mishit the ball due to the lighting, their cú đánh still succeeds.

So How do I Develop lỗi Tolerance? lỗi tolerance comes from kỹ thuật. There are ways to hit the ball that are lỗi tolerant, and ways to hit the ball that aren’t. From your feet, all the way up through your legs, hông, chest, arm, cổ tay, and hand, each body part has a job to do to strike the ball in a lỗi tolerant manner. A correctly taught cú đánh is not a complicated series of limb positions and joint angles to memorize. Don’t be scared off by that list of body parts, though.

It makes lỗi tolerant strokes sound complicated, but they actually aren’t – in fact, simplicity is one of the primary drivers of lỗi tolerance. A correctly taught cú đánh is not a complicated series of limb positions and joint angles to memorize, but rather a few static positions to learn and a few volitional di chuyển patterns to execute out of those static positions. One of the keys to lỗi tolerance is eliminating extraneous di chuyển and extraneous tension from your body.

Every muscle that isn’t driving lực should be relaxed, and every di chuyển that isn’t adding tốc độ đầu vợt should be scrapped. We build our strokes up from the essentials, from the parts of the vung vợt directly responsible for generating tốc độ đầu vợt, and strip away the non-essential parts of the vung vợt that don’t help it, but can definitely hurt it if not executed perfectly.

Fundamentals and lỗi Tolerance Come First

At lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we tập trung on the fundamentals of each vung vợt that create the vung vợt’s lỗi tolerance. The few aspects of elite swings like Rafael Nadal’s thuận tay that are directly responsible for their unparalleled consistency and quality. Daniil Medvedev has a uniquely high take back and loopy thuận tay vung vợt, but since it is fundamentally sound, it’s still extremely effective. This website is your place to learn those fundamentals.

Each article, be it on the thuận tay, the giao bóng, di chuyển, chiến lược, or coaching, focuses on the lõi aspects of that concept that are universal across all high level play.

Occasionally, when discussing something like the thuận tay, we’ll wade into some personal preference optimizations, like Juan Martin Del Potro’s looping đưa vợt ra sau, or some non-harmful (for those players) personal idiosyncrasies, like Medvedev’s uniquely spacious đưa vợt ra sau, but mostly we will discuss the lõi fundamentals that every world class thuận tay shares.

Our goal is to distinctly and clearly separate stylistic choices, which vary greatly from player to player, from the foundational, biomechanical principles that are universal among all elite strokes. There are many great ways to hit a thuận tay, but they all share certain crucial traits.

Those traits are what we tập trung on here, as they are the traits that create hard, heavy, lỗi tolerant strokes. 1 COMMENT: Poida July 21, 2021 So in other words, the cổ tay is loose and moves freely but the vợt/cẳng tay relationship of 90-135 degrees integrity must be maintained? It seems the cổ tay plays a role in getting this relationship or am I mistaken? Answer: Glad it was helpful! As for the 90-135 degree góc, two things.

First, I’ve updated the image on our “Two Rules that Govern Every Shot” article to show the góc we’re talking about. https://faulttoleranttennis.com/two-rules-that-govern-every-shot/ Second, you can go on YouTube and pause any video of a thuận tay – Fed, Rafa, Novak – any of them, at the end of their đưa vợt ra sau. You’ll see their vợt and cẳng tay 90 degrees from each other.

As they twist forward, their vợt naturally lags behind their hand, and then whips around their cẳng tay, all while that góc remains (opening up a little, but non volitionally).

The Athletic Ready vị trí

With almost every skill in tennis, there exists a prevailing myth that natural talent is the primary factor driving success. You’ll frequently hear tennis commentators, awestruck after observing the latest feat of athletic brilliance, remark candidly that said feat is “something you’re born with” or that it “can’t be taught.” For the most part, though, that’s simply not not the case.

Sure, if you’re born with a 50 cm vertical jump (~20 inch), you’re not going to double it to 100 cm through training and effort. But vertical jump is actually one of the purest metrics we have for innate explosive potential; it’s pretty darn simple. Most real athletic feats – things like throwing a baseball, tackling an opponent, or moving on a tennis sân – require a lot of coordination and practice to get the various parts of the body’s chuỗi động học linking up efficiently.

Of course the “natural” athlete, all else being equal, is going to perform better at these athletic activities than a less “natural” athlete, but in practice all else isn’t equal. Natural, genetic potential for explosive di chuyển does exist, but most people play so far below their genetic potential that this limitation is mostly irrelevant. You can play faster. di chuyển, just like everything else in tennis, is primarily about kỹ thuật, and only secondarily about natural, God-given talent.

The (Real) Ready vị trí isn’t Trivial I know, I know – you’ve heard about the “ready vị trí” before. “Bend your đầu gối;” we all know what we’re supposed to do. I thought that too, until the first time I felt what the athletic vị trí really feels like. And on that day, I was immediately the fastest I’d ever been.

The secret to unlocking your own latent athleticism lies in efficient muscle recruitment; if your hamstrings, glutes, adductors, and back muscles are all firing as you move, you’ll be fast. If they aren’t, you’ll be slow. And the first key to getting these prime movers to, well, move you is priming them by waiting in a proper athletic ready vị trí. So giao lại’s go over, in detail, the critical fundamentals underpinning a correct athletic vị trí.

Apply them, and almost immediately you’ll start reacting to balls faster than you thought was possible. A Muscle Recruitment Problem Explosive di chuyển, both forward and lateral, is facilitated by the large muscles in the legs. The more muscle mass you can engage, the quicker you’ll move.

But given our natural sedentary state, and the grotesquely subpar (and often non-existent) training regiments that most non-professional athletes employ, it’s actually a very non-trivial feat to nạp and fire these muscles correctly. Many of today’s athletes are quad dominant, likely due to the vast amount of sitting we do in our every day activities.

Further exacerbating this problem: in training, squatting above parallel fails to stress the critical non-quad muscles, preventing them from strengthening along with the quads. As a result, the muscles of the posterior chain – the glutes, hamstrings, lower back, and spinal erectors – are proportionally both weak and unused. So it’s not surprising that most players fail to fully utilize them while moving on a tennis sân. This failure drastically decreases quickness, thăng bằng, and speed.

Engage the Posterior Chain

The most common sticking point for efficient di chuyển is an athlete’s ability to engage the pulling muscles of the hamstring, glutes, and lower back, all of whom also play a critical role in increasing explosive lực off the ground. While the quad does also play a major role in sân speed – as the primary đầu gối extender, it clearly aids in pressing off the ground – most people engage their quads properly already.

For the anatomically curious, here’s a quick đường cuối sân explanation of how the posterior chain muscles function. The Hamstrings Olympic gold medalist Usain Bolt, post gia tốc phase of his sprint, driving himself forward via a forceful contraction of his hamstring. When your hamstrings contract, they shorten the distance between your calf and your back. The primary result: your calf is pulled towards your femur.

When this contraction takes place while the foot is on the ground, it generates a lực that drives you forward. The hamstring also has the effect of decreasing your leg-torso góc. This is why you can stretch your hamstrings by straightening your legs and bending over. The hamstrings are longest when the back is straight, and you’ll be able to feel that during the stretch; the straighter you keep your back as you bend over, the more your hamstrings will be stretched.

During a sprinter’s peak stride, explosive pulling from the hamstrings is doing most of the work. As the hamstring contracts, the ground is pushed backwards, away from the sprinter, and the sprinter is thereby driven forwards. The Glutes The glutes are the prime straighteners of the hông-torso góc. During lockout portion of the the deadlift, the glutes contract, driving the hông forward and straightening the leg-torso góc.

They are at their longest in the fetal vị trí, and at their shortest when the hông-torso góc is totally straight. Actually, some people are flexible enough that a full contraction of their glutes leads to a hông-torso góc of more than 180 degrees; they’re actually bent backwards a little. A good illustration of glute contraction is the lockout portion of the deadlift, during which the lifter drives their hông forwards, fully straightening their hông-torso góc while under a nạp.

As a result of this straightening, the weight is pulled up off of the floor into the lockout vị trí. With regards to explosive di chuyển, again assuming the feet are in contact with the ground, flexing the glutes and straightening this góc creates a lực driving the body way from the feet in what every direction you’re leaning. The “glutes” are actually a few different muscles working in conjunction.

Different lateral movements are facilitated by different individual gluteal muscles, but each is involved in some sort of straightening at the hông. The Lower Back and Spinal Erectors Roger Federer’s upper and lower back musculature maintaining a totally neutral cột sống as he stabilizes his body in chuẩn bị for a trái tay cắt.

The lower back and spinal erectors stabilize the lumbar during these explosive movements, facilitating an efficient chuyển of lực from the prime movers in the legs into di chuyển. If they’re weak or loose, much of this lực will be lost. When the back rounds due to weak or non-recruited back musculature, the off-the-ground lực is physically damped – since the back is non-rigid, some of the lực goes into compressing it instead of driving the body away from the ground.

Additionally, the back isn’t providing much of it’s own lực to aid the di chuyển, stabilizing and assisting the contraction of the legs. A tight, strong lower back and tight, strong spinal erectors prevent the torso from caving over, and prevent the back from rounding.

In doing so, they also protect the athlete from back and cột sống injury – the more rigid and strong the structure is during di chuyển, the more aligned the cột sống will be while under nạp, and the safer both the cột sống and the muscles supporting it will be. Develop Proprioceptive Awareness Much of the differential in players’ lateral di chuyển speed is explained by the effective strength of their posterior chain.

Not just how strong it is, but also how well the athlete is able to recruit that strength to the goal of exploding off the ground. Learn to feel these muscles. To get into the proper athletic ready vị trí, sit your hông back and low. As you do, hinge forward at the hông while keeping your back straight; you’ll be bent over, which is correct. Your đầu gối will bend a little, but keep your weight balanced over either the giữa of your foot, or the ball of it, depending on the situation.

A quick note on your optimal thăng bằng point – If your lower back or heels hurt after you play, shift your weight forward. If your đầu gối hurt, shift your weight backward. In your ready vị trí, feel for the light muscle contraction of the glutes, hamstrings, and adductors (your butt, the back of your thigh, and the inside of your groin).

If those muscles are lightly tensed, you’ll be able to explode to the ball the second you see where it’s coming; without having to consciously think about it, your body will recruit all of that muscle to đường bóng you off the ground. Pre-nạp the Leg Muscles Think about how you jump. In order to explosively extend your legs, first you have to bend them. Lateral explosive di chuyển in tennis is very similar to jumping, but at a non-vertical góc.

The key in tennis is to pre-nạp the legs (and the posterior chain specifically) by preparing in a vị trí such that the muscles of the posterior chain are already a little loaded, ready to elongate the đầu gối and hông góc explosively at any time. It’s far quicker to explode sideways off of legs already bent and hông that are already hinged, than it is to do that bending and hinging before you explode.

But maybe more importantly, pre-đang nạp your posterior chain gives your body a proprioceptive cue that those muscles should be used. A small amount of tension in those muscles helps communicate to your brain that it’s their turn to fire. The more you’re able to lightly engage the large, valuable muscles of the glutes, hamstrings, adductors, and back while you’re waiting, the more naturally your subconscious will recruit recruit the during explosive lateral di chuyển that follows.

Putting it All Together As we’ve discussed throughout the article, using and feeling the non-quad muscles isn’t trivial for most people, at least not at first. Understanding the light tension you should be feeling is the first step; actually feeling that tension, and developing leg muscles strong enough to support it, is the second. David Ferrer shows off his impressive leg strength as he widens his base and tilts his torso, lowering his vợt to pick up a bóng thấp.

The squat and the deadlift are the best tools for both strengthening the posterior chain and becoming proprioceptively aware of it. These lifts aren’t trivial to learn, but training your entire body with them will pay dividends not just in tennis, but in your overall health and well-being as well. If you have access to a quality strength coach, take lợi thế of it.

If not, things will be more difficult, but with the vast expanse of online resources available and a camera, anyone can master these lifts if they’re willing to put in the time. For some, you’ll get out on the sân, try to feel your glutes and hamstrings, and your body will quickly know what to do. For others, it’ll be a process to get there. But everyone can get there.

Learn to automatically engage these muscles in the ready vị trí, and you’ll be flying off the spot at lightning speed as you phản ứng, without even having to think about it. Foot Striking – The Overlooked Key To Speed The human foot is a masterfully designed piece of biological machinery. It’s job – to safely absorb and chuyển the lực generated when our bodies hit, and then press off, the ground. Lucky for us, it’s really, really good at this.

Unfortunately, do to some questionable decisions by our modern footwear companies (I’m looking at you, Nike), lots of our athletes, both young and old, are very bad at harnessing this natural biomechanical lợi thế. Last year, a 12 year old female student of mine showed up with some new shoes. To say these things were clunky would be like saying water is wet.

I could tell the second she started moving in them that this wasn’t going to work – not only did she look extremely slow and awkward, but I was genuinely worried we might not even make it through the lesson without her rolling her ankle over one of those ridiculously elevated soles. So I told her to take off her shoes. And her di chuyển immediately improved. Not just a little, either. The second she started playing barefoot (in socks, of course), I swear she was moving like Roger Federer.

She was light on her feet, elegantly dancing her way out to the ball, striking it cleanly, and then taking precise, cushioned deceleration steps as she changed direction and recovered back to the giữa. cảm giác sâu and Foot Striking If you saw this girl play in her 2 inch heels, you’d think she was naturally slow. A kinda tall, kinda awkward, unathletic 12 year old. But you’d have been wrong.

This girl had awesome natural control over her feet, she just didn’t have the cảm giác sâu to use it while hopping around in her cushy cinder blocks. A shoe like this is a recipe for slow feet and rolled ankles. Just don’t do it. When you insert two inches of cushioned rubber between your foot and the ground, your brain receives no information about how that foot is interacting with the ground during the strike. All of that lực information gets lost in the sole of the shoe.

And with it goes much of the natural springyness of the arch as well. On the other hand, when you’re barefoot, your brain gets a very precise account of what happens during each strike, and it can adjust accordingly. Your brain won’t giao lại you hurt yourself by banging your feet into the ground improperly. You’ll find that, within minutes of hopping around or jogging barefoot, your striking will naturally adjust to a forefoot or midfoot, shock absorbing kỹ thuật.

The Forefoot Strike The key to biomechanical efficiency with the foot is to leverage the forefoot strike (or for some people, a midfoot strike); strike the ground with the ball of your foot first. This allows your arch to act as both a shock absorber and a spring. Your foot is designed for this. If you strike the way nature intended, your foot (and leg) architecture will naturally and painlessly absorb the lực, and then chuyển that lực into springyness off the ground.

The impact will be efficiently dissipated throughout your foot and leg muscles, and as a result your joints won’t hurt, and you’ll be faster. Given our current footwear market and sedentary conditions, this striking may not come naturally to everyone. A few minutes of striking practice is a great way to warm up your feet before you play, both the muscles and your brain’s connection to them.

Changing Direction with Forefoot Strikes Roger Federer changing direction to trở lại vị trí after hitting a rộng thuận tay. He rotates his hông back towards the giữa, and thus uses regular, runner-like forefoot strikes to generate lực off the ground. Tennis requires a lot of lateral di chuyển – running right, then quickly changing direction and running left. Even for this lateral di chuyển, if you have enough time, forefoot strikes should be used to change direction.

To accomplish this, rotate your hông back towards the direction you’d like to trở lại vị trí. This allows you to, from your feet’s perspective, run that way as if you were just running straight, and thus you’ll naturally strike the ground similar to if you’re running. Mimicking running strikes wherever possible is ideal, because the foot isn’t nearly as adapted to lateral pivots as it is to running.

Transposing lateral di chuyển into front-to-back di chuyển by first rotating the hông toward your destination is very helpful. Lateral Foot Strikes Lateral foot strikes are also fine, but they aren’t quite as natural for your foot. Your foot is best equipped for absorbing lực that is parallel to the way your toes are pointing – usually you’re running forward and your toes are pointing forward.

When you pivot on a foot – say, your foot is facing the lưới and you push off to the left – the lực is orthogonal to your toes, and your foot’s anatomy doesn’t cán vợt it nearly as well. Federer reversing his đà to trở lại vị trí back to the giữa, taking one pivot midfoot strike step (circled) and rotating his hông. Only one pivot step is used in the entire phục hồi vị trí; his first step with his left foot will be a regular forefoot strike. There are a few consequences of this.

First, you want to minimize the number of these strikes you use as you trở lại vị trí; as soon as possible during the process, get your hông turned and start using regular, anatomically advantageous forefoot strikes pointing in the direction you’re moving. Second, don’t put too much lực into any one lateral step. Again, your foot isn’t really designed for this orthogonal striking, so don’t overdo it. Never stop all of your đà with a single lateral forefoot strike.

Your body probably won’t giao lại you, but still, be aware of it. If you’re sprinting and need to stop on a dime, you’re going to have to heel strike (and probably slide, we’ll get to that later), because putting that much lực into a forefoot strike when your foot isn’t parallel to the lực will injure your ankle.

But assuming you have enough time to take a few steps, but still want to take a lateral step or two, just be sure to use 3 or 4 small steps to decelerate, with each step slowing the body a little bit. The Role of Heel Striking What if I really really need to stop and trở lại vị trí? Then you’re going to have to heel strike. Tennis is different from running in that sometimes it requires a heel strike for optimal performance.

The best strike for stabilizing and absorbing the lực of a sharp change of direction, often a slide, is a heel strike. Strike with the inside of the heel and allow the rest of the foot to come down after it. Djokovic pulled out extremely rộng for a thuận tay; his right heel will be the first part of his foot to hit the ground, followed quickly by the rest of his foot. The strike happens very far out from his khối tâm.

When you have to heel strike, try to get the heel as far from your khối tâm as possible. This will reduce the impact on the heel itself. Keep your base as rộng and low as possible, and stick that foot out as far as your groin can support. Just like with every other strike, the more of the impact your posterior chain absorbs, the less goes into your foot – and in this case, your heel.

Since, on a heel strike, you don’t have your brilliantly designed arch cushioning the impact, utilizing the legs muscles is crucially important. Not everyone should implement heel striking into their game. In order for heel striking to do anything other than hurt your feet, ankles, and đầu gối, you need very strong legs and enough flexibility to get them rộng enough to stay balanced while you heel strike. If your legs are too weak, or too inflexible, don’t heel strike.

Just take the extra steps, and use many light forefoot strikes to trở lại vị trí. You need to heel strike far away from the giữa of your body in order for it to be safe and effective; that’s only possible with very strong legs, and a well engaged posterior chain. Foot Striking and Quickness Proper striking utilizes the arch’s natural lực absorption ability to maximize your lực off the ground after each foot strike. Start implementing it, and you’ll see an immediate boost of speed.

Quickness around the sân is primarily about three things: The athletic ready vị trí Playing low and rộng Proper foot striking Of these, foot striking may be the most counter-intuitive at first, since modern footwear has destroyed many people’s natural inclination towards the forefoot strike. Still, it’s a worthy goal to trở lại vị trí it. Practice for a few minutes before each session, and you’ll start to see results on the sân in no time. Which thuận tay tư thế is Best?

In the tư thế mở, the feet are parallel with the lưới, and contact is made about an arm’s length away from where the hitting foot starts. tư thế is similar to cầm vợt – it doesn’t matter that much. With an open, semi-open, or tư thế trung tính, a player can correctly turn away from the ball, prepare the hand, and then explosively rotate back towards the ball during the forward vung vợt. Many players will use different stances on different forehands throughout the trận đấu.

It’s mostly a matter of personal preference; each player should determine what feels best for their particular body. That said, there are still some pitfalls to avoid when it comes to thuận tay tư thế. Get the hông Back Around The lỗi tolerance of thuận tay contact is maximized when the hông square to the target.

That means that if you initiate your vung vợt from something like a tư thế trung tính, in which your hông are pointed sideways during chuẩn bị, you need to ensure you get them rotated all the way back around by contact. In a tư thế bán mở, the feet are set on a diagonal to the target, and body xoay is needed to reach the optimal điểm tiếp xúc. This is totally fine, of course – that xoay hông will generate lots of tốc độ đầu vợt.

But always understand that the reason to use this tư thế trung tính is so that you can perform that explosive xoay hông. If you have a habit of not rotating your hông as you vung vợt, then if you prepare using a tư thế trung tính, your hông will end up still sideways at contact, which greatly hampers the cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance. The best solution for this, of course, is to train yourself to lead your vung vợt with xoay hông, but a good stop gap would be to temporarily switch to a fully tư thế mở.

With a fully tư thế mở, when you don’t rotate your hông during the vung vợt, your hông point at your target at contact. Using Many Stances chuẩn bị exists to prepare for the forward vung vợt. The forward vung vợt you’ll want to attempt will vary based on the situation. You’ll want to use varying degrees of xoay hông on varying shots, and thus you should begin with varying stances. In a tư thế trung tính, the feet are set perpendicular to the lưới.

The optimal điểm tiếp xúc is far from the back foot’s initial placement, and therefore a lot of body xoay is required to reach it. The important point here is that the feet are not what’s critical – what’s critical is where the hông are aligned. The hông have freedom to turn while the feet remain planted, but only so far. You can utilize xoay hông out of any tư thế, and you can fail to utilize xoay hông out of any tư thế. tư thế, like cầm vợt, ultimately is a matter of personal comfort.

Just ensure that whatever tư thế you choose, your hông are square to your target by the time you reach contact. A Warning About the tư thế đóng Earlier, I mentioned that the open, semi-open, and neutral stances are all fine when it comes to thuận tay tư thế chuẩn bị. What I didn’t include was the tư thế đóng. That’s because it’s quite difficult to get the hông all the way around from a tư thế đóng.

It’s doable – if you đường bóng hard off of your hitting leg’s glute and allow that hitting leg to theo sau around your body, you can get your hông around, but this isn’t a very natural feeling motion and, for most players, should be avoided. In a tư thế đóng, the back foot is behind the front front foot, relative to the thân. So much xoay is needed to reach optimal contact that this tư thế is not recommended for the modern thuận tay.

Most players who play the thuận tay with a tư thế đóng don’t do it because they want extra space to roi vung their hông around; instead, they just leave their hông under-rotated during contact and thereby destroy their cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance. Instead of trying to coach these students to use their hông more, it’s probably better to start by having them play the thuận tay from a tư thế trung tính instead.

One area in which the tư thế đóng does excel is thăng bằng – it’s a tư thế from which it’s very natural to sit low and remain stable. This makes it a viable option on low forehands, but, again, ensure that the hông get back around to the lưới by contact.

Your back foot’s theo sau provides a good indicator of how well you’re rotating your hông out of a tư thế đóng – if it follows-through around your body after the cú đánh, resulting in you ending in an tư thế mở, you’re probably getting sufficient xoay on your vung vợt. 5 Ways Elite Players Make Their Own Luck Many events in tennis appear lucky. The ball dribbles over the lưới for a điểm thắng. Someone hits a second giao bóng giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp on the line.

A thuận tay is shanked high and sâu, and ends up landing on the đường cuối sân instead of flying out. From a certain point of view, these are clearly examples of a player just getting lucky, and yet these “lucky” events are not uniformly distributed across the player base. We không to righteously assure ourselves of what should have happened – that lưới dribbler should have hit the lưới. That second giao bóng giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp should have been a lỗi giao bóng kép. That shanked thuận tay should have gone out.

Yet, mysteriously, against good players, these negative events that should counter thăng bằng the corresponding “lucky” ones happen far less often than seems fair. This isn’t a fluke; elite players cause this disparity between "lucky" and "unlucky" results. They make their own luck by employing an accurate chiến lược and hitting with lỗi tolerant kỹ thuật. Here are 5 examples of how. 1.

lưới Dribblers – But They Rarely Hitting The lưới Elite players aim high enough above the lưới that they rarely hit it. Hitting the ball into the lưới immediately loses the point, and thus accurate chiến lược necessitates choosing a shot for which very few of the potential outcomes of that shot involve a lưới error. This means that when an elite player hits a lưới dribbler, you are not seeing a result that’s anywhere close to the giữa of that shot’s distribution; you are seeing an outlier.

The lưới dribbler is one of the most inaccurate shots that that particular shot selection could have generated, maybe one of the 10% or even 5% of results farthest from the giữa of the target. A well chosen target – high enough over the lưới that nearly all of the probability mass is over it, with only a few shots in the outer ring hitting it.

So, no, it isn’t true that “the ball should have hit the lưới.” In reality, it’s far more accurate to say the ball should have gone well over the lưới; after all that’s what happens 9/10 times this player attempts this shot. By aiming high over the lưới, an elite player ensures that even the bottom of their distribution doesn’t contain many misses, which is why they miss into the lưới far less than other players, while still receiving the same amount of lưới dribbling good fortune. 2.

Second giao bóng Aces – But They Rarely lỗi giao bóng kép A high quality second giao bóng target from a relatively accurate player. The entirety of the densest and second densest regions is 100% in. Serves on the line are possible, but uncommon. Faults are exceedingly rare. You’re going to notice a theme here. “Luck” in tennis constitutes a player rolling an exceptionally positive outcome that’s on the outside edge of their shot’s distribution of outcomes.

An elite player who hits a second giao bóng giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp was not aiming for a second giao bóng giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp; an giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp was not the median outcome. Instead, they were aiming to a high margin target, for which only the outside edge of the distribution is either on the line, or over it. This target ensures, first and foremost, that misses are exceedingly rare.

Since a second giao bóng functions very similarly to a typical đánh qua lại ball – a miss loses you the point immediately – targets for second serves are chosen based on similar principles to targets for đánh qua lại shots. The primary difference is that, unlike on đánh qua lại shots, on a second giao bóng, your opponent knows which half of the sân you’re going to hit into.

This makes playing a second giao bóng offensively very difficult, and thus it’s even more beneficial than usual to be conservative on a second giao bóng, since the benefit of aiming closer to the line is lessened. Therefore, good players use conservative targets on second serves. When they hit a second giao bóng giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp, it’s because they missed their target by a good amount – it was an unlikely outcome on the outside edge of their shot’s distribution.

If they’d aimed to a worse target instead, a more chủ động tấn công target closer to the line to start with, that same di chuyển which resulted in an giao bóng ăn điểm trực tiếp with the conservative target would have likely resulted in a lỗi. 3. They Mishit The Ball In – But They Rarely Actually Miss When the thuận tay (as well as everything else) is struck with correct cơ học, it is lỗi tolerant. This means that small inaccuracies in chuẩn bị or execution won’t cause a miss.

By creating a long hitting zone with a consistent string góc, we maximize the amount of the vung vợt during which, if contact is made, the ball will go in. A shank mishit that goes in is at the very end of this viable hitting zone; if we’d mistimed the shot just a little bit worse, we would have missed. The more lỗi tolerant the thuận tay, the more shanks will go in. Rafa Nadal is famous for mishit forehands turning into sâu xoáy trên lobs.

These “lucky shanks” are yet another benefit of proper, lỗi tolerant thuận tay kỹ thuật. 4. Winners on the Line – But They Rarely Hit It Out Elite players aren’t aiming for the line. They’re aiming to a target for which the line is on the outside edge of the distribution. Sensing a mẫu here? Good players aim for targets such that they don’t miss, and occasionally “get lucky” and hit an even better shot than they were aiming for.

Instead of missing when they mistime the ball slightly, they hit an even better shot than usual. While this beneficial effect of more conservative targets is counterbalanced by the average result from those targets being closer to the giữa of the sân, in most cases this difference is trivial, and the downside of it is more than made up for by the vastly decreased amount of misses at the outer ends of the distribution. 5.

Mishit bắt lưới Winners – But They Rarely Miss Volleys Effective volleying is about getting as close to the lưới as possible. When we are close to the lưới, margins are much bigger. First, we get to hit the ball higher. This means we can hit down on the ball far more without missing. Further, we can hit the ball wider, because, since we can hit it down more, it’s easier to get the ball to land down in the sân before skirting out.

We can also hit the ball harder, because, again, the downward trajectory will prevent it from sailing long. The margin created by aggressively closing the lưới creates the lỗi tolerance that allows frequent mishit bắt lưới winners. So on a bắt lưới, our goal is to minimize the distance between us and the lưới at contact time. We manage this in a few ways.

Start close to the lưới Don’t set up at the đường giao bóng by choice; only bước chân phân tách there if your opponent is about to hit, and you were too slow to get closer. Move your body through your volleys Sure, if the ball is rifled at you, you’re going to have to just stick your vợt out, but, in general, volleys involve moving the entire body forward. In fact, with respect to the body, the vợt moves very little.

A player who employs this forward body di chuyển routinely contacts volleys closer to the lưới than one who doesn’t. If the ball is slow, explode forward Unlike at the đường cuối sân, we never wait for the ball at lưới. Any time the ball spends in flight is time we can use to sprint forward and close the distance between us and the lưới. Each additional step towards the lưới makes the bắt lưới that much easier.

The margin created by aggressively closing the lưới creates the lỗi tolerance that allows frequent mishit bắt lưới winners. Consider a common kind of mishit bắt lưới điểm thắng – the ball hits the cổ vợt of the vợt and becomes an unintentional but successful drop-shot. This exact same cú đánh execution, if performed at the đường giao bóng instead of right on top of the lưới, would have resulted in an an ugly looking error into the bottom of the lưới, rather than a won point.

chiến lược and kỹ thuật Create Luck Since tennis is random, luck is a natural part of the game, but the nature of that luck is often misunderstood. Consider a situation in which Player A has a 10% chance of hitting a điểm thắng, while Player B has a 30% chance of hitting that same điểm thắng. If both players succeed in hitting the điểm thắng, Player B has gotten far less lucky than Player A while achieving the same result.

It would be fair to call both of their winners "lucky shots," since, for both players, the chance of the điểm thắng was well under 50%, and yet the fact remains that Player B is going to hit this "lucky shot" far more often than Player A will. "Lucky" is a very imprecise term. Elite players make their own luck by routinely putting themselves in positions where it’s easy to get lucky, while simultaneously avoiding positions from which it’s easy to get unlucky.

They humbly observe the results of their attempted shots and put themselves in positions where those results produce maximum benefit. And on the off chance they do win a critical point on a lưới dribbler or a mishit điểm thắng, they simply put up their vợt in a (faux) apology and move on with the trận đấu unbothered, since, after all, elite players really, really want to win.

Gold: “Roll Your vợt Over The Ball” Ah, "roll your vợt over it" – the epitome of everything that’s wrong with tennis coaching, our Coaching Cues Hall of Shame champion, and probably the sentence that’s caused more thuận tay errors and cổ tay pain than any other ever spoken. giao lại’s begin with a story. Last year, I worked with a 14 year old girl who had one of the most mismanaged thuận tay strokes I’ve ever seen.

Her problem: “roll your cổ tay over/around the ball.” She turned to me, scoffed, and asked, “Why do I suck?” Rarely, if ever, could she make two forehands in a row. Her cổ tay hurt every time she hit the ball, and she wasn’t enjoying the game. There was a point during one of our lessons when I asked her, “Anything not making sense, any questions?” She turned to me, scoffed, and asked, “Why do I suck?” Multiple times during our lessons, she cried, and I don’t blame her.

She was an obedient student who did exactly what her coach told her to do. Think about it from her perspective – The coach clearly tells her how to strike the ball. When she hits it that way, she can’t make two shots in a row, and her cổ tay hurts every shot. The coach can’t be wrong, of course, so what’s the only variable left? Clearly there’s something wrong with her; the only logical conclusion is that she just inherently sucks. It’s an easy conclusion for a 14 year old adolescent to draw.

And unfortunately, a 14 year old girl has no practical way of identifying when her coach has unwittingly set her up for failure. So for her sake, giao lại’s set the record straight on the most egregious misconceptions fueling the most damaging cue in all of tennis coaching. The following is an explanation of how vợt xoay and cổ tay di chuyển actually function during a useful, lỗi tolerant thuận tay.

The vợt Rolling Over Is Part of the theo sau First, one of the reasons this mess got started in the first place: pros “roll their rackets over” when they hit forehands. This is part of the theo sau of the cú đánh. Not the vung vợt, the theo sau. It’s difficult to see the difference in real time, but search up some high fps slow motion footage of top forehands, and you’ll see that the string góc hardly changes through the hitting zone; it only changes after contact.

Often, this xoay is not even caused by forces the player generated themself, but rather by the lực of the ball’s impact on the vợt, which imparts a torque on it. vợt xoay in ALL Planes is thụ động Another common misconception is that players actively engage the cổ tay musculature to roi vung the vợt through the contact zone. This is also false. The cổ tay is thụ động at contact, merely acting as a hinge.

The năng lượng generated by the rest of the kenetic chain whips the vợt around in the cổ tay; it’s not the tiny cổ tay muscles doing that work, and if you try to use them for that, you’ll injure yourself. xoay Through Contact Does NOT Alter the String góc Through contact, the string góc is maintained in order to maximize our margin for error.

Maintenance of the string góc through contact is one of the primary drivers of lỗi tolerance in the thuận tay – the cú đánh’s ability to withstand small mistakes without failing.

This pernicious myth explained in The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay: If we allow the vợt-face góc to change dramatically through contact, say, “rolling over” such that the dây are pointing towards the sky at the beginning, and towards the ground after contact, then our margin for error evaporates; if we hit the ball slightly too early, our overly open dây will send the ball flying out, and if we hit the ball a little too late, our closed dây will smother it down into the lưới.

If we don’t time it perfectly, we’ll miss. By instead keeping the góc consistent through the contact zone, we allow ourselves to consistently strike the ball with the proper up-and-forward diagonal contact, even in cases when the vung vợt is slightly mistimed. “Rolling Over” Does Not Create xoáy trên This one is a simple physics myth. Just because the ball is “rolling” doesn’t mean your vợt also needs to roll to create that motion.

xoáy trên is generated by a strong, upward frictional lực between the dây and the back of the ball at contact. The free body diagram here is from the Understanding xoáy trên Contact section of The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. This is how xoáy trên is generated – by transient forces that act on the ball for a few milliseconds during contact – not by somehow mirroring the ball motion you want to cause with your vợt. This is true of all spin, by the way, not just xoáy trên.

You don’t cắt a giao bóng by “carving around the ball.” You don’t hit a xoáy dưới shot by “scooping under the ball”. That’s not how physics works. Spin is created by friction, by a transient lực which lasts a few milliseconds and applies a torque to the ball, causing it to rotate.

Our goal is to facilitate this contact in as lỗi tolerant a manner as possible, and that means not rotating our vợt face unnecessarily through contact, dramatically shrinking the contact zone during which a quality shot will be produced.

Rotating The vợt Correctly (Not By Rolling it Over) vợt xoay is an essential part of the modern thuận tay, and it is vợt xoay that ends up generating a large majority of the spin during the strike, but not because getting the ball to rotate necessitates that the vợt must rotate. The vợt xoay during the shot dramatically increases the vertical tốc độ đầu vợt at contact, resulting in a larger upward lực on the back of the ball.

That vertical lực is what generates the spin. The xoay we use to increase that vertical lực does not close the dây through contact; that would ruin our consistency. Here’s one final passage from the The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay that sums up correct xoay and hand behavior during thuận tay contact. Internalize it, and you’ll never be tempted to "roll your vợt over the ball" again.

From the section – lỗi Tolerant, Effective Contact: Correct xoay happens in a plane which does not effect the góc of the dây, and therefore it’s not a xoay which ruins our lỗi tolerance. Instead, the vợt rotates about the axis of our cẳng tay, and the cẳng tay rotates with it, while the string góc with respect to the ground remains constant. And again, this xoay is thụ động, not active.

The hand is relaxed, and the vợt, hand, and cẳng tay rotate as a result of forces generated during the rest of the vung vợt, not because the player is actively trying to twist the vợt around their cẳng tay using their cổ tay muscles. [Like the vợt motion is generating a cone and the cone axis, the cẳng tay, is moving, from right to left with thuận tay cú đánh and left to right with trái tay cú đánh]. Our girl from the beginning did get somewhat of a happy ending.

After two months of working together, her strokes were vastly improved, and, even more importantly, she ended each session pain free. Our average mini-tennis đánh qua lại length increased from less than three shots at the beginning, to roughly twenty by the end. She was quite fond of mini tennis once she’d mastered her new, simpler, lỗi tolerant contact.

Her feel for the ball in unique situations became much better, and, now that she was actually practicing the right cú đánh, her consistency improved as we drilled. Given where she could have ended up if we’d never connected, though the ride at times was rocky, I’d say things worked out pretty well in the end. 14 COMMENTS Rob Millsop March 18, 2021 I’d be interested in your opinion regarding where the player, utilizing xoáy trên, should tập trung on making contact on the ball itself.

Players have been told to brush up the backside of the ball–I would venture that makes as little sense as simply throwing a dart towards a dart board, versus focusing on the “bullseye”.

I could brush up on the ball and easily launch it over the back fence..so..at which spot on the ball itself would you advise your student to aim at with the face of their vợt….Lastly–in a typical trận đấu between accomplished players (4.5–5.5), what do you suppose the percentage of balls hit long versus into the lưới, would be?? My charted observations are that a quite a bit higher % of shots go long..than into the lưới. Thanks for your responses!

REPLY Johnny (FTF) May 21, 2021 I’ll address both. 1. I’m not sure that we have the athletic precision to try to hit the ball on a precisely specific place on the vợt, but certainly roughly thinking about hitting the điểm ngọt is a good idea. (Quick note – the optimal spot for a giao bóng, which is hit while the ball is stationary, is actually the “dead spot,” a few inches higher than the điểm ngọt.) In general, I do think that visualizing contact before you make it is a good idea. 2.

From a game theory perspective, if a player is trying to hit a điểm thắng, the same number of balls should be hit into the lưới and long. On đánh qua lại balls, on the other hand, more misses should be long. giao lại me explain. On winners, if you’re hitting more than 50% long, you can probably improve your success rate by moving your target lower over the lưới, and if you’re hitting more than 50% into the lưới, you should move your target higher. That’s pretty simple.

On đánh qua lại balls, however, the math is different. First of all, just from a technical perspective, lots of lưới misses are simply caused by bad kỹ thuật, bad contact, lazy di chuyển, etc, and once you get up to the 4.5 level and higher, those things are far less common. That said, even once you *have* mastered correct kỹ thuật, lưới misses are uncommon because *near* lưới-misses also suck. They become ngắn balls which immediately allow your opponent to take offense.

Near long-misses, on the other hand, are actually great – you’re the one who ends up on offense. This is why high level players aim sâu, and tend to miss long more than they miss ngắn. Good luck REPLY Poida July 20, 2021 Does the actual cổ tay góc change through the rackets xoay?

I suspect that a lot of people are messed up and confused about being told by many coaches to keep the cổ tay locked at 90degrees throughout the vung vợt and there are “training devices” sold that lực the cổ tay back Appreciate your thoughts and insights. REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 21, 2021 Good question.

The 90 degree training devices are definitely useful to get you about 80% of the way to a very technically sound thuận tay, because they lực you to use the bigger muscles in your body (not the cẳng tay and cổ tay muscles) to manipulate the vợt. But, yes, the cổ tay of most top players does unwind between 15-45 degrees throughout their motion. On most of Roger Federer’s forehands, for example, peak cổ tay trễ is around 90 degrees, and contact is around 135 degrees. This is NOT volitional.

He’s merely relaxing his arm and accelerating the vợt with his hông and abdominal muscles by twisting his body. This extra level of relaxation is what adds the extra spin and power that transforms a high quality, solid, trận đấu thuận tay into an elite weapon. The 90 degree insight is most helpful for touch shots (volleys, slices, drop shots, etc), and for adult beginners trying to get a feel for how to manipulate the vợt through space and have it actually work.

REPLY Poida July 26, 2021 Very helpful followup, much appreciated. I initially thought that those cổ tay training devices were detrimental, however, your answer has made me rethink this, “it gets you 80% of the way there.” Re Federer’s cổ tay trễ, wouldn’t 135degrees be hyper duỗi? I’ve heard he has incredibly flexible cổ tay duỗi, and that would explain the blistering speed and rpms, Nadal also appears to get the cổ tay into hyper duỗi.

In general, I’ve noticed that people with good cổ tay duỗi often develop good thuận tay ball shape, speed, and spin vận tốc. Does this correlate to the “hinge” aspect of the thuận tay? Or am I misinterpreting the hinge function of the cổ tay? REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 27, 2021 The looser the cổ tay, the less năng lượng from the thân is lost during the vung vợt. This means that loose cổ tay chuyển close to 100% of the strong muscles’ năng lượng into the vợt.

The consequence of the looseness is that there’s a large degree of writs lay back as the vợt is pulled forward. Most players’ initial problem is that they don’t understand how to accelerate the vợt with their body. Instead, they accelerate it with their arm, cẳng tay, hand, etc. The 90-degree-cổ tay-locker prevents you from doing this and forces you to accelerate the vợt with your body instead. That’s why it gets you 80% of the way there.

It forces you into the fundamental where you use the proper large movers to đường bóng the vợt. Once you’ve already mentally cemented using your legs and abs to đường bóng the vợt, you can get an extra roi vung from loosening up the cổ tay and allowing a natural trễ and bật action, but that’s non volitional and attempting to do it consciously is why so many players have such awful strokes. REPLY Poida October 2, 2021 Thanks for the reply Johnny, sorry I missed this earlier.

Are there a few mental images and specific exercises one could do to break the habit of using the arm/hand to accelerate the vợt and feel the torso/legs doing the work? Trying to do it live ball is next to impossible, even using drop fed balls can “trick da brain” REPLY Johnny (FTF) October 2, 2021 By far the best exercise for feeling the torso/leg đường bóng if you can’t seem to get it is swinging something far heavier than the vợt.

A medicine ball on a string, for example, or a ball hopper (since you’ll probably have one lying around whatever tennis club you use). When you vung vợt something far heavier than the vợt in a thuận tay-like mẫu, it forces you to use your stronger muscles for gia tốc, because your brain will quickly realize it won’t be able to get things thing going with just your arm.

When you put the heavy thing down and go back to the vợt, usually the vung vợt will, all of the sudden, feel great, because all of those big muscles have been primed by the weighted swings. REPLY Poida October 3, 2021 This is a very helpful and interesting thread in that you can synthesize a lot of the content from the exchanges to cement ideas and actions from the book.

I think it’s so vital to understand the current cú đánh cơ học software in the players mind that’s driving the hardware, their body segments with respect to how they move the vợt in space and time. However, if a body segment won’t cooperate, ie. tight cổ tay, then the cơ học get compromised. Here’s something very interesting from one of your earlier replies: ”The looser the cổ tay, the less năng lượng from the thân is lost during the vung vợt.

This means that loose cổ tay chuyển close to 100% of the strong muscles’ năng lượng into the vợt. The consequence of the looseness is that there’s a large degree of writs lay back as the vợt is pulled forward. Most players’ initial problem is that they don’t understand how to accelerate the vợt with their body.

Instead, they accelerate it with their arm, cẳng tay, hand, etc” The analogy I’m thinking of here is that a tight cổ tay is like a kink at the end of a garden hose preventing the full flow of water to the nozzle (hand) from the tap (the big body part movers). It literally “chokes off” the vung vợt. The idea of using something heavy to learn to use lõi power is not only a good learning method but also a good strengthening one.

There’s some good video of Djokovic using a medicine ball to work on lõi strengthening. For the thuận tay, the medicine ball throws with two hands can’t create the proper arm feel from my experience. I like your reference to using a ball hopper or bóng nặng on a string. I’ve seen a tennis tube also used (Justine Henin academy) however I don’t think it was explained properly.

I’ve also seen a video where the coach said the feeling “is like throwing a bucket of water outwards” Here are some vung vợt ideas from Edgar Giffenig who has an international high performance coaching background: https://youtu.be/V0sTkxpT_zc https://youtu.be/n6XtxU1TLa4 REPLY Johnny (FTF) October 8, 2021 I like that analogy “it’s like throwing a bucket of water outwards.” I think that’s an accurate description of how it feels.

It’s actually possible to have a pretty good thuận tay with a tight cổ tay. It can be lỗi tolerant and effective, it just can’t generate the whipping spin or quite the same power as a loose-wristed one. Just set the cẳng tay and vợt at 90 degrees, still accelerate with the stable muscles of the lõi instead of the weak muscles of the arm, and you can hit a pretty decent shot without a lot of cổ tay action.

It’s a lot more like deflecting the ball towards your target than fully flinging your vợt through the hitting zone, but it totally works even up to, say, 4.5 adult đánh đơn, and definitely is good enough for adult 4.0.
