The 20/40/60/80/100/drill is very effective at helping players learn using the lõi to accelerate the arm/vợt system. It’s counterintuitive because logically it makes more sense to increase arm intensity to accelerate the vợt vs using the bigger muscles of the body.

So developing training exercises that create awareness and kinesthetic feel are needed to make it a habit and ingrain it as a stroking blueprint to achieve competency. Three More Examples of the Winning Attitude We’ve already discussed the common trap of incorrectly believing that winning is your goal, when, in reality, it’s something else.

Typically, these winning imposters look similar to winning at first glance (ex: hitting good shots), but are different enough from winning to frequently result in a suboptimal psychological orientation. In order to maximize our potential on the sân, we need to cultivate a true winning attitude. Here are three more examples of cases in which the goal of winning significantly outperforms the other common goals. Not Getting Crushed: You’re playing someone significantly better than you.

They make a few mistakes, combined with a few good shots from you, and you’re able to lose 6-1, 6-3. How much do those 4 games mean to you? Abusing Weakness: Your opponent can’t get it together and is missing every other trái tay out of neutral and even out of tấn công positions when playing a weak shot from you. How often do you hit it to his trái tay side? The Broken String: Your dây break in the giữa of the point. What do you do?

Like last time, we’ll go through and analyze the best mental attitude to foster in these situations, as well as some common pitfalls which can prevent us from getting there. 1. Not Getting Crushed If your opponent’s game is better than yours in every way, you’re going to lose, and probably badly. That’s not within your control. What is within your control is whether or not the trận đấu is productive competitive experience. This means practicing the skill of competing in the face of impossible odds.

We need to cultivate an attitude that will push us to the limit, even when our rational brain is telling us there’s no point. Even against a player much better than you, there are some patterns which will favor you, at least compared to other patterns. Maybe you only win 1/5 trái tay to trái tay exchanges, but the two of you actually have pretty similar forehands. Maybe his lưới game isn’t what it should be, and you don’t get punished as hard as you could for poor phòng thủ shots.

Whatever it is, keep pressing. Spend the entire trận đấu maximizing your win rate, however hopelessly small it is, because it’s that exact same skill that’s needed to win close matches. And remember, tennis is random, so you might just get lucky, if you can keep yourself in the fight. Never abandon the process of observing your performance in various patterns and adjusting your chiến lược accordingly.

This problem solving mindset, the determination to try to win until the end, is an essential part of the winning attitude, even in cases when winning really is impossible. If you have to, just pick a new goal. Win as many games, or even as many points as you possibly can. Just keep yourself competing.

Because here’s the thing – humans beings can do the impossible much more often than the ill-fitted word "impossible" would suggest, so we need to cultivate an attitude that will push us to the limit, even when our rational brain is telling us there’s no point. 2. Abusing Weakness Is it just as fun to hit your opponent 10 backhands, 8 of which they miss outright, as it is to strike 6 winners and 4 errors? That depends on your goals.

If your goal is to win, then winning 8/10 points is more fun than winning 6/10. But feeling an empathetic twinge in your khuỷu tay as you watch your opponent awkwardly shank late trái tay after late trái tay with very little hope of ever succeeding isn’t as glamorous as using your flowing strokes and dynamic di chuyển to take those balls to the sides of the sân and send them elegantly speeding past your opponent. To combat this thinking, convince yourself that winning is glamorous and losing is not.

That way, you won’t be tempted into “better looking” patterns that lower your win rate. Matches are for winning. Practice is for practice. And practice matches are for practicing winning. This particular issue is a little more nuanced than the others, because the chủ động tấn công mẫu really is a more useful skill than the “always hit it to the trái tay” mẫu.

However, the winning attitude is more important than even chủ động tấn công tennis, and therefore, in a trận đấu context, if you are given a mẫu with as high equity as the one described above (opponent missing 8/10 easy backhands), then you should be entering it close to 100% of the time, even when that particular mẫu isn’t very useful in general (because most players you play will have backhands). Matches are for winning. Practice is for practice. And practice matches are for practicing winning.

Practice matches are the tool by which we improve the parts of our game that aren’t optimal for helping us win yet. The key to effective practice matches is to maintain the winning attitude even when practicing a mẫu that you wouldn’t ordinarily use. This is accomplished by pre-committing to the patterns you want to practice and setting rules for yourself before you start, so that during the practice trận đấu you can just tập trung on winning.

Here’s an example: I’m going to giao bóng and bắt lưới on half of my service points. This will probably lower your win probability in the practice trận đấu (that’s why you’re practicing it, right?), but because you pre-committed to it, you can still 100% try to win within the rules you’ve set for yourself. The entire reason for playing a practice trận đấu instead of a real trận đấu is precisely to develop skills which currently lower your win percentage, but will raise it once mastered.

By pre-committing to practicing certain skills, you can do this effectively without having to sacrifice your winning attitude in the process. 3. The Broken String If you’ve been reading this long, you know the answer isn’t “jog to the lưới, get passed, then go change rackets.” Your response to a mid-point broken string is a good barometer of your winning attitude; if your first reaction is to give up on the point, you’re not thinking like a điểm thắng yet.

So for fun, giao lại’s analyze the situation with the winning attitude and win the point. You have two issues right now. First, you won’t be able to generate nearly as much xoáy trên as you’re used to because your string tension has just plummeted. Second, not only is your new mặt dây extremely sub optimal, but you’re also not used to it. This means that your degree of inaccuracy has skyrocketed.

Many plays that are normally too low percentage to use, suddenly become viable options when your dây are broken. To combat this, immediately move your targets in. Primarily use phẳng shots, occasionally some high-margin xoáy dưới shots, and only use xoáy trên as a last resort, since your xoáy trên habits will be the most effected by the broken mặt dây.

Again, on ALL shots, including the phẳng and xoáy dưới shots, use far more conservative targets than usual, since the ball will leave your dây in an unfamiliar way, leading to distributions of outcomes far larger than you’re accustomed to. Break out of neutral at the earliest reasonable time and go to lưới. Volleys are some of the least effected shots by a broken mặt dây.

Now, as I mentioned at the start, many players đọc this as “hit some awful shot, jog to lưới, and get passed.” That’s incorrect. What I said was: break out of neutral at the earliest reasonable time. Against almost every player, you can play a lot of neutral balls (and keep the point neutral) without using any spin. You don’t have to tank the entire point just because xoáy trên has been taken from you, but you should lower your expected winrate.

This means that many plays that are normally too low percentage to use, suddenly become viable options when your dây are broken. Any reasonable chip and charge, just take it and go. Any ball high enough to hit mostly phẳng, just aim at a big target, smack it, and go in. Is your opponent particularly bad at a certain kind of tấn công shot (trái tay cú tiếp cận, perhaps)? If things get a little rough, maybe just gift him one of those and see if he can execute it.

It’s not ideal, but our attitude hasn’t changed just because our circumstances have. We are still trying to win in the highest (though it won’t be high) percentage way possible, given the shots we have left: conservative target phẳng shots, xoáy dưới shots, and volleys. The Mamba Mentality I intentionally ended this article by talking about what, to an uninitiated observer, seems like the least important part of it – what to do when your dây break in the giữa of a point.

That’s because situations like broken dây determine who really wants to win, and who’s operating in another context. This goes for all strange situations, too, not just broken dây. What do you do if your hat falls off mid point? What if you slip and fall? Bad bounce? Sun spots in your eyes after serving? Do you hate playing in the wind? What is your true tập trung, and is it “how do I win this point”? I could explain the answers to all these questions individually, but that misses the point.

The point is the winning mindset. It unifies the answers to all of these things in a way that individual analysis never could. I’ll leave you with this. Kobe Bryant once missed a game điểm thắng because a flash from a camera in the crowd distracted him. The flashes probably weren’t allowed in the stadium.

Instead of complaining about it, or excusing himself for missing, all off-season he had his assistants stand behind the lưới and flash lights at him while he practiced shooting, so that he’d never miss in the same situation again.

Silver: “Point Your Butt Cap at the lưới” Our Coaching Cues Hall of Shame silver medal goes to a cue which creates unhelpful cổ tay tension and destroys effortless xoáy trên: "point your but cap at the lưới." The butt cap does point at the lưới early in the vung vợt, but that vị trí is a dynamic vị trí; it should not be entered volitionally.

TV Can Be Deceiving Many bad cues originate from watching the pros; to the untrained eye, it looks like Rafael Nadal is turning his vợt over through contact, even though he isn’t. As we watch on TV, there are two fundamental information barriers that routinely lead to bad coaching cues. It’s difficult to tell the exact timing of the movements that we’re seeing. Yes, Rafa’s dây did turn over, but that was part of the theo sau, not part of the vung vợt itself.

We can’t tell what the player did intentionally, via conscious effort, and what happened passively as an unconscious result of those intentional actions. The Butt Cap Myth The “point your butt cap at the lưới” fallacy originates from the second barrier. It’s cued because, at a certain point in the vung vợt, every professional player’s butt cap is in fact pointed towards the lưới. The problem? This butt cap pointing is thụ động, not active.

Our silver medalist is a pernicious misconception which causes tight cổ tay and slow swings. When our cổ tay is properly relaxed during the take back, our butt cap will point towards our body, not at the lưới. In order to lực the butt cap to point at the lưới instead, we would need to tighten and extend the cổ tay and pre-rotate the cẳng tay – this ruins the vung vợt, and can get you hurt.

The Butt Cap Reality As explained in the The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, adding tension to lực the butt cap towards the lưới ruins our cổ tay’s performance as a hinge, and thereby causes our tốc độ đầu vợt to crater. From the section – Relax: So instead of tensing the cổ tay and trying to extract some small modicum of lực from those tiny muscles, we completely relax it to use it as a hinge.

Instead of generating lực itself, the cổ tay acts as a hinge to chuyển năng lượng from the rest of the chuỗi động học into the vợt. And the looser it is, the less năng lượng is lost in this transference. This is why a tight cổ tay leads to such a slow vung vợt.

A tight cổ tay is an inefficient hinge that the vợt cannot freely roi vung around, and therefore it doesn’t matter how hard we đường bóng with our legs and thân to start the motion, most of that năng lượng is going to be lost before it ever gets transferred into the ball. When we relax during the vung vợt, the vợt will naturally trễ backwards as a result of quán tính; it wants to stay at rest when your hand pulls it forward.

This natural, non-volitional trễ is what results in the famous "cổ tay trễ vị trí" that’s so common on the tour, not any sort of intentional action. It’s a vị trí that’s entered without conscious effort. Attempting to consciously put your hand into the "cổ tay trễ vị trí" only creates unnecessary tension that prevents the cổ tay from effectively acting as a hinge to chuyển lực. When you, instead, relax during the vung vợt, the vợt does the job on it’s own.

So ignore where your butt cap is pointing during your cú đánh. It’s not a useful piece of information. When you take your vợt back, relax your hand. When you throw it out, up and through the ball, maintain that relaxation. Use just enough tension to maintain a consistent string góc through contact, and to prevent the vợt from flying out of your hand. Other than that, keep the cổ tay loose, throw it in the right direction, and it will perform properly during the vung vợt.

Do You Actually Like Winning? No really, do you? Because most of us instinctively think “well of course I like winning,” without taking the time to really internalize everything that entails. Many assume that they enjoy the winning itself, but instead actually enjoy distinctly different outcomes that only appear similar to winning, outcomes which are different enough to non-trivially alter your psychological orientation towards the trận đấu.

Three of the most common non-winning values are playing up to your own standards, hitting good shots, and engaging in a good competition, which are all similar to winning in many respects, but still different enough from it to cause systemic issues in your mental game. Test yourself to see where your true motivations lie with these thought experiments: 6-0 6-0: You’re playing someone significantly worse than you are. You hit a điểm thắng almost every other shot and win 6-0 6-0 without much trouble.

How much did you enjoy it? Beating The Off Day: You are playing someone who you know is better than you, but today they are having a significant off day. You beat them 6-3 6-4 in a competitive but one sided trận đấu. Are you happy you won? giao lại sân Guilt: The score is 5-7, 7-6, 6-6 and it’s 5-5 in the tiebreaker. You hit a trả bóng which clips the tape and dribbles over for a điểm thắng. You are now serving at 6-5 on điểm trận.

Will your “guilt” of lucking out the last point in any way effect your performance on this one? Each of these examples showcases a situation in which the distinction between winning and another similar but distinct value (e.g. playing well) has identifiable consequences in your thought patterns.

I’ll go through each and explain what your orientation towards it should be if your real goal is to win, as well as common psychological pitfalls I expect to see in players who think they want to win, but actually have other goals masquerading as winning itself. 1. 6-0 6-0 Winning players allow themselves to enjoy a 6-0 6-0 blowout, rather than feel guilty, feel uncomfortable, or “go easy” in any way because the trận đấu isn’t competitive.

They understand they’ve earned this result with their hours and hours training on the practice sân, and their thorough study of kỹ thuật, chiến lược, psychology, etc, off the sân. It’s easy to feel guilty – to feel like you want to giao lại up and give your opponent a fighting chance, but that’s a poor habit build. There is still a vitally important skill you can practice in this trận đấu, and it’s winning every point you should win.

If there are exceptional circumstances at play: maybe your little brother insisted on playing a trận đấu even though you warned him it wouldn’t be competitive, then it’s totally fine to go easy and even tank a few points if you think it’ll improve his experience, but in any competitive setting (early tournament rounds, one sided league matches), that attitude is unacceptable. If anything, it’s insulting to deny your opponent the experience.

They’re stuck with a roughly 0% win probability anyway, at least grant them the experience of playing against an elite player. Accept and Maximize the Situation Accept that there’s nothing you can do to improve the competitive landscape of the event. Don’t go for stupid shots. Don’t hit harder/softer than you normally would. Don’t intentionally avoid the player’s weakness to keep the rallies more competitive.

Not only are these bad habits to build, but they’ll degrade the quality of the already subpar trận đấu further, rather than enhance it. There is still a vitally important skill you can practice in this trận đấu, and it’s winning every point you should win. If you start fiddling around and losing points you should win it’ll cost you later.

In an easy trận đấu like this, it won’t change the outcome, but sacrifice 5% of your easy points in a trận đấu where only 15% of them come easy, and all of the sudden you’ve got a huge problem. 2. Beating The Off Day Accepting that it’s okay to beat someone better than you is partially about remembering that you yourself will have off days, too. Discounting your win on your opponent’s off day is just as unhealthy as discounting your loss on your own off day (which I think we all know is unhelpful).

A win is a win. If someone is good enough to beat you 9/10 times, don’t giao lại them beat you 10/10 times. It’s really that simple. Don’t think about a trận đấu as a single entity win/loss binary, but rather as one instantiation of an overall win rate that would occur if you and your opponent played many times. Think of it this way: they’re better than you, and you can’t change that. You have a 10% chance to win. Well, fight tooth and nail for your 10%.

Scrape out every last one of those 10% wins that you possibly can, and feel good about the fact that it was 10%, and not 0%. It’s very easy to conceptualize 10% as “really small” and, say, 1% as “really small” and see them as the same thing. They are not. Ten percent is literally ten times better. Further, it’s easy to feel that when you do, in fact, win after having a 10% chance going in, that you “just got lucky” and you “didn’t really win.” That isn’t true.

You are fully earning that 1 win out of 10 by competing hard each and every time, and waiting for the slip up. Keep doing that. 3. giao lại sân Guilt The easiest way to avoid “giao lại sân guilt” is to understand that luck happens to everyone. It’s part of the game. Whether you consider it a feature or a flaw is irrelevant. You have to play the game as it lies. Every individual shot is drawn from a distribution of shots at execution time, effectively randomly.

Further, there’s a lot more luck in tennis than you realize. It is incorrect to conceptualize shots as existing in isolation – that điểm thắng that hit the line might have hit the line this time, but often it simply flies out and is a lost point, rather than a won point. Every individual shot is drawn from a distribution of shots at execution time, effectively randomly. If it were possible to never miss, pros would never miss.

But they do miss, because, even for a professional player, striking a tennis ball comes with a little bit of variance; it can’t be perfect every time. Sometimes the shot will go in. Sometimes it’ll veer off course and go out. Sometimes, it’ll veer off just a little bit, and hit the line for a điểm thắng. That’s not as uncommon as a lưới dribbler, so it’s not nearly as “lucky,” but it’s still a little lucky, since you can’t profitably aim for the line in the long run.

Forget About Luck and Win Anyway In the end, a giao lại sân điểm thắng is simply a point you won due to an uncommon upswing in variance. But there’s lots of variance in tennis, so there’s no reason to zoom-in on this particularly vivid instance and somehow use it to discount your win. Are you really only going to “count” your win if you “didn’t get lucky”?

Are you going to go back through the entire trận đấu and look at every mishit, every shot that was close to the line, every slightly mistimed cú đánh and determine whether it helped or hurt the player who struck it, and try to average it all out to discover that you were +3.27 points over expectation on rallies with small mistakes or flukes, and subsequently conclude you don’t really deserve your win and “just got lucky”? No? You’re not going to do that because it’s ridiculous? Good.

Just play the game, and occasionally put your vợt up in a (fake) apology if you have to. The Winning Attitude Whatever your goals are, your mind will subconsciously work to achieve them. If your goal is something similar to winning, but distinct from winning itself, then you’ll be leaving wins on the table. Align yourself. If you really, sâu down, would rather do something like "hit good shots" than win, then that’s fine, but understand that it’s going to cost you matches.

Most players really do want to win, but unknowingly carry the psychological baggage of non-winning goals masquerading as winning itself. We need to train out of those metal habits. The winning attitude, like everything else in tennis, is something you have to practice. Be tenacious, be a problem solver, and value your wins however they come, and you’ll slowly develop a level of sức mạnh tinh thần you hadn’t though possible before. Win Anyway When we’re on sân, we don’t complain. We win.

To the questions below, there is only one productive answer – win anyway. The manner in which we go about this may vary from question to question, but the attitude is the same regardless. Something Injured? Win anyway. Feeling tired? Win anyway. Forget to eat enough? Win anyway. Using someone else’s vợt? Win anyway. Opponent cheating with line calls? Win anyway. Opponent too tall? Win anyway. Opponent too fast? Win anyway. Opponent getting so lucky? Win anyway. Opponent (God forbid) pushing?

Win anyway Analyze, but Never Complain Analysis on sân is useful. Complaining and self-bemoaning are not. Tennis is a good microcosm for life – control what you can; accept what you can’t. If you’re injured, maybe you have to avoid certain shots. Maybe you have to phòng thủ differently. You’re not at your best, but that doesn’t mean you’ve lost. Change, adapt, adjust – win anyway. If your opponent is cheating, can you get a line judge?

If not, what do your ethical standards say about cheating back? If a line judge is impossible, and you’re above cheating back, move your targets in to accommodate the smaller sân, and then move on. Complaining is a waste of mental resources. Every opponent will have different talents and weaknesses. The appropriate disposition towards your particular opponent is detached and analytical, not in any way emotional or defeatist. I don’t care how tall, or how fast, or how lucky your opponent is.

The trận đấu isn’t over until the final ball is struck. Win anyway. Out, Up, and Through. Every player has been coached to “brush up the back of the ball” in order to generate xoáy trên. While this advice is technically correct, it’s very misleading as to how this famous “brushing” actually happens. Most players understand that they need to vung vợt up and through the ball, but what many haven’t been taught is that it’s also essential to vung vợt out to the ball.

There are three critical vectors to the correct vung vợt path, not two. Out, up, and through. Of these three directions, the out part is the most overlooked. Despite it’s lack of popularity though, it is essential to generating a heavy, fast thuận tay, and it’s actually far more important than the up direction. Only a vung vợt containing the out vector imparts forces on the vợt that cause it to naturally rotate around the cổ tay through the hitting zone.

This vợt xoay is the less understood, but more important of the two fundamental di chuyển patterns that are used to generate xoáy trên in tennis. The Two xoáy trên di chuyển Patterns The two di chuyển patterns that generate xoáy trên in tennis are very different in their execution. The first – the well known one – is upward translation of the vợt-arm system through contact.

As the player hits, if the vợt-arm system is moving up, then, at impact, friction with the back of the ball will generate torque on it and create xoáy trên ball xoay. This is the “vung vợt low to high” idea which we’ve all heard countless times. You’ll often see tour level players hit heavy xoáy trên shots while barely even using an upward translational component in their vung vợt.

However, the other di chuyển mẫu that generates xoáy trên is far more important, especially at the highest level – vợt xoay about the cẳng tay. Through the hitting zone, the vợt whips around the cổ tay, about the axis of the cẳng tay. This rotational roi vung constitutes the traditionally named “windshield wiper” motion, and it also creates an upward frictional lực on the back of the ball at contact.

Whether or not the entire arm-vợt system is translating up, when the vợt is rotating about the cẳng tay during contact, the vợt itself is still explosively moving upward through the strike (even if the arm is not). And this upward vợt motion, the motion generated by vợt xoay, is responsible for a lot more of the total upward frictional lực imparted at contact than any upward translational motion is.

xoáy trên on the Tour – Primarily vợt xoay You’ll often see tour level players hit heavy xoáy trên shots while barely even using an upward translational component in their vung vợt – their theo sau ends up below their non-hitting vai. How is that possible? Heavy xoáy trên without the vợt-arm system moving drastically up through contact?

How did they generate all that spin without swinging “low to high?” It works because they’ve generated almost 100% of their spin using the vợt xoay contributor, rather than by translating the vợt up. Dominic Theim striking a waist height chéo sân with heavy spin, despite finishing his vung vợt below his non-hitting vai; his spin and tốc độ đầu vợt are generated almost entirely by vợt xoay through the hitting zone.

Additionally, the heaviest spin players, players like Dominic Thiem and Rafael Nadal, will often use both di chuyển patterns together – they both vung vợt the entire vợt-arm system up, and they relax, vung vợt out, and allow the vợt to roi vung about their cẳng tay. The result is that they kết thúc these shots above their đầu vợt, and by utilizing both xoáy trên generating techniques together, they generate some of the highest RPM forehands on the tour.

Rafael Nadal utilizing both vợt xoay and upward translation to generate maximum spin on a đầu gối height thuận tay from the đường cuối sân. Elite players also utilize the same cơ sinh học on every thuận tay, regardless of điểm tiếp xúc. vợt xoay can be easy to lose, especially on low balls, if adjustment to low contact is performed incorrectly.

Instead of adjusting their contact height using their arm – pulling it closer to their body to get the vợt lower – elite players adjust by sitting down lower and tilting their torso. This way, the arm can flick out and around the body in the same motion relative to the thân at every contact height, even a low contact. And thus, elite players do not lose the anatomical lợi thế of vợt xoay when contact needs to be made lower than usual.

The Role of the Out Vector So what does out have to do with any of this? The out vector is the result of the volitional action by which we generate this vợt xoay, the most important contributor to xoáy trên. By consciously swinging out to the ball and contacting it laterally away from the body, we generate the lực that causes the vợt to naturally roi vung around the cổ tay. As we begin our xoay hông and vung vợt the vợt out to the ball, the vợt naturally lags behind our relaxed cổ tay.

Then, as the vợt gets to the end of its outward trajectory, the hand naturally pulls on it, causing it to roi vung around. Roger Federer’s relaxed cổ tay lags behind his hand as it is swung out to the ball. Then, as the vợt hits the edge of its range of motion and can no longer continue out, it rotates around the cẳng tay and is thereby explosively moving both up and forward through contact.

It’s like the vợt is connected to the end of a string, and when the string goes taut, when our arm is finished extending, then the vợt’s path continues around whatever trajectory it was started on before it hit the end of that range of motion – in this case, that quán tính causes the vợt to rotate about the athlete’s cẳng tay.

This is not the case if the vợt is flung to a điểm tiếp xúc that is laterally closer to the body; in that case, there’s no outward lực on the vợt during the vung vợt, and so the resulting vợt motion as the vợt hits the end of its range of motion is just back-to-front, no xoay. This pure, translational, back-to-front vung vợt was common in the past in tennis, but it’s a vung vợt that can’t generate anywhere near the level of tốc độ đầu vợt and xoáy trên that the modern vung vợt can.

Conscious Effort vs Automatic Results As tennis instructors, it’s important that we make it abundantly clear which movements during a vung vợt are volitional, and which ones are automatic. vợt xoay about the cẳng tay through the hitting zone is automatic – it is NOT created via conscious effort, and the cổ tay and hand musculature do not play ANY role in generating this rotational lực (though they do stabilize the vợt and string góc while that xoay is happening).

This is an extremely common misconception – many coaches harmfully instruct their students to “use your cổ tay,” despite the fact that the cổ tay’s job is just to relax and to allow forces generated by other parts of the body to act on the vợt and roi vung it around that relaxed cổ tay.

cổ tay trễ, vợt xoay, and essentially the vợt’s entire vung vợt path after the volitional initiation of the forward vung vợt constitute dynamic positions; they are entered automatically as a result of other intentional actions taken before them.

Confusion Surrounding “cổ tay Action” When tennis commentators talk about “great cổ tay action,” what they really mean is that the player does a great job of relaxing their cổ tay and allowing their cổ tay to act as a hinge transferring lực into the ball, not that the cổ tay is in any way generating the lực itself.

When Nick Kyrgios jumps off the sân and slaps a 100mph thuận tay điểm thắng, the commentators often compliment his “live cổ tay.” What they are really complimenting is how relaxed it is, and how efficiently it transfers the power from his lõi into the ball. And when they talk about a player having a “strong cổ tay,” that’s simply a reference to the fact that the harder a player swings, the stronger their cổ tay must be to stabilize the vợt during that vung vợt.

It is NOT implying that the player’s “strong cổ tay” is creating that tốc độ đầu vợt by using its strength. The action that actually generates the tốc độ đầu vợt is the thân xoay which flings the vợt out and through the ball. Therefore, the action that a tennis player should think about while playing is just that – vung vợt out to the ball and strike it away from the body. That’s it.

Do that with a relaxed cổ tay, and as your vợt gets to the end of it’s range of motion, it will naturally and automatically rotate. Any conscious attempt at using the cổ tay or the arm to create the windshield wiper motion will typically just lead to harmful cổ tay tension, ineffective shots, and injury. So How Do I Actually Apply This?

The physics at play during the windshield wiper motion are pretty complex, so ignore the physics themselves when you’re on the sân (but understand them off the sân). Instead, just remember the cause and effect: Cause – swinging out to the ball with a relaxed cổ tay Effect – the vợt will roi vung around the cổ tay and rotate through the contact zone We use this knowledge to debug our own thuận tay.

If you feel like, one day, you aren’t getting sufficient xoáy trên on your shot, a failure to properly vung vợt out to the ball may be the culprit. We’ve all had the experience where, certain days, our cú đánh feels great, but then, other days, all of its lỗi tolerance has evaporated and it’s a struggle to even get it in the sân. Perhaps you’ve accidentally started violating the fundamental theorem of tennis. Perhaps you’re fatigued (or just being lazy), and you aren’t playing low and rộng enough.

Or perhaps you’ve subconsciously altered your điểm tiếp xúc, pulling it in towards the body, thereby preventing that dynamic, rotational vợt flick which generates so much spin. Remembering to vung vợt out, up, and through the ball, instead of just up and through it, is one more tool in your toolbox for fighting that inconsistency, and consistently maintaining that heavy, fast, lỗi tolerant thuận tay that makes tennis so fun to play.

The Modern thuận tay – A Biomechanical Masterpiece The following is the introduction to The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, available in eBook and paperback formats on Amazon. When performed correctly, the thuận tay is a cú đánh with a very large margin for error. Proper thuận tay cơ học ensure that the vung vợt is lỗi tolerant – when things go wrong, the vung vợt still succeeds. Mechanically unsound forehands are not lỗi tolerant.

Any small inaccuracy in timing, string góc, setup, etc, will cause a miss. Most thuận tay inconsistency is not proximally caused by those small chuẩn bị or execution inaccuracies, but rather by a vung vợt which lacks the lỗi tolerance to cán vợt them. Once proper cơ học are mastered, the thuận tay is actually very difficult to miss. Understanding this point is essential to evaluating your own thuận tay.

If, as you set up for the ball, your mind feels nervous and shaky – like the smallest mistake will send the ball flying into the back fence or spiraling down into the bottom of the lưới, that is because you are performing the thuận tay fundamentally wrong. Most thuận tay inconsistency is not proximally caused by small inaccuracies, but rather by a vung vợt which lacks the lỗi tolerance to cán vợt them.

Small Mistakes Shouldn’t Cause Misses During a correctly executed thuận tay, you can non-trivially mismanage your vung vợt and still hit the ball in. You’ll notice that professional players, when they get a bad bounce (often near the đường cuối sân of a chewed up sân cỏ), are still able to more often than not hit the ball back into play.

Usually, this takes the hình thức of an awkward, jerky, arm reach in front of them where they kind of swat the ball back over, but we’ll get into the specifics of exactly what’s happening there later. Here’s what’s important: good players consistently succeed in continuing the đánh qua lại even in cases when the ball’s vị trí changes so suddenly. This is not merely natural, improvisational talent. Rather, this sudden adjustment is made possible by the thuận tay’s exceptionally large margin for error.

The quick swat or flick that they execute is the player cashing in on this margin. This ability to adjust is built in to the thuận tay once each body part is doing roughly what it’s supposed to be doing, and slowly disappears as mechanical flaws are introduced. Strong Fundamentals Create lỗi Tolerance With weak fundamentals, any non-perfectly executed cú đánh is likely to fail, whereas with strong fundamentals, each individual cú đánh can be imperfect and still produce an adequate result.

You can be a little early, or a little late. Your vợt tilt can be off by four or five degrees. You can set up a little too close to the ball, or a little too far from it. The ball will still go in.

It is precisely due to this flexibility that when a professional player is faced with a bad bounce, a bounce which suddenly requires the ball to be struck 6-12 inches away from the expected điểm tiếp xúc the player had prepared for, they are able to quickly, often subconsciously, adjust and still hit the ball in. It’s not going to be a great shot, but it will go in, and that’s what’s important.

Even on the full stretch, Djokovic maintains thăng bằng and creates the correct string góc and vợt path through contact, leading to a successful chéo sân cúp chuyền. The thuận tay fundamentals responsible for producing this exceptionally large margin for error are the subject of this book. Many recreational players are never told that it’s a fully attainable goal to set up for a thuận tay knowing you won’t miss, and the things they’re told instead often cause more harm than good.

Two Rules that Govern EVERY Shot The following is a page from The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, available in eBook and paperback formats on Amazon (click here). The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis is two rules that govern every shot in the game. They are as follows: 1. The vợt makes a 90-135 degree góc with the cẳng tay 2. The vợt strikes the ball in front of the body (or, in a pinch, in front of the cẳng tay) Any shot which follows these two rules will work.

I should specify that I usually verbalize this rule as “a 90 degree góc” instead of “a 90-135 degree góc”, even though that’s often an exaggeration; many shots in tennis are played with a 135 degree góc, but I find that verbalizing and thinking about it as “90 degrees” produces the best results, since many students’ natural intuition is to play shots with a straight, 180 degree vợt/cẳng tay góc, and thinking about “90 degrees” prevents this.

Roger Federer during a US Open Practice in 2019, đang nạp his 115 degree cẳng tay-vợt system and preparing for a thuận tay vung vợt Following these two rules puts the vợt in a vị trí to function as a trampoline off of which the ball can easily bounce. The cẳng tay will facilitate vận tốc chuyển back to the ball much better when the vợt is perpendicular to it, rather than in line with it. Additionally, control over the vợt is far superior in this vị trí.

There are many ways to rotate the arm-vợt system while maintaining a 90 degree cẳng tay/vợt góc, and all of these will produce good shots. Further, there are many axes along which the vợt can flick around the hand when cocked at roughly 90 degrees before starting the vung vợt. This is especially necessary for shots performed while on the dead run.

Mentally curing your vợt to stay at 90 degrees will prevent you from attempting to adjust to balls by moving only the vợt đầu vợt without adjusting the entire arm-vợt system; this incorrect, independent vợt đầu vợt di chuyển elongates the góc between the arm and vợt and causes you to lose fine control over the vợt đầu vợt’s movements.

As a last point of interest, as noted in the rule, in a pinch it’s not necessary to contact the ball in front of the body as long as the vợt still hits the ball in front of the cẳng tay. Think of full stretch volleys out to the side, or shots where the ball has bounced behind the player.

In these cases, as long as the player is able to maintain some sort of an góc between the vợt and the cẳng tay and get the vợt flicking forward by the time it strikes the ball, the ball will usually go in. There are many different ways to flick the vợt around the hand.

You’ll notice that good players on the full stretch often end up following through awkwardly in front of them, rather than around their body, because they’re focusing on generating any and all forward flick of the vợt that they can muster from such a compromised vị trí. The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis is just one of many principles discussed in The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, which will be released in March of 2021.

Many pages from the book will be made publicly available completely free as they are completed, so don’t miss out! Great tennis is all about understanding what the game is truly about, and building from there. We don’t start with the joint angles, no “feet here, arms there, khuỷu tay does this, cổ tay does that.” Instead we start with a simple answer to a simple question.

How do I strike the ball to make it consistently do what I want? well July 15, 2021 I do not understand how to get in the 90+degree vị trí. You have to lay the cổ tay back to get there? REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 16, 2021 Most players lay the cổ tay back to varying degrees, but it’s not necessary for the 90 degree góc. For example, on the one handed trái tay, just make a fist out in front of you and put the vợt in it. That’s basically the cầm vợt.

REPLY Poida July 20, 2021 My cổ tay will not lay back much, duỗi of around 40 degrees. Is the cầm vợt vị trí that you mentioned for the 1hd trái tay the same to be used on the thuận tay? A cầm vợt kiểu Tây? Can I still achieve a roi vung action with a tight cổ tay joint? This has been a constant challenge to resolve on improving my thuận tay. REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 21, 2021 Great questions. 1.

The more Western your thuận tay cầm vợt, the less your cổ tay will naturally lay back, and the more Eastern your thuận tay cầm vợt, the more your cổ tay will naturally lay back. What’s important is simply that the cổ tay is relaxed. The small muscles in the cẳng tay giao bóng to stabilize the vung vợt and prevent the vợt from flying out of your hand, rather than serving to generate lực themselves. The specific degree of cổ tay lay back isn’t an important detail. 2.

No, the OHBH and thuận tay cầm vợt is not the same. For the OHBH, the most common cầm vợt is the index knuckle on bevel 1. For the thuận tay, the most common cầm vợt is the index knuckle on bevel 4 (called the semi-western thuận tay). 3. No, you cannot achieve the roi vung action with a tight cổ tay. If you try to lực it, you’ll injure your cẳng tay and khuỷu tay.

The roi vung action comes from a relaxed cổ tay acting as a hinge while the bigger muscles in the body, specifically the hông and abs, twist you around and fling your vợt out to the ball, then up and through it. 4. Relaxation is super tough to get a cán vợt on. The key to relaxation is to learn how to vung vợt slow and relaxed. When most people don’t want to miss, they tighten up. Instead, when you really don’t want to miss, try to just turn your body slowly, but stay super loose.

REPLY Poida July 20, 2021 “students’ natural intuition is to play shots with a straight, 180 degree vợt/cẳng tay góc, and thinking about “90 degrees” prevents this.” This is an excellent and very accurate observation! Just following up on my earlier post, does the 90degree góc relationship change to 180degrees when the vung vợt gets to the “out” vector? REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 21, 2021 Very rarely, but on completely phẳng shots, sometimes.

Most of the time, lots of the xoay is transposed to be about the cẳng tay, creating the “windshield wiper motion.” On a shot that’s completely phẳng, though, where the vợt is swung totally level, it is possible that the cổ tay would go through 180 degrees, before going into gấp in the theo sau. I should stress that this is *only* true on very explosive shots, and only true for players who are experts at staying extremely relaxed with their arm and hand.

For beginners, they should really never have a 180 degree vợt arm góc, and they should envision tennis as moving the entire 90 degree vợt arm system around the sân and through the ball. REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 22, 2021 Great, I’m so happy it was helpful! Yes, the “C” loop is another one that’s extremely misunderstood. On some forehands, the vung vợt path does resemble a C, but that’s more of a coincidence – aiming at the C shape itself is akin to putting the cart before the horse. With respect to the 90 degree góc – I wouldn’t worry about it on your thuận tay.

As long as the cổ tay isn’t actively participating in the vung vợt, and the hông and abs are the primary drivers of your vợt, you’re probably fine. Every player’s cổ tay góc does completely unwind *in the theo sau*, so that góc will not remain static as you vung vợt.

If you watch a high fps slow motion video of your favorite professional player, you’ll typically see that the 90 degree góc is created as the vợt lags behind the hand, and then maintained for most of the vợt’s forward roi vung, and then finally straightens out and subsequently goes into gấp (non-volitionally of course) after contact. As for the anatomical descriptions of the cổ tay action: 1.

The cổ tay is brought into duỗi (passively) by the weight of the vợt, which wants to stay at rest, but is pulled forward by the hand. It typically remains in duỗi until after contact (again, not by conscious effort), although many players’ cổ tay duỗi unwinds by 15-45 degrees throughout the vung vợt. 2. Both radial and ulnar deviation occur during the cú đánh, but the degree of each depends on your thuận tay cầm vợt. These shouldn’t be attempted consciously. 3.

A lot of the “cổ tay xoay” is actually the hand, cổ tay, and cẳng tay all rotating together. Take your vợt and pretend you’re wiping a window with it. A lot of the xoay on the thuận tay is this motion. 4. “Snapping the cổ tay” using your cẳng tay muscle will actually slow down your vung vợt, and over time may give your cẳng tay and khuỷu tay an overuse injury.

Can you get trễ with a cầm vợt kiểu lục địa? Just to clarify, the cổ tay remains in duỗi until after contact (re your point #1 earlier. Regarding the “out vector”, from page 95 “The goal is to orient the vợt-arm-cổ tay system such that the vợt face rotates through contact roughly about the axis of the cẳng tay, so that the string góc isn’t dramatically altered by the xoay.

This typically means holding the vợt with a relaxed, neutral cổ tay, and swinging diagonally up to the ball” I’m confused about the “neutral cổ tay”, if I try to replicate this, the arm góc goes to 180degrees. I thought we need to keep the 90degree góc and the cổ tay laid back through contact…..or is this for beginners? The Federer images on page 96 show his cổ tay laid back and then it looks neutral at contact.

REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 27, 2021 If you watch tour level forehands, 135 degrees is typically about the most obtuse góc you see at contact. It’s fine for the góc to open up from 90 to 135, but if it’s opening all the way to 180 before contact, your điểm tiếp xúc is probably off. For most people, a neutral cổ tay makes a roughly 135 degree cẳng tay-vợt góc. REPLY Poida July 27, 2021 Thanks, I’ll be focusing and practicing maintaining that góc.
