REPLY Is “bật Your cổ tay” Good Advice? Well… no. Not empirically, anyway.

Just look around at your local courts, club, or league, and you’ll see plenty of “cổ tay snapping,” despite seeing precious little, you know… good serving. The cue just doesn’t work, and for good reason. When people hear “bật your cổ tay,” they think cổ tay gấp (the cổ tay bending such that the hand moves closer to the inside of the cẳng tay). The giao bóng is a throw, and throws are powered by an external > internal vai xoay flinging action.

What the apparent “snapping” really constitutes is primarily internal vai xoay, rather than cổ tay gấp. If we look at the greatest server of a generation, Pete Sampras, you’ll see very conservative cổ tay gấp, and yet an extremely exaggerated internal vai xoay. So if “bật your cổ tay” doesn’t work, why do coaches keep saying it? In ngắn – because we are stuck in an inadequate signaling equilibrium.

Signaling Equilibria 101 Human beings are constantly in competition to signal their value to other human beings. Most people are familiar with this idea in a mating context – lipstick and blush for women, shoe lifts and muscles for men – but what many don’t realize is that signaling effects all walks of life, not just sex.

A CEO, for example, has to do more than just make good decisions – he also has to look like someone who’s in charge, he has to act like someone who’s in control, and he has to sound like someone with all the answers. These signals of competence, in the human world, unfortunately often matter more than competence itself.

This is where we, as a civilization, fall into inadequate equilibria – situations whereby both parties are harmed by deviating from the status quo, and yet the status quo is woefully inefficient. This is a massive topic, so I’ll leave two amazing sources here if you’d like to explore human signaling further: Inadequate Equilibria – How and Why Civilizations Get Stuck The Elephant in the Brain Signaling in Tennis Coaching “bật your cổ tay” is a signal.

Every coach says “bật your cổ tay,” and because every coach says “bật your cổ tay,” every other coach also says “bật your cổ tay.” This cue has been enshrined in the Good Tennis Coaching class of cues. When a coach says it, they signal that they can produce cues from this Good Tennis Coaching set of things. Critically, it doesn’t actually matter whether or not “bật your cổ tay” is good or bad advice, it simply matters that it exists in the Good Tennis Coaching set.

So long as that remains true, it’s very unlikely that a coach’s reputation will be harmed by producing it. Your coach gets paid when he successfully signals to you that he’s a good coach. “bật your cổ tay” is not unique. Many of the things your coach tells you aren’t that useful, but merely exist to signal that he can produce well known members of the Good Tennis Coaching set on command. It’s important to understand that your coach doesn’t actually get paid to make you better.

Your coach gets paid when he successfully signals to you that he’s a good coach. Due to that, many of the things he tells you giao bóng the sole function of signaling to you that he’s a good coach – a distinct goal from the goal of making you a better player. This is subconscious of course – almost no coaches are actively aware that they’re doing this. It’s important to state here that it really is your coach’s job to signal to you he’s a good coach.

It’s not wrong that he’s focused on signaling – if he doesn’t make you aware of his coaching ability, you won’t come back. For your coach, the signaling is the more important goal, because his livelihood depends on the signal getting through. The Solution – Goal Alignment It is your job, as a paying customer, to make sure that the coach’s goal of signaling competence and your goal of getting better are aligned. It’s your job to distinguish between accurate and inaccurate signals of good coaching.

Cues like “bật your cổ tay” only permeate the coaching landscape because students, and the paying parents of those students, accept this poor signal of good coaching as an accurate signal. Accepting poor signals of good coaching is what causes students everywhere to continue paying for coaches who aren’t providing much of a differential impact at all. It’s your job to distinguish between accurate and inaccurate signals of good coaching.

The easiest signal to look for in your coach is also the simplest – are you getting better? If yes, that is the strongest signal you’re getting what you pay for. In your first few interactions with a coach, this “making you better” can take the hình thức of very little improvements, rather than big ones – things like improving your understanding of something or answering a long standing question you’ve had.

What’s important, though, is to discard the signals of good coaching that don’t matter, the most common of which is producing sâu Wisdom from the Good Coaching Advice set that isn’t helpful. I come across so many students who discuss disheartening experiences with learning how to giao bóng, and, fascinatingly, these students khung vợt themselves as the issue: “He just kept telling me to bật my cổ tay, but I just don’t know how to do that.

I guess I’ll never be able to giao bóng.” This, clearly, is not a student failure, but a coaching failure. Serving is difficult. Only the most talented natural players are going to figure it out on their own. Allow Yourself to be Judgmental Be ruthless in your search for a good coach. Many students feel guilty leaving a coach, or asking to switch coaches at their club. It feels inappropriate to suggest that a coach may be wrong, and you, the lowly student or parent, may know better.

Who are you, after all, to doubt someone who can produce sacred wisdom like “bật your cổ tay” on command? This is where you need to develop your coaching immune system. Retrain yourself to find the signals in a coach that actually indicate they’ll be able to help you, and ignore the noise. A Compromise – Expensive Signals Certification attempts to solve the signaling problem, and it does an alright job. Certifications are expensive, both in time and in money.

Due to that, they are signals which are hard to fake. Compare something like the “bật your cổ tay” signal to the USPTA Elite Professional certification. For the first, you need to learn a phrase, and the rough rules on when to apply it. Student serves poorly, probably by pushing the ball over “bật your cổ tay.” For the second, you need: to spend hundreds of dollars to spend 10s of hours learning material to complete extensive on and off sân testing Which one is a stronger signal?

Who are you, after all, to doubt someone who can produce sacred wisdom like “bật your cổ tay” on command? By investing so much of their time and effort into the certification process, a coach signals to prospective students a commitment to the craft, and that signal is worth a lot.

While the education aspect of a cert is certainly valuable, what’s even more valuable than the education itself is the message it sends about the coach – a coach with a certification has proven to be the kind of coach that seeks out further education. Remember The Best Signal? Well… The best signal of a good coach is that you’re getting better… right? Actually there’s one caveat to this signal – talented players. Exceptional athletes will improve regardless of their environment.

Yet, despite the athlete improving with our without quality coaching, the athlete’s coach will always get lots of credit. Most people don’t realize this phenomenon at play. Throughout all of sports media, coaches are constantly getting credit for things their players do. One has to wonder, in these cases, had the player been with a different coach, would things really have turned out any differently? A child phenom is a wellspring of money and status that everyone wants to be a part of.

Coaches desperately want their name attached to exceptional athletes. If a certain kid ends up nationally ranked, at a D1 school, or, even better, on the tour, then the reputation of every coach, trainer, adviser, etc that touched that player’s career gets a boost proportional to that success. A child phenom is a wellspring of money and status that everyone wants to be a part of.

Nick Kyrgios, in this mostly fun, but occasionally serious interview post his Aussie Open Double’s win, alluded twice to what I believe was this very phenomena effecting his young life: 2:22 “In the past, I haven’t had that many good people around me.

They’ve taken lợi thế of me.” And contrasting his own situation to Ash Barty’s: 4:47 “The people, around her, that have actually done so much, and actually care about her well being.” Back to First Principles We’re always talking about first principles here when it comes to tennis strokes, but the idea of first principles applies to coaching itself as well. When looking for a coach, you’re looking for a teacher.

You’re looking for a guide through this complex game, and you’re looking for these things because you want to improve. You don’t have to blindly trust your coach that produces sâu wisdom like “bật your cổ tay” from the Good Coaching Advice set. If it’s not helping you, it’s not helping you. But when you have those “ah, ha” moments, or when, momentarily, everything clicks into place, those are the signals you want to look for.

Pay attention to those signals instead, and you won’t be out their “snapping your cổ tay” to produce an awkward 70mph giao bóng into the giữa of the lưới. 5 Signs of a Great Coach A great lesson is worth infinitely more than a bad one. And I mean that literally. It isn’t worth double, or triple, or quadruple its less effective counterpart. No, the difference in value is infinite. With a great lesson you improve. With a bad lesson, you don’t.

That might sound harsh, but the truth is it’s not uncommon to take a tennis lesson and actually get worse. Unfortunately, some of the common advice given by your typical club coach makes you more likely to miss, more likely to stagnate, and more likely to injure yourself than if you’d just hopped on YouTube and researched the strokes yourself. With that in mind, it’s up to you to judge the quality of your lessons and ensure you’re getting the value you paid for.

Here are 5 signs your coach is worth keeping. 1. Great Coaches Cause Cross-Pollination One of the strongest positive indicators of coaching prowess is cross-pollination – the coach’s advice for one part of your game improves another part you didn’t even discuss. This happens when your coach understands the fundamentals behind what they are teaching and instructs based on that.

These fundamentals often apply widely across the entire sport, not just to whatever cú đánh you happened to be working on, and thus when you understand them, you’ll frequently see benefits in your entire game, not just that one cú đánh.

This result falls in stark contrast to the coach who just regurgitates joint góc corrections that he learned when he was taking lessons growing up – sure, sometimes those corrections will be accurate, and they might improve one particular issue you’re having with one particular cú đánh, but because none of the reasoning behind the joint góc correction is communicated, cross-pollination of that improvement is extremely unlikely.

This brings us to sign #2, which is usually necessary for cross-pollination to occur. 2. Great Coaches Explains Things Adequate but not great coaches will typically give solid advice, but they often fail to communicate why you should be following it. Great coaches, on the other hand, will ensure that you understand the reasoning behind their instruction – learning the “why” will be an essential part of the lesson.

The “why” is what sticks with you, it’s what allows you to coach yourself when the lesson is over, and it’s the vehicle by which good advice cross-pollinates to the rest of your game. giao lại’s look at three levels of coaching for a particular problem – you’re struggling with the low thuận tay. Note that there’s more to coaching than just giving correct advice, as clearly the third coach is superior to the second.

Bad – Incorrect and Useless Get that vợt under the ball! – This encourages straightening the cổ tay, a di chuyển which will absolutely ruin the cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance. Gotta get under it! – Unhelpful and unspecific. Pick it up! – Encourages opening the string góc through contact in order to get the ball to go up – also ruins lỗi tolerance.

Better – Good Advice but Unfounded Bend your đầu gối! – Getting better; the student should be adjusting with their legs, but “bend your đầu gối” is both unspecific and often misunderstood. Get your body lower! – Correct, but unspecific as to how. Don’t straighten your cổ tay! – Again, correct, but how? If the student is straightening their cổ tay, they might not understand how to lower the vợt without doing so.

Best – Conceptual and Technical Instruction The low thuận tay is a very similar di chuyển mẫu to the waist height thuận tay, but executed from a different starting point. First, a broad explanation of how the cú đánh should be understood. Get the vợt lower by adjusting with with the larger parts of your body – the legs and thân. This allows the hand, arm, and vợt to do the same thing they do on a regular height thuận tay. A broad explanation of how to adjust, and why to adjust that way.

To lower the vợt, sit down, widen your legs, and tilt your torso – don’t pull the arm in unless you have to, and only straighten the cổ tay as a last resort. Once you adjust with the lõi movers, just relax and rotate your thân to initiate the vung vợt the same way you would on a waist height thuận tay. Specific explanation of how to adjust, and why to adjust that way. This one is long. Longer explanations are typically necessary when a student is consistently mishitting a particular kind of shot.

If a student misses just a single bóng thấp, a simple “tilt your torso” can definitely remind them of what they already know, but if they are consistently missing every bóng thấp, it indicates a more fundamental gap in their understanding; they probably don’t know how to correctly execute the shot they’re attempting. The step that’s required here is instruction, not cuing. Taking a minute to stop and explain something thoroughly isn’t something a coach should shy away from.

Great coaches leave their students with an improved understanding of their strokes, not just with cues to remember. 3. Great Coaches Talk a Lot If you’ve paid $90 for a 60 min tennis lesson, that’s $1.50/minute. You’re already wasting some of that time for ball pick up, so that’s a lot of money for someone to silently feed balls at you. Fundamentally, a lesson is about telling the student what they’re doing wrong and how to fix it, which isn’t a positive experience in a vacuum.

Even when there’s nothing too important to say, great coaches are engaged on a personal level. Small encouragements like “great job,” “doing great,” and “keep it up,” do wonders for the emotional atmosphere of the lesson. Encouragements to negative events – the “you got this” and “no worries” of the world – further this supportive, instructive atmosphere.

Remember, fundamentally, a lesson is about telling the student what they’re doing wrong and how to fix it, which isn’t a positive experience in a vacuum. A friendly spigot of verbal of positive reinforcement helps alleviate this. Speech and encouragement is especially necessary during physically exhausting, conditioning related training, and even more important when these activities are done with kids.

Kids need to learn that training is supposed to be hard; if it’s not harder than a trận đấu, it’s not helping that much, so the coach, as the authority, must clearly and unambiguously communicate that it is good that they’re uncomfortable. 4. But Great Coaches Don’t Correct Too Much While lots of talking is almost universally helpful, too much correction is not.

Though the lesson experience as a whole will be positive, a correction is inherently negative, and as such corrections should only be used when necessary. Overloading a student with corrections is both annoying and counter-productive. It doesn’t matter if a student presents with, say, four major flaws in their thuận tay. A quality coach will pick whichever one they think will be the most impactful to fix and just correct that one.

When the student executes that fix correctly, even if they miss the shot itself, it’s “great attempt” and “I know you missed, but that’s what I want,” that are far more productive than a correction. Great coaches are essentialists in their correction – they only correct when it appears the student is unaware of what they’re doing wrong. The worst thing a coach can do is try to correct all the student’s flaws at once.

To the student, that feels like an endless barrage of confusing and unrelated corrections, and as they go to vung vợt, they have no idea what to actually tập trung on. Even when a student is only working on a single correction, it’s still not appropriate to correct them every time they mess it up. They’re going to mess it up a lot – it is a novel tweak to their cú đánh, after all.

Great coaches are essentialists in their correction – they only correct when it appears the student is unaware of what they’re doing wrong, not when they’ve simply failed to execute something. There’s no reason to berate a student with the same correction over and over, even if they’ve missed a string of balls. Sometimes, a rephrase of the correction is necessary, but not always.

As long as the student is still clearly attempting the right thing, a great coach will break the silence with a simple “you got this” instead. 5. Great Coaches Prioritize Static Positions and di chuyển Patterns Static positions are positions that an athlete stops, or effectively stops in. These are great positions to coach, because they are almost always entered totally volitionally. The trophy vị trí and the end of the thuận tay đưa vợt ra sau are two examples of static positions.

Most strokes are best understood as a static vị trí, and then a di chuyển mẫu out of that static vị trí. The giao bóng is the trophy pose, followed by a throw of the vợt. The thuận tay is the end of the đưa vợt ra sau vị trí, followed by a twist of the thân. Teaching strokes in this way improves the student’s proprioceptive awareness of their actions as they learn.

Most humans can’t feel their exact joint angles and limb orientations in space during an explosive di chuyển, but they can feel them in a static vị trí like the trophy pose.

And once the explosive di chuyển starts, they don’t know what it feels like to “create 60 degrees of external vai xoay,” but they do know what it feels like to “twist,” to “throw,” to “sit down,” or to “jump.” Coaching based on static positions and di chuyển patterns, instead of dynamic positions and joint angles, gives the student the gift of feedback. Not from you, but from their own senses.

If they know what it feels like when it’s right, they can use their own proprioceptive observations to correct their strokes on their own. In the End, Great Coaches Help You Win If you want to simplify the evaluation process, evaluate your lessons on one simple criteria – are you winning more than before you started working with your coach? The classic “I play great in my lessons, but bad in matches” is a misnomer – all this really means is that your lessons aren’t very useful.

What does it mean to “play great” in a lesson if that “greatness” wasn’t transferable to a competitive setting? Is your goal to hit the same slow, phẳng, feed over the lưới 20 times in a row, or is it to win tennis matches? Great coaches will provide you instruction that will improve your game, and your understanding of the game, at a fundamental level, and with them week-by-week, and month-by-month, you’ll see yourself improve faster than you ever thought possible.

Bronze: “Bend Your đầu gối” “Bend your đầu gối” is one of the most overused and oversimplified cues given not just in tennis, but in sports in general. When most coaches say “bend your đầu gối,” they are trying to cue their student into some hình thức of the basic athletic vị trí: the hông are low and back while the lumbar cột sống is neutral.

The athlete is slightly bent over via hinging at the the hông, and the posterior chain muscles (the glutes, hamstrings, and lower back) are lightly loaded and ready to facilitate explosive movements in any direction. The problem is that “bend your đầu gối” doesn’t cue most people into this vị trí. Instead, it causes them to, of course, just bend their đầu gối.

They shift their weight forward onto their toes, decrease their đầu gối góc, and bend forward with a rounded lower back to prevent from falling backwards. This posture deloads the hamstrings, glutes, and lower back musculature; precisely the opposite of what we wanted. And unlike the athletic vị trí, this forward-leaning rounded-back vị trí leads to a striking lack of thăng bằng, coordination, and explosiveness.

The body is in an even more compromised vị trí than before the “bend your đầu gối” cue was given – this alignment is a common cause of both đầu gối and lower back pain.

Instead of naively cuing “bend the đầu gối,” we teach our athletes to proprioceptively feel the athletic vị trí. “Sit Down and Back” What the athlete should be doing is better described as “sitting down.” Sitting down causes the hông to shift down and back, the đầu gối to track both out and sometimes forward, and a forward lean facilitated by hinging at the hông with a neutral, non-rounded lower back.

Thus, by “sitting down” the athlete enters the athletic vị trí, engaging the posterior chain and keeping their thăng bằng over either the giữa of the foot, or the ball of it, depending on the situation. In the correct athletic vị trí, the đầu gối will bend, but for almost every student, that’s a result of entering the vị trí, rather than the di chuyển that caused them to enter it.

In no instance is the forward đầu gối shearing, toe balancing vị trí students (especially kids) get into upon “bend your đầu gối” appropriate. Here’s the tricky part: after a student enters this vị trí, their đầu gối are in fact bent, so to the untrained eye it can look like the goal of the cue has been accomplished.

If the thought process was as simple as: the đầu gối aren’t bent, and they should be, then the coach may believe that the issue (which they perceived as nothing more than “straight legs”) has been fixed. The đầu gối are now bent, after all; that particular joint góc has been “fixed.” But the underlying problem wasn’t actually addressed. The forward đầu gối shearing, rounded back, toe balanced vị trí that most students enter upon “bend your đầu gối” is not the athletic vị trí.

We can only hope the coach notices the flaw, back peddles from the “bend your đầu gối” cue, and finds a different way to get the student into the athletic vị trí. Proprioceptive Awareness Fosters Long Term Success The entire mess can be avoided if, instead of naively cuing “bend the đầu gối,” we teach our athletes how to proprioceptively feel the athletic vị trí (perhaps the most important static vị trí in tennis).

They need to know what it feels like to have their posterior chain loaded, their back phẳng and hinged at the hông, and, yes, also a smaller đầu gối góc. They need to recognize the sensation of being primed and ready to support explosive movements in any direction; this proprioceptive awareness will improve their thăng bằng and athleticism across all sports, not just tennis.

Again, the “bend the đầu gối” cue will not give athletes this awareness, because “bent đầu gối” is just one of many biomechanical changes a person previously unfamiliar with the vị trí will have to take to correctly in order to enter it. “Sit back” or “sit down and back” are far superior cues. Most students will already have an idea of how to coordinate their body to perform a “sit” action, and this cue will hijack that awareness.

Notice that “sit” is a human di chuyển, while “bent đầu gối” is a single joint góc. In general, cuing a di chuyển mẫu is far more effective than cuing a joint góc. Joint Angles Are Secondary Typically, a single joint góc is a symptom, not a cause. It’s not that the “đầu gối aren’t bent,” it’s that the athlete is standing up straight instead of sitting. It’s not that the “hông aren’t square at contact,” it’s that the athlete doesn’t know they’re supposed to be twisting as they vung vợt.

You’ll be amazed at how many individual limb placements and joint angles your student will fix on their own, without you having to mention them specifically, just because they’re trying to do something like “twist” or “throw.” In general, cuing a di chuyển mẫu is far more effective than cuing a joint góc. “Twist,” “jump,” “throw,” “sit,” “lunge.” Start broad, then work to narrow as it becomes clear what the athlete’s particular hangups with the di chuyển mẫu are.

We don’t cue an individual body part until it’s clear that that body part is the critical missing link in an otherwise functional process. Cuing joint angles is for getting the athlete’s di chuyển mẫu the last 20% of the way there, and certainly not for getting a kid unfamiliar with the athletic vị trí into it for the first time. Adjusting To Low Balls The EFFICIENT WAY The key to effective cú đánh production is efficient bio-mechanical alignment.

For every shot, there are certain body alignments which produce tốc độ đầu vợt most efficiently and most consistently. The more often we can align our body properly before swinging, the higher quality, and the more lỗi tolerant, our shots will be. On the thuận tay, our bio-mechanical lợi thế is our ability to roi vung the vợt around the torso unimpinged.

The tricky part is routinely getting ourselves into these alignments while playing many different balls, at different heights, from many different positions on the sân. We need to contact the ball in a myriad of different locations, and yet in doing so we need to use a remarkably consistent relative upper body vị trí. On the thuận tay, our bio-mechanical lợi thế is our ability to roi vung the vợt around the torso unimpinged.

In order to allow this, we need to maintain adequate space between our torso and arm – if the góc between the torso and the arm gets too narrow, the arm won’t naturally flick out and around as we rotate our thân. Therefore, we need to adjust our contact height without significantly changing our arm góc. This is accomplished by adjusting with the larger muscle groups. We always adjust with the largest muscle group we can.

This means we have two primary methods of adjustment: Adjust with the legs, either by sitting down or widening them Adjust with the torso, by tilting it Rafa Nadal adjusting his body to for a regular height (left) versus a low (right) điểm tiếp xúc. On the bóng thấp, his torso is far more tilted, and his legs are far more bent, while the relative alignment of the upper body is almost identical.

The vast majority of our forehands will be played with an identical torso-arm góc, regardless of contact height, with adjustments made via the legs and torso. Legs and torso adjustments are the only two effective adjustments, as they are the adjustments that giao lại us alter the contact height without impeding our torso-arm-vợt alignment – the alignment which facilitates our rotational roi vung. Suboptimal Adjustments Adjustments of the arm and cổ tay are a last resort.

When sufficient adjustment can’t be made by using only the legs and torso – perhaps we’re on the dead run or the ball is coming so fast that we don’t have time to move – then we adjust with our arm (not the hand or cổ tay). Only as a last resort do we adjust by straightening our cổ tay and violating the Fundamental Theorem of Tennis. We either elongate it or bring it closer to the body, as called for by the situation, but we do not violate the fundamental theorem of tennis.

Adjusting with the arm is not ideal, but since we maintain the right góc between the cẳng tay and the vợt, and since we contact the ball out in front of us, the shot will still go in. It won’t be great, but the đánh qua lại will continue. Only as a last last resort do we adjust by straightening our cổ tay and violating the fundamental theorem of tennis.

A common case of this is when the ball is so far away that even on the dead run we are just barely are able to make contact – we simply need every inch of legs, arm, and vợt fully straight in order to reach the ball. If you find yourself in this situation, try to hit it in front of you and pray it goes in. The shot is not going to be lỗi tolerant.

The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis as a Cure For Improper Adjustment Focusing on the fundamental theorem of tennis helps many players break bad adjustment habits. All too often, I see players using their cổ tay to adjust to low balls. They elongate their vợt-cẳng tay góc (violating the theorem) in order to get the vợt đầu vợt low enough to hit a bóng thấp, and in doing so lose all stability and control.

To avoid this, ensure you’re always throwing a relaxed right góc vợt-cẳng tay system through the ball. For most players, thinking about this causes them to naturally adjust with the larger parts of the body, the legs and torso, rather than the cổ tay. Arm adjustments do not violate the fundamental theorem of tennis, but they are not optimal.

You can move your arm closer or farther from your body without interfering with the cẳng tay-vợt system, and thus these shots will still be relatively lỗi tolerant and frequently go in, but they’ll be lacking in power because they don’t harness the rotational chuỗi động học nearly as well as shots where adjustment was made with the legs or torso.

Dynamic vs Static Positions While there are numerous different teaching mistakes that lead to ineffective coaching, many of them stem from the same fundamental misunderstanding – confusing cause and effect. Some body movements are actively, volitionally caused by the athlete; in order to hit an bóng trên cao, the athlete will consciously throw their vợt up and out to the ball.

Other movements, though they do happen, will happen automatically (and often subconsciously) as a result of the forces generated by other volitional actions. Cuing these subconscious movements is a recipe for disaster. A dynamic vị trí is a vị trí that is only entered while in motion, and it is almost never volitional. A static vị trí is a vị trí that is entered into and then effectively held in place, and it is almost always volitional. Here are some examples.

End of the thuận tay đưa vợt ra sau Static – Though most professional players never completely stop here, you could, and it wouldn’t effect the vung vợt that much. The final vị trí of our body before the forward vung vợt is very volitional, and should be entered consciously. cổ tay trễ during the thuận tay vung vợt Dynamic – quán tính causes the cổ tay to trễ backwards as the body’s xoay pulls the vợt forward. This happens passively as a result of a relaxed cổ tay during the vung vợt.

The tighter the cổ tay, the less this happens, and the less effectively the cổ tay functions at transferring lực from the rotational chain into the vợt. Trophy vị trí during the giao bóng Static – Similar to the đưa vợt ra sau in the thuận tay, most players don’t actually have a totally stationary trophy vị trí, but, again, you could, and the forward vung vợt would be almost unaffected.

In fact, you see this when players hit overheads during points – their final vị trí before the vung vợt is almost completely static, and yet their power is barely effected. Back scratch vị trí during the giao bóng Dynamic – Just like thuận tay cổ tay trễ, the back scratch vị trí is reached because of quán tính. When the upper body rotates in a throwing motion, throwing the vợt out to the ball, the vợt and cổ tay naturally trễ behind the arm, and the vai rotates.

This external vai xoay, like the cổ tay trễ in the thuận tay, is thụ động, and, like the cổ tay trễ, is more effective the looser the cổ tay and arm are. Implications for Coaching Very rarely is it appropriate to attempt to directly cue an athlete into a dynamic vị trí, even though the positions themselves are still useful coaching tools. Dynamic positions do give a coach useful insight into how their student is performing the volitional parts of the cú đánh.

If their cổ tay is lagging on their thuận tay, they’re probably swinging right. If their vợt is back scratching on the giao bóng, they’re probably serving right. But these positions are only useful as checkpoints used for evaluation, not as body alignments that students should try to enter with conscious effort. How do we move our body around? By contracting muscles. di chuyển necessarily entails a lack of relaxation.

Almost all dynamic positions in tennis work better the looser the involved body parts are. This means that it is wholly and completely counter-productive for the athlete to pay any conscious attention to those body parts (well, other than keeping them relaxed). How do we move our body around? By contracting muscles. di chuyển necessarily entails a lack of relaxation. In order to move the body, a muscle must flex.

Therefore, we need to make sure the things that we actively try to do, the positions we consciously try to enter, are the ones for which contracting our muscles is appropriate. I want to tighten my glutes and hamstrings in order to prime my legs. I want to contract my thân in order to turn my chest sideways before swinging. What I do not want to do is in any way contract the muscles of my hand or cổ tay (except sometimes slightly in order to change the direction of the ball).

This is why the “point your butt cap at the lưới” cue is so damaging – it leads to a tight cổ tay, which causes precisely the opposite result we were aiming for: when tight, the cổ tay functions less efficiently as a hinge to chuyển lực. The backscratching cue on the giao bóng is almost just as bad. Teaching our students that the back scratch is a static vị trí damages their serves for as long as they believe it. The giao bóng is a throw.

Imagine teaching a baseball player to rotate their vai back, pause, and then throw from there. Ridiculous? Of course. Cue Static Positions (and then actions) Every quality instructor, in all athletic activities, not just tennis, needs to understand which positions in their particular sport are dynamic positions, and which positions are static positions. Static positions should be cued.

When an athlete is hitting their static positions correctly, they will often naturally enter the dynamic positions effectively, too. Dynamic positions are not reached volitionally, so the knowledge of what the vị trí is supposed to look like is useless to the athlete. Dynamic positions should not be cued. If an athlete is moving from a correct static vị trí into an incorrect dynamic vị trí, the solution is not cuing the dynamic vị trí. Doing so just creates harmful tension.

Remember, dynamic positions are not reached volitionally, so the knowledge of what the vị trí is supposed to look like is useless to the athlete. Instead, the coach must figure out which lực vector is misaligned or lacking, that, if correct, would lead to the correct dynamic vị trí. “Throw your vợt out more.” “Rotate a little earlier.” “roi vung your chest around.” These are the kinds of cues that fix strokes.

When attempting to fix a dynamic vị trí, cue actions, di chuyển patterns, rather than joint angles. Your student has no idea (and should have no idea), what the precise góc of their external vai xoay is during the back scratch vị trí as they giao bóng. What they need to know instead is to throw their vợt up and out to the ball from their trophy vị trí.

So giao lại’s stop creating hitches in swings and tension in limbs by irresponsibly cuing dynamic positions, and leave “point your butt cap at the lưới” and “scratch your back when you giao bóng” permanently quarantined in the coaching cues hall of shame where they belong. Gold: “Roll Your vợt Over The Ball” Silver: “Point Your Butt Cap at the lưới” Bronze: “Bend Your đầu gối” How the Feet Liberate the hông A full xoay hông is impossible if both of your feet are firmly planted on the ground.

Trying to rotate your hông while your feet stay planted will put a large amount of torsion lực on your đầu gối and ankles; don’t do it. Therefore, your feet will need to come off the ground, at least partially, in order to hit a proper thuận tay. Each of these di chuyển patterns is simply a manifestation of letting the feet move freely. The feet naturally want to get out of the way when you rotate your hông; your brain knows not to send that torsion lực into your ankles.

So as long as the player allows their feet to move freely, and doesn’t keep them locked to the ground, these patterns will occur naturally. That said, it’s still worthwhile to learn what these patterns are, what they look like, and when to use them. Unconscious competence can only get you so far; in the end, every athlete should know why they’re doing what they’re doing, even if they do it naturally, so that they can replicate it on command.

Here are the four common patterns by which the feet move to allow xoay hông. 1. The Non-hitting Foot Comes Up This is one of the most common di chuyển patterns used during the hông twist. The non-hitting foot comes up off the ground, while the hitting foot remains on the ground.

As the player twists their body around, they rotate on the ball of the hitting foot [right one for rigght handed player], and the non-hitting foot [left one for right handed player] doesn’t restrict this xoay at all, since it’s in the air. Both Novak Djokovic (left) and Roger Federer (right) using the same left-foot-up di chuyển mẫu to allow necessary xoay hông on an trong-vào trong thuận tay. This is a great di chuyển mẫu for đánh qua lại balls and neutral balls.

Typically, a player can’t move forward into the sân using this di chuyển mẫu, so other patterns are used to twist on shorter, slower balls. 2. The Player Pivots On Both Feet Djokovic striking a hard, chéo sân thuận tay by pivoting on both feet together, keeping his weight on his front foot. The double foot pivot is most useful when the player’s weight is moving forward, likely because they’re looking to be chủ động tấn công.

If a player wants to follow their shot through into the lưới, then picking the front foot off the ground and rotating about the back foot isn’t a great option; that won’t generate any sort of forward đà through the shot. Instead, the better option is to pick both heels off the ground and simply allow the feet to twist along with the hông as they rotate.

This di chuyển mẫu works very well on chủ động tấn công shots from the đường cuối sân and on approach shots inside the sân, since, on both of those kinds of shots, the player wants to chuyển their weight to their front (non-hitting) foot and take the ball early. This tư thế is stable and low impact. It doesn’t require jumping off the ground, and works particularly well when returning slow, low balls like slices.

On every full thuận tay vung vợt, a hông twist is required, but due to the nature of a bóng thấp, it would be very difficult to get either of the feet off the ground while striking it. Thus, for low balls, picking both heels up and pivoting on the balls of both feet works best. 3. The Hitting Foot Comes Up and Kicks Back Federer utilizing his classic “hop step” to strike a high, slow thuận tay aggressively and continue forward to lưới.

Another chủ động tấn công di chuyển mẫu that’s commonly used when a player wishes to come forward involves a conscious đường bóng off the hitting leg. The player initiates their twist by driving so hard off the hitting leg that it comes up off the ground, and as the player subsequently twists their hông, this off-the-ground hitting foot naturally kicks back behind them.

It’s extremely important that the player’s non-hitting foot is not restricted during this process, as, again, if it’s firmly planted on the ground, an ankle snapping amount of torsion lực is going into that ankle (or maybe the đầu gối). A common, natural way to cán vợt the large amount of lực placed on the front (non-hitting) foot during this mẫu is to allow it to come up off the ground as the lực pulls it.

This particular di chuyển mẫu, often known as the “hop step,” is a favorite of Roger Federer when hitting approach shots. Don’t be misled by the name, though. There is no conscious “hop” in the traditional sense; the player doesn’t try to hop off the front foot at any point, but rather just allows the forces that want to pull the foot off the ground during the theo sau to do so. 4.

Both Feet Come Off the Ground There are two different applications for the both-feet-off-the-ground thuận tay that are pretty disparate in nature. The first is if the player is lightly on the run – not on the dead run, but still required to hit without setting their feet. Nick Kyrgios is famous for the jumping thuận tay – a shot during which the lower body and thân generate a massive amount of lực, and the arm and cổ tay stay relaxed to chuyển that lực into the vợt.

In this case, it’s usually optimal for the player to perform a small jump as they rotate their hông back towards the target. This allows their feet their full range of freedom during the twist. Having the feet in contact with the ground while moving sideways and twisting the hông is a recipe for injury, and it’s best avoided by taking a small jump when striking a thuận tay on the move.

The second case is when the player wants to hit a shot really, really hard, and both legs are driving so hard off the ground they’re going to fly off of it. This shot is most commonly used on high, slow, ngắn balls, and it’s very effective when executed correctly. The important thing to remember about the jumping thuận tay is that all the tension is in the legs and thân, and none of the tension is in the arm or hand.

The exterior links in the chuỗi động học must stay completely relaxed in order to efficiently chuyển the lực from the prime movers into the vợt. The thuận tay “C” – Myths vs Reality The “C” vung vợt path on the thuận tay does exist, but I’m not a fan of its current mainstream classification as a first principle of the thuận tay, because it isn’t one.

The shape and manner of the “C”’s execution changes a lot from shot to shot, because, similar to other derivative aspects of the vung vợt (like the volitional di chuyển of the arm or the theo sau) the specific “C” motion used on a particular shot is dynamically chosen, often subconsciously. The “C” shape itself is not a biomechanical underpinning of the vung vợt. It’s just created by coincidence in order to giao bóng other, more fundamental goals.

On the thuận tay, the two primary goals are to create up-and-through contact, and to do so by accelerating the vợt by rotating the strong muscles of the hông and abdomen. The resulting various “C” like swings are merely a way to naturally accomplish those goals. I see two issues in coach-player communication, which are common across tennis coaching, that manifest when the “C” vung vợt is coached.

It’s Actually a Warped “C” When an actual “C” is superimposed on Roger Federer’s thuận tay vung vợt path, it becomes clear that the shapes aren’t as similar as they initially appear. The first issue is that a player interprets what the coach tells them to do as being part of the “đưa vợt ra sau,” even though it isn’t. With the “C,” some players do not realize that much of the downward part of the C actually occurs during the forward vung vợt.

This part is quite abbreviated in its depth; while it happens, the vợt is primarily moving forward, not down. This misunderstanding almost can’t even be called a misunderstanding, because the student is trying their best to create a “C” like they’ve been told. As they try to create the “C,” they’re unaware that the actual vung vợt isn’t exactly a “C,” but rather a modified, warped version of that shape. It’s closer to something like the Nike whoosh, not symmetrical in top and bottom at all.

It’s Not Just a Hand Motion Second, a player doesn’t always realize which muscles đường bóng a motion their coach is telling them to emulate. With the “C,” this misunderstanding results in a player moving their hand around directly, primarily using the arm and chest, rather than moving it using the muscles of the lõi. Rafael Nadal’s lõi is unwinding and driving the vợt forward during the bottom arch of the the “C” vung vợt.

While the “C” motion does exist (roughly speaking), the “C” is not a motion created primarily by moving the hand around with the arm and chest muscles. The initial backward motion of the hand is entirely due to the lõi rotating away from the target. The remaining backward motion is khuỷu tay duỗi; it is not due to pulling the hand back behind the chest. The hand is then lowered, and this lowering involves the legs and torso, not merely bringing the arm closer to the body.

Any remaining downward action takes place during the forward vung vợt, and it’s mostly a thụ động effect of not resisting gravity. The hand isn’t actively pushed down by the player. The Underhand Pendulum vung vợt Beginners, especially young children, who are taught the “C” as a first principle, often start driving the ball with the wrong lực generator. They pull the vợt back high, then use gravity to giao lại it fall down and forward like a pendulum.

It hits the bottom of the pendulum motion, then bounces back up and hits through the ball. I see many players who are coached on the “C” as a first principle end up with a purely arm driven vung vợt. These beginners initially coached on the “C” end up with a purely arm driven vung vợt, during which they remain sideways, never unwinding their hông and abs.

This robs them of the most efficient, effective engine available to them on the thuận tay, and develops in them a proprioceptive misunderstanding of how tốc độ đầu vợt is generated. This gravity pendulum effect is a very small effect when it comes to generating lực in the modern thuận tay. Nearly all the lực is generated from forward leg đường bóng and hông and abdominal xoay, not the gravitational pendulum.

Therefore, despite the fact that a modified “C” vung vợt path does exist, coaching to that effect doesn’t appear to đường bóng the typical beginner’s natural sense of cảm giác sâu to the right place. When they try to orient their body to “perform a C,” they end up accidentally teaching themselves that the thuận tay is a gravity driven pendulum, rather than a forward twisting motion.

The “C” Can Prevent Jerkiness Before we move onto what I believe to be a superior way to understand the thuận tay, I’d like to give the “C” shape some credit. The most comfortable way to allow the vợt to flick out to the ball, and then up and through it, is in fact to allow gravity to pull the hand down slightly as it begins to move. The “C” definitely implies that such a downward motion exists.

The hand absorbs the lõi’s rotational năng lượng most efficiently when allowed to follow its natural shallow down-then-back-up mẫu. Without allowing the vợt to initially travel down during the forward vung vợt, the motion is far less comfortable, especially on the khuỷu tay.

If you fully stop the hand after chuẩn bị, and then you jerk it only up and forwards from there, actively preventing gravity from pulling it down, the lõi’s rotational năng lượng won’t be transferred to the arm/vợt/hand system as losslessly. The hand absorbs the lõi’s rotational năng lượng most efficiently when allowed to follow its natural shallow down-then-back-up mẫu.

The Two Part thuận tay Paradigm I like to think of the thuận tay vung vợt as two steps, rather than one: chuẩn bị Forward vung vợt The chuẩn bị phase ends with the player preparing their hand for the forward explosion. This typically involves lowering it to a height from which it can easily flick up and through the ball. As the “C” correctly asserts, the most comfortable way to fling your hand forward typically involves the hand being allowed to move down first.

As a player practices, they’ll gain an (often subconsciously) understanding of to what degree that downward motion happens on each shot, and they’ll learn to lower their hand appropriately during chuẩn bị to account for that. At the end of Rafael Nadal’s chuẩn bị (left), his hand has been lowered and separated from his body. The rest of the hand’s downward trajectory, the bottom of the supposed “C” motion, occurs during the forward vung vợt (right).

By this point, Rafa’s hông and abs have already begun to unwind. After chuẩn bị and before the forward vung vợt exists a waiting period. The waiting period is critical to success in the absence of rhythm. To be able to consistently time balls of various speeds and spins effectively, thuận tay chuẩn bị should be completed as early as possible once the oncoming ball’s trajectory is discovered.

This early chuẩn bị allows for maximum improvisational freedom with respect to the forward vung vợt itself, because the body has a large window during which it’s fully ready to vung vợt, and the vung vợt can be initiated at any point in that window. This waiting period can be implemented as a completely static vị trí, but it’s more effective to slow down or speed up the lowering the hand phase in order to time the incoming ball.

When chuẩn bị is done very early, the hand should be very slowly lowered into vị trí, and similarly, when chuẩn bị is done very late, the hand should be quickly thrown down into vị trí and then immediately yanked forward by the lõi’s xoay from there. Instead of Thinking About a “C”… For the top half of the C, just think about regular thuận tay chuẩn bị. Turn away from the ball, and once you do, lower your hand into vị trí.

Lower your hand quickly, if you’re pressed for time, or slowly, while you wait for the ball, if you’ve prepared very early. For the bottom half of the C, don’t think about the vợt path at all. Visualize the kind of contact you want to make with the ball, relax your hand and arm, and then đường bóng the hand/arm/vợt system forward by twisting your lõi. Find an arm path that’s comfortable for you, for which you can feel the efficient chuyển of lực from your explosive turn into your vợt.

Allowing your hand to dip somewhat and then bounce back up will feel most fluid, but you’ll need to find the exact manner in which you do so via experimentation. If you lực your hand down and back up, the vung vợt won’t work. Federer’s “C” vung vợt split into two motions which better explain what’s happening. During chuẩn bị (left), Federer lowers his hand, preparing for the forward explosion. This can be done quickly or slowly.

The remaining part of the supposed “C” is merely the forward vung vợt (right), during which the hand is driven forward by the lõi, and is allowed to briefly dip down at the beginning. Try to figure out what your hand wants to do as you đường bóng it forward with your hông and abs. If something hurts, don’t do it, and if feels good, keep doing it. The “C” shape is merely a rough visual explanation of what the hand happens to do during the vung vợt, rather than something the hand tries to do.

As the hông and abs đường bóng the hand forward, the hand is allowed to fall down a bit before flying back up through contact. The primary goal of the forward vung vợt is not to create that lower “C” path. The goal is to create explosive up-and-through contact with the ball.

Allowing the vợt to fall and then spring back up is often the best way to chuyển the lực from the lõi’s xoay into that up-and-through contact, but, again, that down-then-back-up motion occurs in service to the rest of the cú đánh, rather than constitutes its engine itself. https://www.feeltennis.lưới/universal-vung vợt/ More on the physics of the vung vợt http://ftptennis.lưới/ftp-tennis-college/ftp-tennis-college-courses/technical-tennis/the-thuận tay/section-02-the-thuận tay-introduction/section-03-the-thuận tay-vung vợt-components/#:~:text=Groundstrokes%20are%20basically%20a%20circular,when%20viewed%20from%20the%20side.

Wait Then Fire – Success in the Absence of Rhythm The final phase of thuận tay chuẩn bị is a waiting period. The thuận tay vung vợt is not a single, continuous motion. There isn’t a uniform, rhythmic flow from chuẩn bị to forward vung vợt. Instead, chuẩn bị and forward vung vợt are two distinct steps, chuẩn bị happening as soon as possible, and forward vung vợt happening once the ball reaches a certain point.

Take Grigor Dimitrov’s điểm thắng, the chuẩn bị for which is depicted below, for proof of this fact. In the image, the ball has only just crossed the lưới, and yet Grigor is already fully prepared for the shot. His entire chuỗi động học is wound, and his vợt is ready. Obviously, were he to vung vợt now, he would miss the ball, so the final step before Grigor initiates his forward vung vợt is to wait until the ball reaches the proper contact zone.

Grigor Dimitrov about to blast an trong-ra ngoài thuận tay điểm thắng past Fernando Verdasco in the Wimbledon 2021 second round. What’s so demonstrative about this shot, specifically, is that Grigor actually doesn’t take a single step towards the ball while preparing: after Fernando’s shot is struck, Grigor quickly realizes that the ball is already traveling directly to his điểm tiếp xúc, and so there’s no need to move his body.

Since he’s already standing on the đường cuối sân, if he executes his shot from this location, he’ll be way ahead in the point. So instead of moving, Grigor simply: Loads his hông, turning them away from the ball Loads his abs, coiling his torso away from the ball Prepares his hand, placing it behind him Once he does that, he waits. He waits until he sees the ball into the perfect hitting zone, and then he uncorks and strikes the điểm thắng.

Success in the Absence of Rhythm Despite all the stoppages and walking around that occurs during a tennis trận đấu, pros almost never miss. Despite their opponent’s doing everything they can to change pace, change spins, and change directions, pros almost never miss. How? Because they understand how to wait between chuẩn bị and forward vung vợt. This waiting period allows them to succeed in the absence of rhythm. Each cú đánh is a little different, and that’s okay.

Instead of one, fluid motion, the cú đánh is two parts: prepare for the strike, then execute the strike. I’ll leave you with a passage from The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, which explains this concept in detail. Waiting is also a critical piece of thuận tay chuẩn bị that is often overlooked. Not every shot will occur in perfect rhythm. In fact, the better your opponent, the less rhythm you’ll have to work with on your forehands.

thuận tay chuẩn bị is not one, fluid, continuous motion that occurs the same way every time. Far from it. Instead, as we keep mentioning, it is a means to an end. Proper chuẩn bị constitutes getting the body in vị trí, turning away from the ball, preparing the hand, and then waiting until the ball is in the proper place to initiate the forward vung vợt.

This waiting doesn’t have to be completely static – the “preparing the hand” phase can be done more quickly or more slowly in order to time the incoming ball – but, fundamentally, the last step of chuẩn bị is a ngắn waiting period between body chuẩn bị and vung vợt initiation. On balls that come extremely quickly, this waiting period may be zero. On balls that come extremely slowly, this waiting period may be long.

Your cue for when to initiate the forward vung vợt is not that a fixed amount of time has passed since you started your thuận tay chuẩn bị. Rather, it is a visual cue. You are visually tracking the ball during your chuẩn bị, and once the ball gets to a certain location, you initiate your vung vợt. The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. “Success in the Absence of Rhythm.” Page 75. 6 COMMENTS Cyril July 3, 2021 It’s just a matter of definition. What is chuẩn bị ?

If you take into account the vợt path, there’s the highest vị trí, the rearmost vị trí, the lowest vị trí, the vị trí at contact and the most forward vị trí. The highest vị trí has to be reached early and I call it the early chuẩn bị phase (power vị trí with body weight mainly on the back leg). From this vị trí you time the rest of the chuẩn bị phase to reach the rearmost vị trí in relation with the start of the chuỗi động học (leg đường bóng).

I’ve seen a video of Federer with the vợt removed in slow mo and it seems his hand never stops moving in the đưa vợt ra sau however clearly there are players who pause. Moving the hand into vị trí is definitely a cảm giác sâu challenge when your trying to rebuild your cú đánh into a lỗi Tolerant FH.

Are there any tips for overcoming this lỗi? REPLY Johnny (FTF) July 23, 2021 Again, I believe most players are not conscious of what their hand is doing during the prepare the hand stage. Some players take a big loop, some take a small loop, some completely pause, and some change it up on every shot.

The important thing, as I stress over and over again in the book, is just that you visualize the contact you want to create, and then, as early as possible, get the hand into a vị trí such that all that’s left in order to create said contact is to explosively twist forward.

I’m fairly certain you don’t need specific proprioceptive awareness of exactly what your hand is doing during “prepare the hand”, but rather just a sense of the vung vợt path you’re going to try to create, and the rough starting point of that vung vợt path. REPLY Poida August 22, 2021 Thanks for this followup, sorry I didn’t respond earlier. Good points regarding the unconscious prep of the hand and the visualization process.

There’s the old saying “paralysis by analysis” and it’s an easy trap to fall into, and unfortunately some coaches unwittingly set up this trap for their students. Similar to baseball, it’s unlikely MLB or elite players think about their bat vị trí when they’re at the plate. They already know which side of their body the ball will pass so their prep is done when they step into the batters box. The only job is to track and explode rotationally into contact they have visualized.

Everything is happing too fast to think about what the bat is doing in the đưa vợt ra sau and transition into the forward vung vợt. In training the thuận tay, and other swinging strokes, perhaps their are lessons we can learn from baseball. REPLY Johnny (FTF) August 23, 2021 Yep. Paralysis by analysis is hard to avoid when coaching a complex shot. Also, the chuỗi động học on a baseball vung vợt is remarkably similar to the tennis thuận tay.

The hông, abs, visual tracking, and maintenance of a still đầu vợt during the entire motion are almost identical. REPLY A Primer on the trái tay một tay The one handed trái tay is a simpler cú đánh than the thuận tay. If that doesn’t seem accurate to you, chances are you’re executing the shot incorrectly.

There are only a few fundamental tenets of the trái tay một tay, the first of which is something we’ve already discussed at length: The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis: The vợt and the cẳng tay hình thức a 90o góc, and the ball is contacted in front of the body. On Dennis Shapovalov’s trái tay một tay, his vợt-cẳng tay góc barely unwinds, remaining close to 90o even well into his theo sau.

This stability allows him to vung vợt extremely explosively, while still maintaining a consistent string góc. The trái tay một tay is far less forgiving of late contact than the thuận tay is, so tracking the ball into a điểm tiếp xúc well in front of the body is of pivotal importance. There’s also less natural unwinding of the cẳng tay-vợt góc on the trái tay một tay than there is on the thuận tay.

On the thuận tay, it’s very common to see the góc unwind from 90o at maximum cổ tay trễ, to more like 135o by contact, and the cổ tay actually goes into gấp by the theo sau. When striking the trái tay một tay, on the other hand, the 90o góc is typically maintained all the way through the vung vợt and theo sau. This leads to a much more consistent string góc, and thereby a much more lỗi tolerant contact, than if the player were to allow their cổ tay góc to unwind.

The Upper Back is King Elite one-handed backhands are driven primarily by the upper back and the abdominals, although some players, like Stan Wawrinka, occasionally add some xoay hông. That’s not super common, though, and xoay hông isn’t necessary to have a world class trái tay. Many players, such as Roger Federer, hardly ever use xoay hông to power their backhands. The simplest effective trái tay một tay utilizes almost solely the upper back to power the vung vợt.

It consists of turning away from the ball, barely pulling your vợt back, and then using that strong upper back to đường bóng your entire vợt-arm system forward through the ball. This can be performed phẳng, by simply driving the vợt through the ball, or with some xoáy trên, by driving the vợt up and through the ball. On the simplest effective trái tay một tay, neither the hông nor the abs are used for vợt speed generation.

Roger Federer striking a service trả bóng điểm thắng using the simplest effective trái tay một tay. He barely pulls his vợt back at all, and then uses his upper back to đường bóng the entire vợt-arm system forward through contact. Very little motion occurs, and the abs and hông are used only for stability. Don’t Pull Your khuỷu tay Forward It’s very important that the trái tay một tay is driven primarily with the upper back and not with the weak muscles of the triceps.

Many players, when they try to mimic what the vung vợt looks like, instead pull forward with the (weak) muscles in their arms, leading with their khuỷu tay, instead of using their (strong) upper back musculature to đường bóng the entire vợt-arm system forward as a unit. The Abs are Queen Many players with elite one-handed backhands, such as Stan Wawrinka and Dennis Shapovalov, engage their abdominals on almost every trái tay.

This is done by turning the lõi away from the ball during chuẩn bị, and then unwinding towards the target during the forward vung vợt. Unlike on the thuận tay, the chest should not face the target at contact on the trái tay một tay. Even when the abs are used to power the vung vợt, the chest will still typically face 90o away from the target by contact. This is because the optimal contact vị trí for the one handed trái tay is with the body sideways from the target, not facing it.

Stan Wawrinka turns his chest away from the anticipated contact during chuẩn bị, and then unwinds it during the forward vung vợt, accelerating the vợt with his abdominal muscles. Typically, the non-hitting arm extends backwards to counter-thăng bằng the hitting arm during the vung vợt, unlike on the thuận tay, on which both arms move together.

The more abdominal xoay is used, the less you’ll see this counter-balancing effect, and the more biomechanically similar your thuận tay and trái tay will be. Adjusting to Contact Dennis Shapovalov adjusting to an exceptionally low trái tay by sitting low with the legs, and significantly tilting his torso. Just like on the thuận tay, adjustment should be made primarily by sitting lower with the legs and tilting the torso.

This will allow you to đường bóng a low trái tay using the same muscles you use when driving a typical height trái tay. The best way to learn proper adjustment is to develop an understanding of what it feels like to đường bóng the ball with your upper back, and once you get more advanced, with both your upper back and your abs. Then, try to do that every time, even on awkward contact heights.

Eventually, you’ll naturally start adjusting by sitting lower and angling your torso, because if you do anything else, you’ll have to fundamentally change the way you đường bóng your vung vợt. 2 COMMENTS Philip Tsai December 22, 2021 Can you explain how, when the elites hit a trái tay một tay, they use the abs and NOT the hông? Also does vợt rotate around the cẳng tay in windshield wiper fashion like in the thuận tay? REPLY Johnny (FTF) January 7, 2022 Typically, the hông stay closed.

Some, like Wawrinka, will unwind a little, but others, like Roger and Gasquet, won’t unwind their hông at all. Instead, they just torque their uppper body around, and đường bóng the vợt out using their upper back. There is some windshield wiper action, but not as much, and not on every shot. For example, when redirecting the ball thẳng biên, you’ll often see the vợt đầu vợt stay on the hitting side of the body. The key, though, is to experiment yourself.

Wawrinka, Federer, and Gasquet all have world class backhands, and yet they strike them pretty significantly differently. Same with Theim and Shapovalov. You’ll arrive at whoever’s version of the cú đánh fits your particular style via experimentation. 5 Critical Serving Insights The giao bóng is a technically difficult shot, but that doesn’t mean you can’t master it. Below are five critical insights that aren’t immediately obvious when you start your serving journey.

Internalize them, and it will greatly simplify your learning process. 1. Don’t throw at the target. The tennis giao bóng is not identical to a throw, but the misunderstanding that a tennis giao bóng is not fundamentally a throwing motion (which it is), is often fueled by one of the small differences between the two: Serving is throwing, but serving at a target is not throwing at a target. The service motion is an upward forward explosion.

Stand at the đường giao bóng and pretend to throw at the đều side service box. Pause at your giải phóng, then put your vợt in your hand, in the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa. If you’ve done it right, your vợt should be slapping the ball straight down, and way off to your left (for a righty). In order to giao bóng into the service box, you need to orient your throwing motion up and right of where you want the ball to go (or left, for a lefty).

The service motion is an upward forward explosion, not a straight forward explosion like pitching. Throw your hand up and past the ball. As your hand passes the ball, your vai will internally rotate and your dây will slap it into the box. 2. 90-90 chuẩn bị Iga Swiatek doesn’t prepare in 90-90 (left), but, like all effective servers, she does enter the vị trí (right) before explosively rotating her torso.

The 90-90 idea is common in baseball, but for whatever reason, tennis players don’t always learn it.

Funny enough, the best female player in the world actually doesn’t use it (to start, she gets into it later in the vung vợt). [Keep a 90 degree góc between the cột sống and upper arm, which is in line with the vai and another 90 degree góc between the upper arm and cẳng tay, hence 90-90] You should, though, keep your khuỷu tay joint at 90o, and keep the humerus (your upper arm bone) in line with your vai, perpendicular to your torso.

This alignment, where the hitting arm is in line with the vai, is the biomechanical vị trí we need in order for our third tip – axial xoay – to safely and efficiently generate high vận tốc. Use the muscles around your scapula (your vai blade) to “connect” your khuỷu tay to your torso. Lock it in – down and back – into that 90-90 vị trí, and when the torso explosively rotates, the khuỷu tay will naturally rotate with it.

Possibly the two greatest height adjusted servers of all time, Andy Roddick (left) and Pete Sampras (right) both employ a nearly exact 90-90 đang nạp vị trí as they prepare. 3. Axial xoay Throwing xoay occurs perpendicular to the axis of the cột sống. During Andy Roddick’s rotational explosion, his vai xoay is exactly perpendicular to his cột sống. Imagine a point on each of your vai. As you rotate, those points are going to draw a circle in the air.

Your vai line should be the diameter of that circle, and your cột sống will point up through its giữa. Since the giao bóng is an upward throwing motion, you are going to be tilted – hitting vai above non-hitting vai – such that when you rotate, your vợt hand is flung upwards. If you struggle with coordinating that initial explosion, work on this axial xoay.

Internalize that swinging up to the ball means engaging your lõi muscles, adopting a balanced, stable lõi posture, and then violently twisting around. Since you’ve used your upper back muscles to attach your khuỷu tay to your torso, this xoay is going to accelerate your arm quickly and safely. The better your posture while rotating, the more efficient your xoay is going to be, and the faster your vợt will go. 4. Long is good.

A few fractions of a second before contact, Ash Barty’s vợt is still open; were the ball contacted here, it would fly well long. If Ash Barty contacted her giao bóng in the image shown here, rather than a few fractions of a second later, she’d sail the ball out past the đường cuối sân. This means that if you yourself sail a ball out past the đường cuối sân, there’s a decent chance your fundamental motion is fine. The corrective tweak to bring the ball down into the box is small.

Toss What if Ash had tossed the ball such that, when she reached this point in her vung vợt, she was contacting it? Well, she’d hit it long, or she’d have to contort her vung vợt to đường bóng the ball down, killing her vận tốc. Tossing the ball sufficiently far forward is necessary such that, when the vợt is striking the ball, it’s on slapping towards the box part of its trajectory.

This will vary depending on the type of giao bóng you’re hitting, but, in general, if you hit a few serves long, try the same motion with a farther forward toss, before making larger changes to your motion. Timing There exists a point during the vung vợt at which the vợt is aiming towards the box. If you are hitting long, that point is later in the vung vợt than your điểm tiếp xúc. Start your rotational explosion a little earlier, or execute it faster.

You need to be deeper into your motion by the time you strike the ball. The primary pitfall I see students run into, when self-correcting their long serves, is that they interfere with the efficient motion they’ve developed. Thinking it’s not “down” enough, they try to volitionally “bật down” using the hand and cẳng tay muscles, thereby breaking the efficiency of the chain. For most students I’ve worked with, the “bật” internalization doesn’t work at all.

I’ve heard it countless times: “They just tell me to bật, but it doesn’t work. I’ll never be able to giao bóng.” Twist your thân, and relax your hand. If you’re hitting long, toss farther forward, or twist earlier and faster. 5. Commit with your eyes. A giao bóng is a much easier interceptive task than, say, a giao bóng trả bóng. You know where the ball is going to be, and, if you’re tossing properly, the ball is moving very slowly at the time you strike it.

Because of this, almost all elite servers don’t watch the ball all the way into contact when serving. To do so would put the đầu vợt in a bit of an unnatural vị trí – it’s much more comfortable to allow the đầu vợt to rotate with the torso as you explode. But this doesn’t mean we can’t harness thị lực to improve our giao bóng. As you’re about to initiate your motion, your gaze should be locked on the ball. Do not allow your gaze to move until you’ve fully committed to your vung vợt.

During the upward explosion (left), Nick Kyrgios’s eyes are still locked on his contact, but by contact itself (right), he has allowed his đầu vợt to rotate forward (matching the natural xoay of his torso). Maintaining your gaze on your target will improve your motor performance. When throwing a baseball, that target is, well, your throw’s target. In tennis, on the other hand, we’re “throwing” our vợt, and thus our “target” for that throw is the ball itself.

At the moment you initiate, you’re visualizing flinging your hand past the ball so the vợt can strike it towards the box. You have a mental image (or brief movie) of the contact you’re trying to create, and your throwing motion is the means by which you create it. Then, after you are fully engaged into your vung vợt, you can begin to giao lại your đầu vợt turn. I find many students pull both their non-hitting arm, and their eyes, down too early in the service motion.

While we don’t have to watch it all the way into contact on the giao bóng, we still need to fully initiate our vung vợt before we allow our gaze to shift. The Pusher’s chiến lược is Better Than Yours The pusher’s chiến lược isn’t very good – hit the ball back into the sân and never play offense. Clearly, the pusher is wasting at least some opportunities to get ahead in rallies at very little cost.

You’ll often observe a pusher cán vợt a bóng ngắn by putting it high and slow over the lưới, and then running back to the đường cuối sân. Yuck. But, for many players, even the pusher’s mediocre chiến lược is still far better than theirs. So many players play their shots with absolutely inane frequencies and targets, using such an ineffective chiến lược that, actually, they too would be better off using the “hit every ball somewhat sâu and over the giữa of the lưới” chiến lược.

Recreational players, especially, choose targets which create distributions including far too many misses and end up wasting tons of equity every shot. And it’s doubly wasteful because, more often than not, their opponents are using those same, terrible targets, and thus if one player could just keep the darn ball in, their opponent would quickly spray it out for them. Why It’s So Frustrating Offense in tennis is difficult. There’s a reason that pushers dominate the lower age divisions.

This recreational strategic inaccuracy is part of the reason “pushers” get so much hate. Pushers are visibly playing a chiến lược which is not optimal; they routinely sacrifice equity by wasting tấn công opportunities. The non-pusher can so clearly and vividly identify how the pusher’s chiến lược is lacking; how can their opponent be any good if they never play offense? The non-pusher loathes the fact that the pusher doesn’t really do anything during the đánh qua lại, that he simply waits for them to miss.

And yet, even still, pushing is a far better chiến lược than the typical rec adopts. It’s just that the strategic flaws in their game are far more opaque than the pusher’s simple refusal to play offense. They don’t realize they’re aiming to terrible targets, and instead just bemoan how often they’re missing. The result is that you feel like you’re losing to someone playing a terrible chiến lược, and in a way you are, but you’re losing because you yourself are playing an even worse chiến lược.

Finding A Better chiến lược First, understand that offense in tennis is difficult. There’s a reason that pushers dominate the lower age divisions. Consistent offense is hard to play, while competent defense is proportionally much easier. A quality tấn công player must hit a huge variety of different shots, from different positions, effectively.

From slow, high balls at all parts of the sân, to transitional volleys, to low volleys and half volleys, to high volleys and swinging volleys, and of course, quality overheads – that’s a ton of different di chuyển patterns to master in order to play effective offense. The pusher has one goal – get the ball back into the sân. His target is huge. Contrast that with defense. You need a thuận tay, a trái tay, a thuận tay and trái tay cắt, and maybe a couple lobs. That’s it.

phòng thủ situations are far more similar to each other than tấn công situations are. Further, the tấn công player is aiming to smaller targets than the phòng thủ player is. The pusher has one goal – get the ball back into the sân. His target is huge. If your goal, unlike the pusher’s, is to hit the ball away from your opponent, you’re placing a much larger burden of execution on yourself than the pusher is on himself. Humility is Your Weapon The key to beating a pusher is self-awareness.

First, understand that the pusher might just actually be better than you. If the pusher can make 10 shots in a row, and you can neither make 10 shots in a row, nor consistently win the point with offense in <10 shots, then guess what… you’re going to lose, and the pusher doesn’t need any offense to beat you.

But assuming this isn’t the case, if you’re losing to a pusher, you are selecting shots which are not conducive to winning at your current skill level; you’re throwing away equity with every decision. Most players over-estimate their shot-making ability, and thus end up aiming to targets they can’t actually consistently hit. I’m not talking about your self-perceived skill level here, I’m talking about your actual, statistical skill level. Impartially observe the results of your shots.

When you aim for a certain target, where does the ball actually land, and how often? The 80% Rule – A Starting Point To beat a pusher, you need to dramatically increase the amount of shots you hit into the sân. Start with 80% as a threshold for how often neutral and tấn công shots need to go in (though it really should be more). Pick targets for which the ball will go in at least 80% of the time.

An intuitive target (left) that is actually far too chủ động tấn công for the depicted player’s skill level; too much of the distribution is out. Using a more conservative target (right) will lead to far better outcomes on this cú tiếp cận. If you can meet that 80% threshold and play offense, go for it, and your offense will actually work. At first, It may feel like you’re "not being chủ động tấn công enough," but that is incorrect. You’re being as chủ động tấn công as you’re capable of effectively being.

And here’s the thing – you’ll still hit winners. Sometimes, you’ll miss your target by hitting wider or deeper than you intended, and the ball will go screaming past the pusher with a satisfying whiz. More importantly, though, you’ll stop missing. By choosing a target you can routinely actually hit for each tấn công shot, your percentage of tấn công shots made will skyrocket.

You’ll stop missing your approach shots, or missing your volleys, or missing the last ball, and instead you’ll consistently (and satisfyingly) be running the pusher around. Now for some tough không. Not every recreational player can meet this 80% threshold with tấn công shots. If it turns out you don’t possess any tấn công shots that you can execute with over 80% consistency, then you simply are not skilled enough to execute the tấn công chiến lược that you’re trying to play.

The pusher should beat you. It’s time to đầu vợt back to the practice sân. And in the meantime, if you really want to win, maybe you should consider pushing, too. A Shot’s Probability Distribution Tennis is random. Each shot is best understood not as a particular result (điểm thắng, error, đánh qua lại ball, etc.), but as a probability distribution of possible results, from which a particular result is chosen at execution time. When a player selects a shot, they pick a target.

They aim at a particular point on the sân, and they aim a certain height over the lưới. These choices are not always fully conscious, but players generally have an idea of the shot they’re attempting as they strike the ball. After this decision is made, the distribution of possible outcomes is set. The image above is a visualization of that distribution.
