Mapping The Territory A probability distribution is a tool we use to describe a range of outcomes.

The diagram above indicates where the ball is likely to land after the shot depicted, a cross sân thuận tay (our pink guy is right-handed), is selected. The target, where the player is aiming, is in red, and the concentric yellow circles represent tiers of likelihood for where the ball will land. The darker the yellow, the more likely, while the lighter the yellow, the less likely.

Unless a player has a systematic inaccuracy built into their cú đánh (for example, they’re always late), the densest part of the distribution is at its giữa, close to the target. That’s where the ball is most likely to land. "Most" is relative; for an inaccurate player, it’s still not very likely to land there, it’s just more likely to land there than anywhere else. The farther from the target we travel, the less dense the distribution gets.

The exact probability density that each circle represents isn’t critical to understanding the lõi idea, and the numbers will vary significantly between players. That said, if numbers are helpful, assume that the inner circle is where roughly 50% of balls land, the giữa ring is where an additional 30% of balls land, and the outer one is where a final 18% land (leaving an extra 2% allocated to wild mishits and whiffs).

Also, the regions are cumulative – the chance that the ball lands in either the inner circle or the giữa ring is 50% + 30% = 80%. An accurate player (left) vs an inaccurate player (right) attempting the same shot. Note that the accurate player produces a much tighter distribution; there’s far less variability in where their shot will land. Skill Level Influences Shot Selection The breadth of a shot’s distribution varies wildly with the player’s skill.

It is essential to be realistic about the distributions that our own shots are generating. If we lie to ourselves, if we convince ourselves that our distributions are smaller than they really are, we’re going to end up aiming for bad targets and missing a lot. You’ll notice that a large amount of our inaccurate player’s distribution is out of bounds; he’s very likely to miss this thuận tay.

In fact, our player on the right is so inaccurate that he should never be using this target; in order for enough of his distribution to be in bounds to frequently continue the đánh qua lại, his just need to aim down the giữa. In contrast, this is a great target for our accurate player on the left. Both the most and second most likely region are almost entirely within the đánh đơn sân, and even many of the 18% most inaccurate balls they hit will still go in.

The vast majority of players would see immediate improvements if they would humbly observe where their attempted shots really land. First, Just Stop Missing The vast majority of players would see immediate improvements (in their đánh đơn game, specifically) if they would humbly observe where their attempted shots really land after choosing certain targets, and then adjusted those targets accordingly.

Be honest with yourself, and then generate a realistic model of your shot’s possible results in your mind. Once you do that, pick targets such that 80-90% of those results are in. Try it next time you’re on the sân, and you might find Missing in tennis is really bad. The player who misses loses the point immediately, with nothing at all demanded of their opponent. Further, nearly all recreational players overestimate their skill and therefore underestimate the spread of their distributions.

This means that most recreational players (and many competitive ones, for that matter) routinely aim for bad targets, for which too much of the probability mass of the distribution is out of bounds. that, suddenly, it’s your opponent who’s doing all the missing. Why Sprint to lưới? Sprinting to lưới changes the geometry of the sân, in a way that allows you to be chủ động tấn công without increasing your miss rate.

There are certain shots your opponent can hit that will lực you to stay in neutral if you’re at the đường cuối sân, but will allow you to play offense if you’re at lưới. Most of these shots are quite easy to execute. If you can’t play effectively at the lưới, your opponents will select these low degree of difficulty, high percentage shots over, and over, and over again, and you’ll find it very difficult to make progress in the đánh qua lại.

You’ll pound and pound with your groundstrokes, but despite your best efforts, you’ll always find yourself making an error before they do. The way to fix this issue is not to “stop making errors,” as so many frustrated pusher victims seem to think it is. Rather, the solution is to fundamentally change the nature of the game.

Here are the four most common shots that sprinting to lưới will beat: A sâu cắt A ngắn cắt A sâu block A moonball In all four of these cases, the shot is very difficult to tấn công from the đường cuối sân, but easy to tấn công at lưới. giao lại’s go through them one-by-one and discuss. The sâu cắt When your opponent slices the ball sâu, it’ll typically stay below your đầu gối after it bounces. This makes the ball very difficult to tấn công.

Your best option when your opponent gets their cắt sâu is to simply play the ball back sâu yourself, and wait for a shorter reply to tấn công. Rafael Nadal attacks with an chủ động tấn công trong-ra ngoài thuận tay. His opponent is defending from well behind the đường cuối sân, so the shot isn’t going to be an outright điểm thắng. What’s the best way to win this point? But what about if you’re at lưới?

Now, what was a very difficult đầu gối height shot becomes a chest height bắt lưới you can block away for a điểm thắng, or, if not an outright điểm thắng, at least an chủ động tấn công ball that’ll set up a điểm thắng in the future. It’s not trivial to bắt lưới a ball with xoáy dưới, but it’s worth the effort to learn how to do it, because volleying your opponent’s slices will allow you to tấn công them in a high percentage way that is geometrically impossible to do from the đường cuối sân.

The ngắn cắt A ngắn cắt that floats is pretty easy to tấn công from the đường cuối sân, but here we’re talking about a ngắn, low cắt. This ball, like the sâu cắt, won’t bounce up above your đầu gối, and as such it’ll be tough to hit hard. Since you’re forced to strike this ball from well inside the đường cuối sân, it’ll be even more difficult than on the sâu cắt to get the ball up and back down into your opponent’s sân, without accidentally sailing the ball out instead.

Nadal sprints forward as his opponent plays a phòng thủ shot from well behind the đường cuối sân. At lưới, of course, it’s a different story. These volleys can still be tough, because you’ll sometimes have to contact them below the height of the lưới, but they’re far more attackable than if you were at the đường cuối sân. Further, if you see your opponent about to respond with a cắt, sometimes they will, either intentionally or unintentionally, respond with a cúp – a really ngắn cắt.

If you’re at the đường cuối sân, you might not even get to the ball, whereas if you’re already sprinting to lưới, the fact that the ball is so ngắn won’t be an issue. The sâu Block One of the easiest ways to phòng thủ against pace is to stand 5-10 feet behind the đường cuối sân and block back every ball sâu. This chiến lược works so well it’s almost a cheat code.

Your opponent has to do everything right – relax, move well, track the ball, set up properly, explode, vung vợt – in order to execute their heavy, fast groundstrokes, and all you need to do is move to the ball and catch it in front of you, and you get to keep the point neutral. Unless… your opponent is willing to sprint to lưới.

Now, all of the sudden, these block shots which used to keep the point neutral have turned into trivial vai height volleys, and unlike the slices we discussed earlier, blocks are not difficult to bắt lưới at all. Since Nadal elected to sprint to lưới, he gets to play the ball as an easy bắt lưới while his opponent is still recovering, with two easy angles for winners. Had he stayed back, he’d be playing the ball from sâu in his trái tay góc.

The Moonball The bane of every recreational player’s existence, the pusher, is easily defeated once you have the right toolkit at lưới. Like the last three shots we talked about, the moonball is very difficult to tấn công from the đường cuối sân. Unless you’ve developed an extremely lỗi tolerant thuận tay, and have mastered both effortless and effortful power, your best option against a moonball is to roll the ball sâu with xoáy trên, and live to fight another shot. Sprinting to the lưới fixes this.

If you do, what was a moonball that was bouncing up above your vai, which you’re forced to hit while your opponent has already recovered, is now either a swinging bắt lưới or an bóng trên cao, which you hit while your opponent is still out of vị trí. Hold on – I have to execute a swinging bắt lưới or bóng trên cao while sprinting in? That sounds hard. It is.

Of the four kinds of shots we’ve talked about, the moonball is certainty the hardest to defeat, because to do so requires shots of the highest degree of difficulty. Learn the Swinging bắt lưới You must, must, must learn how to hit a swinging bắt lưới if you want to maximize your potential on the sân. If you don’t, you’re giving your opponent very easy, low degree of difficulty, phòng thủ shots that allow them to stay neutral. High sâu block shots and moonballs will always beat you.

You’ll hit great cú đánh nền after great cú đánh nền, but it won’t matter, because your opponent will stand 10 feet behind the đường cuối sân and continue rolling the ball back to you sâu until you miss. The swinging bắt lưới is a difficult shot. It requires great visual tracking, proper set up, and a very lỗi tolerant cú đánh, but it’s worth it to master, because it solves this problem.

Leylah Fernandez hits an unbelievable trong-ra ngoài swinging bắt lưới to win the point against Elina Svnitolina, en route to her quarterfinal win at the 2021 US Open. Without the swinging bắt lưới, this point stays neutral. The counter to the sâu, floating phòng thủ shot is to sprint in, and then while the ball floats over to you, keep sprinting in. Get as far in as you can, and then use your lỗi tolerant thuận tay to take the ball out of the air and win the point.

This is what Leylah does, this is what Emma does, and this is what you need to learn to do. Learn the bóng trên cao On the tour, everyone has a good bóng trên cao, so lobs aren’t super common. At the recreational level, and even the higher 4.5-5.0 club level, this is far from the case, first, because lobs are easy to hit, and second, because so many players have bad overheads. If you can’t hit an bóng trên cao, your opponent will quickly figure it out, and going to lưới will be all but pointless.

Again, like the swinging bắt lưới, the bóng trên cao is an extremely high degree of difficulty shot, but mastering it is worth it, because it beats all of your opponent’s easy phòng thủ options. The bóng trên cao is the same basic cú đánh as the giao bóng – a throwing motion that takes place with your vợt in your hand, instead of a ball.

Once you can internalize how to throw your vợt to create the bóng trên cao cú đánh, you’ll have a versatile bóng trên cao that won’t break down under áp lực, and, again, you’ll change the geometry of the sân in a way that wins points. Basic chiến lược – Where’s My Edge? Tennis chiến lược is best understood at the level of individual shot selection decisions; for each unique situation we are forced to play, we have a different chiến lược, appropriate for that particular situation.

Skill levels in tennis vary a lot, not just from player to player, but even from cú đánh to cú đánh for a single player. Due to this, an optimal tennis chiến lược is built not as a single monolith of frequencies and targets, but rather as a group of sub-strategies – disparate sets of frequencies and targets decided separately for each different kind of ball we have to hit.

A simple thuận tay from the thuận tay góc chiến lược – 70% Cross sân, 25% thẳng biên, 5% cúp Instead of over generalizing chiến lược, creating something like a “chiến lược for the trận đấu,” it’s much more useful to create a “thuận tay from the thuận tay góc chiến lược,” or a “phòng thủ trái tay chiến lược.” More useful than improving your “service game chiến lược” is to improve your “ad side second giao bóng chiến lược,” or your “bóng ngắn up the giữa chiến lược.” Whenever we talk about "chiến lược" at lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we are referring to specific, distinct, measurable strategies that involve specific strokes in specific situations, like "ad side second giao bóng chiến lược," not some vague, broad chiến lược spanning the numerous disparate, distinct, and often unrelated decisions both players make throughout the trận đấu.

Different Strategies for Different Shots Our chiến lược for each situation is a description of how we will play that situation – what targets will we use, and with what frequency will we aim to those targets. Different situations will call for different targets and frequencies. Correct chiến lược will vary widely not only from trận đấu to trận đấu, but even from cú đánh to cú đánh. For example, giao lại’s say you have an awesome thuận tay and a terrible trái tay.

Your optimal thuận tay chiến lược is going to be pretty chủ động tấn công, while your best trái tay chiến lược will resemble something along the lines of “just try to get the ball back in the sân.” A viable chiến lược for a player with a great thuận tay and terrible trái tay. The trái tay is played to a conservative target while the thuận tay is played quite aggressively. Find Your Winning Patterns Accurate chiến lược must also factor in our opponent’s game.

The crux of basic chiến lược is observing the patterns we see play out throughout the trận đấu, and using those observations to adjust our shot selection. If our opponent is missing every ball, then if we make one shot anywhere, we’ll win the point, and therefore a “just get it back in” chiến lược is optimal.

On the other hand, if our opponent is great at playing offense, winning 90% of the points when we drop a ball ngắn, then we need to adapt – move our targets deeper, accepting a few more misses in order to stop sacrificing so much equity on ngắn balls.

The target on the left is typically better, since it includes far less misses, but if our opponent is winning 90% of the rallies after we drop a ball ngắn, then the right target is better; we accept some misses in order to get the densest part of our distribution sâu enough to stay neutral. There are tons of different patterns to observe. Maybe we win most of the thuận tay to thuận tay rallies, but lose most of the trái tay to trái tay ones.

Maybe our opponent is particularly bad at handling the low cắt, but great at the vai height thuận tay. When forming a chiến lược (and adjusting it in real time during a trận đấu), the crucial first step is performing an accurate evaluation of all the strokes on the sân (both yours, and your opponent’s), and how they play against each other before strategic adjustment.

Once we’ve identified which patterns are beneficial to us, and by how much, and which patterns are detrimental to us, and by how much, then we choose shots which move us towards the beneficial patterns, and away from the detrimental ones. Basic Shot Selection After we’ve gathered enough information, for each shot we are forced to play, we can rank all of our options in order of equity. We’ll have our highest equity shot, our next best shots, some mediocre shots, and some bad shots.

In a perfect world, we’d like to play our best shot every time, but, of course, our opponent will probably adjust to prevent us from doing that. Still, the information about how our game aligns with theirs before any adjustment is extremely valuable. As the trận đấu progresses and games are won and lost, this knowledge keeps us focused on what’s important and guides our real time strategic adjustment. What would I like to do? How is my opponent preventing that?

How much do I need to deviate to prevent being exploited? Basic chiến lược grounds us so that, as we build more complex strategies, we know exactly why we’re adding that complexity. By keeping the basics in mind, we’ll never fall victim to over-complication or over analysis. We won’t over adjust, won’t out think ourselves, because our best options and highest equity shots are always at the front of our mind.

Our chiến lược isn’t built to be "unpredictable" or "clever." It’s a tool we use to abuse our advantages as much as possible, without accidentally losing them in the process. Mixed chiến lược – An Antidote to Guessing A mixed chiến lược is a chiến lược in which we mix our shot selection decisions for a given situation; we won’t hit any shot 100% of the time. Consider an extremely common situation – the thuận tay from the thuận tay góc.

Against a player with far inferior thuận tay to your own, a pure chiến lược of 100% chéo sân may be appropriate. Most of the time, though, your opponent will be able to phòng thủ effectively off of both wings if they know what’s coming. In a perfect world, you’d like to hit every shot to their thuận tay, but if they know that you’re going to do that, they’ll start to cheat over to the thuận tay side as you vung vợt, and subsequently the lợi thế you gain from hitting to that side vastly diminishes.

Most players are players are both competent enough and willing to make strategic adjustments to prevent you from using your initial highest equity option 100% of the time. In order to maintain your edge then, you need a way to take lợi thế of that adjustment. This is why we play a mixed chiến lược – a chiến lược that mixes in second best shots which are worse in a vacuum, but improve against your opponent’s adjustment. Why Not Just Always Counter Their Guess?

If your opponent is guessing to the chéo sân side, why not always go thẳng biên? And similarly, if they are guessing over to the thẳng biên side, why not always go cross sân? If your opponent is guessing poorly, go for it. Sometimes, your opponent will commit to their guess early enough that you can observe their guess before you vung vợt. If that’s the case, then yes, just hit it away from them.

Your opponent is guessing improperly – from a strategic standpoint, what they are doing doesn’t even constitute guessing; they’re really just altering their phòng thủ positioning. When you hit it away from your opponent in this case, you are leveraging their improper guessing kỹ thuật. That’s great – mixed chiến lược isn’t meant to phòng thủ against opponents who transparently change their phòng thủ positioning.

If it’s obvious, in the moment, how to hit the ball to take lợi thế of your opponent’s adjustment, go for it. But giao lại’s give our opponent credit and assume that their phòng thủ adjustments will be made late enough in our vung vợt that we can’t look up and check what they’re up to – to do so at that point would ruin our lỗi tolerance. In this case, we don’t know how they will adjust before we select our shot. But still, you ask, why play a randomized chiến lược?

Isn’t it better to choose a particular shot each time to counter what my opponent is doing? Leveling Doesn’t Work If my opponent guesses left, I want to hit right, and if they guess right, I want to hit left. So shouldn’t my chiến lược still be to figure out how they’re guessing, and then do the opposite? Why would I play a chiến lược where I randomly select my shots, like 65% chéo sân 35% thẳng biên, when, depending on what my opponent does, there’s always one of those two I’d rather do?

Because you don’t know what they’re going to do. Trying to approach your decisions this way leads to absurdity: I want to go chéo sân, because my thuận tay is better than theirs. They know that, so they’re going to guess chéo sân, so I’m going to go thẳng biên. But they know that I know that they’re going to guess chéo sân, and then I’ll go thẳng biên instead, so I’ll actually still go chéo sân.

But they also know that I know that they know that I know that they’re going to guess chéo sân, and so go-thẳng biên instead, and then because of that still go chéo sân… so I’ll go thẳng biên. But they ALSO know that I know that they know that I know that… One of the MAJOR casualties of this line of thinking is the nonsensical reasoning it produces on important points: Now it’s điểm trận.

They KNOW that I like chéo sân the best, and that I’ll use it on điểm trận, so I have to go thẳng biên Ah, but of course I would think that, it’s điểm trận after all, so I should actually go chéo sân because surely they’ll guess thẳng biên. It’s impossible to execute under these conditions.

It looks like you’re doing analysis, like you’re making progress towards some objective truth about what the right shot to hit is, but really you’re just pointlessly flailing around in an infinitely recursive mental loop, producing no strategic insight whatsoever. This kind of reasoning, often called “leveling,” is often mis-applied in the above way. The infinite recurse is not useful thinking in and of itself – it only leads to an unproductive guessing game.

Luckily, this leveling death spiral can be avoided, so you can just relax and execute on điểm trận. Mixed chiến lược to the Rescue We know that quality opponents will make strategic adjustments to prevent us from playing our best shot 100% of the time. These adjustments will improve their performance against our best shot, at the cost of worsening their performance against other shots. All adjustments are tradeoffs.

Consider our case from earlier where our best shot is our chéo sân thuận tay, so our opponent often cheats over to the chéo sân side to cover it. This positioning is better against the chéo sân thuận tay, but worse against the thẳng biên thuận tay. When our opponent is adjusting late enough, we have to make our shot selection decisions under uncertainty.

In order to maintain our equity against a particular adjustment, we need to start hitting those initially second best shots which are improved against that adjustment. But remember, we have to select our shot without knowing whether our opponent has chosen to hold their ground, or to cheat over and cover the chéo sân. If we knew he was holding his ground, we’d want to go chéo sân, and likewise if we knew he was cheating over, we’d like to go thẳng biên. But we don’t.

We can’t use a 100% chiến lược, because we have no idea which 100% chiến lược to use. When our opponent is adjusting late enough, we have to make our shot selection decisions under uncertainty. This means playing our shots with frequencies that are effective regardless of how they decide to phòng thủ. So how often do we hit each shot? The answer – it depends on how good you are at everything.

How to Find Your Frequencies There are actually game theoretical rules that determine exactly how much we can press our lợi thế without leaving ourselves open to a counter-chiến lược. Mathematically, there exists an unexploitable, balanced, mixed chiến lược which, when adopted, makes it irrelevant how our opponent defends – no matter how they choose to guess, our equity remains unchanged.

In the case of tennis, the mathematical explanation, tends to be more academic than practical (we still provide it, of course). From a practical standpoint, when deciding how to mix your frequencies to counter an opponent’s adjustment, your highest equity shot before that adjustment was made is still your starting point. It’s the shot where your greatest lợi thế lies, and as such the one you’ll try to use most.

Titrate With A Second Best Shot As your opponent adjusts to your HESBA, you’ll see its effectiveness start to decrease. You’ll notice that, before, you were winning 4/5 points every time you selected it, but now, for some reason, you’re only winning half. Changes are, your opponent is cheating over while you aren’t looking.

To combat that, start mixing in another shot with a low frequency, a second best shot in a vacuum, but a shot which improves against the adjustment your opponent is making to your HESBA. This shot will likely be very effective at first, as your opponent has probably adopted something close to a pure “counter the HESBA” chiến lược.

After the defender commits to guessing, the attacker will notice the chéo sân thuận tay becoming less effective, and should employ a mixed chiến lược, mixing chéo sân and thẳng biên shots, to maximize his equity against the guesses. Increase the second best shot’s frequency until you feel like your HESBA is effective again, and you’ve arrived at a relatively balanced mixed chiến lược. You might be tempted to go farther than this, but I’d recommend against it.

You don’t want to get stuck in a leveling game. You’re not trying to make the best decision on this particular shot, but rather trying to select the best chiến lược overall. You want to put your opponent in an impossible vị trí with your frequencies. If they try guessing chéo sân more, they’ll just start losing harder to your thẳng biên shots, and if they start guessing thẳng biên more, the chéo sân shots will be too punishing.

Like I mentioned earlier, there’s actually an equilibrium point at which it mathematically doesn’t matter how your opponent defends, your equity is always the same. That’s what we’re aiming at, and qualitatively, titrating up your second best shot which punishes their adjustment until your HESBA feels effective again is how you get there.

Balanced chiến lược – The Mathy Version For every probabilistic decision in a non-cooperative game, there exists a balanced (typically mixed) chiến lược which creates indifference in the opponent – it doesn’t matter how the opponent defends, their equity will be the same. As a probabilistic game itself, tennis is no different. We’re going to do a quick example where we derive a mathematically unexploitable thuận tay cú tiếp cận chiến lược.

Before we move on, understand that knowing how to actually calculate the game theoretical equilibrium for a particular situation is obviously not essential to playing correct tennis chiến lược – it’s not like we’re out there crunching numbers on the sân. However, the math is still instructive, if for no other reason than to both prove that there’s more than just gut feel behind correct chiến lược, and to examine the implications of the results.

Our Example – A thuận tay cú tiếp cận The attacker is hitting an cú tiếp cận. They will hit it either trong-ra ngoài or chéo sân. The defender is going to guess one way or another as the attacker swings. This attacker can hit both directions well, while the defender defends far better out of the thuận tay góc than the trái tay góc (and both players are right handed).

Before any strategic adjustment, the attacker’s highest equity shot is the cú tiếp cận into the defender’s weaker trái tay. As such, the attacker’s HESBA is the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay. Here’s how often the attacker wins the point, given the defender’s guess.

The attacker hits trong-ra ngoài, and wins: 75% of points when the defender guesses trong-ra ngoài 100% of points when the defender guesses chéo sân The attacker hits chéo sân, and wins: 100% of points when the defender guesses trong-ra ngoài 50% of points when the defender guesses chéo sân The payoff matrix for our two players in this cú tiếp cận situation; each box contains the attacker’s win percentage after the cú tiếp cận has been struck, and the defender’s guess made. A few things to note.

When the defender guesses wrong, he loses 100% of the points. (We’re not modelling in the few cú tiếp cận misses here). This means that phòng thủ strategies like “always guess chéo sân” are easily exploitable by always hitting the opposite way. Note the effect of the attacker’s HESBA. Even when the defender guesses correctly to the trái tay side, he still only has 25% equity in the point, while the attacker has 75%.

On his thuận tay side, where he defends well, a correct guess yields 50% equity, despite the phòng thủ situation. Before we move on, I’m going to spoil the solution for you so that you have a reference as we discuss various strategies for the attacker moving forward – the attacker can guarantee an 83.3% winrate if he uses an accurate chiến lược. The proof of that will come later.

A Simple, Exploitable chiến lược First, we’re going to analyze an obviously exploitable chiến lược for the attacker – 100% trong-ra ngoài thuận tay. This is the optimal chiến lược against an opponent who isn’t willing to guess – if it wins 75% against an opponent who guesses that way, it probably wins over 90% against an opponent who isn’t guessing – but against this defender, it’s leaving points on the table. The defender, to exploit this, will choose a 100% guess trái tay side chiến lược.

The result – Attacker 75%, Defender 25%. Not as good as the 83.3% I promised we could get. So how can we do better? Well, the defender’s “100% guess the trái tay side” chiến lược leaves the thuận tay side rộng open. When the defender guesses trái tay, and we hit to the thuận tay, we win 100% of the points. We’d không to utilize that fact, but not so much that the defender can start profitably guessing that way instead.

giao lại’s have the attacker try throwing in just a few chéo sân forehands and see if it improves their equity. Their new chiến lược: 80% trong-ra ngoài 20% chéo sân Now, if the defender employs his same “always guess trái tay side” chiến lược, the attacker wins 20%*(1) + 80%*(.75) = 80% of points. That’s 5% better than the 75% from before, but still 3.3% worse than the optimal 83.3%. The Nash Equilibrium We can do even better.

There exists a point in every non-cooperative game called a Nash Equilibrium – the point at which neither player can improve their performance by altering their chiến lược. This point is reached when both players are playing their respective unexploitable strategies. In tennis, our unexploitable chiến lược (as an attacker) is the mix of frequencies such that our opponent’s decision is indifferent – it doesn’t matter how they phòng thủ, they can’t improve their equity in the point.

They could always guess one way, always guess the other way, or mix the frequencies of their guesses however they wished, and their win percentage would be the same. (The principles for a defender making their attacker indifferent are the same, but we’re sticking with the attacker’s perspective for this article.) Our goal is to find this mix of frequencies.

At lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we routinely refer to a chiến lược using these frequencies as a “balanced chiến lược,” a “balanced mixed chiến lược,” or an “unexploitable chiến lược.” Note that a balanced chiến lược is not always optimal – if your opponent is playing a clearly exploitable chiến lược, using an exploitative chiến lược will yield better results than using a balanced chiến lược, but balanced strategies are far more stable, and better under áp lực, so we strongly prefer them.

Our Attacker’s Unexploitable chiến lược To solve for our attacker’s unexploitable mix of frequencies, we find the frequencies such that all phòng thủ strategies for our opponent produce identical results. Here are the possible phòng thủ positions, and how many points the attacker wins against each.

Defender Defends trong-ra ngoài Attacker wins: 75%*(frequency hitting trong-ra ngoài) + 100%*(frequency hitting chéo sân) Defender Defends chéo sân Attacker wins: 100%*(frequency hitting trong-ra ngoài) + 50%*(frequency hitting chéo sân) Now, we need to set these expressions equal to each other – we need to find the frequencies such that our defender is indifferent; whether he defends trong-ra ngoài or chéo sân, (or, by duỗi, any mix between the two), his win percentage will be the same.

giao lại’s call our frequency chéo sân “Fc” and our frequency trong-ra ngoài “Fi”. 75Fi + 100Fc = 100Fi + 50Fc 50Fc = 25Fi 2Fc = Fi And, since these are frequencies, they must sum to 100%. Fi + Fc = 1. Solving this system, we see Fi = 2/3 and Fc = 1/3.

Our unexploitable chiến lược is: 2/3 trong-ra ngoài 1/3 chéo sân The Attacker’s Unexploitable Win Percentage When the defender guesses trong-ra ngoài, the attacker wins: (2/3)*(75%) + (1/3)*(100%) = 5/6 = 83.3% of rallies When the defender guesses chéo sân, the attacker wins: (2/3)*(100%) + (1/3)*(50%) = 5/6 = 83.3% It’s the same! It doesn’t matter how the defender defends.

If the attacker uses 2/3 trong-ra ngoài and 1/3 chéo sân, they will always win 83.3% of the rallies regardless of the defender’s guess. In Defense of Balanced chiến lược The above analysis means that, as our attacker steps up in the sân and prepares for an cú tiếp cận thuận tay, he has 83.3% equity in the point. If the attacker adopts the balanced chiến lược above, that equity is a mathematical guarantee; there’s nothing the defender can do about it.

Notice that the attacker has a significant edge here – 83.3% is high, because the attacker is in a very good spot, and according to the numbers in our example, quite effective at converting ngắn balls. Trying to win to win more than 83.3%, by trying to exploit an apparent suboptimal phòng thủ chiến lược from the defender, constitutes a risk. It might work, perhaps the defender really is defending incorrectly, and the attacker can abuse that and win 90% of these points, but there’s no guarantee.

In general, exploitative strategies are better when you’re worse. If your guaranteed equity isn’t enough to win the trận đấu, you need to hope your opponent makes strategic mistakes, and then attempt to exploit those mistakes. Once your opponent catches onto your exploit, of course, they’ll exploit you back, and then you’ll have to adjust again. The game of chicken begins.

If you’re the worse player in the trận đấu – if your equity with a balanced chiến lược is simply too low to compete – you may not have a choice but to try and win it. On the contrary, if you’re the better, then simply play an accurate balanced chiến lược, and you can’t lose. Highest Equity Shot Before Adjustment For every situation from which we have to select a shot, there exists a best option barring strategic adjustment from our opponent. This is our highest equity shot before adjustment, our HESBA.

That might sound complicated, but in reality you probably already have a good idea, in various situations, of what this shot is. You can tell that your chéo sân thuận tay is great, but your thẳng biên thuận tay always skirts rộng. You know that your cắt trái tay almost always keeps the point neutral, while your xoáy trên trái tay is wild and unreliable. These shots constitute your highest equity options in a vacuum, before any strategic adjustment from your opponent.

And in a perfect world, your opponent would never adjust, and your could simply choose your HESBA 100% of the time. The HESBA Keeps Us Grounded Knowing our HESBA in each situation is essential to strategizing during the trận đấu; it’s the shot we want to use most, all else being equal, and our chiến lược exists for the purpose of pressing the lợi thế we gain from it.

Most opponents will adjust to our initial highest equity shot, and, after that adjustment, the shot will no longer be our highest equity. But remembering that it was our best option before adjustment was made, that it is still our best option in a vacuum, is critical to not out-thinking ourselves or over-analyzing our opponent. The knowledge of what we’d like to do, if our opponent wasn’t countering it, should never leave the front of our mind.

Remember, as our opponent adjusts their chiến lược, and as we adjust ours to compensate, our highest equity shot before adjustment doesn’t change. The highest equity shot at the moment may change, but the facts that were true at the beginning of the trận đấu – your trong-ra ngoài thuận tay is great and their trái tay is weak – those facts haven’t changed. The knowledge of what we’d like to do, if our opponent wasn’t countering it, should never leave the front of our mind.

As the trận đấu progresses, our opponent will adjust to counter our HESBA, and we’ll start mixing in some second best shots to compensate, but at some point during the ebb and flow of strategic adjustment, they may relent and allow us to hammer our best option again. Always keeping our HESBA in mind prevents us from wasting, or failing to notice, this opportunity. Instead, we’ll notice it, and we will immediately pounce.

A Common HESBA – The trong-ra ngoài Approach For many players, the highest equity shot before adjustment when given a ngắn, bóng cao up the giữa is an trong-ra ngoài thuận tay cú tiếp cận. Most players can hit their trong-ra ngoài thuận tay reasonably well, and, equally important, most opponents don’t phòng thủ well from the trái tay side. Thus, the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay cú tiếp cận (from a righty’s thuận tay to a righty’s trái tay) is the best option for the attacker, the attacker’s HESBA.

Before adjustment by the defender, on any given bóng ngắn, every other shot the attacker could attempt is worse than the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay. phòng thủ Adjustment The defender can typically increase their equity in the point by preventing the attacker from hitting 100% of their forehands to the trái tay side. To accomplish this, the defender must start to cheat over, often doing so right as the attacker swings.

Once this phòng thủ adjustment is made, the attacker’s current highest equity shot changes, but their highest equity shot before adjustment has not changed. This particular adjustment leads to the chéo sân thuận tay becoming more attractive for the attacker. It wasn’t attractive before adjustment, but now that the defender has adjusted, it is. Mixed chiến lược and the HESBA In order to counter frequent phòng thủ adjustment, the attacker should employ a mixed chiến lược.

The defender’s willingness to guess with their defense prevents the 100% trong-ra ngoài thuận tay chiến lược from being effective – if the attacker adopts this chiến lược, the defender will guess trái tay side every time, and much of the attacker’s lợi thế will evaporate. Therefore, the attacker needs to mix in some second best shots which are improved by the defender’s adjustment. This will prevent the attacker from being exploited by the defender’s guesses.

In this case, the best candidate is probably the chéo sân cú tiếp cận, (although the chéo sân cúp could work almost just as well). Even Mixed Strategies Overweight the HESBA In our thuận tay approach example, if the opponent is guessing roughly evenly between the two sides, a chiến lược like 60% trong-ra ngoài, 40% chéo sân would probably be appropriate.

We don’t want to trận đấu our opponent’s defense frequency, because doing so wouldn’t leverage our HESBA – we’d be sacrificing equity by not entering our preferred mẫu more than our second best mẫu. Consider two different attackers as an example.

Attacker 1’s trong-ra ngoài thuận tay is better Attacker 2’s chéo sân out thuận tay is better The defender is committed to guessing which way the ball will go as the attacker swings, so a 100% HESBA chiến lược will fail, since the defender will always end up guessing correctly. Each attacker will use a different mixed thuận tay cú tiếp cận chiến lược.

Both will mix their frequencies, lest the opponent guess correctly on every shot, but Attacker 1 will weight towards the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay, while attacker 2 will use more chéo sân. Two effective mixed strategies against a guessing defender, one for an attacker whose trong-ra ngoài thuận tay is superior (left) and another for an attacker whose chéo sân thuận tay is superior (right). Note the higher frequency and the more chủ động tấn công target chosen for each attacker’s superior shot.

chiến lược Flows From the HESBA When deciding how to mix your frequencies, your HESBA is your starting point. It’s the shot where your greatest lợi thế lies, and as such the one you’ll try to use most. Remember, the only reason Attacker 1 is hitting any of their approach shots chéo sân at all is because their opponent is cheating over to the phòng thủ trái tay side. They’d like for him to stand in the giữa so that they could hit every ball to the trái tay, the mẫu where they win most.

So even after their opponent adjusts, the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay is still going to be a good shot, just not as good as if their opponent hadn’t adjusted at all. The attacker is still going to use the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay, a lot in any mixed chiến lược, just not as much as they would if their opponent was still just waiting in the giữa. We want to feel a sort of lực pulling us towards using our HESBA throughout the trận đấu.

Our opponents will adjust to it, and we’ll mix in other shots to counter those adjustments, but by always remembering our HESBA, and knowing why we have it, we should be able to avoid over-strategizing ourselves out of an easy win. A Commonly Overused Mixed chiến lược One of the most common situations in which players adopt a suboptimal mixed chiến lược is đánh đôi trả bóng of giao bóng. In many matches, no, you don’t have to hit your giao bóng returns thẳng biên. Ever.

Stop wasting equity on that low percentage shot (until you’re forced to). A quick note before we move on – I’m not talking about the case when your opponent is so bad at volleying that they miss everything. If that’s the case, high percentage returns up the line are great. (Note, though, that in this case your are not trying to hit it past them; you’re just trying to give them a bắt lưới so that they miss).

At the 3.5 level, this isn’t actually that uncommon, so understand that the following discussion isn’t about that situation. I’m referencing trận đấu conditions with a volleyer who is competent – they will routinely put away easy volleys, and they’ll keep the rallies neutral on tougher volleys. Alright, back to not overdoing it with the thẳng biên trả bóng.

The thẳng biên trả bóng is Hard to Execute The thẳng biên trả bóng has THREE factors working against it, making it a difficult shot to execute. You are: Aiming to a small target Hitting over the highest part of the lưới Changing the direction of the ball Notice that a shot like the gold standard of high percentage shots – the chéo sân thuận tay out of the thuận tay góc – has none of these attributes.

On that shot, we’re aiming to a large target, over the lowest part of the lưới, and simply flipping the ball’s direction. The thẳng biên trả bóng is the opposite. Any one of these three aggravating factors on their own should give you pause when selecting a shot, especially with high frequency. All three together? That’s a recipe for a lot of misses. Only Mix In Second Best Shots When Forced To I get it – in đánh đôi, the chéo sân shot is far less effective than in đánh đơn.

The lưới player can poach the chéo sân shot and hit a điểm thắng of off it FAR more consistently than a player standing at the đường cuối sân ever could. But remember, just because it’s possible that your high percentage chéo sân trả bóng will get volleyed doesn’t mean that it will. This possibility alone is not enough that you should now be trying hit to a small target, over the highest part of the lưới, while changing the direction of the ball in the process.

Make the lưới player prove to you, by aggressively and consistently poaching, that returning chéo sân is not always your highest equity option. Don’t Deviate Too Quickly The optimal trả bóng chiến lược against an adequate lưới player who doesn’t poach. All returns should go cross sân. If the lưới player has hit 6 bắt lưới winners on your returns in the last few games, then I get it, you’re going to have to throw some returns up the line.

But if you’re going to start aiming to that low percentage target, you’d better make sure it’s worth it. I’ve seen players who hit thẳng biên on roughly 50% of their returns, and of those make only about 50% in (much less win the point on them), meanwhile the lưới player has successfully poached zero times in the entire trận đấu. Do not abandon the chéo sân trả bóng, your highest equity shot before adjustment, until proven otherwise.

If you hit a solid, sâu chéo sân trả bóng and it isn’t poached, do it again. And do it again, and again. Even after your opponent poaches once, do it again. Is that poach repeatable? Maybe on the next poach, the lưới player misses, because his volleys aren’t amazing (which is why he wasn’t poaching often in the first place). Before adding in the worse-in-a-vacuum thẳng biên trả bóng, ensure that doing so actually increases your overall win rate. In many cases, it won’t.

If it Ain’t Broke, Don’t Fix It Tennis is about winning points. If one mẫu is winning you 9 out of every 10 points, then it doesn’t matter if your opponent knows it’s coming. There is no reason to “keep’m honest” if you’re winning almost every time. Just keep repeating the same thing, and keep winning. Mixed strategies are necessary to prevent yourself from being exploited when your opponent is adjusting in real time.

Very often, though, they either won’t adjust, or they simply won’t have an effective adjustment to go to, even if they wanted to. In these cases, don’t waste equity by playing a mixed chiến lược, using second best shots at some frequency. Instead, just hammer your best option over, and over, and over again, and watch the points roll in.

The Optimal Frequency is Often 100% Many players assume that doing the same thing over and over again is inherently bad, because it’s “predictable.” This is a misconception. Our goal isn’t to be unpredictable, our goal is to win, and as such, doing the same thing over and over again is often precisely our best option. Make your opponent prove to you they can both predict and effectively counter your best option before you start throwing in change ups.

The only thing that prevents us from choosing our best option (our HESBA) at every decision point is our opponent’s willingness to adjust to that option. If our opponent is unwilling to adjust, then there is no reason for us to deviate either. Before adjustment, our best option is better than all of our other options. While that remains true, we should select it 100% of the time. Not 70% of the time, not 80% of the time, every time.

Once you’ve identified a certain shot as better than the rest, your opponent must change something for that to no longer be the case. Make your opponent prove to you they can both predict and effectively counter your best option before you start throwing in change ups. The only reason to deviate from your initial highest equity option is if your opponent has adjusted by enough that said option is no longer the highest equity. Your opponent is defending incorrectly.

Until they adjust and move to the green vị trí, there is no reason to attempt a thẳng biên thuận tay. An Example You have to play a waist height đánh qua lại ball from the thuận tay góc. Is it better to go chéo sân, or thẳng biên? That depends on the various skills of both you and your opponent, but, in a vacuum, there is an answer.

Imagine that, in this case, your skill level with both shots is roughly equal, but your opponent doesn’t know where to stand; he defends from the giữa of the sân, regardless of where you are hitting from. Your highest equity shot before adjustment (HESBA) is chéo sân. Until your opponent adjusts his phòng thủ vị trí, you should be hitting this ball chéo sân 100% of the time. For four games, your opponent doesn’t adjust.

In the fifth game, he starts cheating way over to the chéo sân side. All of the sudden, the line is so rộng open that thẳng biên forehands are now significantly higher equity than chéo sân forehands. Thus, we can now switch to 100% thẳng biên until our opponent adjusts again. Using a Mixed chiến lược Eventually, it won’t take our opponent four games to adjust.

Once our opponent is adjusting in real time, rather than only once every few games (and many quality opponents will do this from the first ball), a chiến lược of 100% thẳng biên or 100% chéo sân is no longer optimal. The second we adopt one of those strategies, our opponent will adjust his phòng thủ vị trí to exploit it.

Playing effectively against an opponent who makes strategic adjustments of his own necessitates that we replace our basic chiến lược with a mixed chiến lược, in which none of our frequencies are 100%. Most mixed strategies resemble something along the lines of “use our better shot most of the time, but the other shot just enough to keep him honest,” but the details of that are a topic for another article.

Mixing Frequencies is NOT Required In most competitive matches, both players will make strategic adjustments that are powerful enough to alter their opponent’s best option. However, this is FAR from a given. Sometimes your opponent won’t notice they’re being crushed by a certain mẫu, and thus won’t adjust to counter it. Other times, it won’t matter whether they notice or not – they’re simply not skilled enough to phòng thủ it, no matter what they do.

Before you start mixing in second best shots, make your opponent prove to you that they are both willing and able to counter your first choice with a strategic adjustment. Against many patterns, your opponent will do just that, but in a significant minority of situations, they won’t. You’ll have one crushing mẫu that you can enter unhindered throughout the entire trận đấu, and there’s nothing your opponent can do about it. When you find a mẫu like this, abuse it for all it’s worth.

Think Rafa vs Federer at Rolland Garros. For 10 years, Rafa did practically nothing but hit chéo sân forehands into Federer’s trái tay, and Rafa never adjusted his frequencies because Federer couldn’t stop it. Mixed strategies get a lot of không because they “aren’t predictable.” For whatever reason, recreational players don’t like doing the same thing over, and over, and over again, even if it’s working. But you should. To do otherwise is is to pointlessly sacrifice equity.

Certainty is a Feeling (and Cheat Code) Certainty as an epistemic state is a lie. A human being can never truly be certain about something, and most people have far higher confidence in their predictions than is warranted. That said, certainty is very useful for tennis. The feeling of being certain that a particular shot will work seems to increase the chance of that shot actually working.

Likewise, the feeling of being very uncertain that a particular shot will work – preparing for the shot while nervous and second guessing yourself – appears to significantly decrease the chance of that shot working. So what gives here? If certainty doesn’t actually exist, why does it seem so useful for tennis? The key here is to separate certainty the mental state from certainty the epistemic state.

One cannot actually be certain, but one can certainly feel certain: any human who predicts something with 100% probability is wrong, but a human who merely feels certain about something is simply manifesting a mental state that is very useful to human functioning. Teasing out the Feeling from the Matter of Fact In tennis, you want to feel certain that your shots will work, even though the fact of the matter is that they won’t always work. This applies at both a micro level and a macro level.

You want to feel certain that you won’t miss, even though you will. You want to feel certain you’ll win the next point, even though it’s likely a toss up, and you want to feel certain that you’ll win the trận đấu, even though you’ll probably lose about half the matches you play (and if you don’t, find better competition). To cultivate our feeling of certainty, we need to separate that feeling from our knowledge of the actual probabilities involved.

An Example – Neutral Forehands Image that you know, from your practice, that you can execute your sâu, chéo sân thuận tay at a frequency of 95/100. You’ve done many sets of 100 chéo sân forehands, and you always make between 90-100 of them, typically only missing 4 or 5. This fact is not actually useful in the moment, while trying to execute a chéo sân thuận tay. Therefore, instead of paying any attention to it, you want to feel certain that the shot is going to work.

In fact, if you feel uncertain, if you get nervous knowing that you’ll miss about 5% of the time, chances are that it won’t actually be 5% – that miss-rate will skyrocket. Is this just lying to yourself? Some people will feel like they’re “deluding themselves” if they attempt to feel certain about a shot, while simultaneously knowing, factually, that the shot is far from 100% accurate.

To these people, I would argue that the “fact of the matter” when it comes to certainty only appears to be relevant, but in reality it’s not. From a factual standpoint, there exists no accurate expression of certainty; the mental state itself is always in err. It never maps to an outcome that is 100% likely, because such outcomes don’t exist. It is merely a feeling which causes you to act a certain way. Certainty exists to delude you into performing better.

In fact, from an evolutionary perspective, certainty was almost definitely very useful precisely when the outcome was very uncertain. Consider the first sailors who colonized Australia, crossing the ocean in boats made of reeds. Don’t you think they had a “false” sense of the actual probability of completing that journey alive? Yet, because of their certainty (the mental state), they forged ahead, and today, Australia is a flourishing human colony.

Your feeling of certainty is an illusion, at its very lõi, and it always has been, from its very inception. Certainty exists to delude you into performing better. That’s why the feeling of certainty was selected for in the past, that’s why your brain manifests it today, and that’s why it’ll help your tennis tomorrow. Still worried about lying to yourself?

Both for tennis and in general, I’d encourage you to adopt a relatively Machiavellian worldview with respect to your brain: Treat your brain as a tool which you can use to accomplish your goals (like becoming better at tennis). In domains like science and engineering, the “truth” is very useful, but in the mental domain, it’s so difficult to even separate the truth from a convenient fiction your brain has erected that worrying about it is often more trouble than it’s worth.

Don’t limit your progress by trying to ensure your feelings are “true.” They’re just feelings. Instead, just try to manipulate your consciousness into the state that’s most useful to you. In our case, the feeling of certainty helps our shots succeed. We like to think that this feeling maps onto outcomes that are certain, and we know, for a fact, that our shot’s success is not certain, so that feels like a contradiction.

However, we ignore this contradiction, because the mental state of certainty is useful in spite of it. What if I just can’t feel certain? Maybe the fact that you miss a lot makes it impossible for you to feel certain on the sân. Try this mental re-framing exercise: Instead of being certain that your shot will work, be certain that your shot is the correct choice. Think of tennis like poker. If you’re at the bottom of your range with 2 blockers to the nuts, you’ve gotta bluff.

Of course, it might not work in this instance, but you can still be certain that it’s the correct play. It’s not certainty of outcome, but rather certainty of process. As you prepare to send your cú tiếp cận whistling into your opponent’s trái tay góc, yes, you have a miss-rate, and yes, that miss-rate is nonzero.

But given your practice, your chiến lược, and your execution to this point, you know this is the correct choice, even if it doesn’t happen to work on this particular shot. “He always knows he’s going to win.” A practice partner and I were recently discussing the mental side of tennis, and he mentioned a friend of his who has the best mental game he’s ever seen. The guy plays absolutely lights out on áp lực points – you can’t even tell the score is close.

How? “He just knows he’s gonna win.” But… but he’s not going to win, a lot of the time. That belief is false. How does he maintain it after losing a few times? Your feeling of certainty doesn’t need to be connected to reality. The truth is, your feeling of certainty is not a belief, it’s merely a feeling, and that feeling itself is useful, epistemology be damned. thuận tay POWER Checklist There are two kinds of “thuận tay power.” The first is effortless power.

Effortless power is the power we gain simply by using proper cơ sinh học. It’s gained while barely sacrificing any consistency or lỗi tolerance, and utilizing it only slightly raises the shot’s degree of difficulty. All players should strive to practice the cơ học that yield effortless power. The second is effortful power.

Effortful power is volitional – you’ve decided you’re in a situation where hitting really, really hard is appropriate, and so you’re going to try to hit really, really hard. The most obvious example of an appropriate situation for effortful power is a ngắn, high, floating thuận tay. I want to be clear that effortful power is a personal choice; even on a floater, not every player elects to crush it.

Some players hardly ever choose to hit really, really hard and play almost every shot with lots of margin and spin, while other players (Nick Kyrgios and Jack Sock, for example) treat almost every high, slow, thuận tay as if it should break the sound barrier. Almost every item on this list creates effortless power when applied, but can also be further focused on or exaggerated, to create additional effortful power. The decision as to whether or not to execute said exaggeration falls to you.

Before We Start… Everything that follows is subordinate to a proper điểm tiếp xúc. As you implement the tips below, it’s very easy to start messing up where you’re contacting the ball, relative to your body. In order for the modern vung vợt to work as intended, the ball must be contacted away from the body – both laterally away from the body and out in front of it. That’s the only điểm tiếp xúc that allows the vợt to naturally flick through the ball and strike it in a xoáy trên way.

If, in the course of implementing these checklist items, you start hitting the ball late, or start catching it too close to you, then any power gained from the implementing the change will be offset by the less efficient motion. Also, you’ll notice that nothing we talk about here has anything to do with the arm itself. Power does not come from swinging harder with the arm; only tension, misses, and injury do.

You can use your abs or your chest, you can đường bóng forward with your legs, you can rotate your hông – there are many efficient ways to generate power, but pulling or twisting harder with your arm isn’t one of them. Strong muscles create power, the arm muscles themselves merely stabilize and control. 1. đường bóng off of the back leg This is a proprioceptive cue – to increase power, you should actually think about driving hard off your back leg and feel the hitting-leg butt muscle contracting.

This đường bóng will both push your whole body forward and cause your hông to rotate back towards the target. Both of those consequences – the xoay hông and the forward đà – are essential for effortless power. To increase power, you should actually think about driving hard off the back leg, and feel the hitting-leg butt muscle contracting. Here’s an experiment you can do to teach yourself the di chuyển: set your feet in a tư thế bán mở. Turn to your upper body to your thuận tay side.

Shift your weight onto your back leg, bending it. Forcibly straigthen your back leg. This contraction will both straighten out your hông, and if you đường bóng hard enough, send your whole body forward. It is the primary muscle contraction that starts the rotational chuỗi động học. The degree to which you đường bóng of your back leg will vary from situation to situation.

On a particularly easy ball, you can elect to inject effortful power, by actively focusing on maximizing the strength of that hitting leg contraction: đang nạp it lower, and exploding off of it as hard as you can. Ideally, we’d like to do this on every ball, but we won’t always have the necessary time, or be in the correct vị trí to.

That said, unless you’re hitting on the dead run, still initiate the vung vợt with your back leg, even if it isn’t as foreful as it would be if you’d had longer to prepare. Doing so will always add effortless power. 2. Relax lực production necessitates tension; driving really really hard off the back leg, requires a really really tight contraction of the muscles in that leg – specifically the quad, glute, and hamstring, and calf.

Hold the vợt just tight enough to prevent it from flying out of your hand. The strange part about tennis, though, is that while some of our body parts forcefully contract to generate lực, other body parts need to stay completely relaxed to chuyển that lực into the vợt. The cổ tay and the hand are two of the body parts that stay relaxed. The more relaxed they are, the faster the vợt goes.

Hold the vợt just tight enough to prevent it from flying out of your hand, and use just enough cổ tay tension to maintain the string góc through contact. 2.5. The Arm, The Chest, and Relaxation The arm itself functions as a rope to chuyển the lực generated by your lõi into the vợt.

Three common volitional lực applications that will impede the vung vợt are: Trying to twist the vợt harder with your cẳng tay (Trying to point your butt cap at the lưới?) Volitionally supinating the arm Volitionally pronating through the shot These things will happen, but they will happen as a natural consequence of the way the arm was thrown into the shot. If they don’t, there’s an upstream chuỗi động học issue – it will very rarely be fixed by focusing on the arm itself.

Though the hand and cổ tay stay loose during the vung vợt, other parts of the arm will contract for stability. The biceps, for example, tighten up to prevent the khuỷu tay from hyper-extending, which is good. We don’t giao lại the arm trễ back, stretching the chest. The chest contracts to lực the arm out in front of the body. It’s important we do not allow arm trễ, the way we allow (and encourage) cổ tay trễ. As the body explodes around, the chest muscle forces the arm out away from the thân.

We don’t giao lại the arm trễ back, stretching the chest. You might wonder, why not? Wouldn’t we benefit from a stretch phản xạ of the pectoral muscles? Yes… kind of. In the end, tennis is still primarily a sport of control, not power. It’s amazing just how much power the modern vung vợt lets us generate while still maintaining control, but control is always our first goal. Using a stretch phản xạ at the chest is, generally, not worth it.

Occasionally, on the dead run, with no ability to recruit the legs or hông for power, if you want to go for it, go ahead. Guys like Medvedev and Lopez will sometimes do this, and rip 100 mph forehands from seemingly impossible positions. But only try this on defense. In a neutral or tấn công situation, lực your arm to travel around with your chest, and your contact will be far more consistent. 3. Breath in, then exhale through contact Always breathe in, relax, and then exhale as you vung vợt.

If possible, breath in through the nose, rather than the mouth. Nasal breathing oxygenates the body better than mouth breathing – you actually get more oxygen per ounce of air inhaled through the nose, than you do through the mouth. Nitric oxide, a chemical produced by the sinuses during nasal breathing, is essential for many health markers; everything from being an anti-inflammatory agent to reducing blood clotting.

Of course, if your nose is congested, or if you’re so out of breath that the nasal pathway simply isn’t rộng enough to get enough air, just inhale through the mouth and don’t worry about it. That said, during practice, and during any regularly strenuous đánh qua lại, begin forming the habit of nasal breathing, and your conditioning will thank you. As you vung vợt, exhale. The forceful contraction necessary to accelerate the vợt happens at the beginning of the vung vợt.

Exhaling through this contraction is good. First, it functions as a rhythm cue, helping you execute your cú đánh the same way every time. Second, forceful exhalation or grunting will increase your lực production, functioning similarly, but not identically, to the Valsalva maneuver (holding your breath through a contraction). As the vợt comes through the hitting zone, you should be nearing the end of your exhalation.

The forceful contractions necessary to generate lực are now mostly complete, and of critical importance now is to avoid impeding your relaxed vợt roi vung through contact with tension. 4. Turn your vai Roger Federer (left) turns his vai farther, relative to the hông, than Juan Martin Del Potro (right). Both still lead the vung vợt with their xoay hông, and both hit exceptionally hard. giao lại’s start with the effortless power version of the xoay vai.

A large amount of thuận tay power is generated when the player rotates their chest/vai forward towards the target as they vung vợt. This requires that their chest be turned away from the target during chuẩn bị, such that it can rotate back towards the target during the forward vung vợt. Ideally, you always want to turn your vai farther than you turn your hông.

This creates something called “hông vai góc separation,” and this separation encourages the hông to unlock first, and then the chest/vai to follow, creating a rotational roi vung through contact. The góc separation isn’t necessary for effortless power, though. As long as the hông unlock first, even if the vai and hông turn away by the same amount, the rotational chuỗi động học will still accelerate the vợt as expected. 4.5.

Turn your vai a lot Robin Soderling was famous for taking a massive xoay vai – turning his vai almost 90 degrees farther than his hông – and crushing forehands because of it. All players initiate the vung vợt with their hông, whipping them around before the vai, but not all decide to implement this by drastically turning their vai farther than their hông to begin with. Each player has a different preference for their personal thăng bằng between power and control.

Every player needs to experiment with their own chuẩn bị. That said, a huge xoay vai is very helpful in generating power – it’s just hard to control. Next time you have a high, slow, ngắn thuận tay, try turning your vai away more than usual. Just your vai, not your hông. You should feel the muscles in your lõi pre-stretch as you do this. It might take some practice to get the timing down, but unlock all that loaded power correctly, and you’ll absolutely crush the ball.

Just remember to relax, extend through contact, and watch the ball all the way in, otherwise you’re more likely to hit the back fence than a điểm thắng. 5. Pull your non-hitting khuỷu tay away Both xoay hông and chest xoay create power. The easiest way to ensure your chest whips around towards the target is to consciously yank your non-hitting arm away from your body, thereby initiating that chest xoay. Forget this part, and you may under-rotate, leaving significant power on the table.

Rafael Nadal prepares with his non-hitting khuỷu tay well on the hitting side of his body, then pulls it out to the right (he’s lefty) as he initiates his forward vung vợt, thereby whipping his hông and chest back towards the lưới and violently accelerating the vợt. 6. Don’t over-rotate the hông Optimal power is achieved when 100% of the lực generated by xoay hông is transferred into the vợt.

This is really hard to do (although doing it with less than perfect efficiency will still result in blistering forehands). Attempt to have your xoay hông stop right as they’re pointed at the target, or slightly past it. Rotational power is great, but a rotational roi vung is better. A roi vung isn’t just thrown hard at the target, it’s đà is violently halted mid-flight in order to chuyển all of its động năng into its tip.

Your rotational roi vung doesn’t have to be perfect – many tour players often hit winners with their hông over-rotated a bit – but for that maximum biomechanical power, stop the xoay at precisely the correct time, and all of the thân’s rotational năng lượng will transform into tốc độ đầu vợt through the ball. Andy Murray (left) hitting a 124mph điểm thắng trong-ra ngoài, and Fernando Verdasco (right) hitting a 103mph điểm thắng thẳng biên.

Each player’s hông are still pointed only slightly farther than their target sâu into their theo sau, indicating an efficient chuyển of xoay hông into vợt gia tốc. There are many players on the tour who routinely hit winners by throwing their hông through contact, and theo sau by bringing their back foot around. This is fine – if it suits your game, do it. However, if you want to slap 124mph forehands like Andy Murray, you’ll have to over-rotate less. 7.

Are you still relaxed? As you start focusing on other things, you’ll forget to relax. First you’re holding the vợt too tightly. Next you’re forgetting to exhale through contact. Now you’re over-rotating your hông, now you’re under-rotating your hông. Relaxation is the key that unlocks power, xoáy trên, and consistency in the thuận tay. No matter what else you’re thinking about, no matter what technical improvement you’re currently working on, breathe in, relax, then exhale as you vung vợt.

Dominate the Slicer With Heavy Low Forehands The following is a page from The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, available in eBook and paperback formats on Amazon (click here). At lỗi Tolerant Tennis we teach fundamentals. Our aim is not to provide assorted tips and tricks that apply only in certain cases, but rather to educate our readers about the essential biomechanical di chuyển patterns that underpin each shot.

We teach principles here – principles that lead to lỗi tolerant, effective strokes in every situation, whether the shot is being played high, low, rộng, or close. That said, the low thuận tay still deserves its own section, because it is such an important, frequent, and mistake prone shot. The Common Pitfall Against Slicers In a trận đấu against someone who only hits slices, you’ll be forced to play nearly all of your forehands from đầu gối height or lower.

Without an effective, lỗi tolerant low thuận tay, these matches are extremely frustrating. Dan Evans, the #1 ranked British player in the world, is famous for almost exclusively slicing his one handed trái tay. The cơ học that make the thuận tay lỗi tolerant, the cơ học we teach in this book, are very natural on waist height and vai height forehands.

That’s why, in a normal trận đấu against a non-slicer, your thuận tay feels fine; almost all of those forehands are played from waist height or from vai height, and as a result, you naturally apply lỗi tolerant cơ học. Everything we’ve discussed – from chuẩn bị, to body xoay, to vung vợt path – once you’ve practiced a little, using those cơ học will be your first instinct on a typical height thuận tay.

But on a low thuận tay, they’ll initially feel very unnatural; if you’re not consciously focused on using on them, you’ll probably accidentally abandon them. And that’s when you get a bunch of mishit low forehands, lost points, and a smashed vợt. Utilizing Waist Height cơ học To transpose our lỗi tolerant cơ học to a lower điểm tiếp xúc, we need to adjust our chuẩn bị appropriately.

We’ve already discussed adjustment, but it bears repeating – you adjust to a low thuận tay by sitting lower with your legs and tilting your torso, not by pulling your hand closer to your body. Adjusting this way allows you to maintain the same alignment of your vợt, hand, and arm relative to your thân that you use on a waist height thuận tay, thereby allowing you to rotate your hông and thân in the same manner as on a higher shot.

Roger Federer adjusts to a low thuận tay by bending his legs and tilting his torso, allowing him to rotate his body the same way he does on a waist height thuận tay. When preparing for the low điểm tiếp xúc, many players’ first instinct will be to pull their hand in, rather than tilt their torso.

This kind of hand adjustment is sometimes necessary on exceptionally low balls, but just be aware that the closer you pull your hand in, the more you inhibit the chuyển of năng lượng from the thân’s xoay into the vợt. Rotational gia tốc At a given angular vận tốc, translational vận tốc is always greatest farthest from the giữa of xoay (the bigger the circle, the faster the points on the circle are moving).

You can prove this to yourself at home – pinch your khuỷu tay into your side, bringing your vợt in close to your body, and twist forward like you would on a vung vợt. Now hold it away from you, and do the same. Can you hear all that extra wind through the vợt when it’s far? The more the vợt is allowed to flick away from the body, the more the thân’s xoay will accelerate it.

Preparing the Hand On any thuận tay, it’s nice when we can prepare our hand below the anticipated điểm tiếp xúc, because then it’s really easy to throw it up through the ball. However, this isn’t actually strictly necessary. On the low thuận tay specifically, the out vector of the vung vợt actually helps đường bóng your hand down, thereby making it possible for it to move up through the điểm tiếp xúc, even if it didn’t start below the điểm tiếp xúc in the first place.

This is a physical consequence of your tilted torso – as your khuỷu tay extends and your vợt flicks out (aka, away from your torso), that out góc is now diagonally down, rather than straight out. Rafael Nadal prepares for a đầu gối height thuận tay with his hand a small distance above the điểm tiếp xúc. As he rotates with a tilted chest, his khuỷu tay extends, naturally pushing the hand down, thereby allowing it to still move slightly upward through contact.

Since that khuỷu tay duỗi will đường bóng our hand down, even if we prepare our hand at the level of our contact, or even slightly above it, the cú đánh can still succeed. The up vector of this kind of vung vợt will be muted, since the hand will barely be below the ball at any point, but that doesn’t mean we can’t generate xoáy trên.

Windshield Wiper Spin is Enough Due to the vợt’s rotational roi vung (about the axis of the cẳng tay) through the hitting zone, the vợt đầu vợt itself will still be moving up extremely quickly through contact, even though the hand isn’t moving up through contact much at all. Thus, the strike will still generate significant xoáy trên.

Just the rotational roi vung of the vợt alone, the thụ động “windshield wiper” motion generated from rotating the thân and throwing the vợt out – just that vertical tốc độ đầu vợt will often provide enough xoáy trên to keep the ball in play, even absent a strong up vector in the vung vợt path. But How Low is Too Low? David Ferrer playing a thuận tay that is so low that a successful xoáy trên cú đánh is impossible; he plays the ball as a slight cắt instead.

Sometimes, though, the ball is so low that there’s simply not room for a xoáy trên vung vợt path; if you tried, either your vợt would hit the sân, or the ball would hit the bottom of your khung vợt and fly out. It’ll take practice to train your instinct for when this is the case, but make no mistake, there does exist a height below which a successful xoáy trên thuận tay is impossible. When you find yourself there, you’ll have to cắt or push the ball back.

All of our typical fundamentals – from the fundamental theorem of tennis, to adjusting using your prime movers first, to tracking the ball all the way into the dây – they apply just as much on a thuận tay cắt as they do on a xoáy trên cú đánh. Stay focused, stay relaxed, and catch the ball out in front of you.

Maintaining The vung vợt Path As you vung vợt at a low thuận tay, your mind is yelling at you that, “this ball is low, so you’ve gotta really pull it up.” While this thought is technically accurate, in practice, it tends to be a recipe for disaster.

Even when the điểm tiếp xúc is low, our conscious tập trung needs to be on the fundamentals which create lỗi tolerance in our cú đánh, not on “getting it up.” This tends to lead to three common mistakes: Forgetting to extend through the ball Forgetting to vung vợt out to the ball Opening or changing the string góc through contact 1.

Forgetting the Through Vector Rafael Nadal’s impressive duỗi away from the body during the theo sau after a đầu gối height thuận tay, indicating a strong through vector was utilized during the forward vung vợt Over tập trung on the up vector will often cause you to accidentally forgo the through part of the vung vợt.

As a result, you’ll spin the ball down into the lưới – the shot will have plenty of xoáy trên, but your purely up vung vợt path won’t supply the required forward đà to đường bóng the ball back over the lưới. Luckily, this mistake falls into the class of issues that’s fixed simply by thinking about it. Instead of allowing your mind to yell “Get it up! Get it up!” at you, tập trung on your forward duỗi.

Tell yourself, “Out, up and through,” and ensure that the “through” part of that cue is strong in your consciousness as you vung vợt. 2. Forgetting the Out Vector The mind’s natural over emphasis on up will also often make you forget the out vector, and forgetting the out vector also severely damages the cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance. As we’ve discussed, the out part of the vung vợt path is actually just as responsible for xoáy trên generation as the up vector.

By allowing the vợt to flick out to the ball, rather than just straight towards it, we impart forces on it which cause it to roi vung around the axis of the cẳng tay through the hitting zone, creating the “windshield wiper” motion. That motion results in a ton of vertical tốc độ đầu vợt through contact, and thereby xoáy trên, but without the out vector, it doesn’t happen. Fundamentally, the low thuận tay is the same vung vợt. We’ve said it before, but we’ll say it again – Out, up, and through.

It’s extremely easy to forget about both the through vector and the out vector when swinging at a low thuận tay. The out, up, and through vung vợt path, which causes the vợt to roi vung around the axis of the cẳng tay through contact, is responsible for at least half of the cú đánh’s xoáy trên. Abandoning it for a purely up and through vung vợt path is quite counter-productive, especially on a low thuận tay, a shot for which each additional RPM grants significant extra lỗi tolerance.

It’s extremely easy to forget about both the through vector and the out vector when swinging at a low thuận tay, because your mind is so strongly focused on up, but lose those other vectors, and you lose the cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance with them. 3. Subconsciously Turning the dây Up Through contact on a đầu gối height thuận tay, Rafael Nadal’s dây are parallel to the lưới.

The mind’s natural over emphasis on “getting the ball up” will also tempt you to alter your string góc, angling the dây towards the sky. Unless you vung vợt really, really slowly, this string góc will cause the ball to sail out. I know it’s counter-intuitive, but even on a low thuận tay, the dây, through the hitting zone, should be either parallel to the lưới, or angled towards the sân.
