At contact, there exists a ton of frictional lực between the vợt and the ball while the vợt is flicking up and through it; that friction will đường bóng the ball up. You’ve just gotta trust it. If the string góc is altered such that the dây are pointing up at contact, then a rotational vung vợt utilizing a relaxed lagging and snapping cổ tay simply won’t work – the ball will fly off the vợt at an góc such that it always goes long.

No “Rolling the vợt” If you try to fix your open string góc problem by rolling your vợt over the ball through contact, that will completely destroy the cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance; unless you time the hit perfectly, you’re going to miss. This kind of vợt xoay (as opposed to xoay about the axis of the cẳng tay) is the quintessential lỗi tolerance killer – hit the ball just slightly too early, and it’s going long, but hit it slightly too late, and it’ll hit the lưới.

Maintain your consistent string góc through contact, just like on any other thuận tay. Trust the friction, and trust your natural xoáy trên, the result of your relaxed cổ tay and your out, up, and through vung vợt path. You Can’t Beat a Slicer Without a Low thuận tay To the uninitiated, the low thuận tay seems like a completely unique shot – a different cú đánh to practice, separate and distinct from the waist height forward. This is not the case.

The biomechanical techniques that make the thuận tay so lỗi tolerant are very easy to accidentally abandon on the low thuận tay. What is the case, though, is that the biomechanical techniques that make the thuận tay so efficient and lỗi tolerant are very easy to accidentally abandon on the low thuận tay, because on the low thuận tay they’re initially quite counter intuitive. And that’s why the typical player’s game falls apart against slicers.

In the normal trận đấu, they’re using a lỗi tolerant cú đánh – they often slightly mistime or mishit the ball, but they probably don’t even notice, and it doesn’t matter, because the resulting shots are still effective. But against Mr. cắt, they’ve accidentally abandoned that lỗi tolerant cú đánh, and now, unless they strike those low forehands perfectly, they miss them.

Effective Low Forehands Dominate Slicers To beat the slicer, you have to keep the points neutral, even when forced to play bóng thấp after bóng thấp. To beat a particularly good slicer, you’ll also have to hit passing shots on some of those low balls. With lỗi tolerant cơ học, this is easily doable. The slicer can no longer win just by letting you miss out of neutral.

Though it might not come naturally at first, those cơ học that we utilize on waist height forehands can be used on the low thuận tay as well, and they will give you that same lỗi tolerance lợi thế – not every strike will have to be perfect; they’ll still go in. Practice implementing these cơ học on low forehands during your training, and you’ll be able to hit 10, 20, or 50 low forehands in a row without missing.

It’ll take some conditioning – the leg musculature required to get that low, that often is certainly non-trivial, but hey, that just takes more training. After that, it’s just a regular trận đấu – once you stop missing đánh qua lại low balls, you lực the slicer to try to find tấn công opportunities the same way you yourself are trying to. The slicer can no longer win just by letting you miss out of neutral. And it’ll be a lot more fun, certainly more fun than mishitting every ball and not knowing why.

Thou Shalt Not đưa vợt ra sau When I say that the bắt lưới should utilize “no đưa vợt ra sau,” I mean no đưa vợt ra sau. Not a quick đưa vợt ra sau, not an abbreviated đưa vợt ra sau. No đưa vợt ra sau. The vợt travels directly from its vị trí in the ready vị trí to the player’s anticipated bắt lưới điểm tiếp xúc. This makes the bắt lưới distinctly different from the cú đánh nền – it’s a unique shot that can barely even be called a “cú đánh” in the first place.

The bắt lưới di chuyển, on a technical level, is really better described as placing the vợt behind the ball, and letting the ball bounce off of it, rather than as any sort of “vung vợt.” To understand why we completely eliminate the đưa vợt ra sau when volleying, it’s important to first understand why we ever elect to use a đưa vợt ra sau at all, and what factors are unique about lưới play that cause us to abandon it.

Why We đưa vợt ra sau from the đường cuối sân During Jen Brady‘s theo sau, her vợt góc is completely closed – were she to accidentally strike the ball like this, it would travel straight down into the lưới. The đưa vợt ra sau on our groundstrokes is worth the time it takes, and it’s worth the inconsistency it adds. giao lại’s look at our thuận tay.

Technically, our thuận tay đưa vợt ra sau is sacrificing some lỗi tolerance – we would miss less if we held the vợt out in front of us, turned only slightly away from our target, and then turned back without allowing our cổ tay to trễ and bật at all. If we did that, though, the resulting shot wouldn’t be very effective. So we sacrifice just a bit of our cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance in order to incorporate the rotational chuỗi động học. This makes the vung vợt take longer, and it makes it harder to time.

We also relax our cổ tay and allow the vợt to roi vung around that relaxed cổ tay through the hitting zone. This dramatically increases our tốc độ đầu vợt, but also causes the vợt to turn over during the theo sau, and it decreases the amount of time and space during which clean contact can be made. If we’re too late, we’ll bury the ball into the lưới, and if we don’t aim the vợt properly, we’ll hit the ball off the khung vợt.

Luckily for us, when executed properly, the thuận tay cú đánh is still extremely lỗi tolerant, despite the small disadvantages that the đưa vợt ra sau adds. By rotating the hông, maintaining the string góc through contact, and mastering the various other technical skills we teach here at lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we need only sacrifice a small amount of our margin for error to generate a heavy, spinning, đường bóng shot instead a spinless, phẳng, floating ball for our opponent to cán vợt.

đưa vợt ra sau is Always a Cost/Benefit Analysis Daniil Medvedev’s unique đưa vợt ra sau doesn’t ruin his cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance – his vợt stays on the hitting side of his body, and he still keeps his cổ tay extremely relaxed as he rotates around.

I want to take a brief detour here and mention that the proper way to evaluate any particular student’s unique or funky đưa vợt ra sau is precisely with this idea – is the increase they are getting in comfort, power, or spin worth what they are paying in consistency and timing? For most extraneous di chuyển back there, the answer is a resounding “no;” almost every player should use a very abbreviated đưa vợt ra sau with very little arm di chuyển at all.

But when it comes to the xoay đơn vị – the name for the di chuyển during which the player turns their body away from the target, so that they can explosively rotate back towards it during the forward vung vợt – that “đưa vợt ra sau” is well worth its cost. At the đường cuối sân, This Cost is Minor Make no mistake, even when utilizing an anatomically perfect đưa vợt ra sau, we still do pay a cost for that extended thuận tay chuẩn bị.

It takes time, and it adds a ton of difficult to control (relatively speaking) explosion to the vung vợt. It requires that we prepare early, and that we execute the cú đánh during a small timing window in order strike the ball correctly.

At the đường cuối sân, we have enough time and enough visual information that these restrictions aren’t deal breakers – we cash in that extra time and information by turning our hông away from the ball and pushing our vợt back, so that we can then explosively rotate and fling the vợt around our body during the forward vung vợt. Why We do Not đưa vợt ra sau at lưới At lưới, the situation is drastically different. We have no time.

By the time we’ve recognized where the ball will arrive, any “vung vợt” we’ve elected to take needs to be practically complete already. This also means that our brain is provided far less visual information to process before it has to commit our body to a di chuyển. To succeed under conditions like these, we need to build into our strokes every possible modicum of lỗi tolerance we can find.

Volleys Often lỗi lưới play happens so quickly that volleys will almost always be executed “imperfectly,” in the lỗi tolerance sense. Small faults – an imperfect điểm tiếp xúc, an imperfect string góc, or a slight mistiming of the ball – are inevitable with so little time to prepare and strike. If volleys didn’t work when timing or execution was imperfect, they’d never work.

We could still attempt a đưa vợt ra sau under these conditions, and if we did, the volleys we managed to execute perfectly would still be quite effective, but since that attempt isn’t lỗi tolerant, it won’t succeed frequently enough to be worth it; the conditions at lưới are just too challenging. Even though there might technically exist enough time to execute a back-then-forward vung vợt at lưới, in practice, that di chuyển is too complex for the quick conditions.

It simply can’t be correctly executed consistently enough to be profitable, given the lưới’s pressures. lưới Play Necessitates lỗi Tolerance The lưới isn’t like the đường cuối sân – we don’t get to move to the ball, relax, take a sâu breath, track the ball over the entire length of the sân, and contact it right where we want it. Quite the opposite, in fact.

We see our opponent strike the ball, and, in the next instant, our brain has to recognize its path, project the điểm tiếp xúc, and then command our body to maneuver the vợt behind that point. We lunge for it, stick our vợt out, and attempt to redirect the ball’s pace to a new target. Under these circumstances, if volleys didn’t work when timing or execution was imperfect, they’d never work.

Move the vợt Directly to Contact The vợt should travel directly from its vị trí in the ready vị trí to the player’s anticipated bắt lưới điểm tiếp xúc. To succeed under the lỗi prone conditions at lưới, eliminate the đưa vợt ra sau entirely. The vợt should travel directly from its vị trí in the ready vị trí to the player’s anticipated bắt lưới điểm tiếp xúc, without going backwards at all.

This direct-to-contact vợt path maximizes our lỗi tolerance – it minimizes the vung vợt time while simultaneously maximizing the part of the vung vợt during which correct contact can be made. The result is that our bắt lưới will very often succeed in the face of small imperfections in tracking, vợt placement, or cú đánh timing.

Turning Sideways and đưa vợt ra sau When you stand still and turn sideways, your vợt does, in an absolute sense, move backwards, which could technically constitute a “đưa vợt ra sau.” It doesn’t move backwards relative to the torso, but it does move backwards in an objective sense. If a ball is rifled at you, it’s okay for this backwards vợt motion to occur.

During Roger Federer’s trái tay bắt lưới chuẩn bị, the vợt has moved “backwards” relative to his feet, but it’s still in the same vị trí relative to his chest as it started in. (Photo credit) But on any normal bắt lưới, this backwards motion should be offset by the fact that your entire body, as a unit, is moving diagonally forward towards the điểm tiếp xúc as you prepare.

Therefore, despite the vợt moving backwards relative to, say, your front foot, it never moves backwards relative to the sân. This is ideal, as a stable, forward moving vợt is a vợt that will strike a crisp bắt lưới. All that said, turning sideways on the bắt lưới is beneficial, as it makes the bắt lưới easier to execute.

Note that, on the bắt lưới, we’re actually striking the ball while sideways – we’re not turning away from the ball so that we can rotate back towards it later on in the vung vợt, like we are on the thuận tay. How to Turn on the bắt lưới Roger Federer executing a low bắt lưới from a tư thế đóng. His hông and chest are turned 45 degrees away from the lưới, and he contacts the ball well out in front of him.

On the thuận tay bắt lưới, turn your thân away from the lưới by about 45 degrees, and on the trái tay bắt lưới, turn away by at least 90 degrees (you can even face your own đường cuối sân if you want). On both volleys, it’s best (again, time permitting) to hit out of either a tư thế trung tính, or a tư thế đóng. These movements put your body in a better anatomical vị trí to easily make lỗi tolerant, fundamental theorem of tennis adhering contact out in front of you.

đưa vợt ra sau of the arm is the biggest bắt lưới killer, rather than any accidental đưa vợt ra sau from turning the torso. Just keep in mind that you’re only turning the torso on the bắt lưới in order to get in a better anatomical vị trí to make stable contact out in front of you. You are not turning in order to rotate back towards the lưới and generate power. Use the turn for stability, and you’ll be fine. It’ll Feel Strange, At First Initially, the bắt lưới “vung vợt” will feel very counter-intuitive.

It feels extremely natural turn our vai and our hông sideways, and then to bring the vợt back with our arm. Our intuition is yelling at us that, if we want to hit the ball forward, we first need to bring everything back so that we can do so. This intuition is wrong. The “vung vợt” on the bắt lưới is really no vung vợt at all; it’s just a repositioning of the vợt.

Further, when we watch the players on the tour bắt lưới on TV, it is very difficult to tell what constitutes a forward vung vợt and what constitutes a đưa vợt ra sau. That’s actually true on the thuận tay as well, a cú đánh on which many players take an excessively arm-y đưa vợt ra sau because that’s what it looks like the tour level players are doing, even though they aren’t. So don’t be disillusioned if the abolition of the đưa vợt ra sau feels awkward at first.

It should feel strange – the “vung vợt” on the bắt lưới is really no vung vợt at all; it’s just repositioning of the vợt. You’ll quickly notice, though, how solid the occasional, correctly executed, zero đưa vợt ra sau bắt lưới feels, and you’ll never want to go back. Eventually, your brain’s reward system will start associating that firm, crisp bắt lưới feeling with the complete abolition of the đưa vợt ra sau, and you won’t be tempted to do it anymore. Eventually, but not right away.

Training The Zero đưa vợt ra sau bắt lưới The most effective way to train yourself to abolish your bắt lưới đưa vợt ra sau is to practice volleying with another player who is also at lưới – bắt lưới-to-bắt lưới. When hitting bắt lưới-to-bắt lưới, đưa vợt ra sau will be impossible, because the ball will come at you too fast. This will lực you practice taking your vợt directly from your ready vị trí out to a điểm tiếp xúc in front of you, lest you shank and mishit every ball.

Rafael Nadal drives a bắt lưới at Jack Sock’s feet at the Laver Cup, 2017. Sock, an elite đánh đôi player and an elite volleyer, successfully hits a quick, phản xạ bắt lưới, catching the ball low and in front of his feet, and Team World ends up winning the point. Keep your feet, torso, and eyes engaged. When you have time, turn sideways as you move the vợt forward to the điểm tiếp xúc, and the shot will feel more comfortable.

When you don’t have time – when the ball is hit exceptionally quickly, or when it’s hit right at you – just ensure that the vợt is at a 90 degree góc with your cẳng tay, and send it straight out towards the ball. Forward, forward, forward. You’ll be surprised how many balls will simply hit it and bounce back over. Develop the Feel of Federer Many people assume that “touch” and “feel” are simply natural talents. They are not – like everything else in tennis, touch and feel can be trained.

While there certainly are players who, with practically no coaching, will quickly develop an impressive feel for the game, that doesn’t mean that feel is unattainable for the rest of us. Quite the opposite, in fact. Precisely because players like Roger Federer are such naturals, we mortals are able to learn which biomechanical alignments are useful for developing feel, and which ones aren’t by studying them.

The cơ học of Feel This video (timestamped at 2:22) is the best, most informative practice video I’ve ever seen when it comes to studying feel. It showcases the most important concept that creates feel in every situation – The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis. From The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay section – Two Rules that Govern EVERY Shot The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis is two rules that govern every shot in the game. They are as follows: 1.

The vợt makes a 90-135 degree góc with the cẳng tay 2. The vợt strikes the ball in front of the body (or, in a pinch, in front of the cẳng tay) Any shot which follows these two rules will work. No matter what shot Roger is hitting, he always catches the ball in front of him, and there is always an góc between his vợt and his cẳng tay. He adjusts his entire arm when he has to adjust – he does not adjust by straightening out his cổ tay.

Roger Federer playing a low trái tay cắt (left), a regular trái tay cắt (giữa), and a trái tay block (right). In each of the three different situations, he contacts the ball out in front of his foot, and he maintains an góc between his vợt and his cẳng tay. That’s really all there is to feel.

Whether you’re playing a thuận tay cắt, trái tay cắt, thuận tay block, trái tay block, cúp, or even a regular thuận tay or trái tay, gaining a feel for the ball is as simple as gaining an understanding of how to adjust your vợt in space without straightening out your cẳng tay-vợt góc and without catching the ball late. Practicing Feel Feel is a skill that you can practice and master, just like the thuận tay or trái tay.

Mini-tennis is a great drill for it – you and your partner stand on opposite service lines and lightly hit the ball back and forth across the lưới. Relax your hand, but use just enough tension to maintain your 90-135 degree góc. Roger Federer casually playing a ngắn, low thuận tay (left) and a thuận tay which has gotten to close to him (right). In each case, he takes the ball well in front of his body, and maintains an góc between his vợt and his cẳng tay.

tập trung on hitting the ball in front and tập trung on adjusting your entire arm–vợt system to the ball, rather than just your vợt. Play around with taking the ball earlier than feels natural – the correct điểm tiếp xúc on a touch shot will often initially feel quite far in front of you. None of the power of your shot comes from your cẳng tay – your cẳng tay merely stabilizes the vợt during the cú đánh.

Use can use a slight body xoay, or you can use your chest to gently pull your entire vợt-arm system forward, but do not use the muscles of your hand and cổ tay to vung vợt – that causes misses and injury. If you prefer a competitive setting, đánh đôi tennis is the way to go for feel training. In đánh đôi, you’ll see all sorts of low shots, ngắn shots, and shots you need to play quickly out of vị trí that necessitate great feel to execute.

Keep your vợt in front, and keep your cẳng tay-vợt góc set. I guarantee you’ll slowly develop that coveted feel that makes tennis so fun to play. Training to Crush The Pusher In order to beat a pusher, you need high percentage tấn công shots. Most players at the recreational level do not posses high percentage tấn công shots, and yet they attempt tấn công tennis anyway. This leads to the frustration death spiral.

It feels like you’re in a winning vị trí – you’ve got a ngắn, bóng cao over the giữa; all you have to do is hit one hard thuận tay, and maybe a bắt lưới or two, and the point is yours. But you haven’t actually developed your phẳng, hard, thuận tay, and you haven’t actually practiced those one or two volleys you’ll have to hit. In your current state, this really isn’t a winning vị trí, even though it feels like one.

Until you train your tấn công game to the point where you routinely convert these shots at a 90+% clip, you’re going to lose from tấn công positions all the time. You miss, you feel like you shouldn’t have, you get frustrated, miss some more, and then get more frustrated. And the pusher wins 6-2 6-1, even though you were the “better player.” Fixing Your chiến lược To beat a pusher, you need to keep the ball in play.

Pushing encourages players to attempt shots that they can’t actually convert with a high enough frequency to be profitable – we need to stop that. We’ve already discussed this concept at length, but here’s the upshot. To start, select shots that you know you can make with 80% probability or better. That means that for every 4 times you make the shot, you miss it 1 time.

That’s a good ratio to start with – it’ll prevent you from missing all the time and heading down the frustration death spiral to a quick defeat. An intuitive target (left) that is actually far too chủ động tấn công for the depicted player’s skill level; too much of the shot’s outcome distribution is out. Using a more conservative target (right) will lead to far better outcomes on this cú tiếp cận.

You might have to adjust in real time – if you’ve just missed 3 of the same shot in a row, move the target in and aim higher over the lưới. If you’ve made 15 in a row, you can feel free to be a little more chủ động tấn công. You Have to Practice Your Offense If you’ve never actually practiced your offense, playing against a pusher is always going to be tough.

The trận đấu is going to be tighter than you feel like it should be, and you’re going to have to play a lot safer to win than you feel like you should have to play. But here’s the thing – what you’re seeing on sân is the truth. Until you spend the time on the practice sân to actually train your tấn công tennis, you should expect that your tấn công tennis will be poor when playing a trận đấu. So, in that vein, here’s the best drill for training it.

Offense-Defense My favorite drill for practicing tấn công tennis is one I call “offense-defense.” It’s useful for the defender, too, but it’s less realistic from their perspective. Where the drill really shines is training your offense. Here’s how it works: There’s one tấn công player and one phòng thủ player. Both players start at the giữa of the đường cuối sân. This is important – to maintain the realism of the drill, neither player should move from the giữa until the ball is fed.

The phòng thủ player feeds the tấn công player a high, bóng ngắn to either side. Then, the two players play out the point chéo sân. Any shot that doesn’t land chéo sân (as indicated by extending the giữa-đường giao bóng to the đường cuối sân) is out. The đánh đôi alleys are out. That’s it. A graphical depiction of offense-defense – green is in, everything else is out. Player’s start in the giữa, then move once hte ball is fed.

The drill can be done on the đều side (right) or on the ad side (left). In this depiction, the tấn công player is approaching with a thuận tay on both sides. How it Works in Practice The player who receives the high, bóng ngắn will hit a chéo sân cú tiếp cận and come to lưới. The phòng thủ player can try to hit a cúp chuyền (tough because there’s so little sân), they can try to cú lốp, they can try to keep the ball low – whatever they feel is appropriate in this situation.

In Offense-Defense, both players are trying to win. That’s why the skills you train while drilling it carry over into matches. Here’s the beautiful thing, though, why the drill is so helpful – both players are doing everything they can to win the point.

The rules are set up such that the rallies will be longer than typical offense defense rallies, leading to more practice than would be possible under true trận đấu conditions, and yet the shots actually making up those longer rallies are still quite realistic. This falls in stark contrast to many other drills, during which one player is not playing to win. For example, as a coach, when I feed my student volleys, I’m not trying to hit it past them.

When I hit it hard at their feet so they can practice digging the ball, I’m not trying to get them to miss. While this kind of practice definitely has its place, it doesn’t translate to matches nearly as well as the practice from a drill like offense-defense does. In Offense-Defense, both players are trying to win. That’s why the skills you train while drilling it carry over into matches. Why not play full sân?

We play half sân such than an Offense-Defense đánh qua lại can continue even when both players are genuinely trying to win the point. If the drill were full sân, many of the points would just end with an cú tiếp cận điểm thắng – each player would hit far less balls/hour. We’re essentially playing every đánh qua lại from after the time when the defender has guessed correctly where the tấn công shot is going. Playing half sân beautifully solves this problem.

We’re essentially playing every đánh qua lại from after the time when the defender has guessed correctly where the tấn công shot is going. That’s perfect, because, in a trận đấu, those are the rallies that continue anyway. The tấn công player won’t be able to blow the ball by the defender easily in half sân, and will typically have to play multiple volleys in a row to win the point.

This is also extremely good, because the discipline to construct a point using high percentage tấn công shots is essential for quality lưới play. Skills That Offense-Defense Trains Here’s a non-exhaustive list of the amazing benefits of Offense-Defense.

On the tấn công side: Hitting chéo sân approach shots Moving through an chủ động tấn công shot to lưới Split stepping at the appropriate time while coming in Hitting an approach bắt lưới from the đường giao bóng Choosing when to drop bắt lưới and when to đường bóng bắt lưới Constructing a point with high percentage volleys Overheads cú lốp retrieval And on the phòng thủ side: Digging out a hard ball hit at your feet Keeping the ball low Reading the lưới player’s vợt and body to anticipate the shot phòng thủ touch shots Immediate transition from defense to offense on a weak bắt lưới Lobbing Adjustments and Suggestions Depending on your level, playing with the đánh đôi alleys in may be appropriate.

The goal of removing the alleys is to lực the players to learn to aim their shots, and to lực the rallies to continue. That said, the goal isn’t for both players to just miss and miss and miss. If you can’t consistently hit the half sân target without the khu vực đôi’s extra width, add it in to keep the points going. You’d never hit a cúp while grinding vợt fed forehands, but it makes perfect sense to hit them in offense-defense.

This drill works great on both the thuận tay and trái tay side. On the trái tay side, I recommend alternating feeds between feeds that are higher and closer to the giữa of the sân, on which the tấn công player should hit an trong-ra ngoài thuận tay, and feeds that are lower and wider, on which the player should approach with the trái tay. Mix it up – throw in the occasional cúp approach just like you would in a trận đấu.

Don’t treat this as a “drill” in the sense that shots like drop shots, or slices, or trick shots are inappropriate. You’d never hit a cúp while grinding vợt fed forehands, but it makes perfect sense to hit them in offense-defense, especially if your opponent is cheating way back in the sân. Because your goal is to win the points. That’s what makes the practice translate. That’s what makes the drill work.

Offense-Defense Actually Translates I prefer not keeping score during Offense-Defense so that players don’t feel pressured into avoiding the very tấn công shots they need to practice. If you can only hit, say, your phẳng approach thuận tay at 50% right now, this is the time to get it to 80%, not to abandon it because it’s too inconsistent. That said, decide what you want to work on – the shots you’re going to use that are not trận đấu ready – and then genuinely compete from there.

Do that, and the practice will translate to matches in a way that so much practice doesn’t. As you run up for a bóng ngắn in a trận đấu, it’ll finally feel familiar – you’ll finally feel confident, like you’ve done this 100 times before, because, after drilling with offense-defense, you will have done it 100 times before.

As you bước chân phân tách for your bắt lưới, as you move in and strike the bắt lưới, and then as you shuffle back, line up the bóng trên cao, and blast the final điểm thắng past your opponent – each of those shots will have been practiced. The #1 thuận tay Consistency Killer I can’t trust my thuận tay. I’ve heard the above sentence more than any other when it comes to fixing the thuận tay. It’s an extremely common problem that I struggled with myself as a teenager for many years.

The fundamental problem, though, isn’t the lack of confidence itself, not directly. The problem is a mechanically flawed, non-lỗi tolerant cú đánh that shouldn’t be inspiring confidence. Trust = lỗi Tolerance The typical player’s thuận tay cú đánh will fail unless executed perfectly; if said player executes their thuận tay even slightly wrong (for example, by slightly mistiming the ball or by slightly mishitting it), they are going to miss.

Under those circumstances, of course the player doesn’t trust their cú đánh; it’s untrustworthy. Your brain is smart – it’s lack of trust in your thuận tay, the reason it sends the “don’t be confident in this” signal as you go to hit, is that it should not trust a lỗi sensitive cú đánh to succeed consistently, especially under any sort of áp lực. On the other hand, a lỗi tolerant cú đánh is extremely trustworthy.

Since the cú đánh will succeed in the face of a small lỗi, you can trust it even in cases where small faults are unavoidable, like when you’re tired or nervous. It is this property – succeeding when things aren’t perfect – that inspires your brain’s confidence. Therefore, we improve your thuận tay confidence by improving the cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance. We’ll start by fixing the #1 lỗi tolerance destroyer on the thuận tay – changing your string góc through contact.

Some Quick Terminology A graphical depiction of Novak Djokovic playing his thuận tay with a slightly closed string góc. When I say “string góc,” I am referring to the góc between the dây and the ground. We refer to certain string angles as “open,” “neutral,” or “closed” depending on orientation.

Open string góc – dây angled up towards the sky Neutral string góc – dây parallel to the lưới Closed string góc – dây angled down towards the sân The modern thuận tay is hit with a slightly closed string góc, which is adjusted slightly depending on điểm tiếp xúc and desired ball flight path. Maintaining the String góc The string góc should barely change through the hitting zone.

The longer you maintain your string góc through the hitting zone, the more lỗi tolerant your vung vợt will be. This is a simple case of collision physics. If a ball strikes an open vợt face, it will deflect up more, and if it strikes a closed vợt face, it will deflect down more. There is a certain range of string angles such that the ball will fly over the lưới and come down before landing out.

In order for your thuận tay to work, you need to contact the ball while the dây are at an góc in this range. The góc between Novak Djokovic’s mặt dây and the ground remains remarkably consistent throughout his entire hitting zone, leading to a very large timing window for striking the ball and producing a quality result. The longer you can maintain a correct góc through the hitting zone, the less precise you have to be with your vung vợt timing.

Look at Novak Djokovic’s thuận tay above – if he were to contact the ball in any of khung vợt 2, 3, 4, or even 5, his shot would go in. His string góc is roughly the same for his entire hitting zone. This is just one of the many things he does that allows him to practically never miss. What String góc to Choose? Rafael Nadal playing a đầu gối height thuận tay with an approximately neutral string góc.

The lower the ball is, the more open your dây should be, and the higher the ball is, the more closed your dây should be. Again, this is simple collision physics – you want low balls to fly higher off your vợt and high balls to fly lower off your vợt. The change in string góc between shots is very minor. On low balls, for example, xoáy trên shots are played with a neutral or even slightly closed string góc, not an open string góc.

There is a ton of frictional lực pulling the ball up through contact; it’s not necessary to actually deflect the ball up at all, even on a bóng thấp. Only xoáy trên lobs require an open string góc. I will reiterate – the most important aspect of string góc on any shot is that it is unchanging through contact. Whatever shot you’ve elected to hit, however you’ve decided to orient your string góc through contact, make sure that góc barely changes through the hitting zone.

Don’t Players Rotate Their Rackets? Yes, but in a different plane, a plane which does not change their string góc through contact. Elite players rotate their rackets (passively) about their cẳng tay through the hitting zone, not about the cán vợt. Novak Djokovic’s vợt rotates counter-clockwise through the hitting zone, but the xoay doesn’t effect the string góc, which remains slightly closed throughout the vung vợt. Look at the back view of Djokovic’s thuận tay above.

The vợt is rotating, but it’s rotating in such a way as not to alter the góc of his dây through contact. From khung vợt 1 all the way to khung vợt 4, his string góc is slightly closed. That string góc remains consistent, even though the vợt whips around his cẳng tay throughout the cú đánh. Relaxation and String góc At first, it’s tough to relax your cổ tay and maintain your string góc.

You have to pull the vợt with your lõi movers at a pretty specific vector to avoid your cẳng tay going crazy and rolling all over the place. On the other hand, it’s very easy to maintain a consistent string góc with a tight cổ tay – this is why you naturally tighten up when you know you need to make a shot; your brain understands that a tight cổ tay isn’t ideal, but at least you’ll be able to maintain a string góc that will keep the ball in play.

But relaxation is the holy grail of thuận tay xoáy trên and power, so it’s a worthy goal to practice your string góc maintenance with a relaxed cổ tay. Shadow Swings Shadow swings are the best tool for teaching yourself to maintain your string góc through contact. The vợt can (and often will) roll over during the theo sau, but throughout the hitting zone the string góc must remain constant.

Turn sideways, place your vợt into its starting vị trí for your thuận tay, and then lightly rotate your thân and vung vợt forward. Start at half speed and work up from there. Relax your hand, and vung vợt out, up, and through an imaginary ball in front of you. Observe your string góc through the hitting zone. Is it changing? The vợt can (and often will) roll over during the theo sau, but throughout the hitting zone that góc must remain constant.

Tweak the way you pull your arm with your chest until the góc is in fact remaining constant through the vợt’s rotational roi vung. You can add a small amount of tension with your hand/cẳng tay to maintain the string góc, but ensure that your cổ tay is still loose enough that, when you initiate your xoay, your vợt naturally lags behind your hand, and then flicks around your cẳng tay as you vung vợt.

Ensuring Shadow Swings Translate Additionally, ensure two things on your shadow swings: Your hông get all the way around (back to parallel with an imaginary lưới) each vung vợt. Don’t leave them under-rotated. Your imaginary điểm tiếp xúc is out in front of and laterally away from your body. It’s very easy to lose those two points when you’re not hitting a real ball, but in order for the shadow swings to translate, we need to ensure specifically that these two good habits stay ingrained.

The góc Actually Changes a Little You’re tweaking your shadow vung vợt, and you finally find a comfortable, whippy, natural feeling vung vợt where the string góc isn’t changing very much through the hitting zone. You notice, though, that despite your best efforts, the góc is still closing a little as the vợt comes around. That’s fine. On a correct thuận tay, the string góc typically does roll over a very small amount through the hitting zone.

This is a very muted effect and it certainly isn’t volitional. Novak Djokovic’s string góc closes by only about 10 degrees from the time right before contact, to the time right after contact (from approximately 75o to 65o from horizontal). giao lại me clarify the three differences that distinguish this small, acceptable góc change from the typical one that destroys the thuận tay’s lỗi tolerance. 1.

It’s Non-Volitional This góc change happens completely naturally as a result of other forces during the vung vợt. The player is not at all trying to change their string góc through contact – they aren’t trying to “roll their vợt over the ball.” That means that this góc change never results in any unnecessary tension in the cổ tay, never hampers tốc độ đầu vợt, and naturally adjusts itself appropriately based on the exact situation of the cú đánh. 2.

Correct góc is a Range There does not exist one specific string góc at which the cú đánh must be contacted. Rather, there exists a range of correct angles, contact at any of which will lead to a decent shot. A slightly closed góc will cause the ball to fly lower over the lưới, and land shorter, while a slightly open góc will cause the ball to fly higher over the lưới and land deeper. But that doesn’t mean you’ll miss.

The small góc change that occurs naturally isn’t big enough that the góc of your dây leaves this acceptable góc range – it’s still the case that if you’re a little early, or you’re a little late, your cú đánh will succeed. As a result, your cú đánh is still lỗi tolerant, and thereby still trustworthy. 3. Contact Height Effects Desired String góc On a typical shot, the ball is moving down through the hitting zone and vợt is moving up through the hitting zone.

In this situation, the very slight góc change through contact can actually help the ball stay in when the vung vợt is mistimed. If you contact the ball early in the vung vợt by accident, that also means you contact the ball higher, and in that case you actually want a slightly more closed string góc.

And, similarly, if you contact the ball late by accident, you’ll contact it lower and want a slightly more open string góc. (This is different if you’re hitting on the rise, one of the reasons that shot’s degree of difficulty is higher.) Just Get Rid of the Bad Habit I only mention fact that a very small góc change does occur so that you don’t happen upon a correct vung vợt and think that it’s wrong.

When you relax your hand, rotate your body, vung vợt out, up, and through, and hit the ball in front of you, the small change will occur completely automatically, without you having to think about it. All I really want is for you to understand the role that string góc plays in lỗi tolerance and to find the vung vợt path that causes it to remain mostly constant through the hitting zone.

Any very small string góc change that still occurs, without you focusing on it, won’t effect your vung vợt’s lỗi tolerance. On the opposite end of the spectrum, we have the large, lỗi tolerant killing string angles change that most players unintentionally employ. These changes are volitional; the movements are usually bad habits that transpire due to intentional effort by the player to do something like “roll your vợt over the ball.” That’s what we have to get rid of.

That’s what’s killing your thuận tay’s lỗi tolerance. Restore that lỗi tolerance, and you restore your confidence. The Power of Neutral At lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we talk a lot about tấn công tennis. That’s for good reasons, two specifically. When your strokes lack lỗi tolerance, it’s your tấn công tennis that starts to break down first tấn công tennis has no ceiling. You can only push for so long; eventually, you need to learn how to take initiative.

So while tấn công tennis definitely deserves all of the attention we’ve paid to it so far, neutral tennis still demands it’s own dedicated discussion. Neutral is a very under-rated phase of the game. What happened in Monte Carlo over the last few days beautifully illustrates the importance of neutral tennis, and thus it gives us the perfect framework to discuss it today.

If you haven’t been following, here’s the run down: Wednesday: Novak Djokovic def Jannik Sinner 6-4 6-2 Thursday: Dan Evans def Novak Djokovic 6-4 7-5 Now, to the naive eye, Jannik Sinner is a much better player than Dan Evans, and, currently, Jannik is also ranked higher. Jannik hits harder off of both wings. He’s younger, faster, and routinely blasts flashy winners by his opponents.

Yet, even given all that, it wasn’t Jannik Sinner who was able to hand the best player in the world his first loss of the season. Rather, it was Dan Evans, a player who hardly ever even attempts a xoáy trên trái tay. As of April 16th, 2021, Jannik Sinner is already the #22 player in the world, only 4 spots behind his fellow Italian countryman Fabio Fognini, despite being only 19 years old. What Power Typically Accomplishes For the most part, hitting hard is great, and hitting harder is better.

For one thing, hitting hard makes it easier to hit winners, and if you hit a điểm thắng, of course, you win the point outright. For another, your opponent has less time to phản ứng to faster balls, so it’s much more difficult for them to get into an optimal vị trí for their vung vợt. Lastly, even when your opponent does manage to get into vị trí against your powerful shot, their vung vợt is still more difficult to time, because your shot is coming so fast at them.

The result – it won’t be long before one of your powerful shots draws a weak reply. The less lỗi tolerant your opponent’s strokes are, the more effective raw power is against them. The less lỗi tolerant your opponent’s strokes are, the more effective raw power is against them. Recall the concept whereby, when you vung vợt faster, the errors in your cú đánh execution are more pronounced. Well, the exact same principle applies if your opponent is hitting faster at you.

In that case, too, any small execution mistakes on your part will be magnified; when the ball is coming faster, you have to be more precise than you otherwise would to produce a reply of equal quality. A faster moving ball spends less time in your optimal contact zone than a slower moving ball does. Overall, hitting really hard out of neutral situations typically makes sense, provided you can still maintain decent percentages.

If you hit really hard out of neutral, chances are, pretty soon, your opponent will give you a weak reply, and you’ll be able to transition to offense. The Novak Djokovic Effect Here’s the problem with using power against a defender as good as Novak Djokovic – it’s not enough to get out of neutral.

Even against most tour level players, Jannik Sinner’s game is powerful enough that he can quickly and consistently navigate his way out of a neutral đánh qua lại and into an tấn công đánh qua lại, simply by blasting the ball at them. But Novak’s game is different. Novak Djokovic has the most lỗi tolerant strokes on the entire tour. He once played an entire 2.5 sets at Wimbledon without making an lỗi tự phát.

Against a player like that, powerful shots alone don’t facilitate a chuyển from neutral to offense. Neutral is Neutral If you’re hitting really hard and not getting to offense because of it, that’s a problem. It’s a problem because you are always sacrificing some amount of consistency, however small, in order to hit the ball harder. You need to gain some benefit to offset that sacrifice. If you aren’t gaining anything, you’d be better off just hitting slower.

Now, of course, you can’t hit so slowly that your opponent can easily transition to offense, but there actually exists a rộng gap between these two levels of aggression. Roughly, here are the three zones of shots that we’re talking about: Shots hit hard enough to get you to offense The rộng range of shots that keep the point neutral Shots hit so weakly that your opponent gets to offense You are always sacrificing some amount of consistency, however small, in order to hit the ball harder.

If you aren’t consistently getting to offense when hitting hard, then you’re in zone #2, not zone #1. But if you’re in zone #2 already, is a power shot really what you want to attempt? The goal in zone #2 is just to keep the point neutral and await a future opportunity to do more than that. Is a hard, fast shot your highest percentage way to continue the point?

You don’t have to hit so weakly you end up in zone #3, but, again, there are a lot of shots that merely keep the point neutral, a lot of shots in zone #2. There does not exist a simplistic dichotomy between hitting hard and merely rolling the ball in. Lots of shots exist between those two extremes that aren’t particularly chủ động tấn công, but that are chủ động tấn công enough that your opponent can’t easily tấn công them.

If you’re keeping the point neutral, accept that you’re keeping the point neutral, and then keep the point neutral as best as you can. If you select lower percentage shots, even shots that only sacrifice a small amount of consistency, you need to ensure you’re gaining a benefit from that selection. Jannik Sinner’s power shots are extremely consistent. That’s why he’s already one of the best players in the world. But they weren’t consistent enough on Wednesday.

Against a defender as skilled as Novak, the lợi thế he gained from his power was almost completely neutralized; after that, all that was left was the very small consistency sacrifice, and against a world class player, that small edge was enough to decide the trận đấu. Dan Evans and the trái tay cắt Dan Evans is famous for his trái tay cắt – a cú đánh by which the player hits the ball back with underspin, rather than xoáy trên.

Dan Evans’s trái tay cắt hardly ever directly facilitates a transition to offense. Occasionally, he’ll strike an chủ động tấn công cúp off of that wing, or he’ll take a ngắn trái tay, đường bóng the cắt sâu, and approach the lưới, but, for the most part, Dan Evans’s trái tay cắt has one function – keep the point neutral. Dan’s trái tay is excellent at this function. It doesn’t matter that Dan Evans rarely attacks off the trái tay wing.

In đánh đơn tennis, especially, the first thing every player needs to tập trung on is not losing the point. When Dan Evans hits a trái tay cắt, he hardly ever loses the point on the next shot. When Dan Evans hits a trái tay cắt, that cắt almost always keeps the point neutral. That matters. Neutral is Okay What everyone should take from Dan’s win over Novak is that hitting many shots in a row out of neutral is totally acceptable. Don’t panic; you don’t need to press for offense immediately.

Dan Evans is playing against the best player in the world, and he elects to, almost exclusively, hit a knifing cắt off the trái tay wing. He knows a player as good as Djokovic will hardly ever miss that ball, which means that Dan’s cắt, in turn, is very rarely going to win him the point outright, but he’s okay with that. Dan has a massive thuận tay which he uses for plenty of offense, so, again, there’s no need to panic and do something stupid off of the trái tay.

Don’t panic; you don’t need to press for offense immediately. Further, at the recreational level, you can actually win many matches without ever playing offense at all. Think about how many times you’ve lost to a pusher in your life. How is it possible, given that they never play offense? It’s possible because the pusher is an expert at keeping the point neutral.

It’s not trivial to take offense against them – unless you’ve specifically practiced that skill, you’re probably not going to be able to. Neutral is the starting point for đánh đơn tennis. Offense is flashy, but every point is just worth one point. Executing a beautiful string of approach shots, volleys, and overheads to ultimately smack the ball by your opponent is worth one point, and accidentally missing a trái tay out of a neutral situation is worth one point.

So, while it’s definitely exhilarating to play tấn công tennis, if you want to improve your đánh đơn results, try focusing a little more on consistently keeping the point neutral. Get good enough at that, and you just might beat Novak Djokovic. Free the Foot, Free the hông At lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we teach fundamentals. Today, though, we’re going to dive into a detail. Why? Because this detail, specifically, messes up so many players that it’s worth addressing on it’s own.

In the same way that we’ve dedicated articles to just the low thuận tay, we’ll dedicate articles to other non-fundamental specifics, in cases when those specifics cause enough issues to be worth addressing by themselves. During the forward vung vợt on the thuận tay it’s actually impossible to execute a proper hông twist if the feet remain fully planted on the ground during your vung vợt. The feet must come up off the ground, at least partially, in order for the hông to fully rotate.

The Role of the Back Foot For the back foot, specifically, this can be challenging. During thuận tay chuẩn bị, you nạp up on your back foot, placing your weight on it, such that during the forward vung vợt, you can đường bóng off of it. However, you have to actually đường bóng off of it, lest too much weight remain on this back foot as you vung vợt, thereby preventing it from partially leaving the ground and freeing the hông to rotate.

Here’s the physical checkpoint that determines whether you (or your student) is doing it right: on almost every thuận tay, the back foot ends pointing at the target. The back foot’s job is to press off of the ground, thereby initiating the chuỗi động học. In order to do this, it must be planted on the ground, at first. This early weight on the back foot can be a double edged sword, though.

That weight needs to, eventually, get off of the back foot, such that the back foot is free to rotate along with the hông. The feet are connected to the hông. When the hông rotate, the feet want to rotate as well, and when the hông end square to the target at contact, the feet want to be square, too. Both Roger Federer and Rafael Nadal allow their back foot to come almost fully off the ground as they vung vợt on the thuận tay.

This allows the foot to rotate along with their hông, leading to it naturally pointing at the target by contact. Look at the two images above, of Roger Federer’s and Rafael Nadal’s feet as each comes to contact. For each, the back foot is pointing towards their target. It starts facing away from the target (just like the hông), and then, as the player rotates during the vung vợt, the foot itself naturally pivots back towards the lưới. Should I Do This Consciously?

For some students, consciously thinking about the back foot ending towards the lưới helps them rotate. Specifically, this cue can assist with students who are under-rotating, students who are rotating too late, or students who are keeping too much weight on the back foot during xoay. 1. Under xoay When you under-rotate, chances are your back foot won’t get all the way around to the lưới, just like your hông.

Often, if you think about “your back foot ending towards the lưới,” your body will naturally increase your hông twist in order to make that happen, which solves the fundamental problem, insufficient xoay hông, perfectly. 2. Late xoay Sometimes, a student will “rotate their hông,” in a sense, but they don’t actually đường bóng their vung vợt with that xoay hông.

On a xoay hông which drives the cú đánh, the back foot needs to partially leave the ground, because if it doesn’t, there’s too much lực on the ankle when the hông rotate. The back foot needs to partially leave the ground, because if it doesn’t, there’s too much lực on the ankle when the hông rotate.

When the hông, instead, engage late in the chain, they generate much less power; the hông twist isn’t an explosive hông twist, with the goal of driving the chuỗi động học, but rather a weak twist with the simple goal of turning the body, because the student has a vague idea that the body is supposed to be turning. It’s possible to execute this out of sync hông twist without the back foot leaving the ground at all. In this case, the “back foot at the lưới” cue can also help.

When a student thinks about ensuring their back foot gets around, their consciousness moves to their hitting leg and their hông twist, the appropriate place when starting the thuận tay. This typically causes them to correctly initiate the vung vợt by driving off of that foot, instead of adding an out of sync xoay hông later. 3.

Too Much Weight on the Back Foot Especially when using the non-hitting-foot-up di chuyển mẫu, it can be easy to keep too much weight on the back foot, preventing it from sufficiently rotating. Again, simply thinking about getting the foot around usually solves this problem. Your brain understands that, in order for a foot to rotate, it needs to be deloaded. You’ll naturally increase your spring off of that foot in order to get it around towards the lưới.

The hông Lead Foot xoay I’ll close with a point of caution concerning this cue. While it is extremely helpful, it’s important that any coach using it still understands the fundamentals behind it. This cue is a trick to get a student to employ xoay hông. It’s possible to rotate the foot without rotating the hông. This kind of foot xoay, absent xoay hông, is not in any way helpful to the cú đánh.

The cue isn’t merely an instruction, but rather a physical checkpoint for the student: “Did your back foot rotate back towards the lưới? No?

Then đường bóng off of it a little more and allow it to come up off of the ground more, until it does.” The cue is not: “Use the muscles in the calf to twist your back foot towards the lưới.” That said, the back foot’s orientation is a very useful detail for both executing and debugging the thuận tay, and learning how to cue it properly is a great tool to add to your coaching repertoire.

Compact, Efficient, Elite – Aslan Karatsev’s Versatile thuận tay In almost every article, I say something along the lines of “lead with the hông” or “power comes from the hông, not the arm,” but how do you actually put that concept into practice? What does it mean for power to come from the hông, not the arm, when it comes to the actual, physical positions your body enters during a thuận tay vung vợt? Most players’ initial instinct is that the arm should đường bóng the vợt.

The idea of “swinging” a thing is typically understood as taking said thing in your arm, pulling your arm back, and then throwing your arm forward. It’s really only athletes, whether natural or trained, who have determined that, in the context of something like hitting a baseball or striking a tennis ball, it’s actually better to “vung vợt” not by pulling and throwing the arm itself, but rather by twisting the thân and allowing the arm to come along for the ride.

Though it’s initially counter-intuitive, the arm barely participates in either the đưa vợt ra sau, or the forward vung vợt. The thân drives the vợt. Aslan Karatsev’s Compact chuẩn bị Aslan Karatsev has been on a tear recently. He qualified for the Australian Open in 2021 and then made the semi-finals.

Since that breakout performance, he has: Won the Qatar Open đánh đôi Title (with fellow Russian Andrey Rublev) Made the finals of the Serbia Open, beating (1) Novak Djokovic along the way Won the Dubai Championship, an ATP 500 level event, beating Dan Evans, Jannick Sinner, and Andrey Rublev en route to that title There are hundreds of things that make Aslan Karatsev a great player, but we’re going to tập trung in on his thuận tay and, specifically, his “đưa vợt ra sau” (or lack there of).

Karatsev is one of the players on the tour who most exemplifies the idea that arm vung vợt is completely unnecessary for power generation. He hardly brings his hand back at all during his thuận tay chuẩn bị, and thus, instead of using his arm to generate power, he simply unwinds his kinetic cuộn. From a front view, often, during Aslan Karatsev’s thuận tay chuẩn bị, we can see both his hand and his vợt (right) on the hitting side of his body.

Even when he twists his chest and vai exceptionally far (left), we can still see almost his entire vợt on the hitting side of his body, at the completion of his đưa vợt ra sau. Abbreviate, Abbreviate, Abbreviate the Arm di chuyển Many players take their arm back unnecessarily far. This adds a significant burden of execution to their vung vợt while providing hardly any benefit in trả bóng. Why is this so common?

Too far doesn’t feel like it’s too far, and, further, the the proper distance often doesn’t feel far enough. Aslan Karatsev prepares to pass Jannick Sinner in the 2021 Dubai Championships quarterfinal, en route to winning the trận đấu, and ultimately title. In the above shot, Aslan Karatsev is about to flick a chéo sân cúp chuyền điểm thắng past Jannick Sinner, despite the fact that Sinner’s cú tiếp cận is struck exceptionally hard and hit away from Aslan. How does he do it?

Not only does Karatsev prepare his vợt on the hitting side of his body here, but also his entire hand, cổ tay, and cẳng tay, all on the hitting side of his body before he initiates the vung vợt. Further, in this particular case, Karatsev has barely wound up his kinetic cuộn at all. His chest is turned away from the ball a little, but not to nearly the degree that we see on his chủ động tấn công shots.

Taken together, these chuẩn bị techniques mean that the distance his vợt needs to travel to get to the ball is very small. This makes the shot far easier to time, because there is far less vung vợt time during which small faults can effect the vung vợt. There’s literally less di chuyển to mess up. This is the only way that a difficult phòng thủ shot responding to a high quality chủ động tấn công shot (the kind of shot Karatsev is playing here) can be even remotely lỗi tolerant.

nạp in the hông Pocket As an approximation, “nạp in the hông pocket” pretty accurately describes the starting vị trí at the end of thuận tay chuẩn bị. We don’t literally want to start our hand at our hông pocket, because, of course, that’s too close to our body, but with respect to the back/front đang nạp vị trí, the hông pocket is perfect. The hông pocket is typically on the front of the leg, and that’s a good place to prepare the hand.

There’s really no need to send the hand all the way around the body back to the back fence. Any additional small gia tốc you might get from preparing the hand farther back is dwarfed by the reduction in lỗi tolerance that doing so introduces. But then how do I get any power? Twist. If you want a little power, twist a little. If you want a lot of power, twist hard. If you really want a lot of power, twist back farther during chuẩn bị such that you can twist forward more during the vung vợt.

But twist the body, don’t pull the arm back. Aslan Karatsev prepares to blast a thẳng biên điểm thắng past Lloyd Harris in the Dubai Championships final, 2021, en route to winning the title. Here’s a shot of Aslan preparing for what ultimately becomes a thẳng biên-điểm thắng. Yes, a điểm thắng, despite the abbreviated arm đưa vợt ra sau. To the naive athletic mind, it’s quite surprising, when looking at Aslan’s arm chuẩn bị, that this shot is struck exceptionally hard.

Everything is on the hitting side of the chest. His vợt, hand, cẳng tay, and even his upper arm are all in front of his chest before he swings. How does he hit this ball so hard, despite the fact that the arm hasn’t been pulled back in order to do so? The secret how far Aslan has wound his abdominals – his thân is turned a full 90o away from his target.

His arm need not participate in the power generation process when he prepares this way, because his explosive thân xoay will generate all the gia tốc he needs. Trust The thân, Abandon the Arm It’ll feel weird, at first. When you try to implement a hand chuẩn bị similar to Aslan Karatsev’s, you’ll feel constricted. How am I supposed to get any vung vợt going from this vị trí? The vợt is already so close to it’s final điểm tiếp xúc. Just try it, though.

Once the ball gets to its proper location, both away from you and in front of you, twist. If anything, this chuẩn bị will help you catch the ball well in front of you. You’ll have to, because your vợt is starting so much farther up in the sân than you’re used to. Twist hard, catch it early, and you’ll be surprised just how much pop your shot still has.

I’ll leave you with a quote from the thuận tay chuẩn bị section of The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, our book, in which all of this content is explained in heavier technical detail: Nearly all of the thuận tay killing đưa vợt ra sau mistakes aren’t caused by players failing to take chuẩn bị steps that they should be taking. Rather, they’re almost always caused by players doing extra things during chuẩn bị that they shouldn’t be doing.

Great In Practice, Bad in Matches Why is it that so many players feel like they play great in practice, and yet can’t produce that same level of tennis in matches? In two words: Mental Resources. In a trận đấu situation, there’s so much more to tập trung on than in practice, which puts a strain on your limited attention. Often, the things that work great in practice, but fall apart in matches, are the things that you tập trung on in practice, but don’t have the mental resources to tập trung on in matches.

An Example giao lại’s say you have a bad habit that you’re working out of your game during practice. For this example, we’ll use a common one: you under-rotate on your thuận tay. You and your practice partner grind chéo sân forehands so that you can practice fully rotating into contact. Within 20-30 shots, you’ll be rotating properly, because you’re able to tập trung on your xoay as you vung vợt.

Further, this tập trung won’t demand 100% of your mental attention; you’ll also be able to tập trung on whatever else it is you typically tập trung on as you go to hit. Whether it’s your feet, your contact, your breath, etc. – whatever it is that you typically spend your attention on when you strike a thuận tay, you’ve got enough mental resources to keep focusing on that and to add in the extra tập trung on proper xoay. Now giao lại’s change to a trận đấu.

All of the sudden, there’s way more to tập trung on: Where is your opponent? How are they defending? What shot are you going to hit? What happens if you miss? How big should you go? Etc, etc, etc… You also have to giao bóng before you play your thuận tay, which adds yet another novelty to the trận đấu situation, and adds another list of items demanding your mental attention.

In order to really break a bad habit, you need to practice the correct version so much that you can perform it properly without mental tập trung. di chuyển in a trận đấu is different. With your practice partner, you were grinding forehands out of the thuận tay góc, but in a trận đấu, you might have to sprint that way before contact, further complicating the di chuyển and chuẩn bị.

Under these conditions, you do not have the mental resources for everything, and the first thing to go is going to be the thing that’s most novel – in your case, proper xoay. In practice, you thought you’d gotten rid of that bad habit, but the only reason you were rotating properly in practice was because you had enough mental resources to tập trung on it. Once you get into the trận đấu, that’s no longer the case.

This means that, in order to really break a bad habit, you need to practice the correct version so much that you can perform it properly without mental tập trung, because, in a trận đấu situation, mental resources are so limited. 1. Practice Until Unconscious Competence Our first key insight into playing better in matches – practice your good habits until you can do them without thinking.

If you can perform your good habit correctly while focusing on it, that’s great, and it’s definitely better than reverting back to your bad habit, but until your newly minted good habit has moved into the phase of unconscious competence (you perform it correctly without thinking), it’ll always demand mental attention during matches. Since mental attention is so limited in matches, there’s a good chance it won’t get it.

Newly formed habits aren’t the only things that get forgotten during the mental attention squeeze that occurs during matches, though. Here are some other things that often get the axe, and for which just a little bit of extra tập trung goes a long way. 2. bước chân phân tách After You giao bóng I know, I know, it sounds trivial. But are you sure you always actually prepare for the next ball after you giao bóng?

Especially if your giao bóng is in flux right now – maybe you’re tweaking your cơ học or you’re dealing with an injury – it’s extremely easy to become so distracted by your giao bóng that you forget to prepare for the point after you strike it. It’s extremely easy to become so distracted by your giao bóng that you forget to prepare for the point. A few days ago, I was hitting with a friend of mine who hadn’t played a trận đấu in a while, and he was making this exact mistake.

I’d chip the trả bóng, not a cúp, just a ngắn cắt, and he wouldn’t even move for it, despite only needing a couple steps to hit it back. It might sound strange, but this is actually a pretty common problem. Definitely look out for this if you routinely play against returners who miss your giao bóng (if you’re male, đánh đôi nam nữ is a killer for this). If you hit 5, 6, 7 unreturned serves in a row, your brain will get lazy.

Your subconscious will ask you: why do I have to bước chân phân tách when the giao bóng isn’t coming back anyway? Don’t giao lại yourself fall into this habit. 3. Prepare Early In practice, there’s so much less to think about that most players naturally take the vợt back on time. In matches, this isn’t always the case. If, as the ball comes over the lưới, you’re thinking about 10 different shots you can hit, and how you’ll trở lại vị trí after each one, chances are what you’re not doing is beginning your chuẩn bị.

Turn early, and then wait until the ball gets there, rather than waiting to turn away until the ball is already close. In The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, the last section of chuẩn bị is titled “Inhale and Wait.” An entire section is dedicated to this idea because it’s so often overlooked that waiting is a critical piece of proper thuận tay chuẩn bị.

Rafal Nadal has already fully prepared his vợt to execute a trái tay cắt, despite the fact that the incoming ball hasn’t even passed the đường giao bóng yet. It’s very tempting to only start your chuẩn bị once you feel like you’re basically ready to vung vợt, but that’s too late. In order for the cú đánh to be lỗi tolerant, you need to prepare extremely early.

That way, even if the ball comes at you quicker than expected, or if it takes a bad bounce, or even if you just execute the cú đánh improperly, you’ll still have plenty of time to adjust, and therefore the cú đánh will succeed anyway. Do not try to time your chuẩn bị such that chuẩn bị and vung vợt is one continuous motion. Start immediately once you recognize where the ball is coming.

Move to the ball with plenty of time to spare, inhale, and then wait until you see the ball reach the zone at which you want to begin your vung vợt. 4. Margin, Margin, Margin Especially in đánh đơn tennis, you need to use higher margin shots than you think, and it’s common for a player’s sense of what is an appropriate level of aggression to be miscalibrated during practice. During practice, missing doesn’t really matter.

Sure, your brain will hit you with a little bit of negative emotion, but it’s nothing like losing a point in a trận đấu. If you try to hit a thuận tay điểm thắng in practice, and you convert it 60% of the time, the positive emotion of the successful winners may outweigh the negative emotion of the missed shots. In a trận đấu, of course, this emotional response is incorrect: if you have an opportunity to hit a thuận tay điểm thắng, your equity in the point should be far higher than 60%.

The target on the left is too chủ động tấn công; too much of the shot’s outcome distribution is out. The shot on the right, which will miss far less of the time, is a much better decision, even when playing tấn công tennis. 5. Practice Your Offense We’ve talked about this before, but it bears repeating. You have to practice tấn công tennis if you ever want to be good at tấn công tennis. This is a huge issue with many practices.

In many contexts, it’s actually impolite to tấn công your practice partner’s bóng ngắn and blast a điểm thắng by him. The solution is to add in dedicated tấn công drills to your practice sessions like Offense-Defense. That way, when you get into a trận đấu and you attempt tấn công tennis, it won’t be so novel. Again, when a typical player goes up to smack a ngắn thuận tay in a trận đấu, there’s a good possibility this is literally the first time all week they’ve attempted that shot.

Of course it’s going to be low percentage. A graphical depiction of Offense-Defense. In this drill, one player (the phòng thủ player) feeds the other player (the tấn công player) a ngắn, bóng cao, and then the two players play out the point chéo sân. Summing Up In a trận đấu, you won’t have nearly as many mental resources at your disposal as you will in practice. Here’s how to play well anyway. Practice until unconscious incompetence. bước chân phân tách after you giao bóng. Prepare early.

Aim to bigger targets. Practice your offense. Also, I’ll leave you with one more: Play matches! Whatever kind of trận đấu you want to be good at, whether that’s đánh đơn, đánh đôi, đánh đôi nam nữ, or something else, you’ve got to play that kind of trận đấu many times over in order to master it. Doing so will allow you to practice the specific kinds of shots that are common in that format, and to grasp the nuances of what makes a player good at it. Good luck!

phẳng, Spin, and Everything in Between The primary way you adjust your thuận tay is by altering the tilt of your torso through contact. As we’ve discussed before, there exists only a small range of optimal di chuyển patterns that allow for a thuận tay vung vợt powered by explosive body xoay. In order for your thuận tay to be explosive, you need to perform a di chuyển mẫu in this small range. Tilting the Torso All high quality forehands involve using explosive torso xoay to power the vợt.

The vợt, thrown by that explosive torso xoay, flicks out to the ball, and then up and through the ball through contact. When the rotational chuỗi động học is harnessed properly, nearly 100% of the body’s rotational năng lượng is transferred into the vợt by contact, leading to a whipping effect which creates tremendous tốc độ đầu vợt. We tilt the torso to change the vợt path through contact without altering the relative vị trí of the hand, arm, chest, hông, etc.

This way, we can alter the resulting contact while still harnessing the chuỗi động học in the exact same biomechanical manner. This allows us to alter our shot while still accelerating the vợt aggressively. Roger Federer striking a đầu gối-height thuận tay (left), a waist-height thuận tay (giữa), and a vai-height thuận tay (right). In the above image, Roger Federer is tilting his body to adjust his điểm tiếp xúc.

On all three forehands, Roger maintains the same relative vị trí of his vợt, hand, arm, and chest. This allows him to generate tốc độ đầu vợt the same way on these three different shots. Want further proof? Here’s what happens if we rotate the images to align the arm positioning. Thanks to one of our readers, Djordje Kojičić for reaching out via email and providing these images. The creation of this graphic indicates a sâu understanding of the concept, so, again, thanks a lot.

What you’re seeing here are the exact same three forehands as above. The images are just rotated. Roger’s cơ sinh học are so consistent from shot to shot that you almost can’t tell which shot he’s hitting when you rotate the images like this. His chuỗi động học is always executed the same way. Applying This to Your game You have to get out of the mindset that the arm determines the vung vợt. It doesn’t (at least, not alone).

The arm can certainly make adjustments, but most of the time you have to sacrifice tốc độ đầu vợt in order to adjust with your arm. While preserving the fully intact chuỗi động học, the arm can only make fine tuned adjustments which only effect the ball’s trajectory a little. If you try to do more than that with your arm, often either the lỗi tolerance of the cú đánh will evaporate, or the effortless tốc độ đầu vợt will disappear instead.

Therefore, adjust your body in order to adjust your cú đánh, the way Roger does above. In next few sections, we’ll discuss exactly what this looks like for a few different kinds of forehands. Hitting phẳng The simplest thuận tay is the phẳng thuận tay. It can be performed most easily when the ball is around vai height. On this shot, the torso remains essentially upright, and you simply twist and giao lại the vợt flick straight through the ball, with very little up motion through contact.

Nick Kyrgios, the king of the phẳng thuận tay, prepares his hand at the height of the ball (left), and then, with very little torso tilt at all, explosively rotates straight through the ball (right), blasting a thuận tay trả bóng điểm thắng past Andrey Rublev. This shot won’t feel easy if coaches have been pounding “brush up on it” into your đầu vợt for 10 years, but if you’ve been reading lỗi Tolerant Tennis instead, it will be.

Here, we don’t give advice that isn’t transferable – “brush up on it” is only (kind of) true for a xoáy trên shot, but for phẳng shots, obviously, that paradigm breaks down. Here’s a much better road map for the thuận tay cú đánh: Accelerate the vợt with body xoay Ensure your hông and chest are back around by contact Contact the ball away from you and out in front of you Under these criteria, a phẳng shot and a xoáy trên shot are not fundamentally different.

On a phẳng shot, merely nạp the vợt behind the ball, roughly at the same level as the ball, rotate, and giao lại vợt flick straight through it, instead of đang nạp it below the ball and allowing it to flick upward. Getting xoáy trên xoáy trên necessitates that the vợt face is moving up significantly through contact. Now, this doesn’t necessarily mean that your arm must also be moving drastically up, but the vợt face must be. First, a refresher on the physics of xoáy trên contact.

From The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay: In order to hit a xoáy trên shot, the player’s vợt must exert an upward lực on the back of the ball at contact, causing it to rotate. In addition to this upward lực, the player must also supply the large translational lực necessary to đường bóng the ball back over the lưới with speed.

This is accomplished by contacting the ball while the vợt is moving both upwards and forwards through it, with the dây tilted slightly downward towards the sân while doing so. The upward friction between the ball and dây at impact will both đường bóng the ball up, giving it enough height to clear the lưới, and also cause the ball to rotate in a xoáy trên manner.

The translational impact – the fact that the vợt is also moving forward through contact – will impart the ball with significant vận tốc towards the other side of the sân. The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. “Understanding xoáy trên Contact.” Page 28. The best way to get the vợt flicking up through the ball as you vung vợt is to nạp the vợt slightly below the ball on all xoáy trên shots.

This will cause your arm to move up a little on every shot, and it’ll also cause the xoay about your cổ tay to be more vertical, increasing xoáy trên. Rafael Nadal playing a đầu gối-height thuận tay with heavy xoáy trên by tilting his torso by about 30o, transposing much of his vợt’s vận tốc into vertical vận tốc. To get more xoáy trên than that, tilt your torso as you rotate. That’ll transpose the same relative vung vợt into a more vertical vung vợt with respect to the ball.

That transposition of the same vợt vận tốc is what allows you to hit a loopier shot, a shot for which more of the vận tốc you generate goes into xoáy trên and less of the vận tốc you generate goes into driving the ball forward. Here’s Rafael Nadal implementing this idea. The thuận tay strike you see in the image travels a full five feet over the lưới, before quickly spiraling down on the other side of the sân.

giao lại’s break down the key ingredients that create that beautiful result, one of which is the critical body tilt. Rafa is incredibly strong. No matter how good your kỹ thuật is, you can’t hit as heavy as he does without strength. Rafa harnesses that strength by rotating his hông and chest into contact, utilizing his entire body to accelerate the vợt to blinding speeds. Rafa tilts his torso down by about 30o such that more of this vợt motion is transposed into the vertical plane.

This, ultimately, is what transforms his blinding tốc độ đầu vợt into vicious spin. Spin Exists on a Continuum To close, I want to specify that “xoáy trên shots” and “phẳng shots” are not a dichotomy, despite the fact that they’re so often presented as such. Instead, xoáy trên exists on a continuum. There are spinnier shots and flatter shots. There are really spinny shots and really phẳng shots.

But even on “phẳng” forehands or “phẳng” serves, most pros still elect to add at least a small amount of xoáy trên to increase their margin for error. That’s why I’m not teaching you how to hit a “phẳng thuận tay” or how to hit a “xoáy trên thuận tay.” Rather, I’m simply teaching you what body orientation produces what result. The more spin you want, the more you have to tilt, and the more your vợt needs to go up.

Likewise, the flatter you want to hit, the more you need to level out, and the higher you need to nạp your vợt. giao lại’s take a look at the Queen of Clay for one final example of this principle. Iga Swiatek striking a vai height thuận tay (left) and a đầu gối-height thuận tay (right) with different torso angles, while practicing for the 2021 Australian Open.

Iga Swiatek loads her vợt below the ball on both vai-height and đầu gối height forehands, because she prefers to use xoáy trên on all her shots. This decision comes down to player preference. Some players, like Nick Kyrgios, elect to smack high forehands almost completely phẳng, while others, like Iga, prefer to use a little xoáy trên to get that extra margin for error. But even though Iga is definitely a high spin player, she still only tilts her torso on lower balls.

This torso tilt further increases the verticality of the vung vợt, allowing her to get significant lưới clearance on a bóng thấp, and this same tilt would clearly be inappropriate on a higher ball. Ultimately, every player needs to intuit that their torso góc is their primary tool for altering their vung vợt path. If you can only alter your vung vợt with your arm, you’re going to be very limited in the range of shots you can successfully execute.
