Learn to alter the torso, and you’ll unlock a new world of thuận tay flexibility. All Forehands Are trong-ra ngoài Forehands I work with a 10-year-old girl named Anna who presents with an interesting issue. When she’s pulled out rộng on the thuận tay, her cú đánh looks great, but when the ball comes right to her, her cú đánh breaks down.

On the rộng shot, she naturally prepares her body on time, finds a nice điểm tiếp xúc out in front of her and away from her, and in doing so rotates into her shot and contacts the ball with her hông and chest square to the lưới. When she’s pulled out rộng on the thuận tay, her cú đánh looks great, but when the ball comes right to her, her cú đánh breaks down. On forehands that come right to her… not so much.

She’s often late, often under-rotated, often contacting the ball too close to her body, and the entire cú đánh looks awkward and forced. If you’d only seen her hit a rộng thuận tay, you’d be impressed, and then subsequently very confused when you saw her hit a typical shot. It’s not just Anna, though. I’ve seen this in many students’ games, most notably, my own.

I myself have had this exact same problem where my rộng thuận tay is much better than my regular shot, and that’s why I was able to help Anna so quickly. This brings us to today’s insight: All forehands are trong-ra ngoài forehands. Move Away, Move Away, Move Away When a ball comes straight at you, you have to get out of the way of it before swinging.

And I mean significantly – you can execute the shot if you only move a little, but it won’t be very effective, and if you do try to hit it hard, it won’t be lỗi tolerant. Even some forehands that end up on your hitting side are still trong-ra ngoài forehands. Forget the notion that there are three locations for a thuận tay: rộng forehands, forehands that come right to you, and trong-ra ngoài forehands.

There are only two: rộng forehands, for which you move right, and trong-ra ngoài forehands, for which you move left (lefties, switch those directions). Again, all forehands that come down the giữa are trong-ra ngoài forehands, and even some forehands that end up on your hitting side are still trong-ra ngoài forehands. You need to move away from the ball more than you think you have to. A Visual Explanation giao lại’s look at Rafael Nadal for an example of a player who moves exceptionally well on every thuận tay.

Below is an image of Rafa preparing for a ball that’s been hit to his hitting side. This isn’t even a ball that’s come straight at him, but one that, were he not to move at all, would actually land to his left (Rafa is lefty). Even though the ball is coming to Rafa’s hitting side, he will still move his body away from the ball before swinging, in order to harness xoay as he swings. Rafael Nadal preparing for a thuận tay on his hitting-side, yet still moving away from the ball.

Each of the top, giữa, and lower yellow lines is the same length as the corresponding line in the other images. The Feet Rafa plays this thuận tay from a neutral-tư thế (slightly semi-open). This allows him to easily turn his hông away from the target, such that he can rotate them back towards the target through the vung vợt.

As shown by the bottom line on each image, Rafa’s back foot is very close to where the ball will land to start, and then he moves it much farther from the ball during chuẩn bị. The hông The hông themselves must also move away from the ball to facilitate explosive xoay. It’s a smaller difference than for the back foot, but you’ll notice that during chuẩn bị, and even during contact, the hông are farther from the ball than they started.

This is because Rafa needs this little bit of extra space to harness the chuỗi động học properly; were he any closer to the ball, his arm wouldn’t be able to freely roi vung around like it’s doing. The Hitting vai Rafa’s hitting vai also starts very close to the ball, and turns well away from the ball during chuẩn bị. Then, even at contact, it’s still farther from the ball than it started.

Again, if Rafa were closer to the ball at contact, his arm wouldn’t be as free – he’d feel jammed and awkward. Make Kids Hit trong-ra ngoài This concept is why I always have children practice trong-ra ngoài forehands, even from our very first lessons at 5 years old. The trong-ra ngoài thuận tay isn’t a “specific kind of thuận tay” or a “thuận tay variant” – the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay is the thuận tay.

If you aren’t comfortable hitting a thuận tay on your trái tay side, then you’re not comfortable with a di chuyển mẫu that is fundamental to the thuận tay cú đánh: moving away from the ball before swinging. Teaching kids to hit trong-ra ngoài from the start drills that “move away” di chuyển mẫu into them as an essential, necessary part of the thuận tay cú đánh.

This emphasis on moving away before hitting helps to avoid the common problem of only having an effective rộng thuận tay, but improperly spacing the ball when it comes right to you. thị lực Will Transform Your trận đấu Play thị lực might be the single most underrated tool in every tennis player’s toolbox. Sure, every coach tells their students to “watch the ball,” but what does that really mean? And why is it that proper visual tracking is the single most important key that unlocks fluid trận đấu play?

giao lại’s dive in. Mental Resources A mental resource squeeze takes place during matches. During matches, you have far more to think about than during practice. In practice, when you have less to think about, you typically have ample mental resources to spend on the routine things that make your game effective (like focused visual tracking).

All of the sudden, in matches, you suddenly find that you no longer have enough attention for everything, and the result is that certain, critical aspects of your game get too little tập trung to function. thị lực is often one of those things. A few weeks ago, we discussed 5 common things that tend to get squeezed out, but today we’re going to dedicate a full article to thị lực, because it’s probably the single most important.

Incorrectly Watching Your Target If you played baseball or softball growing up, you know that it’s essential to watch your target as you throw. This keeps your đầu vợt stable and orients your entire throw chain in the proper direction. In tennis, it’s the opposite. If you watch your target, you’re going to miss.

Instead, you need to track the tennis ball attentively for its entire flight, then, right before the ball reaches you, set your eyes on contact, and leave them there until your cú đánh is complete. For kids who grew up playing a throwing sport, this is very counter-intuitive, especially on an chủ động tấn công shot.

Many players are great at watching the ball when they’re hitting neutral balls, but once they have a clear target – something like a cúp chuyền – they immediately pick their đầu vợt up to look at their target, instead of watching their contact. How Do I Aim Precisely, Without Watching My Target? A graphical depiction of the outcome distribution for a player with a very precise chéo sân thuận tay. The range of outcomes is quite small, compared to the size of the sân. You don’t.

Tennis isn’t a game of precision, contrary to what mainstream analysis would have you believe. Tennis is a game of probabilities and outcome distributions. In tennis, the best players aim to targets that are big enough that their shots still succeed even when they miss by a small amount. It is not the precision of your aim that matters, but rather the precision of your contact. At lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we teach you to create strokes which are lỗi tolerant.

lỗi tolerant strokes will work if you’re a little early, or a little late. They’ll work if you’re a little too close to the ball, or a little too far from it. But no cú đánh in tennis will work if you hit the khung vợt instead of the dây (not 100% true actually, lỗi tolerant strokes often go in when you hit the bottom of the khung vợt). Therefore, it is not left-right aiming or back-front aiming that demands your attention during matches, but rather clean contact.

That’s why visual tracking is so important – clean eyes create clean contact. Catching the Ball On-Time thị lực allows you to time the ball properly. As explained in The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, the final phase of chuẩn bị for any cú đánh is a waiting period before vung vợt initiation. This period may be long on a bóng chậm, or zero on a bóng nhanh, but there is a waiting period. It is a visual cue that triggers your initiation of the forward vung vợt.

thị lực determines when this waiting period is over. You track the ball over the lưới, and as it flies towards you, you watch it all the way into vung vợt initiation, and then from there all the way into contact. It is a visual cue that triggers your initiation of the forward vung vợt. You’ll have to get used to your own vung vợt speed and điểm tiếp xúc, until you know exactly what it looks like when the ball has reached the point where you have to vung vợt.

Improvisation The Fundamental Theorem of Tennis is the key that unlocks effective improvisation. With thị lực, it’s almost impossible to violate this theorem, and therefore it’s almost impossible to miss. If you’re tracking the ball over it’s entire flight path, by the time it gets to you, your brain has ample information about it’s trajectory to construct an effective vung vợt. This visual information is especially necessary to improvise shots out of novel situations.

Recall the two rules of the fundamental theorem of tennis: The vợt and the cẳng tay make a 90-135o góc The vợt contacts the ball in front of the body Mastering early contact is almost identical to mastering visual tập trung. Mastering #1 requires becoming familiar with how to manipulate your vợt in space without straightening out your cẳng tay-vợt góc.

Once you’ve developed a solid intuition for how to do that, all that’s left is to master #2, and you’ll be able to improvise with the best of them. Mastering early contact is almost identical to mastering visual tập trung. In order to always catch the ball on time, you have to see the ball into your điểm tiếp xúc, and initiate the vung vợt once the ball gets to a certain location.

No matter where you are on sân, no matter whether your feet are set or not, no matter where the ball is in relation to your body, once you see that the ball has arrived at a điểm tiếp xúc in front of you, and if you vung vợt any later than now, you’ll be late, just vung vợt. You might have to lean your torso. You might have to push your arm weirdly.

You might have to do a whole host of strange things to make the shot work, but it’s infinitely better to allow your brain to improvise those motions, and strike the ball in front of you, than it is to catch the ball late. What Should You Watch, Specifically? giao lại’s break it into a chuỗi: 1. Your Opponent’s Body Before your opponent has struck the ball, you’ll usually be able to glean more information from your opponent’s body than from the ball itself. 2.

Your Opponent’s vợt-Face As your opponent hits the ball, the góc of their vợt-face tells you where it’s going. It’s not possible to see the exact moment of impact, but generally tracking their vung vợt mẫu will give you a good guess as to the ball’s upcoming trajectory. 3. The Ball (for a long time) From your opponent’s vợt, over the lưới, as it bounces, and then up to your vợt, you should be tracking the ball.

If you’re feeling very comfortable with your game, you can try to peek at how your opponent is defending, but this is risky, and, again, should only be done if you’re feeling really “on” that day. Depending on how fast the ball is moving, you might not be able to track the ball smoothly. On a giao bóng trả bóng, for example, a good chiến lược is to use a two jump saccade method: Track the ball off your opponent’s vợt until you know where it’s going to bounce.

Jump your gaze to the predicted bounce, and hold it there until you know your điểm tiếp xúc. Jump your gaze to your điểm tiếp xúc and hold it there until your cú đánh is complete. This will take some getting used to, but holding your eyes still while a fast moving object crosses your field of thị lực helps your brain out a lot. When your brain is trying to calculate an object’s vận tốc, it must factor out any eye di chuyển in order to get an accurate result.

If your eyes are still, this extra complexity is removed. 4. Your điểm tiếp xúc, Until Your cú đánh is Complete Why watch the điểm tiếp xúc as the ball comes off your dây, too? Simple: if you watch the contact as the ball leaves your dây, it guarantees that you won’t accidentally look away too early.

By keeping your eyes in place longer than necessary on every shot, it ensures that even if you get a little antsy and look up earlier than usual, that early look won’t cause you to mishit the ball. Routinely keeping your eyes in place longer than necessary makes your thị lực lỗi tolerant.

Roger Federer tracking the ball, before the ball contacts his dây (left), and then maintaining his đầu vợt vị trí well after contact has been made (right), while executing a low trái tay bắt lưới điểm thắng, during his loss to Felix Auger-Aliassime in the Halle Open, 2021. thị lực = Being in the Zone Roger Federer is famous for keeping his eyes on his điểm tiếp xúc well after the ball is gone.

He’s also famous for being the player with the most fluid strokes, the most aesthetically pleasing game, and the best improvisation in tennis history. Is this a coincidence? I don’t think so. Being in the zone is equivalent to being hyper-focused on your thị lực. The two are one and the same. If you have a cú đánh that’s not working, or an injury weighing you down, or some other thing of the sort, that ailment will break you out of the zone by distracting you from your visual tracking.

If you’re craving the famous “flow” feeling when you play matches, and just can’t seem to find it, there’s a good chance thị lực is your answer. If you’ve found the feeling in practice, it’s probably because, in practice, you were naturally focusing on your visual tracking without too much effort. In matches, that tập trung was squeezed out by other things. Restore your visual tập trung, and you’ll restore your flow. The Serving Throw Vector The giao bóng is a throw.

The serving motion in tennis utilizes the exact same throw chain as all the throwing motions across sports. The baseball pitch, the volleyball spike, the NFL touchdown pass – all are accomplished by coordinating certain muscles in the exact same way. But how exactly do we adapt this throw chain for tennis? That’s what we’ll answer today. Up and Away Step up to the serving line, but instead of bringing your vợt, just bring a tennis ball. Hold it in your serving hand.

Now, throw it up and away from you. If you’re right handed, launch the ball such that it travels about 25 ft over the right lưới post. Why? Because the throwing motion you just used to throw the ball 25ft over the lưới post – that’s the exact throwing motion that you’ll use when serving. You might be thinking: wait, why don’t I throw the ball at the service box? Good question. You don’t “throw” your vợt level, like you would a baseball, but rather diagonally up.

When you have a vợt in your hand, the angles work differently than when you’re launching a ball off of your ngón tay. If you want giao bóng by harnessing the same biomechanical process that throws a ball, then you have to “throw” your vợt to the right of your target (for lefties, to the left of it), such that when your cẳng tay naturally rotates over at the top of your throw, the dây will hit the ball towards the box.

Further, you don’t “throw” your vợt level, like you would a baseball, but rather diagonally up. This causes the vợt to flick over the top at the end of the throw. Depending on how you time it, this will either create xoáy trên on your giao bóng, helping you keep it in, or it will slap the ball phẳng, down into the sân, also a good thing. If there were a baseball, instead of a vợt, in Roger Federer’s hand here, this throwing motion wouldn’t throw the baseball anywhere near the service box.

Instead, Roger would be throwing the ball over the green awning off to the right of the sân. The cầm vợt kiểu lục địa The cầm vợt kiểu lục địa is necessary in order to harness your throw chain when serving. It’s the cầm vợt that orients your dây towards the service box as the vợt turns over at the apex of the up and away throwing motion. During the throw, the edge of your vợt will race towards the ball.

Once your hand gets far enough away from you, it’ll hit the end of its range of motion, and your cẳng tay will naturally rotate forward. When this happens, if you’re holding the vợt in the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa, your dây will slap the ball directly into the service box. If you’re holding the vợt in a different cầm vợt, say, the cầm vợt kiểu Đông, the ball will spin off to the side. Common Issues The most counter-intuitive part of a proper giao bóng is that the hand doesn’t travel towards the target.

It makes sense that this is tricky. Here’s a non-exhaustive list of shots on which your hand basically travels towards your target: thuận tay trái tay thuận tay bắt lưới trái tay bắt lưới cắt And here’s the list of shots where your hand doesn’t travel towards your target: giao bóng bóng trên cao It’s a niche thing, tough to get the knack for.

Most players giao bóng with the cầm vợt kiểu Đông because their brain really wants to throw their hand at the service box, instead of up and away, and if you’re going to throw at the service box, you have to use Eastern for the shot to work.

The tough part about switching to a real, throw-chain-engaging giao bóng is that you need to make two changes simultaneously: Throw up and away, instead of straight at the box Use the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa If you only throw up and away, but you continue using the cầm vợt kiểu Đông, you’ll spin the ball off to your right (for righties). If you use the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa, but continue throwing your hand straight at the box, you’ll have to awkwardly contort your cổ tay and body in order to make it work.

If you do both together, it’ll work, but it takes some time to get used to. Starting Recommendations Each player’s optimal serving throw vector is a little different depending on their particular body. Here’s what I recommend as a starting point: Always pretend you’re throwing your vợt 15-20 feet over the lưới. The shorter you are, the higher over the lưới you should “aim” your throw chain.

And with respect to the left-right direction (for righties): On the đều side: Throw your vợt towards the ad side service box On the ad side: Throw your vợt towards the ad side lưới post This will give you a good starting point. It’ll help you feel, roughly, what the giao bóng feels like when the throw chain is incorporated properly.

Stop Losing Winnable Matches One of the beautiful things about learning a fundamentally sound, lỗi tolerant thuận tay, is that the speed of said thuận tay can be easily dialed up or down, without fundamentally changing the vung vợt. The cầm vợt should be loose, and the vai allowed to freely rotate at all speeds, and then, in order to vung vợt harder, the player simply drives forward with their legs harder, and twists with their hông and abs harder. That’s all there is to it.

I like to put my students through a drill I call 20/40/60/80/100. It’s a drop feed thuận tay drill where we start at 20% of their maximum speed, and once they make 5 in a row, we move onto the next speed. The goal is to explore their percentages at each speed, in order to figure out their proper trận đấu speed. The slower you hit, the more lỗi tolerant your cú đánh. Slowing down your vung vợt allows you to be less precise and still get the ball in.

The slower you hit, the more lỗi tolerant the cú đánh. At 100% speed, unless your string góc is perfect, you’ll probably hit the lưới, or hit it long, whereas at 20% speed, even if your string góc is off by 20-30o, you might still hit the ball in. It’s the same thing with being early/late. At 100% speed, if you’re late, you’re always sailing the ball rộng, whereas at 40%, you can be pretty late and still have it sneak inside the sân.

For most players, their trận đấu speed is somewhere between 50-80% of their maximum speed. It can also be different from shot to shot; some students, especially kids, might be able to play their thuận tay at 60%, but really need to play their trái tay at closer to 40%. Cautions Do NOT slow down your vung vợt by tightening up. That’s the opposite of what we’re trying to achieve – you’ll go backwards in your development as a player.

While you will still get the same lỗi tolerance benefit from a tight slow vung vợt that you get from a loose slow vung vợt, you’ll be practicing the wrong cơ học as you play matches, so your long term cú đánh development will suffer. Instead, simply dial down the explosiveness of your hông and thân xoay.

Height Over the lưới I teach my students to aim 3-4.5 feet (1-1.5m) over the lưới on forehands struck from behind the đường cuối sân, and allow them to go a little lower over the lưới on the trái tay hai tay (it’s a flatter shot in general). This allows them to miss low and still get the ball over, a necessity for succeeding in matches. Your desired string góc does change depending on how high over the lưới you’re aiming, and how hard you’re hitting.

This is something most players do subconsciously – it just feels like “aiming” high or low over the lưới. Aim high enough over the lưới that 80-90% of the shots you attempt with that target fly over the lưới. If too much of the outcome distribution of your attempt is into the lưới, you’ll miss too much to win đánh đơn matches attempting that shot.

It can be a little difficult to change your speed in real time, without warming up again, because you might need a different string góc, and it could take a few strokes for your brain to adjust. The harder you’re hitting, and the more xoáy trên you’re using, the more closed your dây should be, and vice versa. So What’s My trận đấu Speed? In đánh đơn tennis, tấn công and neutral shots that you make less than 80% of the time are basically useless.

Your trận đấu speed is the speed at which you can execute your shots at a rate of 4/5 or higher. (phòng thủ shots with a <80% success rate can be attempted, because you have so much less equity in the point when you’re attempting them.) For most players, their appropriate trận đấu speed is around 60%. When I teach this, I actually verbalize it as 9/10, because people tend to believe that their percentages are higher than actually are. For most players, their appropriate trận đấu speed is around 60%.

For a very technically sound cú đánh, it might be closer to 80%. Your attempted height over the lưới and your target on the other side of the sân also need to be adjusted to meet this 80% threshold: aim high enough over the lưới and far enough inside the line that you don’t miss more than 1/5 shots. The player on the left is aiming too close to the line: too much of the shot’s outcome distribution is out, and therefore this player won’t be competitive with this target.

The player on the right is aiming to a far better target, where more than 80% of the probability mass is in, making this shot a competitive đánh đơn shot. What if That’s Not chủ động tấn công Enough to Win? Then you’re probably going to lose, plain and simple. You have two options: 1. Play More Aggressively giao lại’s say your 80% shots are getting you blasted off the sân. You can dial it up past your usual trận đấu speed, accepting more misses, in order to keep the points neutral. Here’s the problem, though.

If you dial it up to a speed where you’re only making ¾, or worse, 2/3 forehands in the sân, then neutral really isn’t good enough. If the player who was blasting you off the sân just makes 5 balls in a row, they’re practically guaranteed to win the point. This hyper-chủ động tấn công style is often referred to as “red-lining” by tennis commentators.

Many have attempted this against players like Djokovic and Nadal, whose defense is so good that it feels like the only way to win is to go for shots you really shouldn’t. Occasionally, it works. If you’re feeling really, really on that day, you just might be able to execute at 5-10% higher level than you typically can. Usually, though, you just miss your way into a quick blowout loss. 2. Hope Their Level Drops This chiến lược is under-rated.

Every tennis player is human, and every tennis player goes through ebbs and flows over the course of a trận đấu. Even if you go out there, play at your usual trận đấu speed, and simply never give up until the end, you might find yourself in a tight trận đấu at some point. But there’s more to your opponent’s level than just their own game. Have you exhausted all of your strategic options? Do you have drop shots in your arsenal? Slices? Moon balls? giao bóng and bắt lưới? Moonball and bắt lưới?

Before you start red-lining and praying your hyper-chủ động tấn công shots catch the line, make sure to attempt a few different strategies that you can actually execute at a reasonable percentage. At the recreational level especially, many players who can blast you off the sân if you’re using one chiến lược have significant weaknesses against others. Experiment, and you might not have to red-line at all.

trận đấu Speed Accuracy Wins and Loses Matches If I took two players whose trận đấu speeds should be 60%, and forced one player to play at 80% instead, the trận đấu wouldn’t be close. The player at 80% would miss so many shots that the small lợi thế they gained from hitting harder would be completely overpowered by the increased miss rate. Here’s the dangerous thing though. giao lại’s say the 80% player didn’t know his trận đấu speed was supposed to be 60%.

He might say things like: “I just had an off day.” “I should have won.” “I just made so many errors.” And, in a way, these are true. Except that the fix is most certainly not just to hope things go better next time. The fix is to find the appropriate speed at which to play. The speed at which you can slightly mistime the ball, or miss your string góc by a few degrees, and have the ball still go in. No one’s perfect. If you only win when you play perfectly, guess what: You lose.

The Mystery of the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa Look at the two grips above. By some definitions, both can be considered the “continental” cầm vợt, and yet the two grips are very different. One example of this is that the cầm vợt on the left is a great choice for a trái tay một tay, whereas the cầm vợt on the right will hardly work for that cú đánh at all. On the trái tay một tay, the vợt needs to rotate about the cẳng tay through the hitting zone without the string góc changing while that happens.

The cầm vợt on the left allows this easily, while the cầm vợt on the right does not. Pitfalls of Imprecisely Teaching Grips Two players who are merely taught to “use the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa” for their one-handed backhands might end up with starkly different results. If the player adopts the cầm vợt on the left, the cú đánh will work as intended, and they’ll believe that the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa “works” for the trái tay một tay. Only teach a cầm vợt in the context of why that cầm vợt is useful.

If, on the other hand, the player adopts the cầm vợt on the right, they will likely find that their contact is extremely inconsistent, and they’ll have no idea as to why. The fundamental level fix for this issue is not to simply be more specific when teaching the cầm vợt. Rather, it’s to only teach a cầm vợt in the context of why that cầm vợt is useful.

For example, show the student that, if they hold the vợt in the cầm vợt on the left, it’s very easy for that vợt to rotate through contact and strike the ball.

When grips are taught this way, as functional, mechanical tools in service of correct contact, rather than as “the way you should hold the vợt,” much of the confusion disappears. “Continental” Means Many Things On this common thuận tay bắt lưới cầm vợt, the index knuckle is on bevel 2, and the knuckles are aligned 45o off parallel from the cán vợt.

In contrast to the trái tay một tay, the thuận tay bắt lưới vastly prefers the cầm vợt on the right, as it allows easier fine grained adjustment with the hand when aiming the bắt lưới, as well as more surface area contact between the ngón tay and cán vợt that can be used to support the vợt and prevent the vợt đầu vợt from moving during the strike. In both of these grips, the index knuckle is resting on bevel 2. What’s different is the orientation of the cán vợt in the hand.

The knuckles on the left cầm vợt run parallel to the cán vợt, while on the right cầm vợt they run about 45o off from the direction of the cán vợt. Therefore, the index knuckle’s resting spot is not the only reference defining your cầm vợt. Even when fixing index knuckle vị trí, it’s possible to rotate the cán vợt in your hand, leading to a starkly different alignment, and significantly different vợt di chuyển through contact.

cầm vợt Improvisation The solution to cầm vợt confusion is not to master the exact hand orientation of every cầm vợt, and ensure that you get your student to mimic the exact hand orientation of some particular cầm vợt as you coach them. Far from it. The solution is to accept a different paradigm for the role of the cầm vợt altogether. I almost wish that grips didn’t have names, as I think that would solve a lot of the unnecessary confusion surrounding them.

It is my opinion that cầm vợt in tennis should be primarily discovered by the student, rather than taught as a first principle by the coach. Fundamentally, cầm vợt is not a first principle. The first principles are contact and vợt gia tốc. On the thuận tay, for example: The vợt must strike up and through the ball. The vợt should be accelerated using the strong muscles of the legs, hông, and abs.

cầm vợt is merely a means to an end; a degree of freedom the player should tweak until they find a way to hold the vợt that allows them to achieve those fundamental goals best. Some students don’t realize that the way they orient the vợt in their hand is a usable degree of freedom. They’ll readily change their feet, they’ll change their legs, their chest, their arm, and yet, for whatever reason, it never occurs to them that they’re allowed to change how they’re holding the vợt.

Directly Coaching a cầm vợt A common version of the semi-western thuận tay cầm vợt, a cầm vợt for which it’s very easy to maintain a correct string góc through contact, and also a cầm vợt many players find naturally. I only correct cầm vợt to fix downstream problems, and when I correct a cầm vợt, it’s always with an explanation. I’ll have a student who always hits the thuận tay long vung vợt to their điểm tiếp xúc, and then freeze.

I’ll rotate the vợt in their hand to fix their string góc, and explain that if they hold the vợt in this new way, they’ll be able to use the natural vung vợt they just used, but the ball will go in.

In other contexts, like the bắt lưới, I’ll also explain cầm vợt choice in the context of function: we don’t have time for large cầm vợt switches at lưới, and so we hold the vợt in a way that allows us to quickly get the dây oriented towards the other side, no matter on which side of our body we’re forced to hit. The underlying theme here is that each cầm vợt is meant to accomplish something, some goal external and independent from the cầm vợt itself.

It’s not a critical technical component of a vung vợt like, say, engaging the lõi is. Every player, at every level should internalize that cầm vợt is a usable degree of freedom, and should feel free to alter it in the process of exploring any of their strokes. Heal Your Body In Motion You’re out on the sân and something hurts. Maybe it’s your khuỷu tay, maybe your đầu gối, maybe your heel. Don’t panic. Pain is a part of life, especially when it comes to athletics.

It is a signal, your body’s way of communicating with you about what it can and can’t cán vợt. Pain is how your body protects itself. This guide will transform your understanding of pain and injury, and it will dispel the common disposition towards pain of anxiety and brokenness, replacing it with one of confidence and control. We’ll be specifically addressing the most common kind of pain felt by athletes – pain from minor soft tissue injury.

Nearly all common tennis injuries are soft tissue injuries – đầu gối pain, cổ tay pain, cẳng tay pain, low back pain, tennis khuỷu tay, plantar fasciitis – all of these ailments are, at their lõi, a soft tissue system that has been over-stressed. From a 5000 foot view, rehabbing a soft tissue injury is very simple. You must do two things: Figure out what’s causing the injury, and stop doing that.

Take the injured body part through it’s natural range of motion under a light nạp The rest of this guide will explain this seemingly simple process in greater detail. Prevent Further Injury The first step of rehabbing an injury isn’t trying to get the injured body part to repair itself, but rather to prevent further injury to that same body part. You must discover the root cause of a chronic injury, and then fix it.

This is mostly trivial for acute injuries: injuries where the body part went from totally uninjured to injured all at once. You can occasionally take steps to avoid these – wear lower heeled shoes if you’re an ankle roller, for example – but for the most part, acute injuries are simply random. For chronic injuries, on the other hand, prevention is squarely within your control. Many, many, many people forget that prevention is the first step towards rehab.

You must discover the root cause of the chronic injury, and then fix it, so that you are not constantly re-injuring that same body part while you’re trying to rehab it. Many players, instead of investigating the cause of their chronic injury, immediately start medicating with things like non-steroidal anti inflammatory drugs ice rest various braces and taping schemes If you do this, your injury is not going to heal.

Until you implement some sort of technical or conditioning fix, you are re-injuring whatever body part is hurting every time you step out on the sân. The reason your particular soft-tissue system got over stressed in the first place is that there is some part of your tennis game that is too stressful for that system to trở lại vị trí from. Until that changes, the injury won’t heal.

The Nature of Injury During everyday existence, and especially during sports, all tendons, muscles, and ligaments are constantly undergoing a stress-phục hồi vị trí-adaptation cycle. During a tennis session, more stress is put on these systems than during everyday life, but not so much so that the body can’t trở lại vị trí it. When played with proper kỹ thuật, tennis constitutes a very healthy level of stress.

The body will trở lại vị trí from that stress before your next session, and that phục hồi vị trí will đường bóng further adaptation of the stressed systems, making you into a fitter tennis player the next time you step out on the sân. Insofar as the body can fully trở lại vị trí from your daily activities, the body is considered uninjured. Injuries occur when too much stress is placed on the body, and the body can no longer trở lại vị trí from that stress.

Therefore, the first step in diagnosing and treating any injury is to make sure you know why too much stress was placed on your body, so that you can prevent that excess stress in the future. The manner by which you were injured greatly influences the manner by which you will heal. Acute Injuries giao lại’s imagine you’ve accidentally banged your khuỷu tay on the door. The day after the accident, you step out on the tennis sân, and as you hit your first few balls, you notice your khuỷu tay hurts.

The injury you’re feeling here is an acute injury, caused by the acute stress of banging your khuỷu tay on the door. In this situation, your tennis game itself is not responsible for creating the excess stress that led to injury. Tennis is certainly more stressful than everyday life, which is why you’re feeling pain during tennis that you didn’t feel walking around, but tennis is likely unstressful enough that your khuỷu tay can trở lại vị trí from its injury even while continuing to play.

When uninjured, your khuỷu tay can easily trở lại vị trí from the stress of your tennis game, which is why your khuỷu tay never hurt before you banged it. Due to that, it’s likely that, even when slightly injured, your khuỷu tay can both cán vợt the light stress of the game and still have enough phục hồi vị trí resources left over to slowly heal the khuỷu tay’s injury. Occasionally, though, that won’t be the case, and you’ll have to rest your acute injury.

Sometimes, even though your khuỷu tay was able to trở lại vị trí from the stress of tennis completely in its uninjured state, in its injured state, that stress is too much – your body doesn’t have enough resources to both trở lại vị trí from the stress of tennis and make progress towards healing the injury. In this case, intensity has to be dialed down (even to the point of complete rest).

The proper level of activity is the level at which the injured body part is capable of fully recovering from the stress you put it under, and then further capable of healing itself a little bit each day. Chronic Injuries giao lại’s now imagine that you’ve been playing tennis consistently for the past few weeks, and every session your khuỷu tay has hurt more and more, and now it’s finally to the point where it hurts every time you hit the ball.

There is something about your tennis game itself which is too stressful for your khuỷu tay to trở lại vị trí from. This is a chronic injury. There is something about your tennis game itself which is too stressful for your khuỷu tay to trở lại vị trí from. Medicating with rest, ice, and NSAIDs alone will not work, because they do not fix the underlying issue: your tennis is too stressful for your khuỷu tay.

In this situation, you are injuring your khuỷu tay while playing tennis, and therefore you’re never going to be able to rehab that khuỷu tay while playing tennis the same way you have been, because you are re-injuring it constantly as you play. kỹ thuật Fixes The hardest part of injury management is discovering, and subsequently fixing, whatever technical flaw is causing the excess stress on your injured body part.

Don’t be discouraged by this process, which can be quite frustrating – it is possible to play tennis pain free. The human body can move around the sân and produce all the shots necessary for the game without putting so much stress on itself that it breaks down. You want your arm to hurt when you hit the ball wrong. In tennis especially, pain is a very good kỹ thuật coach.

For almost all hand, cổ tay, cẳng tay, and khuỷu tay injuries, your body will give you real time feedback as to whether or not you’ve struck the ball properly. Do not play tennis on pain killers. Pain is a signal that you desperately need in order to stay healthy as a tennis player. You want your arm to hurt when you hit the ball wrong. That’s what allows your brain to self correct.

If you play on pain killers, understand that you’re putting a lot more stress on your body than you otherwise would, because you’re allowing yourself to ignore your body’s pain signals. Compete in that state very infrequently, and only when you’ve made a conscious decision that it’s worth it. Pain as Your Guide During Healing Pain will tell you whether or not any particular activity is too stressful, and is therefore detrimental to the healing process.

After any activity session with an injured body part, answer these questions: Did it hurt more and more as I used it? Did it hurt more the day after use than it did the day before? If the answer to either question, specifically the second question, is “yes,” then said activity session constituted too much stress for your injured body part. You need to reduce the nạp on that body part until it’s further healed. Notice that does it hurt while I’m playing? is not one of the questions.

That’s because it isn’t super relevant. If you have, say, tennis khuỷu tay, then it’s probably going to hurt on most shots, even when they’re struck correctly. Your body is communicating to you that it’s injured, and you’ve gotten the message. After that point, it is not the pain itself that matters, but the derivative of the pain.

Pain that is getting better is just another way of saying “an injury that’s healing,” whereas pain that is getting worse is another way of saying “injury in progress.” Activities that make your pain worse and worse should be halted immediately. Play Through Your Injuries Rest is rarely a good option for injury rehab. Unless your pain is getting worse as you play, or has been static for at least a week, you should play through your injuries.

The body is self healing, and it heals far better when in motion than when at rest. Your body uses the electrical signals from the muscle contractions themselves in order to orient itself as it rebuilds your soft tissue. In general, injured body parts taken through their range of motion under a light nạp heal better, faster, and more functionally.

In general, injured body parts taken through their range of motion under a light nạp heal better, faster, and are more functional after they heal, than injured body parts that are immobilized. This fact is well known among coaches and the better physical therapists out there, but I’ll still start you down the research rabbit hole here, in case you’d like to explore the science for yourself.

Many studies have been performed on rats looking at the beneficial effects of đang nạp and motion on soft tissue phục hồi vị trí, typically utilizing the Achilles tendon. Immobilized and deloaded tendons healed worse, and scarred more, while mobile and loaded tendons routinely showed better outcomes.

In one study, for example, researchers found that đang nạp is beneficial no matter when in the healing process it happens, debunking the common belief that injuries should be rested and immobilized initially, and only loaded after inflammation dies down. The results showed that just four đang nạp episodes increased the strength of the healing tendon. This was evident irrespective of the time point when đang nạp was applied (early or late).

In another, functional tendon gene expression was higher in rats who had their torn tendons loaded, than in ones who had them immobilized: In the late phase of healing, tendon-specific genes (scleraxis and tenomodulin) were upregulated with đang nạp, and the healing tissue might to some extent represent a regenerate rather than a scar.

This is why it’s so common for tennis players to develop a chronic injury, take two weeks off, have the pain go away, and then resume playing only to find the pain immediately resumes. The tissue was never restored to its previous functional state, it merely scarred, and therefore still can’t cán vợt the stress of the sport. Other Forms of Rehab Most injuries will resolve themselves if you fix your kỹ thuật and then continue to play.

For the ones that don’t, things like light exercises with resistance bands and massages have their place as well. These techniques apply a very light stress to the area, in an attempt to stimulate phục hồi vị trí. For example, massage is a very good therapy for plantar fasciitis – injury of the plantar fascia, the fascia running along the heel and arch of the foot.

The massage creates a light stress on the soft tissue, and the resulting phục hồi vị trí and adaptation response from the body to address that stress results in a lưới healing and strengthening effect on the entire area. Band work for an khuỷu tay or cổ tay issue follows a similar mẫu.

You apply a light stress on the injured body part by taking it through its natural range of motion under a light resistance, and that light stress prompts a healing response which creates a lưới beneficial effect for the injury. As I said before, pain is your guide. This kind of rehab work is subordinate to the same two pain questions listed above. The day after your rehab work, your injury should feel better, not worse. Chill Out Your body probably isn’t broken.

Injuries are fixable, and you can fix them without putting your entire life on hold. Everything in your body is designed to heal while in motion. Prehistoric man couldn’t rest his sprained ankle for two weeks – he’d starve. Prehistoric man couldn’t rest his sprained ankle for two weeks – he’d starve. So don’t worry about a little pain, just make sure your injuries aren’t getting worse. If they are, you have to be an engineer.

It’s up to you to diagnose the root cause and fix it, and after you do, the injury will start healing. In the words of Lupe Fiasco in Battle Scars: “Never giao lại a wound ruin me.” The only truly difficult thing stopping you from rehabbing your injury is a lack biomechanical knowledge (and maybe eventually age). If you’ve got a remarkably sticky problem, research, research research.

Even if it takes 3 months, and you end up with an entirely new understanding of the human foot and modern footwear, eventually you’ll figure it out. Take control over your own body, and use it while it’s injured. If you do, you’ll discover the remarkably phenomenal powers of regeneration the human body naturally possesses, and if you’ve been listening to mainstream doctors your entire life, maybe for the first time.

Leylah Fernandez – The Roger Federer of the WTA Sensational 18-year-old Leylah Fernandez stands at 5’ 6’’ 104 lbs (168 cm 47 kg). Her opponent in the 4th round of the 2021 US Open was former grand slam champion Angelique Kerber, standing at 5’ 8’’ 150 lbs (173 cm 68 kg).

If I told you that the svelte 18-year-old had gotten the better of her bigger, stronger, more experienced counterpart, you’d probably imagine a trận đấu where Leylah moved quickly, played solid defense, put balls back in play, and outlasted her veteran opponent. But that isn’t what happened at all. The teenager hit Angelique Kerber off the sân on Sunday. How is that possible, given that Angelique Kerber was the bigger, taller, stronger, and more experienced player?

What makes Leylah Fernandez so good? We’ll start with the skill that underpins the rest of Leylah’s game: her extremely lỗi tolerant thuận tay (though her di chuyển and dự đoán are also world class). After that, we’ll discuss the chiến thuật she uses to routinely hit bigger, taller, stronger women off the sân, which are only possible because her thuận tay is so lỗi tolerant: Leylah takes the ball extremely early. Leylah changes direction and aims to big targets.

Leylah finishes her points with swinging volleys. Leylah’s lỗi Tolerant thuận tay As Leylah completes her chuẩn bị, her entire hitting-arm apparatus is still well on the hitting side of her chest. Since she uses primarily lõi xoay to accelerate the vợt, her cú đánh doesn’t require much đưa vợt ra sau at all. In order to win in pro tennis, you need at least one weapon. Leylah’s is her thuận tay.

She has flawlessly implemented all of the lõi principles we espouse here so frequently at lỗi Tolerant Tennis. First, Leylah uses a very abbreviated “đưa vợt ra sau.” Her arm chuẩn bị constitutes nothing more than the necessary motion in order to prepare her hand for the forward explosion. She turns her hông and chest away from her target She extends her khuỷu tay and she lowers her hand. That’s it. No extra looping, no extra motion. Next, Leylah drives with her hông, followed by her abs.

She doesn’t attempt to accelerate the vợt with the weak muscles of her arm, cẳng tay, or cổ tay, and instead drives forward with the strongest muscles in her (tiny) body. This is how she hits so hard, despite only weighting 104 lbs (47 kg). Leylah and Roger’s Unit vung vợt Despite being a talented lefty capable of utilizing heavy xoáy trên, Leylah Fernandez employs a thuận tay kỹ thuật that is actually far more similar to Roger Federer’s than it is to Rafael Nadal’s.

Leylah uses a very compact vung vợt, with less arm di chuyển during the đưa vợt ra sau, and therefore less arm roi vung during the forward vung vợt, than Rafa does. Both her and Roger’s compact forehands exemplify the “xoay đơn vị, Unit vung vợt” idea that’s so critical to an effective, lỗi tolerant cú đánh.

From The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay: Ultimately, your goal during abdominal xoay on the forward vung vợt is to learn what it feels like to rotate your entire upper body – your chest and both arms – forward together as a single unit, just like you rotated them backward as a single unit during the xoay đơn vị. This xoay will be explosive, stable, and effective. You won’t have arms flailing this way and that, and the xoay will be driven by the strong, stable abdominal muscles in your lõi.

It’s a xoay that can be performed extremely explosively and extremely quickly without injury, unlike, say, pulling the vợt forward using the hand and arm. The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. “xoay đơn vị, Unit vung vợt.” Page 91. In general, the more stable and compact a player’s execution of the unit vung vợt, the earlier they’re able to take the ball without missing.

Roger and Leylah are able to play the hyper-chủ động tấn công style they play only because their kỹ thuật is such that they can create consistent, correct contact out of very novel positions. Since all they require to produce their cú đánh is a quick, compact, stable lõi xoay, Roger and Leylah can improvise out of positions out of which other players simply can’t.

Leylah’s lỗi Tolerant Contact By contact, Leylah’s hông and torso have both fully unwound towards the target, and the vợt has naturally flicked out in front of her, through the ball, as a result. This alignment provides her maximum freedom of adjustment through the hitting zone. Leylah is essentially square to her target by contact. At this point, she’s fully unwound both her hông and her abs, and all of that năng lượng has been transferred into the vợt.

She’s achieved the perfect lỗi tolerant contact vị trí: the vị trí depicted on the left, in which she has ample freedom for adjustment. If she were to accidentally set up too close to this ball, she could adjust by pulling her arm in toward her chest, and she’d hit roughly the same shot. If she’d swung too early, she could lean her torso forward, and maintain roughly the same arm-vợt-string orientation. Probably most importantly, this cú đánh works even when she’s a little late.

She still accelerates the vợt with her lõi, and she still relaxes her hand, but if this ball were a few inches farther back as she’s hitting it, she’d simply theo sau over her đầu vợt instead of in front of her, and in doing so create roughly the same vợt path through contact as on a perfectly timed shot. Leylah’s Deadly chiến thuật The lỗi tolerance of Leylah’s thuận tay allows her to play the game in a fundamentally more chủ động tấn công way than players with lesser strokes are able to.

Because she’s able to strike the ball properly even when things don’t go perfectly, she can attempt shots that, for most players, are too difficult. Specifically, she can take the ball far earlier than other players, she can change the ball’s direction consistently, and she can sprint to lưới and hit swinging volleys to kết thúc her points. When a typical player tries to play like Leylah plays, they miss too much, because the shots that Leylah attempts are almost impossible to execute perfectly.

Leylah, on the other hand, can use those shots in matches, because her cú đánh is designed in such a way as to succeed even when executed imperfectly. We’ve explained the cơ học that create this lỗi tolerance above. Now we’re going to explain the tactical ways by which Leylah exploits it. 1. Leylah takes the ball extremely early. How can a 105 lb girl hit winners against some of the fastest, most athletic women in the world?

Answer: she takes the ball so early that they don’t have time to phản ứng. What matters on a tennis sân is the time between the moment your shot leaves your vợt, and the moment your opponent must hit it back. You can decrease this time by either hitting harder, or by reducing the distance between you and your opponent when you strike the ball. You’ll rarely contact a ball you take early exactly how you expected to. Hitting early is extremely difficult.

The ball is moving faster as you strike it, decreasing the size of your timing window. You’re hitting the ball soon after it bounces, giving you far less time to phản ứng to that bounce. Often, the ball is rising, meaning you need a different string góc to make the shot work. You have less time to track the ball, less time to move, less time to prepare, etc. The result is that you’ll rarely contact a ball you take early exactly how you expected to.

In order to succeed when taking the ball early, your cú đánh must be lỗi tolerant. Leylah’s is, and due to that she can take the ball early five times in a row without missing, despite the fact that each of those attempts probably included some small mistake in timing or chuẩn bị. 2. Leylah changes direction and hits to big targets. Another very difficult task is to change the direction of the ball. Leylah does this constantly.

Again, it’s very difficult to space this shot properly, and it’s very difficult to time this shot properly. Because Leylah’s thuận tay is lỗi tolerant, she can attempt these shots without missing. Very importantly, she doesn’t try to hit winners right away when she changes direction. Instead, she aims to big targets, high over the lưới.

Actually, during the first set against Angelique Kerber (which she lost), and parts of the second set where she lost giao bóng, Leylah was not aiming to good targets, and the result was an unsustainable miss rate. Once she started aiming farther from the lines and higher over the lưới, she stopped randomly missing out of neutral and actually ended up hitting more winners, because she wasn’t unnecessarily knocking herself out of so many points right at the start.

Leylah takes the ball so early and disguises the direction of her shots so well that there’s no need for her to lực the issue out of neutral. Eventually, she’ll catch her opponent leaning the wrong way on one of her neutral balls, and she’ll get a super weak reply. Which brings us to her final primary chiến thuật… 3. Leylah finishes her points with swinging volleys. If you can’t hit a swinging bắt lưới, there are points that you simply cannot win.

Your opponent can throw up sâu lobs over the giữa as phòng thủ shots, and your only option is to take the point back to neutral. On the other hand, if you can hit a swinging bắt lưới, these points are all but over – if you execute your shot, you’re going to win the point. Without a fundamentally sound, lỗi tolerant thuận tay, swinging volleys are all but impossible. Just like the early thuận tay or the change-of-direction thuận tay, the swinging bắt lưới thuận tay is extremely difficult.

You yourself are moving, likely sprinting forward, and the ball is moving extremely quickly, since it hasn’t even bounced yet. Further, the ball likely has far more vertical vận tốc than it does on a typical đường cuối sân shot. Without a fundamentally sound, lỗi tolerant thuận tay, swinging volleys are all but impossible. Even at the tour level, only a few players (like Roger Federer and Leylah Fernandez, to name a few) have really integrated them as a lõi part of their game.

The instant Leylah realizes that one of her well disguised groundstrokes has gotten her opponent on the phòng thủ, she’s looking to tấn công. Often, it’s clear that the opponent’s only option is a phòng thủ, floaty shot. When this is the case, Leylah sprints to lưới. While that phòng thủ shot is in the air, Leylah continues sprinting. She gets as far in as she can and then takes a very compact thuận tay vung vợt at the ball while it’s still in mid-air.

The result is that her opponent is out of vị trí, the sân is open, and Leylah gets to hit the ball from so close to her opponent that they have barely any time to phản ứng. Leylah Fernandez and the Modern game The younger generation of WTA players are bringing the hyper-modern thuận tay to the women’s game like never before.

Players like Layla Fernandez, Iga Swiatek, Jen Brady, and ultimate champion Emma Raducanu all utilize primarily explosive lõi xoay to accelerate their forehands, and as a result, these girls can do things consistently that their older, more traditional counterparts simply can’t do. It’s extremely fun to watch, and I’m excited to see these girls dominate for years to come.

Battle of the Girls who tấn công We’ve talked before about how, in đánh đơn tennis, shots that you can execute at less than 80% are essentially useless – you’ll lose too many points from missing those shots for whatever small tấn công lợi thế you’d gain to be worthwhile. In this vein, when you watch the 2021 US Open final between Leylah Fernandez and Emma Raducanu today, watch for this important question: Who can transition from neutral to offense using high percentage shots?

When Leylah Fernandez beat Aryna Sabalenka in the semi-finals, both players were extremely good at this, which is why the trận đấu was so close. Aryna, with her massive thuận tay, was able to routinely transition out of neutral and into offense without missing. If she’d executed better, who knows, she might have pulled it off. Here’s what’ll go into it today. Can Leylah Change Direction?

Leylah’s game relies on taking the ball early and frequently changing its direction, eventually wrong-footing her opponent without the need to attempt a low percentage shot. Against Angie Kerber and Elina Svitolina, this worked wonderfully. Against Aryna… not quite as much.

Leylah could rarely get to lưới because Aryna hit the ball so hard and so sâu that changing the direction of the ball was really, really difficult, and the result was that Leylah had to aim to bigger targets in order to not miss, and only wrong footed Aryna a few times. Luckily for Leylah, her offense was just barely good enough that Aryna had to be slightly more chủ động tấn công than she was capable of.

When Aryna would take her foot off the gas even a little, in an effort to make slightly less errors, Leylah was there, taking the ball early and getting ahead in the points. giao lại’s see if Emma can do the same thing. Can she keep the ball sâu enough, or far enough from Leylah, that Leylah can’t consistently change direction and get to offense (or even hit winners) without having to be super chủ động tấn công? How Many Swinging Volleys Does Leylah Hit?

If Leylah’s hitting swinging volleys, it means she’s getting Emma mất thăng bằng and sprinting to lưới. Against Aryna, again, this rarely happened. Against Angie and Elina, it happened all the time. Emma can phòng thủ against the swinging bắt lưới in one of two ways. First, she can do what Aryna did – keep the ball sâu enough and hit hard enough that Leylah can’t easily get her into a phòng thủ vị trí.

But there’s a second way I think Emma could beat this, and that’s by hitting better phòng thủ shots than her previous counterparts were able to. Emma’s di chuyển and thuận tay kỹ thuật are top notch, specifically when she finishes over her đầu vợt. She has a loose cổ tay and an extremely explosive lõi, which are both necessary for generating tốc độ đầu vợt out of compromised positions.

She might be able to hit higher quality phòng thủ shots when on the run than her previous opponents, forcing Leylah to play the ball at lưới while stretched out rộng, or at her shoelaces, or well over her đầu vợt, instead of comfortably at her vai. Can Emma lực Leylah to lỗi giao bóng kép? Emma’s thuận tay trả bóng is awesome. One of the beautiful things about developing such a lỗi tolerant, fundamentally sound thuận tay like Emma has is that you can easily change the length of the vung vợt.

When Emma returns aggressively, she doesn’t take a huge vung vợt. Instead, she moves her body weight forward, and performs a quick, compact turn with her hông and abs, often following through by running to lưới. Against Aryna, Leylah went for some pretty big first and second serves at times, in order to get herself out of trouble. Will Emma be able to lực her to do the same? Emma likes to run around slow second serves and crack them on the thuận tay.

It’s possible that Leylah won’t be able to consistently keep the point neutral with her second giao bóng. Can Emma Get to Offense First? For all of Leylah’s previous opponents, if they wanted to get to offense first, they had to miss a lot to do it. With Emma, that might not be the case. Emma actually plays quite similarly to Leylah – she stands close to the đường cuối sân, takes the ball early, and comes to lưới to kết thúc her points. This means that Emma plays high percentage offense.

Her willingness to kết thúc at lưới means that she doesn’t need to go for as much on the first ball to grab the lợi thế. There’s often a dance in these kinds of matches – how good does my cú tiếp cận have to be? First you might start super high percentage, and then get passed a lot. Next you crank it up a bit, and now you’re not getting passed anymore, but you’re missing too many approach shots. Since both of these girls play great, fundamentally sound offense, both will be playing this game.

Since both have extremely lỗi tolerant groundstrokes, I expect both will pass well out of most situations, but only time will tell how the dance unfolds. The Future of the WTA This trận đấu, the 2021 US Open final between two exciting teenagers, represents the future of the WTA. Two girls with well developed, modern, lỗi tolerant strokes, who both stand in close, take the ball early, play high percentage offense, and kết thúc their points at lưới.

As sad as it is, we all know the era of the big 3 in men’s tennis can’t last forever, but with electric players like Leylah Fernandez and Leylah Raducanu dominating the women’s tour, maybe the girls can step in and fill some of that eventual hole in our hearts. Emma Raducanu’s Elite, Explosive thuận tay There are many ways to hit the rộng thuận tay, but Emma Raducanu’s way is the best way.

At lỗi Tolerant Tennis, we believe in finding the optimal cơ sinh học for a particular vung vợt, and then adjusting our chuẩn bị so that we can utilize those same cơ học out of a variety of situations. The rộng thuận tay is one of such situations. At contact, Emma Raducanu’s hông and chest have been fully unwound, and are now roughly facing her target.

This alignment indicates that shes’ successfully accelerated her vợt using the strong muscles of her lõi, and it provides her with ample freedom of adjustment during the vung vợt. The biomechanical alignment in question here, the alignment that Emma takes lợi thế of on her elite rộng thuận tay, is having both the hông and the chest square to the target on contact.

The modern thuận tay is performed by turning the hông and the chest away from the ball during chuẩn bị, and then explosively unwinding that turn during the forward vung vợt. xoay of the hông and chest powers the vợt. The arm simply acts as a lever, and the cổ tay acts as a hinge, the function of both being to chuyển the lực generated by the lõi into the vợt. Further, the hông-and-chest-square body vị trí is the most flexible vị trí for last minute adjustment.

This is further explained in The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay: When the hông and chest are correctly square to the target at contact, the hand, and thereby the vợt, is very free. It can move up and down easily, and it can move side to side easily. This allows for a rộng range of contact points at which correct, lỗi tolerant contact can be made.

Further, because the vợt is so free in this vị trí, your brain can use the visual information it gets while tracking the ball to subconsciously adjust your vung vợt in real time. As we discussed earlier, we often see players on the tour suddenly and successfully adjust to bad bounces – the reason they can do so is that they’re using a body orientation which provides enough freedom for said adjustment. The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. “Square to The Target at Contact.” Page 29-30.

Emma’s Elite rộng thuận tay On the rộng thuận tay, Emma utilizes the exact same cơ sinh học that she does on a routine thuận tay. This allows her to get in and out of her thuận tay góc quickly, and while doing so produce a shot of almost identical quality to what she can on a trivial ball. Emma Raducanu flawlessly striking a chéo sân thuận tay while moving to the thuận tay góc during her first round trận đấu against Stefanie Vogele at the 2021 US Open.

As Emma runs into the thuận tay góc, her goal isn’t merely to get to the ball, but to roi vung her body around as she gets there. As early as the second khung vợt above, you can see Emma đang nạp her front leg. Even while moving to her right, Emma drives off of this front leg, thereby rotating her body back to her left as she arrives at the ball. This results in the ideal contact vị trí we seen in the last khung vợt: Emma’s hông and chest are aligned directly at her chéo sân target.

Emma’s explosive lower xoay allows her to recruit the legs and hông into her rộng thuận tay vung vợt. This is especially important in girls tennis – whereas the typical male athlete’s natural upper body strength might giao lại them get away with using solely their abdominal, that’s much more difficult for a female player. By fully recruiting her leg musculature in addition to her abs, Emma generates the same heavy, fast, sâu balls out of her thuận tay góc as she does from the giữa of the sân.

A Less Optimized cú đánh Elina Svitolina doesn’t rotate as early, or as much as Emma Raducanu when playing a thuận tay from the thuận tay góc, and as such, she leaves both power and lỗi tolerance on the table. To further illustrate the point, we can contrast Emma’s kỹ thuật to that of Elina Svitolina (Leylah Ferndandez’s quarter-final opponent).

Despite the screaming, Elina is actually leaving a lot of power on the table when she strikes a rộng thuận tay, because she leaves both her hông and her chest under-rotated. Due to that, she gets far less contribution from both her legs and her abs than Emma does. It’ll also be more difficult for Elina to execute last minute improvisation from this contact vị trí. Since her hông and chest aren’t square with where she’s aiming, she’s sacrificing some of her lỗi tolerance.

On this particular shot, Elina’s contact is earlier than her hông-chest would indicate, meaning that if she were to mistime this ball by striking it too early, she’d probably miss. Since Emma strikes the ball right as her hông and chest are square to her to her target, even if she strikes the ball too early, her dây will likely still be at the correct góc, and her shot will go in.

rộng thuận tay vs Very rộng thuận tay Many players don’t attempt Emma’s full body xoay on their rộng thuận tay, and are instead content to leave their lõi under-rotated. This severely dampens both their cú đánh’s lỗi tolerance and its power. I suspect this is because they see great players, like Roger Federer, often leave their hông closed while striking rộng forehands, not realizing that these players only leave their hông closed on very widest of those shots.

Leaving the hông closed is only necessary when you are still on the dead run while hitting the ball. Roger Federer leaves his hông closed by necessity (left), because he’s still on the dead run while striking the ball. The abs still unwind as normal. Typically, when pulled out rộng (but not quite as rộng), Roger Federer whips both his hông and chest around (right), just like he does on a typical thuận tay, creating a more biomechanically efficient vung vợt.

Even in cases where the hông must remain closed, the chest should still unwind as it normally does. The thuận tay is a chuỗi động học. If you’re forced to forgo the hông’ involvement in that chain, it doesn’t mean you have to forgo the rest. Just engage as much as you can, and you’ll probably produce a pretty decent shot. Emma’s cú đánh Naturally Initiates Her phục hồi vị trí After striking a rộng thuận tay, Emma Raducanu’s body is perfectly aligned to sprint back to the giữa of the sân.

Check out Emma Raducanu’s phục hồi vị trí vị trí immediately after striking a rộng thuận tay. Her hông are oriented towards the giữa of the sân. This is precisely the lower body alignment we want if our next action is sprinting back towards the giữa. By rotating into the rộng thuận tay as she strikes it, Emma not only strikes a better ball, but also ends in a lower body alignment that is perfect for recovering.

Most players (like Elina Svitolina, who we discussed above) don’t reach this vị trí during their cú đánh theo sau, because the cú đánh didn’t include enough xoay to get here. So not only does Emma’s well executed xoay increase both her cú đánh’s power and its lỗi tolerance, but it also gives her a 2-3 step lợi thế when recovering after the shot.

Develop Your Coaching Immune System Due to modern information technology, you have literally thousands of hours of tennis coaching at your fingertips, at all times. Go on YouTube and search for something as simple as “tennis giao bóng,” and you can easily spend five hours watching video after video. Good coaching is great.

It can help you blast through a plateau, giving you an insight you hadn’t thought of before, and when you start experimenting with that new idea, you quickly realize it’s efficacy and begin training it into your game immediately. But what about bad coaching? Bad coaching has the potential to harm your tennis game, but only if you giao lại it. The difficult part of exploring the world of tennis coaching is actually differentiating the good from the bad. How do you separate the wheat from the chaff?

Isolate the signal in all the noise? In order to step out into the wild west of online tennis coaching and survive, you need to develop a strong, well calibrated coaching immune system.

Coaching Immunity in Kids I’ve observed many, many young kids that end up with effective, efficient, fundamentally sound strokes, despite receiving all the sorts of the bad coaching I’ve discussed at length on this website. (Forcibly turning the vợt, consciously pointing the butt cap, etc.) How is that possible? Those kids simply ignored the bad advice.

Certain kids, due to their personalities, intuitively understood that their primary criterion for evaluation was how they themselves felt about their strokes. The coach’s advice, by contrast, was a secondary consideration; it was subordinate to their own feeling out on the sân, and it was therefore only considered in service to that feeling. When a student with this attitude receives bad advice, they try it for a few shots, feel like it doesn’t work, and then abandon it.

The 14-year-old girl whose story I told in The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay had a very different experience. Despite the fact that her cổ tay hurt on every thuận tay, she was still obeying all the volitional-vợt-xoay, cổ tay-flick tropes that her coaches spouted at her. Unlike a kid with a well developed coaching immune system, this girl’s personality wasn’t such that she was willing to discard the coach’s advice, even though it didn’t work.

Bad Advice as a Pathogen Bad tennis advice is like a pathogen. Without an immune system to tấn công and kill it, bad advice will infect and damage your tennis game. Many kids have a naturally strong coaching immune system. Their natural attitude is that they have the authority to determine whether or not their strokes are working, and this orientation grants them protection against pathogenic advice. They are bold enough to experiment with their own bodies.

They internalize the coach’s words as mere experimentation suggestions, not as gospel. They internalize the coach’s words as mere experimentation suggestions, not as gospel. This immune system quickly kills anything like the “roll your vợt over the ball” pathogen, and their thuận tay and cổ tay remain unharmed. It doesn’t matter if they’re taught to “turn sideways,” without ever being taught to unwind that turn during the forward vung vợt.

Their experimentation will quickly show them that their cú đánh works far better when they unwind their hông as they vung vợt, and so they do that. Their immune system then protects this insight – despite the fact that the coach is constantly repeating “turn sideways,” they quickly discard any thought that they need to stay sideways, My 14-year-old student didn’t have a well developed immune system.

Her natural inclination was that the coach is the authority, and the coach must be correct, and the fact that her cổ tay hurt on every thuận tay was something wrong with her, rather than something wrong with the coach. Therefore, there was no immune response, and the pathological advice damaged her. What Constitutes the Immune Response? When you get out on the sân, you have the authority to decide what works, and what doesn’t.

You’ve got to trust the signals your own body sends, both positive and negative. Listen to them, and you’ll be able to take control of your own tennis. Pain If anything hurts, it’s almost definitely wrong. If you’re on your own, abandon it, and if you’re with your coach, tell him/her immediately that it hurts, and they’ll likely have an adjustment for you. More Results, Equal Effort Normally, if you try to hit harder, you hit harder, and if you try to hit softer, you hit softer.

But sometimes, you’ll get a piece of advice that allows you to hit harder without putting in more effort. That’s gold – keep doing that. Further, it’s typically the case that in order to miss less, you’ll need to hit softer. Again, sometimes you’ll get advice (perhaps on proper visual tracking), that will allow you to miss less without hitting softer.

These kinds of signals, whereby you achieve better results without putting in more effort, strongly indicate you’ve implemented something worthwhile into your game. Comfort Level Does Roger Federer look comfortable out there as he slices and dices his opponent? How about Iga Swiatek when she rips a thẳng biên thuận tay điểm thắng? Leylah Fernandez as she redirects the ball and closes the lưới? Emma Raducanu as she effortlessly floats in and out of the thuận tay góc? Of course they do.

Tennis at the highest level flows naturally. If you’re trying to follow a particular piece of advice, and it’s uncomfortable, chances are it’s wrong for your body. Usually, this happens when advice isn’t properly derived from first principles, but rather from lower level details. A classic example – teaching the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa on the giao bóng without simultaneously teaching the proper hand path. These two technical adjustments go hand-in-hand; neither functions without the other.

Unless your student innovates the correct vung vợt path on their own (and many do, especially natural throwers), the cầm vợt kiểu lục địa will feel absolutely awful, and they’d be better off just using eastern. Learning = Experimentation Tennis is a game that, ultimately, you must teach yourself. Think of coaches as mere guides along your own self learning journey, a journey fueled by your own experimentation.

I don’t just want my students to become better players; I want them to become better coaches for themselves. Many people internalize a tennis lesson as “learning how to play tennis from the tennis coach,” but that isn’t quite right. Unless you’re a complete beginner, a tennis lesson should be used in order to answer a question. Everyone reaches roadblocks in their experimentation, and a tennis coach can help you blast through them.

Even when your practice is going well, you might still want advice on what to explore next in order to make the biggest or fastest improvement to your game. In any of these situations, the primary driver of your progress is you. You, experimenting with your own body, figuring out how to orient it to produce the results you want. Insofar as coaching assists in this process, it is allowed to integrate into it, and insofar as it harms it, it is discarded.

A Complete Approach I always stress the necessity of experimentation to my students. I implore them not to take my word as gospel, but instead to experiment with what I’m saying, to pay attention to how their body feels as they try to integrate it, and to take note of the results. I don’t want a student to get out on the sân and think “oh, what was it that coach said?” Not at first, at least.

Instead, I want them to go, “wait, I know it felt different in the lesson,” and then try to remember what it was that clicked for them which made it feel like that. Ultimately, I don’t just want my students to become better players; I want them to become better coaches for themselves, and in order to do that, they’ll need a strong immune system. Your Brain Will Trick You (Into Missing) Sometimes, it’s amazing now “lucky” Novak Djokovic gets.

He hits line after line without missing, appearing to aim to targets not even 6-inches rộng, and yet never hitting the ball out. But what’s really happening here? Even players who frequently hit the lines are not aiming for them. They are aiming well inside the line, and missing their target rộng. Finding Your Target You shouldn’t tolerate many misses in your đánh đơn game at all. Adjust your targets accordingly: If you’re missing rộng, move your target in until you aren’t anymore.

If you’re missing sâu, move your target shallower until you aren’t anymore. If, when you do that, you start hitting the lưới, increase your height over the lưới, and decrease your xoay speed, until you aren’t missing into the lưới anymore. If you can’t beat your opponent with those adjustments, you’re almost definitely not going to win. You can try playing more aggressively than you’re capable of, but the end result will be an lỗi tự phát count that prevents you from being competitive.

Hitting the Lines When you miss your target, you’ll sometimes hit the line. That’s what we want. We do not want the ball to go out when we miss our target, because we’re going to miss a lot. This is the idea of lỗi tolerance applied to chiến lược – we need to select shots that can tolerate a small mistake in execution, a small lỗi, without leading to an outright miss.

I saw a practice session recently where a student hit 5 lines in a row, but I could tell by the rest of the forehands he was hitting that he wasn’t aiming for them – he was just getting lucky. That’s awesome. Roll with it. The goal is to hit a lucky điểm thắng when you miss your target, rather than losing the point. Your Brain’s Deception On the 6th ngắn thuận tay, this student hit the ball 2 feet into the ally.

I asked him, “Where were you aiming on that shot?” His answer, “Only like a foot inside the line.” He’d giao lại his previous hot streak get inside his đầu vợt, making him believe he had been aiming to that small target all along. He hadn’t. He was aiming farther than a foot inside the line, and had just happened to miss rộng a few times in a row. The player in both images hit a rộng thuận tay điểm thắng that landed close to the line.

Were they aiming for a conservative target, and merely missed their target rộng (left), or can they consistently tấn công that spot on the sân while rarely missing (right)?
