Your brain will try to reverse engineer your intent from the outcome. It desperately wants to have tried to hit the line when the line is hit, because that validates you as a great tennis player.

When you hit the line, or hit any shot you’re not aiming for, for that matter, your brain will often rewrite the past and make you feel like you were aiming there, even though you weren’t. You’ve got to prevent this if you want to maintain your level throughout a trận đấu. If you don’t, here’s what will happen: You start out aiming to appropriate, large targets. You get on a hot streak, missing to the outside of those targets, resulting in winners.

You adjust your target closer to where those winners were landing. Those same shots that you accidentally hit wider than you were aiming, which used to be winners, are now rộng errors. Frustration and confusion. Heat Check Sometimes after you hit a few winners, you’ll start to get a little anxious, psyched out by your own performance. Am I really this good? Should I really be going for these shots?

First, remind yourself that you are not “really going for these shots” – you are merely missing your targets rộng, and the result is that you’ve hit a few winners in a row by chance. Your brain will try to reverse engineer your intent from the outcome. As for “am I really this good?” – go ahead and test yourself. It’s possible that, today, you really can succeed while aiming at smaller targets than usual.

In basketball, a “heat check” is a colloquial term for a pass you throw to a teammate who’s made many shots in a row, with the goal of letting them shoot again, just to see if they’re still hot. Feel free to heat check yourself in tennis. Go for a smaller target, accelerate a little more than you normally do. Who knows, maybe it works, but make a plan. If you miss a few heat checks, go back to your tried and true targets.

Chances are, you were actually aiming at those larger targets all along, but your brain just reverse engineered history to make you think you were aiming to smaller targets, even though you weren’t. When you hit an upswing in variance, during which you frequently miss wider than you were aiming and pile up the resulting winners, you’re going to feel like a million bucks. Accept it, but don’t be fooled by it. Feel free to heat check yourself, but understand that you’re probably just getting lucky.

If you move your aim closer to the lines because you’re “on fire today,” you’re probably in for a frustrating conclusion to the trận đấu. 1 Trick to Eliminate 90% of lưới Misses Tour level players hit surprisingly high over the lưới.

The amount of lưới clearance they’re getting is pretty difficult to see from the typical broadcast view: the bird’s eye camera, and as such, I think many club level players spend their entire tennis life aiming too low over the lưới, without ever realizing that they’re doing so. In order to avoid lưới misses, you must aim higher over the lưới. I know that sounds trivial, but that is step 1. Understand that you need to move your target higher.

The rest of this article will be about how to do that while making minimal sacrifices elsewhere. Rotational Power Transposition Grigor Dimitrov clearly employing vai tilt while playing a routine waist height thuận tay, getting a solid 4 feet (122 cm) of lưới clearance while doing so. Big words, I know, but giao lại me explain. Our goal on the thuận tay is to use the large muscles of the legs, hông, and thân to accelerate the vợt.

In order to do so, we rotate into our shot, and that xoay powers the cú đánh. We can transpose this xoay to create spinier shots, or flatter shots, by tilting our torso, or leveling it out. With a level torso, our lõi xoay will generate a vợt path that is almost completely back-to-front, with very little low-to-high trajectory at all.

What little low-to-high trajectory we have will be from allowing gravity to pull the hand down slightly, and then having the hand flick back up, but none of it will come from the primary rotational engine of the cú đánh. Casper Ruud getting 6 feet (183 cm) of lưới clearance on a heavy, chủ động tấn công, chest height, trong-ra ngoài thuận tay.

When we tilt our vai, that same cú đánh, which used to be entirely back-to-front, now has a vertical component, and the more we tilt our vai, the more vertical that vung vợt becomes. The vai tilt is our primary tool for altering the spin/phẳng nature of our shots, while still harnessing the same fundamental chuỗi động học to power the vung vợt.

Some of the fundamentals manifest slightly differently with a tilted torso – the player’s weight tends to be less over their back foot, and the vung vợt is more punishing of a tight cổ tay – but the first principles of a loose cổ tay, explosive xoay, and out-and-in-front điểm tiếp xúc are the same. You Almost Always Tilt Casper Ruud expertly digging out a low thuận tay by employing an chủ động tấn công amount of vai tilt, relaxing the cổ tay, and then rotating through the shot as normal.

We’ve dedicated two articles to low forehands specifically: one about this same vai tilt concept, and the other about applying these concepts to dominate opponents who cắt. While vai tilt is crucial for effective, heavy, low forehands, it’s also essential for waist height forehands, and even chest height forehands. Yes, even when the ball bounces up to your chest, most of the time you’ll still want a slight vai tilt as you rotate, in order to add verticality to your vung vợt path.

That added margin over the lưới is crucial for playing high-margin tấn công tennis. After you get the hang of tilting, you’ll realize just how high over the lưới it’s possible to hit while still driving through your shot.

Many Players Have Never Considered Tilting If a recreational player doesn’t realize just how high over the lưới elite players are hitting, even while playing offense, then they won’t recognize that there’s anything amiss when their own tấn công shots routinely pass only 1 foot (30 cm) over the lưới, rather than 2-3 feet (60-91 cm). When they miss into the lưới, then, they assume it’s because they’re simply not good enough, never considering that perhaps they’re aiming to a bad target.

The solution is to employ varying degrees of vai tilt on almost every thuận tay below the vai level, even when playing offense. Grigor Dimitrov employing a slight vai tilt on two chest height, chủ động tấn công forehands, using slightly more tilt when hitting thẳng biên (right), than when hitting chéo sân (left), due to the increased height of the lưới which a thẳng biên shot must clear.

Varying Your Tilt Almost all thuận tay situations call for some degree of tilt, but how much it calls for is up to you. As we’ve discussed before, low balls call for a lot of tilt. Chest height, chủ động tấn công balls call for less. Grigor Dimitrov employing a slight vai tilt on a waist height, phòng thủ thuận tay from the thuận tay góc, while electing not to rotate as explosively as he does on a neutral or tấn công shot.

On phòng thủ shots, you might actually require less tilt, because you’re not rotating as explosively and so you you don’t need as much spin to keep the ball in. The beauty of the vai tilt is that it doesn’t fundamentally change the vung vợt, so you don’t need to practice each kind of thuận tay as it’s own, isolated unit. Instead, just internalize vai tilt as one degree of freedom, among many, that you have at your disposal while playing the thuận tay. You can dial it up and down as needed.

This is a vastly superior mental model of how to create height over the lưới than one of yelling “get it up” at yourself, and then ripping up with your hand and arm through contact (though adding a little upward volitional arm di chuyển along with the tilt is fine). Next time you’re out on the sân, see if you can integrate vai tilt into your shot. Once it begins to feel natural, you’ll gain a control over your lưới misses that previously seemed impossible.

giao bóng is Out = Great trả bóng, Explained Your opponent hits their giao bóng long. “Out,” you say, as you vung vợt at the ball anyway. Lo and behold, the shot you produce on this trả bóng, a trả bóng that, by all accounts, should be more difficult than a typical trả bóng (because the giao bóng was out), turns out to be excellent. You make clean, solid contact, and effortlessly send the ball flying sâu into your opponent’s sân. And then the second giao bóng comes, and you hit the khung vợt. Or you hit it rộng.

Or you miss into the lưới. What’s happening here? Why do we seem so darn good at returning serves that are out, and yet, when the ball lands in, everything falls apart? Self 1 and Self 2 In The Inner game of Tennis, Timothy Gallwey distinguishes between two “selfs” when it comes to the human mind as it relates to tennis.

Self 1 – the narrator, the logical thinker, the analyzer, the “you” that you generally consider you Self 2 – the self that controls the body, the subconscious, the animalistic self It’s a very similar concept to the idea of System 1 vs System 2 thinking from Thinking Fast and Slow by Daniel Kahneman. A useful way to understand the human mind is as two distinct processing apparatuses mixed together as one.

First, there’s the mind we’re familiar with – the one that narrates our life, chooses between goals, mediates, judges, etc. When most people attempt to answer the question, “who am I?” it is this part of the mind with which they identify. But the second kind of thinking, the second “self,” is equally important in your functioning. It’s the part of the brain that controls your heartbeat, regulates your temperature, and makes you walk.

It’s the part that allows you to flinch away from danger before “you” have even noticed the threat, the part that notices di chuyển in your peripheral thị lực, listens for danger while you sleep, or wakes the mother when her baby cries. It is this second self, self 2, that is the master of controlling the body. Sure, self 1 can order simple movements – order your hand to move up, and it’ll move up, but that’s about the extent of the complexity self 1’s orders can produce.

Even for an act as simple as typing, self 1 does not order the specific movements – “type ‘yt, now type ‘y’, now type ‘p'” – but rather merely orders the hands to “type the word ‘typing'”, and then allows self 2 to take over and control. Ceding Control to Self 2 When your opponent serves a lỗi, and you vung vợt anyway, you often naturally and automatically cede full control to self 2, without even thinking about it.

On the other hand, when they giao bóng the ball in, self 1 is typically occupying much of your mental capacity while you attempt to strike the trả bóng. Here are some of the consequences of giving self 1 too much control while playing tennis. 1. Added Tension When the giao bóng lands out, you naturally loosen up. Since no consequences will arise from the vung vợt, not even the untrained mind has any reason to be tight. No muscles that shouldn’t be firing fire.

Almost everyone tenses under any amount of stress, and almost all tension impedes tennis swings. Even the small amount of stress of “hey, don’t miss this one” can cause subconscious tension in the rest of your body, interfering with cú đánh production. 2. Worse Subconscious Adjustment Your body does a lot of things without self 1 noticing they’ve happened until after the fact. If self 1 is in control, though, those things can’t happen.

Making small, improvisational adjustments on something like a giao bóng trả bóng is one of such things. When someone serves the ball at you at 120 mph, there’s not enough time to go, “oh, I’m too close to it. That means I need lean away. Careful not to break my 90 degree vợt góc, or I’ll shank the ball, etc, etc.” Instead, you need to have trained the habit of how to adjust in practice, so that, in a trận đấu, you can just giao lại it happen.

Otherwise, self 2 won’t have the mental resources to actually execute the adjustment. 3. Hidden Habit Interference The more conscious you are, the less your body can naturally resort to its ingrained patterns of di chuyển. There are things your body does that are happening below the conscious level, but, counter-intuitively, if you try to pay attention to them, they can’t happen anymore. If you’re really “on” one day, chances are you are letting self 2 take over more than usual.

Back in high school we had combination locks on our lockers. Every day I’d show up and open my locker out of pure habit. Even though my eyes and my hands were being used, they were being used by self 2, not by self 1. One day, I looked down and… I’d forgotten my combination. I had no idea what it was. Unlocking the locker had been so fully taken over by self 2 that self 1 actually didn’t know how to do it anymore. This happens to a smaller degree in tennis, too.

If you’re really “on” one day, chances are you are letting self 2 take over more than usual. Your body is implementing subconscious habits that you’ve built, that you can’t consciously control, but if self 1 takes back over, these subconscious habits disappear. The Limits of Self 2 Almost every tennis player in the world would benefit from ceding more control to self 2. What, then, is the function of self 1?

First, on any particular cú đánh, there might be a small, simple way self 1 can influence the di chuyển – one clear, concise order self 1 can send, and then giao lại self 2 cán vợt the rest. “đường bóng with the upper back” to initiate the 1-handed-trái tay is one example. For players who have a habit of driving with, say, their triceps, the added tension from giving the “upper back” order is well worth the change in cú đánh cơ học.

Once this đường bóng is mastered and automatic, the player can go back to fully relaxing into the shot. Almost every tennis player in the world would benefit from ceding more control to self 2. Tactically, again, self 1 can jump in for quick, concise orders. If you’ve decided to play something like, say, 20% drop shots, self 1 can jump in and order “cúp,” and then step aside and allow self 2 to execute.

The tricky part here is that if self 2 decides to attempt a cúp, and self 1 jumps in and says “hey, that’s way more than 20% drop shots you’ve been hitting!”, self 2 will lose control, the body will tighten up, and the shot will almost certainly fail. As you can see, even when trying to offer what is correct strategic advice, self 1 can actually impair your chances. We’ll discuss more of the interplay between self 1, self 2, chiến lược, and chiến thuật in the coming weeks.

For now, see what happens if you clear your mind and allow self 2 to take over while you play. It’ll be difficult to do, but occasionally you’ll get it, and those shots will feel freer and better than all the rest. Accelerate Late, Like Jannik Sinner Most tennis players accelerate too early on the thuận tay. As a result, they have long, inconsistent, slow swings. Players who accelerate sufficiently late, on the other hand, have abbreviated, consistent, fast swings.

Jannik Sinner’s world class thuận tay gives us an excellent example of this late gia tốc. The image above is screencapped from a practice session between Jannik Sinner and Novak Djokovic at the 2021 ATP Finals. I encourage you to watch it, as it provides a unique góc we don’t often see, an góc that’s crucial to understanding what’s really happening on a well struck thuận tay.

We’re discussing Jannik’s thuận tay today, but, if you watch the clip, you’ll see Novak implementing all of the same ideas. What is Late gia tốc? At some point during your thuận tay vung vợt, you have to inject speed with your legs, your hông, and your abs, and then allow your hand to giải phóng out so your vợt can flick through the ball. This isn’t trivial to do.

If you time it incorrectly, there won’t be an efficient chuyển of lực through the chuỗi động học, and no matter how hard you đường bóng with your lõi movers, the vợt will barely accelerate. The correct moment to inject pace with your explosive forward twist occurs quite late in the vung vợt, and for many people, later than is intuitive. The primary gia tốc phase happens after the khuỷu tay has passed through the plane of the chest.

The arm-vợt apparatus is in front of the body when most of the pace is injected. The vị trí on the left is the last khung vợt before Jannik initiates his forward vung vợt. The vị trí in the giữa is when his khuỷu tay passes through the plane of his hông, and it marks the point at which he begins the arm’s forward gia tốc in earnest. The vị trí on the right is after contact has been made. The initial phase of the forward vung vợt is much slower than the explosive fling through contact.

Jannik Sinner’s elite cú đánh demonstrates this fact. The initial xoay, and accompanying slight forward vung vợt of the arm, during which Jannik’s hand is brought from its farthest back point, forward to the level of his hông, takes 11 frames. Then, from the time his khuỷu tay has just passed his hông, to the time his forward vung vợt is completed, and the ball is gone, only 7 more frames have passed. Unless you have an extremely good thuận tay already, this should surprise you.

The hand is actually covering far more distance in the second phase, from hông through contact, than it covered in the first, from chuẩn bị to hông. If the vung vợt were performed at a constant speed, more time would be spent in the contact phase than in the first phase, yet on Jannik’s thuận tay, we see the exact opposite. The first phase takes significantly longer than the contact phase – 157% as long, in Jannik Sinner’s case.

Explosive thuận tay Contact The contact phase of the thuận tay is very brief, but not because the vợt isn’t traveling far. On the contrary, the vợt travels through a great distance as it flicks through the hitting zone, it just does so extremely quickly, and so very little time passes during said flick. This part of the vung vợt is a compact, explosive, forward twist, during which the vợt is flung forward. This forward fling starts at, or just in front of, the hông, and ends after contact.

During it, the vợt is far from the body, with sufficient space between the khuỷu tay and the chest so as to allow the khuỷu tay to pass through the plane of the chest unimpeded. During the first, less explosive part of the forward vung vợt, Jannik brings his khuỷu tay past the plane of his chest (left). Then, during the explosive part, Jannik unlocks his chuỗi động học and flings his vợt out to the ball (right).

His vợt-arm system moves freely during this phase, due to the generous space between his khuỷu tay and his chest. Before the forward fling, the forward vung vợt isn’t nearly as explosive. The vợt is brought forward, yes, and the hông and chest do begin to unwind, but the lõi movers aren’t ripping forward explosively to accelerate it until the vợt has already passed through the hông. If you try to explode too early, the hand won’t be able to giải phóng properly, and the vung vợt won’t work.

The injection of speed comes late in the process, only fractions of a second before contact. The lực, generated primarily by the legs, hông, and abs, is injected extremely quickly and all at once, not gradually and uniformly over the entire course of a long, looping forward vung vợt. In Jannik’s case, the process occurs in only 7 frames. Why Accelerate Late?

We need to hold the vợt loosely on the thuận tay, because if we hold it tightly, no matter how explosively we twist, the vợt won’t be able to roi vung through the air. Try it yourself (with anything, doesn’t have to be a vợt): Hold your chosen item tightly, and vung vợt it. Now hold it loosely, and vung vợt it. You’ll notice a clear difference in the speed of the object when holding it loosely.

Trying to inject lực into your vung vợt while the vợt is still behind you leads to a discoordinated, inefficient chuỗi động học. Therefore, to vung vợt as fast as possible, we need to maintain this looseness as we inject lực. To accomplish this, the khuỷu tay has to be in front of the body, and it has to be sufficiently separated from the body.

Trying to inject lực into your vung vợt while the vợt is still behind you leads to a discoordinated, inefficient chuỗi động học – very little of the lõi’s lực actually gets into the vợt.

On the other hand, if you give the khuỷu tay sufficient space, and then allow it to pass by the chest before injecting pace, you’ll find that the lực you generate cleanly and efficiently transfers into the vợt, causing it to effortlessly roi vung through contact. 11 COMMENTS Jack November 28, 2021 How can we implement this? I feel once the hông is initiated, the rest of the vung vợt goes fast. The first picture set where 11 frames and arrow were shown, he has already initiated the hông turn, didn’t he?

REPLY Johnny (FTF) November 28, 2021 Yes, that’s correct – the forward xoay has already started in that second image. The point is not to move your hand forward first, and then turn. The point is that your khuỷu tay should pass by your hông very early in that process, and much of the explosive turn should happen after the hand is already in front of you. Many people have differing levels of arm-đưa vợt ra sau.

Rafa and Kyrgios, for example, pull their khuỷu tay back behind their back, while players like Federer and Karatsev don’t. The principle we talk about in this article unifies all those swings – the more volitional arm đưa vợt ra sau, the more volitional arm forward vung vợt is necessary to get into a vị trí similar to the one Jannik is in here.

It’s hard to describe exactly what the proper vung vợt feels like, but the important insight is that it doesn’t occur at a uniform speed, but rather it’s much faster through contact.

REPLY Philip January 29, 2022 Why not explosively accelerate from the đưa vợt ra sau rather than from when the hand breaks the plane of the hông as long as the cổ tay-arm-vợt unit is loose, the fundamental rules are followed, the humeral-pectoral góc stays pretty much constant and the arm does not initiate the forward vung vợt? After all, vận tốc = gia tốc x time (forgive the mathematical inaccuracy). REPLY Johnny (FTF) January 29, 2022 Good question.

If your humeral pectoral góc is staying roughly the same during the initial part of your vung vợt, you should do exactly as you said. In fact, for players with very abbreviated backswings, like Alsan Karatsev, this is the case, and they almost can’t vung vợt wrong, because they begin every cú đánh in a vị trí from which you can just rip it forward. For other players, though, there’s a lot more arm di chuyển during chuẩn bị, and during the initial phase of the forward vung vợt.

For them, it’s critical that the arm gets out in front of the hông and chest before gia tốc happens in earnest (getting to the humeral-pectoral góc you’re imagining), otherwise the chuỗi động học doesn’t work properly. Not in many is so noticeable (I’d say for sure in Federer and Nadal) Abandon Anger Anger wastes mental năng lượng and clouds judgement, two clear detriments in any competitive setting, so while anger certainly has its uses, “useful emotion in the game of tennis” isn’t one of them.

To better understand anger in tennis, we’ll first draw a line between two classes of mistakes, which I’ll call: Bad Results Process Mistakes Anger towards bad results – towards missing a particular shot or your opponent hitting a lucky điểm thắng – provides very little benefit, in any context. It’s difficult to giao lại go of, but you’ll be far better off once you do. If you struggle with anger towards bad results, internalizing that tennis is random is extremely helpful.

When you get angry because you missed a well chosen shot by two inches, it’s the same as a poker player tilting because their opponent hit a 2-outer on the river. Of course it’s annoying, and it makes sense that you’re upset, but there’s nothing you can do, or could have done, to change it, so your emotional response can’t lead to any life improving action. When you play a game of probability, the cards fall how they may. The rest of this article will be about how to abandon justified anger.

Process Mistakes are Frustrating For process mistakes, anger is often a justified response, but just because it’s justified doesn’t mean it’s useful. Here are some examples of process mistakes: Making the same poor shot selection decision over and over. Letting up on the final shot of an tấn công combination, and missing because of it. Thinking you’ve hit a điểm thắng, not getting ready, and then losing the point because of it.

Not running for a ball because you think it’s out, only to have it land inside the line. These kinds of mistakes feel more fixable than bad results, which is why our emotions run so much hotter when making them, but despite that feeling, mid-trận đấu anger isn’t productive to fixing these mistakes either. The anger that wells up during a trận đấu is primarily a result of the outcome not matching your expectations. Your belief – I move well. I run balls down. I play smart. The reality – I move lazily.

I giao lại balls go. I play dumb. That dissonance is what’s infuriating (or can be; some people are wired such that it doesn’t bother them, but it’s far less likely they clicked on this article). You Are Your Coach To prove to yourself how inappropriate of a response anger is, even to process mistakes, imagine having a student (many of my readers are also coaches, so no need to imagine).

If your student made these mistakes, you might feel angry, or frustrated, or at the very least, disappointed, but how would you phản ứng? Would you yell and scream at them? Tell them they’re terrible at tennis? Walk out on the sân, take their vợt, and cú đập it? Of course not. Like a responsible adult, you’d calmly and analytically evaluate what was working and what wasn’t, and, as gently as you could, try to nudge your player in a better direction.

Trained Patterns of Behavior Process mistakes feel fixable mid-trận đấu, because human beings like to pretend we have full control over our system 2, personal narrator, logical level thinking: Sure, I can’t perfectly execute a thuận tay di chuyển every time, but of course I can choose the right shot, can’t I? That’s a choice after all, and I have full control over my choices, don’t I? Sorry, no. That’s not the way the human brain works.

Process mistakes are trained patterns of behavior that you need to re-train in order to fix. Process mistakes are trained patterns of behavior. Once you’re already in a trận đấu, you’re working with what you’re working with. Mid-trận đấu, the useful orientation towards the information you’re receiving is non-judgmental, emotion free evaluation. Figure out which shots and patterns are working, and which shots and patterns are not.

Expectations aren’t useful mid-trận đấu, because expectations lead to dissonance, and that dissonance leads to anger. In any given trận đấu you play, the behavior you’re seeing on the trận đấu sân is precisely the behavior you’ve cemented in practice: Don’t sprint for ngắn balls in practice? Guess what, in a trận đấu, shouting at yourself to “stop being lazy and run for it” isn’t going to fix that habit. Routinely forget to track the ball in practice? Same thing.

No amount of “watch the ball, idiot!” will fix that mid-trận đấu. Practice is the time to be hard on yourself. While you practice, you can scold yourself for not moving, or for playing too casually at the end of a đánh qua lại, or for making poor shot selection decisions. Practice is the time to allow yourself to be frustrated by the dissonance between where you think you should be, and where you are, because practice is the time when you can actually do something about it.

During a trận đấu, you can’t expect your built in patterns of behavior to change on a dime. I know it feels like you can “just move your feet” or “just tập trung more,” but the truth is, you can’t. You can’t consistently do those things without first building the habit of doing them. Letting Go of Expectations It’s difficult to giao lại go of your expectations. There’s a reason certain process mistakes frustrate you so much. For some people I’ve met, it’s moving poorly.

They absolutely hate when they move lazily – when they don’t sprint, or they don’t get in a proper ready vị trí before the ball comes, it infuriates them. Whatever your particular most hated process mistake is, the reason you hate it so much is because you really, really don’t want to be the kind of player that makes that mistake. Well, time to be the bearer of bad news. You are that player. And getting angry about that won’t change it.

What will change it is practice dedicated to replacing the habit you hate. It won’t be an instantaneous process, either, and while you’re on the path to re-wiring your habit, you have two choices: you can continue to cú đập rackets every time you relapse, or you can… just not do that. Instead of “I DIDN’T MOVE MY FEET [expletive], [expletive], [expletive]!!” it can just be… “I didn’t move my feet.” Fact of life. Evidence I’ve observed while playing, and I’ll adjust my practice accordingly.

The Power of Patience If you can keep the ball heavy, sâu, and, most importantly in the sân, for more shots in a row than your opponent can, you’re almost always going to win. Occasionally, one player can be such a great tactician that they’ll beat a superior ball-striker, but that’s rare. Most of the time, tấn công tennis and chiến thuật exist in the context of attacking your opponent’s imprecise ball strikes: my opponent has dropped a ball ngắn, now how do I maximize my win-rate?

If you’re annoyed that your opponent isn’t dropping many balls ngắn, though, you might be tempted to change it up – to frequently change the direction of the ball, or to aim closer to the lines, but doing so will increase your miss rate, offsetting much, and likely all, of the potential benefit. So what’s the solution then? It’s actually pretty simple. Just wait longer for the opportunity. One of the most valuable skills a tennis player can cultivate is patience.

The cross sân shot provides 82.5 feet of sân to hit into, 4.5 feet more than the thẳng biên shot, which crosses 78 feet of sân. Winning With Geometry How much more margin for error do you have when hitting chéo sân, than when hitting thẳng biên? One foot? Two feet? One meter? The answer may surprise you: 4.5 feet (1.5 meters). It’s like you have an entire extra đánh đôi khu vực đôi to miss into when hitting chéo sân.

Not only that, the lưới is a full 6 inches (15 cm) lower in the giữa than it is at the edges. Due to these geometrical differences, you can miss-execute your shot by more without missing when aiming chéo sân. You can miss an extra 6 inches low without hitting into the lưới, and you can miss an extra 4.5 feet long, without missing out.

đánh qua lại Tolerance One of the favorite drills of Paul Annacone, a drill he used frequently with Roger Federer, is a chéo sân feeding drill where the coach feeds the player a variety of semi-difficult balls into the thuận tay góc. None of the balls are easily attackable. The goal is to hit them all into the sâu, chéo sân góc on the other side. And when I say “them all,” I mean them all. The higher your percentage on this shot, the better you’re going to be.

The idea of this practice is to hình thức a habit of aiming to the correct target, and aiming sufficiently high over the lưới, such that you practically never miss. The drill trains hundreds and hundreds of repetitions of: bước chân phân tách Move to the ball Strike it trở lại vị trí Without repetitions, the above process can’t be executed successfully enough for đánh đơn tennis. It’s just too complex.

A đánh qua lại tolerance drill is to give the player sufficient practice driving the ball chéo sân again, and again, and again, so that, in a trận đấu, they’re confident in their ability to do so. Without this confidence, it’s common for a player to frequently bail out of a đánh qua lại with a low-percentage shot. They’ll do something like hit thẳng biên, even though their opponent isn’t out of vị trí. By doing so, this player increases their own miss-rate, but doesn’t get any benefit from it.

It’s easy for a coach to say, “Hey, don’t do that,” but what really helps is building the habit of not doing that. đánh qua lại tolerance practice teaches a player that they are capable of being patient, so that, once they get into the trận đấu, they’re willing to play neutral tennis and wait for a real opportunity. Wardlaw Directionals NCAA women’s tennis coach Paul Wardlaw coined a system, known as the “wardlaw directionals,” for playing high percentage tennis from the đường cuối sân.

The idea of the system is to help players build the habit of selecting the highest percentage shot in every situation. The Wardlaw system is built on two main ideas: On an outside shot, the incoming ball crosses the player’s body. The highest percentage play is to hit the ball back where it came from. 1. “Outside” vs “Inside” Shots An outside shot is a shot that crosses the player’s body as they receive it. Typically, ground-strokes are outside shots.

When two players are rallying thuận tay-to-thuận tay, for example, each of those forehands is an outside shot – as the ball comes in, it crosses the player’s body, and then they hit it. An inside shot, on the other hand, is a shot which does not cross the player’s body. You’re actually probably familiar with the most common inside shot already – the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay. On the trong-ra ngoài thuận tay, the ball does not cross the player’s body, hence why it’s considered an inside shot. 2.

Change of Direction The second lõi idea of the Wardlaw system is whether or not a player changes the direction of the ball as they strike it. It is easier to execute a shot without changing the ball’s direction. On an inside shot, the ball does not cross the player’s body. The highest percentage play is to change the ball’s direction. The most common tennis shots do not change the ball’s direction. giao lại’s use again the example of the thuận tay-to-thuận tay đánh qua lại.

The direction of the ball is not changing as the players hit: one player hits the ball chéo sân, and the other player returns it back chéo sân. Change of direction shots are very common on ngắn balls, however. Often, a player will tấn công a weak chéo sân shot by changing the ball’s direction and hitting it up-the-line. So what’s the system? On outside shots, don’t change direction, and on inside shots, do change direction.

In general, the Wardlaw system believes more in biomechanically sourced lỗi tolerance than geometrically sourced lỗi tolerance. When accelerating through a xoáy trên cú đánh nền, a player naturally rotates their hông and chest, making the across-the-body shot the lower degree of difficulty shot. On outside shots, we get all three benefits: We can hit across our body. We hit into the longest part of the sân, over the lowest part of the lưới. We don’t have to change the ball’s direction.

On inside shots, on the other hand, we prioritize the biomechanical ease over the other benefits, and elect to hit across our body even though it requires changing the ball’s direction. Geometrically, though, we typically don’t have to sacrifice much to do this: we’re typically standing close to the giữa of the sân, meaning we can change direction while still going over the lowest part of the lưới, and we’ll have roughly equal space to hit into no matter which góc we aim at.

chéo sân is King The chéo sân shot is the lynch pin of đường cuối sân tennis. It is geometrically king because the sân is longer, and the lưới is lower, and is biomechanically king because the hông and chest naturally want to turn towards it as a player swings. đánh qua lại tolerance creates your tấn công opportunities.

Whatever đường cuối sân chiến lược you decide to implement, whether a Wardlaw chiến lược or not, the heavy, sâu, chéo sân shot should be at the foundation of that chiến lược, because it leverages both geometric and biomechanical lợi thế. Players who practice how to execute sâu, chéo sân shots at an extremely high clip, and then cultivate the willingness to do so, over, and over, and over again, win more matches than those who don’t. đánh qua lại tolerance creates your tấn công opportunities.

When you watch tennis, it isn’t always obvious how patient the best tấn công players, like Roger Federer, are being. chiến thuật, drop shots, lưới play, offense – all of it is very sexy and very enticing, but without patience it doesn’t work.

Roger Federer isn’t Roger Federer because he attacks every ball, rather, Roger is the best in the world at recognizing an tấn công opportunity when one is provided to him, and when one isn’t, he hits chéo sân like everyone else. “I Just Started Missing.” Ever wonder why sports books try really, really hard to get you to place parlay wagers?

It’s because the parlay bet, a bet type where you bet on multiple events, and ALL of them have to happen for you to win, leverages an idea that human beings are notoriously bad at comprehending: compound probability. When multiple, independent events all have to go right for you to win, the probability of the entire chuỗi happening is the product of the probabilities of the individual events.

For example, the chance of flipping a fair coin and getting heads is 1/2, while the chance of flipping 4 fair coins, and getting ALL heads is 1/16, eight times less likely. The Surprising Effects of Compound Probability giao lại’s imagine you possess a shot which you know, from your practice, has a 90% success rate – maybe it’s your sâu, chéo sân thuận tay. That’s pretty good, right? You’re only missing this shot 1 out of every 10 times you attempt it. You use this shot out of neutral situations.

Sometimes, your opponent drops you a bóng ngắn, and you can transition to offense, and other times, they trả bóng the ball back chéo sân, forcing you to hit this shot again. If you’re forced to execute this shot: 3 times in order to win a đánh qua lại, your chance of winning the đánh qua lại is (.9)3, or 73%. 7 times in order to win a đánh qua lại, your chance of winning the đánh qua lại is (.9)7, or 49%. If your opponent can lực you to hit just 7 of these shots, they’re actually favored to win the point.

Despite the fact that the shot you’re hitting is, by all accounts, very consistent, if your opponent can lực you to hit just 7 of these shots, they’re actually favored to win the point (51 to 49). Most people don’t have a great sense of what happens to the odds of highly likely events (like a shot which succeeds 90% of the time) when they’re added together.

Even a probability close to 1, like 0.9, rapidly decreases when multiplied by itself. “I’m making so many errors.” Many players end up confused when they encounter the following situations: They are used to playing 3 shot rallies, and winning 73% of them. They lose about 27% of their points to errors, and they’re okay with it.

All of the sudden, they play an opponent who forces them to play 7 shot rallies instead of 3 shot rallies, and now they’re only winning 49% of points, and making errors on 51% of them. They perceive this change, incorrectly, as “making more errors.” Here’s the thing though – between situation #1 and situation #2, their cú đánh success rate remains unchanged.

It feels to the player like they are playing worse, because their đánh qua lại success rate has cratered from 73% to 49%, but, in reality, they’re playing how they always play. Their opponent is forcing them to make more balls than usual. Your opponent has changed, and you haven’t. Your opponent is now making you play 7 shots instead of 3. It’s also common for these two situations to occur against the same player, but at different times in the trận đấu.

Often, an opponent’s consistency will increase as they get used to your ball, especially if you hit an especially novel ball – whether it’s heavier or flatter than is typical at your level, or if you’re a lefty. Early in the first set, your opponent’s miss rate is higher while they adjust, and they can only lực you to play 3 shots. Later, as they get used to your shot, they can lực you to play 7 shots instead, and all of the sudden the trận đấu is tight.

Again, you feel like it’s you who has changed, because you now lose 51% of the points to an error, rather than 27%, and again, this is incorrect. Your opponent has changed, and you haven’t. Your opponent is now making you play 7 shots instead of 3. You’re being forced to execute more shots in a row without missing in order to win the points. Solutions If you take anything away from this article, giao lại it be this – 90% isn’t that high.

For a shot that has to be executed over, and over, and over again throughout a trận đấu, missing 1/10 is actually a lot. If we’re able to train our sâu, chéo sân thuận tay up from 90%, to just 95%, the examples we gave at the top become far more appealing: 3 shots to win a point: 86% chance to win 7 shots to win a point: 70% chance to win Here’s another way to say this. If you’re hitting your thuận tay at 90%, that’s 10 misses every 100 balls. If you’re hitting it at 95%, you miss 5 less times.

Those 5 points go a long way over the course of a set. If just one or two of them end up costing you a game, all of the sudden, you’re losing the set 6-4 instead of winning it. It’s not you, it’s them. Once you’re already in the trận đấu, you’re working with whatever success rate you’re working with – you’re hitting at 90%, and now that your opponent has adjusted, 90% isn’t good enough.

Instead of lamenting that “you’re making so many more errors now,” you need to correctly identify that your neutral đánh qua lại balls are no longer good enough to win the trận đấu, and adjust. Understand that your opponent has improved, and unless you alter you’re chiến lược, you’re going to lose. Perhaps you change from 75% Wardlaw / 25% Change-up, to 50/50. Maybe you increase your cúp percentage, maybe you start serving and volleying.

Whatever non-standard tools you have, the non chéo sân grinding parts of the game that you are best at, now’s the time to increase the frequency at which you go to them. The heavy, chéo sân ball is certainly the góc stone of tennis, but there’s a lot more to the game than just that. Every tennis player has their unique strengths, their own tools for breaking out of bad patterns, and matches where your đánh qua lại ball isn’t good enough are the time to break those tools out.

The Swinging bắt lưới is a Cheat Code There’s one shot in tennis that changes the geometry of the sân in a way unlike any other: The swinging bắt lưới. This shot allows a player to tấn công in ways that are physically impossible with any other, and, especially on the thuận tay side, they can execute those attacks in an extremely high percentage way. The swinging bắt lưới’s primary use case is to take away every tennis player’s best phòng thủ option – the sâu block/roll shot over the giữa.

When a defending player is in a compromised vị trí, the easiest, highest percentage way for that player to reset the point is to block the ball back high, slow, and sâu, while aiming to a very large target. This high, slow, sâu block shot is difficult to miss, even if the incoming ball is challenging, and yet it still typically succeeds in returning the point to neutral. Why Evangelize The Swinging bắt lưới?

It actually isn’t the swinging bắt lưới itself that’s the cheat code – it’s rolling the ball sâu when on defense that is. It’s too effective, and too easy. The swinging bắt lưới is the only consistent way to tấn công this phòng thủ cheat code, but it isn’t as popular as it should be, because it carries with it a very high degree of difficulty. Here’s what makes it difficult: The ball typically has a large, downward vận tốc when you strike it. The ball is also accelerating down as you strike it.

You’ll often be moving while you execute your vung vợt. The điểm tiếp xúc will vary widely from shot to shot. Imperfect Contact Due to #1 and #2, perfect contact is unlikely. When the ball has both a large vertical vận tốc and is also accelerating as it comes in (due to gravity), a small error in timing or spacing is almost guaranteed. Your cú đánh must succeed in the face of that small error.

Hitting While Moving It’s uncommon, on the swinging bắt lưới, that you get to set your feet, prepare in rhythm, and vung vợt. In fact, the very idea of the shot is to explode forward and take time away from your opponent, intentionally breaking the rhythm of the trận đấu. When sprinting through the shot is necessary, the thuận tay swinging bắt lưới is performed with the simplest cú đánh possible. To do this successfully requires a very fundamental understanding of the thuận tay.

If your understanding of the cú đánh exists as a series of rules like “vung vợt low to high” and “đường bóng with your legs,” then the swinging bắt lưới won’t be possible for you. If you don’t know what the thuận tay cú đánh really is, at its lõi, then you won’t be able to correctly adapt your vung vợt for the novel situation that is each individual swinging bắt lưới attempt. When sprinting through the shot is necessary, the thuận tay swinging bắt lưới is performed with the simplest cú đánh possible.

The chest is minimally turned away from the target, and the hông are mostly open. Since you’re sprinting forward anyway, you’ll have plenty of power, and adding more with xoay will often cause more misses than it’s worth. lỗi Tolerance Itself Is A Weapon Some players try to strike every ball perfectly. That’s fine, but that should be a secondary goal, because the ability to strike a ball imperfectly and still have the shot succeed, that ability itself is a weapon.

It allows you to go for more chủ động tấn công, more difficult shots, without missing – shots where perfect striking is very unlikely. This is one of the reasons that I, perhaps more than many coaches, evangelize the swinging bắt lưới so heavily. Because it’s a very difficult shot to execute perfectly, many see it as an inherently low-percentage shot, but that’s not actually the case.

If you design your cú đánh to be lỗi tolerant, then it’ll work even when executed imperfectly, and that fact is what allows you to take lợi thế of amazing shots like the swinging bắt lưới. The chain of reasoning that leads away from the swinging bắt lưới is too unimaginative: It’s too hard to execute a swinging bắt lưới perfectly. The swinging bắt lưới is low percentage. The lợi thế you gain isn’t worth the miss rate.

Whereas, we start with a first principle idea of the lợi thế we want, and work backwards from there: The swinging bắt lưới provides a massive geometrical lợi thế. It’s really hard to execute perfectly. Design and practice a thuận tay cú đánh that succeeds when executed imperfectly. Use that cú đánh to hit the swinging bắt lưới, and cash in on that geometrical lợi thế.

The thuận tay cú đánh Is Phenomenal In the intro to The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay, we marvel at just how effective the modern thuận tay di chuyển mẫu is at producing a tennis shot. Well, we can press this lợi thế further by using this di chuyển mẫu at lưới.

The ability to use your thuận tay cơ học in transition – through no man’s land and when sprinting forward – and also while at the lưới, papers over a lot of the biomechanically tricky shots you’ll run into in those areas: Shots at your đầu gối in transition go from awkward chip shots, to regular low forehands. Shots at your vai go from awkward high volleys to trivial high forehands.

Shots at waist height go from, again, awkward mid-sân, transition volleys to, essentially, approach shots that you don’t bounce. Not to oversell it, it’s certainly more difficult to strike these forehands out of the air than it is off the ground, but if your cú đánh is sufficiently lỗi tolerant, not prohibitively so. One More Use Case One last, fun use case of the swinging bắt lưới is actually phòng thủ. Yes, the swinging bắt lưới improves your defense as well.

When an opponent is at the lưới, and you’ve just hit a dipping cúp chuyền, you need to sprint forward, because the highest margin, most effective bắt lưới is ngắn. When you do that, you’ll end up split stepping in no-man’s land, so what do you do if the ball is volleyed back sâu instead? Hit it out of the air, of course. Just take your regular thuận tay or trái tay vung vợt and either hit it right at the player’s body, or go for another cúp chuyền.

This is the only effective way to phòng thủ against a player at lưới. If you stay at the đường cuối sân, you’re giving up way too much equity with the easy ngắn volleys. The Upshot You’ll notice a mẫu here – the swinging bắt lưới allows us to take away our opponent’s best option. It’s difficult, but by mastering this difficult shot ourselves, we make our opponent’s shots even more difficult. The swinging bắt lưới allows us to take away our opponent’s best options.

Whether we’re taking away the easy sâu phòng thủ shot, or we’re taking away the easy ngắn chip bắt lưới, we’re using our ability to strike the ball out of the air to take away our opponent’s best options. Maybe “cheat code” is the wrong word for the swinging bắt lưới – it takes a lot of repetitions to get it right; it’s not as simple as entering a few buttons into your controller. “Cheat code eliminator” is more accurate.

The swinging bắt lưới takes away your opponent’s cheat codes, and that’s as good as having one yourself. “You Can’t Teach That” What is a “thuận tay,” really? For many, it’s simply a tennis cú đánh, but for us, it’s something else. We consider “the thuận tay” to be a class of related di chuyển patterns. The entire class constitutes “the thuận tay.” To master “the thuận tay,” you need to master not just one specific cú đánh, but rather the entire class of thuận tay-like movements.

There are four main attributes to any shot you attempt: Target on the other side of the sân Amount of spin Height over the lưới vận tốc of the ball The class of thuận tay movements contains many different exact strokes, each of which manipulates these attributes in one way or another. Mastering the thuận tay constitutes mastering this di chuyển class to such an extent so as to be able to manipulate any or all of these four goals at will. And that’s quite difficult to do.

The Challenge Most forehands are not correctly built from first principles, but rather from downstream details and implementations. The result is that most players have no idea how to correctly manipulate one or more of these four goals without their vung vợt breaking down. Here are some examples: Trying to move your target by looking at it. Trying to get more spin by “wristing” the ball using your cẳng tay. Trying to increase height over the lưới by “swinging up” with your arm.

Trying to increase vận tốc by accelerating with your arm muscles. Each mistake belies a critical misunderstanding of how its corresponding goal is achieved. I could go through and explain why each one of these is a bad idea, but that’s not the point. The point is that each mistake belies a critical misunderstanding of how its corresponding goal is achieved.

In order to manipulate an attribute of your thuận tay, whether it’s target, spin, height, or vận tốc, you need to understand, within the class of di chuyển that’s happening on the thuận tay, how that attribute is properly controlled. Building From the Ground Up Exact di chuyển patterns and vung vợt cơ học are not first principles on the thuận tay. They are derivatives off of those first principles, and because they are derivative, they’ll change from situation to situation.

This is why, at lỗi Tolerant Tennis, none of the drills we recommend involve practicing a particular vung vợt or di chuyển mẫu. Instead, the drills we recommend place the player in the most common and demanding situations, and giao lại them fend for themselves. We teach you the first principles, then throw you to the wolves and giao lại you apply them.

The goal is for you to discover the most common exact vung vợt cơ học and di chuyển patterns on your own, as your brain attempts to construct a shot from the fundamentals. Now, we’ll occasionally make an exception when the details are difficult to find on your own – when liberating the hông, many students need to be told to unplant their feet – but this kind of instruction is the exception, not the rule.

Coaches are guides along the path of a student’s own experimentation, and there are certainly a few times the guide must instruct a little more than usual, but they’re rare. Natural Talent Natural talent helps a lot in tennis. For any or all of the four attributes of a cú đánh, many players understand how to control it without requiring much instruction. For most players, at least one of the four fundamental goals does not come naturally. Some players just get how to aim their strokes.

Others find hitting spin or phẳng to be trivial – just hit spinnier or flatter, of course. Some can easily vary their height over the lưới, and when it comes to vận tốc, usually strokes break down when accelerated, but, for some players, they simply think “hit harder,” and it works.

For most players, though, at least one of these four fundamental goals, and almost certainly more than one, does not come naturally, and because it doesn’t come naturally, there exists no athletic understanding of how to manipulate it. “You Can’t Teach That” Actually, you can. Almost always. There are very few things that actually can’t be taught, like height and fast twitch muscle fiber density; almost everything else can be. The “you can teach that” line irks me because it’s not quite accurate.

Well, actually it is: “You can’t teach that,” but I can. Here’s how. I teach my students first principles, then giao lại them experiment. All of the brilliant improvisational shots you see on the tour are derived from the same fundamentals as the regular ones. The best improvisational players are the ones with the best athletic understanding of those fundamentals. Roger Federer blocking a trả bóng điểm thắng past Thomas Berdych en route to winning the 2017 Australian Open – exceptional, but not unteachable.

When Roger Federer blocks a tour level giao bóng to his trái tay up the line for a điểm thắng, that looks like natural talent, and it is, but it’s also something else. It also demonstrates a precise athletic understanding of how to control his body in space in order to block that giao bóng. This athletic understanding, for Roger, came naturally, but that doesn’t mean it had to come naturally. I can guide you along the path to hitting the same shot.

I can tell you to use your upper back to stabilize and đường bóng the vợt, to hold the vợt at 90 degrees with your cẳng tay, to track the ball off the bounce, into a điểm tiếp xúc well in front of your body. We can train what it feels like to đường bóng the vợt with your upper back, instead of with your triceps. We can take one, two, three hundred reps where we just tập trung on tracking the ball into the right điểm tiếp xúc.

Learning the Class Instead of the cú đánh Everything that happens on a tennis sân is a result of simple di chuyển, and that di chuyển can be broken down into even simpler parts. The brilliant shots you see on TV – those are certainly out on the edge of their particular di chuyển class – but they’re still movements, still governed by the same rules as the rest. Improvisation of all kinds is simply the brain working forward from a correct đường cuối sân understanding.

Whether natural or taught, the stronger the fundamental understanding, the better the resulting improvisation. Whether natural or taught, the stronger the fundamental understanding, the better the resulting improvisation. Here’s something I see every day – all of my students occasionally kết thúc over their đầu vợt on their forehands, and yet I’ve never told a single one to do that. They were merely applying the fundamentals.

Their brain has internalized the thuận tay as twisting explosively into the ball, and, in order to do that when late, the theo sau goes over the đầu vợt. The theo sau happened automatically, not by design. Learning a tennis cú đánh is about learning the fundamentals that govern that class of di chuyển patterns, and then practicing with those fundamentals as a starting point.

Learn like that, and unique situations – high or low contact, passing shots, shots on the run, shots stuck behind you – they won’t be novel, low-percentage, awkward feeling situations. They’ll just be… situations. Situations in which you apply the same fundamentals you apply on every other shot. Train like this, and your rate of improvement will skyrocket. Losing the Feeling The perfect thuận tay giải phóng is elusive.

That loose, explosive, natural flick through contact feels amazing when it works, and when it’s not there it feels… frustrating. One day you’ll have it, and the next day you won’t. Actually, the variation is even more extreme than that. One set you’ll have it, the next you won’t. Sometimes you’ll even lose it game-to-game, and sometimes even shot-to-shot.

Long term, every player’s goal should be for that perfect giải phóng to become habit, thereby leaving the realm of conscious competence and entering the realm of unconscious competence. In order to lay down the tracks of habit in the brain, a human being must perform the same action, the same way, repeatedly. The tough part, when it comes to the thuận tay, is that since the perfect giải phóng is so elusive, it’s hard to actually execute these beneficial repetitions.

You could have an entire two hour practice session, during which you hit a few thousand balls, and none of them could be high quality reps. Raise Your Practice Standards Unlike during a trận đấu, practice is the time to be hard on yourself. The repetitions where the cú đánh feels wrong aren’t that valuable. You are your practice, and if you’re practicing things that don’t feel right, you’re becoming a tennis player whose strokes don’t feel right.

Your practice is only useful insofar as it constitutes practicing things that you actually want in your game. Here’s an example. giao lại’s say you: bước chân phân tách on time Move explosively to the ball Prepare well vung vợt poorly That repetition was… decent. You’re practicing good habits with your di chuyển and chuẩn bị, and you’ll certainly get a minor conditioning improvement from a session of reps like these.

The practice would be so much more useful, though, if the vung vợt was also working, because when the vung vợt isn’t working, the executing-the-vung vợt phase of the practice di chuyển has no value – during the vung vợt, you aren’t practicing the di chuyển you want to habituate. How do we fix it? You’re in practice, and your stoke feels wrong. Repetitions are not the answer, experimentation is. Here are a few suggestions for how to get the feeling back: 1.

Weighted Swings This is the single most likely thing to help reset your brain and get you swinging correctly again. Adding weight to the vợt, or swinging something heavier than a vợt (like 2 rackets, or a ball hopper) forces your body to use its strongest muscles to generate gia tốc. If your cú đánh feels awful, try taking a few weighted shadow swings, then go back to your own vợt, and then repeat.

Usually, the extra weight will lực your body to move in a different, more efficient way. Never plant your feet while swinging something heavy. giao lại the weight of the object do the work of the vung vợt. giao lại it pull you forward. Notice how far out in front of you the dynamic action of the weight occurs. As you do this exercise, ensure your feet remain free. Remain on the balls of your feet, and also allow the entirety of either foot to come off the ground as the weight moves.

Never plant your feet while swinging something heavy, as that’ll put heavy stress on the đầu gối. My father actually tore his right ACL doing just that – he planted his feet and twisted while launching a heavy piece of furniture into a dumpster. 2. Dial Down the gia tốc Mistakes tend to be exaggerated at higher speeds. Accelerate less when the cú đánh feels bad, and just work on creating clean contact. Remember, if you can’t do it slow, you can’t do it fast.

Taking fast reps with the wrong cú đánh isn’t productive – you aren’t getting better at tennis while doing it, so why bother? 3. Audit Your cú đánh Is it every cú đánh that feels bad, or just some of them? Is it only the low ones? Only the high ones? Only the fast, or the slow ones? If there’s a particular situation under which your cú đánh feels great, then it means your issue on the others is not the vung vợt itself, but rather improper adjustment to a non-standard situation.

Experiment with different forms of adjustment, like tilting the torso, or sitting lower with the legs, and see if you can fix it. 4. Cycle Through Your Cues Throughout your tennis life, you’ve told yourself many, many things. Most of them did nothing, but a few of them really worked. Start cycling through your cues from the past, and see if any of them make the cú đánh click.

Testing Your Contact There’s actually a great test for thuận tay contact, specifically: Without your vợt, drop a tennis ball in front of you, and then attempt to catch it with your thuận tay vung vợt motion. If your thuận tay feels off today, I guarantee you’re going to hit the ball away instead of catching it. It’ll feel like practically the entire motion is transpiring in front of the body. If you hit the ball away, it’s because you are accelerating too early in the vung vợt.

We need to wait until the khuỷu tay has passed through the plane of the hông before accelerating in earnest, otherwise, the chuỗi động học just doesn’t work the same. Once you fix it, and you’re catching the ball every time, it’ll feel like practically the entire motion is transpiring in front of the body, and that’s exactly right. Both the thuận tay and this practice drill take place primarily in front of the body.

The correct thuận tay motion is not best understood (as it commonly is) as: đưa vợt ra sau > forward vung vợt but rather as: place the hand down > fling it way out in front of you The thuận tay is a motion that occurs in front of the body. Once that becomes habit, the inconsistency will end. The Secret to Saving Your đầu gối When a person is standing straight up, their weight is balanced over the giữa of their foot. The đầu gối, like the foot, is a beautiful, well engineered piece of biological machinery.

And also like the foot, the đầu gối is very easy to injure. When a person is standing, their weight is pushing down through their entire leg, and the bones of the leg are supporting most of the lực. The đầu gối is in no danger in this vị trí, and we’ll use this as our starting point. The đầu gối pain comes when we nạp the đầu gối, do so improperly, and then end up placing far more stress on the đầu gối than it can trở lại vị trí from.

đang nạp the đầu gối In order to forcefully đường bóng off the ground, an athlete must nạp the đầu gối. Our goal, though, is to nạp the entire supportive structure of lower body, specifically the strong muscles of the posterior chain, rather than just the đầu gối itself. The more nạp the glutes, hamstrings, and lower back take, the less nạp on the đầu gối, and vice versa.

Forward đầu gối Shear Shear lực occurs at the đầu gối when an athlete’s weight is far over their toes, and the đầu gối is being forced to support that weight with little help from the strong muscles of the legs and back. The exact biomechanical explanation of this lực is beyond the scope of this article, but you can feel it yourself.

We’ll examine two distinct, different đang nạp positions and their effects on the đầu gối – exaggerate them, and you’ll be able to feel the stress on your đầu gối (or lack there of) yourself. The Typical Untrained Person When an athlete uses a “đầu gối first” approach to generating ground lực, their weight is moved far forward, creating shear stress on the đầu gối. Note the very limited hông gấp here.

The typical 21st century human being is quad dominant – there’s a proportional strength imbalance between the quads and the hamstrings, and the quads are proportionally stronger. This is primarily due to sitting, during which the quadriceps is contracted, while the hamstring is elongated. The result is that most recreational athletes attempt to use their stronger quads, rather than their weaker hamstrings, to generate ground lực when they have to push off.

This takes the hình thức of bending the đầu gối forward, then using the quadriceps to explosively extend them. The vị trí shown here is the beginning of that process – the forward đầu gối bend. When in this vị trí, you’ll feel stress on the front of your đầu gối. The athlete’s khối tâm isn’t balanced over the foot, and, due to that, more lực is on the patella tendon and accompanying đầu gối structure, and less lực is on the hamstrings and glutes.

The result is typically the somewhat inappropriately named “overuse” injury. More stress is placed on the đầu gối during each training session than it can trở lại vị trí from before the next, and due to that, tissue damage accumulates. The cause of the injury here is not so much overuse as it is misuse. By preparing to explode in a different way, we can significantly decrease the stress on the đầu gối during each session, down to a point where it can easily trở lại vị trí before the next.

The term “overuse” is misleading, because the problem isn’t that we’re using a tissue too much, it’s that we’re using it wrong. The Proper Athletic vị trí When an athlete sits back, and hinges at the hông in order to remain balanced, they can nạp their legs, and prepare to explode, while still maintaining thăng bằng over the giữa of the foot.

In order to protect the đầu gối during explosive athletic di chuyển, we want to nạp the strong muscles of the posterior chain – the hamstrings, glutes, and lower back. đang nạp these muscles reduces the nạp on the đầu gối. Effective athletes are not quad dominant, but instead have well trained posterior chain strength as well. When an athlete like this attempts to generate ground lực, they sit back, rather than just bend their đầu gối forward.

When they explode off the ground, though the quadriceps certainly do participate, the hamstrings and glutes also play a large role: When the hamstrings contract, the shin is pulled towards the femur – think of it as pulling the ground behind you, thereby driving you forward. The glutes fire to further straighten the hông-torso góc, driving the athlete even further forward. This extra muscle recruitment leads to vị trí #2 being more balanced and more explosive than vị trí #1.

And, most importantly, it’s safer. In vị trí #2, the start of a biomechanically friendly explosive motion, the posterior chain is loaded far more, and the đầu gối, far less. Adopt the two positions back to back, and you’ll be able to feel the difference. In #1, you’ll feel the nạp at the front of your đầu gối. In #2, you’ll feel the nạp on your glutes and hamstrings. Note that vị trí #2 still possesses the triple gấp necessary for athletic di chuyển – the ankle, đầu gối, and hông are all flexed.

The đầu gối is still being loaded, and used to đường bóng off the ground, it’s just being loaded less due to the greater hông gấp, which moves stress onto the upper leg muscles instead. Strength Training Without a solid proprioceptive understanding of how to properly thăng bằng using the muscles of the posterior chain (or natural, athletic talent), you’re likely putting this shear lực on your đầu gối unintentionally.

Due to the quad dominance discussed above, most recreational athletes will not move correctly without training. Instead, they’ll use vị trí #1, and variations of it, all around the sân, neglecting the critical hông gấp that moves stress off of the đầu gối, and onto the strong, blood-flow rich, quick recovering muscles of the thighs. Strength training provides two critical benefits that protect the đầu gối (as well as the rest of your connective tissue). 1.

Strength (duh) The stronger your muscles, the more nạp they can take, and the less nạp your tendons and ligaments needs to support. As your muscles fatigue, your brain subconsciously alters your kỹ thuật. Often, your brain has a great intuitive understanding of how to do something strenuous, but that kỹ thuật starts to break down under fatigue.

As the muscles fatigue, your brain subconsciously alters your kỹ thuật to move some of the nạp from the fatiguing muscles to the connective tissue (so that the di chuyển still succeeds). The stronger you are, the less you fatigue, and the less your brain feels the need to do this. Also, competitively, strength provides significant benefit as well, granting you the ability to hit harder without increasing your level of effort. 2.

Proprioceptive Awareness The underrated, but perhaps even more important benefit of strength training is that it teaches you to feel the muscles you’re supposed to be đang nạp. Strength training teaches you to feel the muscles you’re supposed to be using. Most untrained athletes cannot feel a contraction in their hamstrings. They don’t understand how to flex their glutes, and them to straighten their lower back is like asking them to do advanced calculus.

Adult males get injured every day carrying tiny amounts of weight improperly. We’re talking about 40-50lb (20kg) loads here, and people are throwing out their backs. The problem isn’t strength, but rather the lack of athletic understanding of how to recruit the lifting muscles properly. Strength training trains not only the muscles, but the nervous system. It teaches an athlete to use and nạp these muscles, and allows them to move in a safer, faster, more explosive manner.

The Felix Flick I get this question a lot: If I’m trying to master the modern thuận tay, who should I emulate? Look no further than 2022 Rotterdam champion Felix Auger-Aliassime. We always talk about how the optimal thuận tay vung vợt is a brief, explosive, motion. The vợt is quickly accelerated by the strong muscles of the hông and abs, and, as a result, it naturally flicks forward through the ball. Well, Felix’s cú đánh demonstrates this concept perfectly.

Felix Auger-Aliassime prepares for what becomes an trong-ra ngoài thuận tay điểm thắng against Stefanos Tsitsipas, during the 2022 Rotterdam final. In the image above, Felix’s final chuẩn bị vị trí before his forward explosion, his vợt, hand, khuỷu tay, and upper arm are all on the hitting side of his chest. Further, his khuỷu tay is already past his hông, even before he’s initiated his forward vung vợt. This chuẩn bị style is like a cheat code for timing the ball, and I’ll explain why.

We’ve talked before about how, in order for the chuỗi động học to work, you must allow the khuỷu tay to pass through the plane of the hông before accelerating in earnest. Felix simply prepares the khuỷu tay at the location from which it’ll be driven forward, thereby bypassing that entire complication. Since he prepares by placing his arm-vợt system directly in the spot from which he wants to roi vung it forward, the only thing left to do after chuẩn bị is to roi vung it forward.

There’s no need to move the arm, or the hand, or alter the orientation of the vợt – everything is fully prepared for the forward explosion already, leaving only a single variable: when to initiate that quick, explosive lõi xoay. Felix Auger-Aliassime’s beautiful chest and hông alignment at contact; both are pointing almost directly at his trong-ra ngoài target.

Felix also employs the biomechanically optimal level of xoay – he whips his hông and chest exactly towards his trong-ra ngoài target, and then lets his arm flick forward from there. The result is that this shot is extremely lỗi tolerant. If Felix is a little early, or a little late, his string góc will still be correct, because he is contacting the ball right in the giữa of the forward flick phase. More to Come I was really happy to see Felix win Rotterdam.

As one of the players with the nicest thuận tay cơ học on tour, it was such a shame that he was struggling mentally. Make no mistake, though – Felix’s problem was never how he was striking the ball. Felix strikes the ball in a hyper-efficient way. His style enables him to accelerate aggressively with an extremely simple vung vợt. That simplicity lets him to time the ball extremely well, allowing him to both aim and disguise his shots with ease.

Felix’s thuận tay is going to be one of the premiere weapons on the tour for the next decade, and I’m excited to keep watching it.

The Rafa-Style theo sau The “buggy roi vung thuận tay.” The “reverse thuận tay.” The “over the đầu vợt theo sau.” It goes by many names, but my favorite pays homage to the first man to really abuse it on the pro tour – “Rafa-Style.” Nearly every player on the tour, both male and female, employs the Rafa-Style thuận tay in some instances, and for some, like Rafa himself, the cú đánh is a staple of their game. So what is it, anyway?

The first thing to understand about the Rafa-Style theo sau is what it isn’t – it is not our primary way to tấn công low balls, or to add spin to our shots. Players theo sau above the đầu vợt due to increased volitional upward arm di chuyển during the forward vung vợt.

A vung vợt with that extra verticality will impart more xoáy trên than one without, and a vung vợt like that will be more likely to đường bóng the ball up, but the above-the-đầu vợt vung vợt is not the ideal way to accomplish either of those goals. As we’ve discussed many times on here, volitional arm di chuyển isn’t as powerful as lõi di chuyển, because the muscles that pull the arm up are far weaker than the strong muscles in the hông and thân.

In an ideal situation, we create our vung vợt by using our strong thân muscles to fling the arm forward. So what is it for? The Rafa Style theo sau is for catching the ball late. The optimal điểm tiếp xúc on the thuận tay is well out in front of the body. When we strike the ball there, we are able to initiate the vung vợt with either our abdominal xoay, or if we have more time, our back leg đường bóng, and in doing so explosively fling the vợt out away from us, through the ball.

If we catch the ball farther back, this doesn’t work. We can try to twist around and giao lại our vợt fling out through the ball, but we won’t be able to, because by the time we get to the ball, we’re not in the forward fling stage of our vung vợt yet. The solution is to theo sau above the đầu vợt. When we’re late, and we still initiate the vung vợt with our abs, we need the forward flick part of the vung vợt to happen earlier.

In order to accommodate this, we pull the vợt up, and use the vertical space above our đầu vợt to complete the di chuyển. Rafael Nadal striking two different forehands. On the left, he catches the ball at his front foot, and follows-through naturally around his vai, whereas on the right, he catches the ball well behind his front foot, his hông and chest remain slightly under-rotated, and he follows-through above his đầu vợt.

Normally, we allow the vợt to flick forward through the horizontal space in front of us. This is ideal for driving the ball, and, best case scenario, it’s what we want to do. But in a pinch, we can use the vertical space above our đầu vợt instead. We twist, pull the vợt up while we do, and in doing so are able to still recruit our twisting chain, and get most of that năng lượng into the ball.

The Rafa-Style theo sau will always generate less pop than an identical gia tốc that was able to flick forward instead of up, but that sacrifice is very often worth it, because the ability to catch the ball late provides many surprising advantages, which we’ll discuss next. 1. lỗi Tolerance In order to be a useful trận đấu shot, or thuận tay must succeed when executed imperfectly. This means, among other things, that it must succeed when we’re accidentally late.

The Rafa-Style theo sau allows this. You move to the ball, prepare, initiate your vung vợt… and you quickly realize it’s too far back to allow the vợt to naturally flick through it (luckily your tracking off the bounce was good enough to notice this in time to adjust). You’re late, now what? In order to be a useful trận đấu shot, or thuận tay must succeed when executed imperfectly.

Well, immediately after you twist your abs forward, you can just vung vợt the vợt up instead of forward, and your cú đánh will still work. The ability to do this in real time creates lỗi tolerance – you can be late by accident, and stay in the point. 2. Offense lỗi tolerance and offense go hand in hand. The more lỗi tolerant your cú đánh – the bigger the mistakes it can tolerate without failing – the higher degree of difficulty tấn công shots you can attempt.

Think of it this way: If you go for difficult tấn công shots, you’re going to mess up. You’re never going to hit them perfectly, because they’re so challenging. But guess what, if your cú đánh works even when you mess up, then you can attempt these difficult shots, mess up, and still win those points. Rafael Nadal striking two thẳng biên forehands (he wins both points).

On the left, he catches it a little late and finishes over his đầu vợt, and on the right, he catches it sufficiently early and finishes across his body. It doesn’t matter that you rarely strike the shots perfectly, because even when struck imperfectly they still work. It is in this manner that lỗi tolerance allows us to be more chủ động tấn công. The Rafa-Style theo sau is one of the reasons that Rafael Nadal has the best change-of-direction thẳng biên thuận tay in the world.

This shot is notoriously difficult to time, and Rafa also often times it imperfectly. But it doesn’t matter. When he times it perfectly, he drives through the ball, his vợt flicks around his vai, and he hits a điểm thắng. When he’s late, he finishes over his đầu vợt instead, and, most of the time it’s still a điểm thắng. Rafa explodes forward to these shots, taking them extremely early.

This guarantees his opponent is out of vị trí, but the payment for that lợi thế is that it also greatly increases the shot’s difficulty. His proficiency with the over-the-đầu vợt theo sau, which allows him to accommodate late contact, is the key that unlocks lỗi tolerance when doing this. 3. Disguise It’s easier to hit a target without disguise.

If you allow yourself to prepare your body differently for different targets, you can actually take the exact same vung vợt for each one, and just adjust your chuẩn bị so that the same exact vung vợt goes to a different target. But in a trận đấu, we need disguise – a shot isn’t useful if our opponent knows where it’s going. This means that the ability to hit late and succeed is a huge lợi thế.

We’ll use a very common Federer trick as our example here – disguising the thuận tay out of the trái tay góc. Federer runs around the trái tay, and prepares for an trong-ra ngoài thuận tay. Because he’s prepared for an trong-ra ngoài thuận tay, he’s preparing for a điểm tiếp xúc that is later than would be ideal for an trong-vào trong thuận tay. So how does he, in fact, strike the ball trong-vào trong when he wants to? If you’re following the theme of this article, you already know: He finishes above his đầu vợt.
