Roger Federer striking back-to-back inside forehands against Novak Djokovic in the ATP London 2019 finals, en route to winning it. When driving the ball trong-ra ngoài (left) he follows-through around his vai, and when driving it trong-vào trong, he follows-through above his đầu vợt. The ability to successfully strike the ball late allows him to successfully strike an trong-vào trong thuận tay using a điểm tiếp xúc much closer to the optimal trong-ra ngoài điểm tiếp xúc.

Any power he sacrifices in not being able to fully đường bóng through the shot is more than made up for by the disguise. Should I Practice It? Probably not directly. At least, you shouldn’t practice routinely catching the ball late. What you should practice are the applications of the Rafa-Style shot. Practice disguising your forehands – preparing for an trong-ra ngoài điểm tiếp xúc, but then using the Rafa-Style kết thúc to pull it up the line instead.

Practice taking more risk on offense, and adjusting with the Rafa-Style theo sau when necessary. In practice, make a note when you utilize this theo sau – “huh, I was late there.” You don’t want to be late. If you practice being late, then when you’re later than you want, you’ve already used up all of your margin for error, and you’ll miss. That said, you do want to train into your muscle memory that, on the off chance you’re late, this is how you strike the ball.

You’ll stay in the point, and then try not to be late next time. How to Hit the Perfect thuận tay (April fool ) The calendar just switched over to April, and I’d like to christen the new month by recapping the technical underpinnings behind a perfect thuận tay. Before we start, a quick word of warning: Don’t click any of the links in this article.

I’m leaving them here for history’s sake, but as it’s now April, I’ve realized they constitute gross, dangerous misinformation regarding the thuận tay, and they should never be viewed. Definitely don’t đọc The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. Muscle Activation What part of your body holds your vợt? Your hand. As such, nearly all of your mental attention should be focused on your hand, or at least your arm, during your vung vợt. In general, the closer your mental tập trung is to your vợt, the better.

Focusing on your hand is better than focusing on your arm, and focusing on your arm is better than on the rest of your body. Some people suggest powering the vung vợt with xoay of the abs and hông, or with leg đường bóng. What they fail to understand is that those large muscles are so far away from the vợt that it’s impossible to control a vung vợt accelerated by them. Instead, just use your hand and arm. cổ tay trễ Every pro uses a trễ and bật action on their thuận tay, so we will too.

The first step is to ensure you pre-rotate the cổ tay during your chuẩn bị. Look at Federer as he comes into the ball. See the cổ tay trễ? That’s the vị trí your hand and cổ tay need to be in. Federer’s pulls his cổ tay back as he comes into the ball, leading to all his spin and power. The easiest way to guarantee you hit this vị trí at contact is to just start there. When you see the ball coming, pull your vợt back. This initiates your đưa vợt ra sau.

As you pull back, rotate your dây towards the lưới. Some people suggest preparing with your lòng bàn tay down, but this, of course, won’t work – you’ll just hit the ball straight down into the ground. So point your dây parallel to the lưới, and you’ll be able to hit the ball up. To find the proper cổ tay vị trí, remember this: I should be able to see the butt-cap of your vợt from the other side of the lưới.

Every pro uses this vị trí at contact – the butt-cap facing the opponent – so we will as well. Now, this next part is critical: Make sure you hold the vợt tightly. If you hold it loosely, your dây are going to rotate towards the sky as you vung vợt, and the ball will sail long. So really grab that thing, and tense up your cẳng tay. That will guarantee that your string góc stays consistent, so you can maintain the pro cổ tay trễ vị trí throughout the vung vợt, and hit the ball in.

cổ tay bật Spin and power are generated by a cổ tay bật, whereby the vợt rotates around and hits the ball, either up for xoáy trên, or straight for power. Here’s how we do that: Starting from the butt-cap towards the lưới vị trí we discussed in cổ tay trễ, the next step is to initiate your forward vung vợt. You do this by yanking your butt-cap towards the ball. This should be pretty easy, as the butt-cap is already facing your opponent’s side of the sân.

Felix’s super strong cẳng tay curls the ball for spin, leading to his ability to strike winners from all over the sân. Right as you get to the ball, you’re going to violently twist your vợt around in a windshield-wiper like motion. This motion feels a bit strange at first, so here’s how you can learn it: Grab a doorknob, tightly, and then twist it closed as hard as you can (if you’re left-handed, twist it open). That’s the motion we’re using here.

It’s very important that this twist occurs at the correct time. You need to track the ball with your eyes so that you can initiate this cẳng tay twist at the precise time the ball is arriving on your dây. If you’re a little too early, or a little too late, you’re probably going to hit the khung vợt. Practicing timing is extremely important, because the vung vợt needs to be executed perfectly to work.

Some coaches suggest designing a vung vợt that succeeds even when you make a mistake, but those coaches are probably just bad players who can’t time the ball. If you mistime the ball and it goes in, that’s just luck. We want to train tennis strokes whereby our winning and losing is based on skill, not luck. Practicing timing is extremely important, because the vung vợt needs to be executed perfectly to work. If you’re hitting with spin, you also want to curl the vung vợt a bit.

The vợt should rotate over the ball in order to impart xoáy trên on it. This is what Rafa does, and it’s why he finishes over his đầu vợt so much. If you’re hitting phẳng, you aren’t going to curl. Instead, you’re going to flex your cổ tay – yank your hand towards your cẳng tay as hard as you can. That’ll give you a cổ tay bật like Nick Kyrgios, and it’ll send a 100 mph thuận tay past your opponent. theo sau The theo sau might be the most important part of the vung vợt.

Every pro follows through with their vợt flicking around their non-hitting vai, so we will as well. Right as you’re snapping your cổ tay through contact, you’ll also want to yank the vợt across your body into the theo sau vị trí. This will ensure you always get that precise around the vai theo sau that Federer uses. In summary, the thuận tay vung vợt is best executed as follows: Pull the vợt back.

Pre rotate the cổ tay – both the dây and your butt-cap should be facing the lưới. Yank the butt cap towards the ball. The instant before you strike it, bật your cổ tay around in a windshield-wiper motion. While you’re doing this curl the vợt over a bit for xoáy trên, and pull your arm across your body into your theo sau. That’s it.

Follow these 5 steps, and you’ll be able to hit a perfect thuận tay every time, as long as you practice your timing enough, and remember to hold your cầm vợt tightly to prevent the string góc from changing as you vung vợt. Summing Up Don’t do any of this. My khuỷu tay hurt just writing it. Happy April. Alcarize Your thuận tay There’s one defining aspects of Carlos Alcaraz’s world class thuận tay that often gets overlooked – generous use of gravity.

Carlos’s unique cơ học are precisely the result of this technical decision, so giao lại’s dive in and analyze. The High khuỷu tay theo sau Ever notice that Carlos Alcaraz has higher khuỷu tay than most while following-through? That high khuỷu tay gives us a clue as to what’s happening during his forward vung vợt.

Recall from The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay that the theo sau is a diagnostic tool for tennis players and coaches; it doesn’t matter in-and-of itself: As far as contact itself is concerned, the theo sau physically, literally doesn’t matter, because it transpires after the ball is already gone. We only discuss it and evaluate it at all because it can give us useful information as to what happened during the hitting phase. But, again, the theo sau itself doesn’t affect the vung vợt.

It’s merely a diagnostic tool at our disposal. The lỗi Tolerant thuận tay. “A Brief Comment on theo sau.” Page 107. So what does the high khuỷu tay indicate about Carlos’s forward vung vợt? While he’s exploding forward, Carlos Alcaraz allows gravity to pull his vợt down far more than most players do. The high khuỷu tay theo sau is the result of that exaggerated downward hand di chuyển.

Because he lets his vợt drop lower than most during his forward vung vợt, his vợt flicks up higher than most during his theo sau. Carlos Alcaraz vs Novak Djokovic Novak Djokovic has one of the most lỗi tolerant forehands in the world, and though he does employ some gravity on the shot, he doesn’t utilize it nearly as aggressively as Carlos does. Below is a comparison of Carlos and Novak each hitting a chest height thuận tay.

Their final chuẩn bị positions, pictured below, look pretty similar – they each prepare their hand at a similar height with respect to the expected điểm tiếp xúc. Pictured is the last khung vợt of chuẩn bị before initiation of the forward explosion. In the next khung vợt, the hông and lõi will begin to unwind. Once the forward explosion starts, though, the significant difference in gravity usage shines through.

Pictured next is the lowest the hand gets at any point during the forward vung vợt, and we can see that Carlos’s hand gets significantly lower than Novak’s. At the lowest point during the forward vung vợt, Carlos Alcaraz has allowed his hand to drop well below his hông, whereas Novak has kept his hand much higher, roughly at waist height. Carlos barely resists gravity at all during his forward vung vợt.

He allows the vợt to fall down a generous amount, and then lets it bounce off the bottom and flick back up through his target. Novak, on the other hand, drives the vợt primarily forward during his vung vợt, resisting most (but not all) of the gravity, and creating a much more diagonally straight, rather than down-then-up, vung vợt trajectory. Remember that both are playing nearly identical contact points here.

It’s surprising when looking at the mid-vung vợt hand positions, but both swings kết thúc right at chest height – Carlos’s vợt just travels a much longer down-then-up trajectory to get there. Both strokes kết thúc at chest height. The mid-vung vợt hand vị trí difference is not a result of different anticipated contact heights, but rather different gravity usage during the vung vợt. Should I Use Gravity?

Carlos Alcaraz has one of the best forehands in the world, and Carlos Alcaraz aggressively harnesses gravity during that cú đánh. Novak Djokovic also has one of the best forehands in the world, and Novak Djokovic is much more conservative with his gravity usage (as is Roger Federer). The answer, like the answer all questions in this class, is to experiment. There are hundreds of world class players that each employ gravity to their own degree. Figure out what works for you.

Often, certain cơ học are merely tradeoffs, rather than objectively better or worse. You’ll notice that there’s nothing lõi to the thuận tay that Carlos and Novak do differently from each other. Both prepare early, create generous khuỷu tay space, place their hand off to the side, and wind their hông and lõi away from the target.

Both then explosively rotate their hông and lõi back towards the target while relaxing their hand, they allow the cẳng tay to supinate and the cổ tay to trễ back, and keep their gaze still through contact. The only difference is that Carlos lets his hand drop farther, bounce more off the bottom of that drop, and then flick up higher during his theo sau, than Novak does. There’s no “right” and “wrong,” in tennis.

In some cases, there is certainly “more efficient” or “less efficient,” but, often, certain cơ học are merely tradeoffs, rather than objectively better or worse. Gravity usage is one such tradeoff, and you should experiment with it as such. Why You Can’t trả bóng a Kick giao bóng (Saccadic Blindness) When you watch professional players trả bóng giao bóng, they rarely look rushed, and when a player hits heavy spin at them, they never look bothered.

There’s a clip from an exhibition tournament were Federer requests a giao bóng “Right to the thuận tay, really slow.” What’s really slow to him? “Like 113 miles, if you can, please.” 113mph (182 km/h). That’s what Roger Federer considers a “really slow” giao bóng, when it comes right to his thuận tay.

Now, 113 isn’t slow if it hits a góc, or if it spins into the body (which the giao bóng in the clip actually did), but as far as a pure fastball, 113 is actually slow for a high level returner, and yet most 3.5 adult players would be lucky to even hit the dây on a 113 giao bóng, much less make the trả bóng. Today we’ll draw inspiration from the game of Cricket to break down one of the primary causes of this stark disparity in tennis trả bóng ability – thị lực.

Saccades A saccade is a rapid eye transition from one fixation to another. tập trung on an object near you. Now, switch your tập trung to a different object. Congratulations – your eyes just performed a saccade. In fact, your eyes have performed many small saccades while reading this article. Every few seconds as you đọc, your eyes perform a saccade from the words you are currently reading, to the next few words, and then do so again, and again.

Saccadic eye jumps are the most common method by which the gaze shifts. You perform at least 100,000 saccades every day. Invisible Blind Spots You are blind while your eyes perform a saccade. Since the image would be blurred and useless anyway, your brain simply turns off your thị lực while the saccade occurs. Wait. What? It doesn’t feel like my thị lực is blinking in and out all the time. That’s because your brain fills in the momentary blindness with a projected image.

Usually, it’ll just take whatever image your saccade ended on and backfill your memory with that image. Yes, your brain rewrites history to mask the momentary blindness. (This isn’t the only time on the sân your brain will rewrite history.) For everyday life, this is all well and good – I just looked from my book to my computer screen. I was blind for a few milliseconds during the saccade, but according to my memory, I was already looking at the computer for those few milliseconds, not blind.

Does that falsity effect my life? Not at all… typically. That changes when you’re playing a visually demanding sport. Terribly Missed Returns Your brain’s insidious cover-up of this mid-saccade blindness (known as “saccadic masking”) can create a lot of confusion while you’re on the tennis sân. Have you ever been so off on the giao bóng trả bóng that you mishit nearly every trả bóng, and on most, you weren’t even close?

This is most common in novel situations, say, the first set against a lefty, or against a player who hits with heavy kick. You try to “watch the ball,” and you feel like you’re seeing it, sort of, and yet, somehow, you can never catch it on the dây cleanly. Your apparent thị lực on the ball is actually your brain backfilling the blind spot from your saccade.

What’s likely happening here is that your apparent thị lực on the ball is actually your brain backfilling the blind spot from your saccade. It’s fake. Your eyes are trying to catch up with the ball, and once they get there, late, your brain lies to your consciousness and displays that you’ve been seeing it the entire time.

That’s why what you’re “seeing” isn’t assisting you with the vung vợt – you’re not actually seeing the ball; the image you’re perceiving is just a projection during saccadic masking. It’s possible you almost never see the bounce clearly on your trả bóng and don’t even know it. Most of the time, the giao bóng is slow enough, and the bounce is predictable enough, that it doesn’t matter.

Then, in a novel situation, that post bounce information becomes crucial to success, and because you’re blind when it’s revealed, you miss terribly. With this in mind, we need to perform our saccades strategically to ensure we never miss the most important information of all: the immediate post-bounce trajectory. Beat the Bounce Cricket batsmen perform what’s known as a predictive saccade as the ball comes towards them.

Their eyes jump from their first fixation, the giải phóng point, to their second fixation, the bounce area, before the ball is actually bouncing. The location for this predictive saccade is chosen based on visual information gathered from the ball’s early flight trajectory. High level batsmen perform this predictive saccade earlier than low level batsmen. The better the batsmen, the more quickly they are able to identify the location of the bounce and jump their eyes there.

The goal is to pick up the ball right before it hits the ground. The ball’s trajectory off the ground then determines the vung vợt. On fast bowls, low level batsmen aren’t able to predict the bounce early enough to fixate there before the ball arrives. This means they’re effectively blind to the bounce, and are denied the most important trajectory information necessary for the vung vợt: how the ball comes off the ground. As a result, on sufficiently fast bowls, they miss almost every time.

Do Tennis Returners Beat the Bounce? Watch a slow motion video of your favorite tennis player returning giao bóng, and you’ll notice a behavior very similar to that of the high level cricket batsmen: the returner’s đầu vợt moves to the bounce area before the ball gets there. They track this way for the very reason discussed above. In order to actually see the bounce, you have to jump your eyes to its general location before the bounce actually happens.

If, on the other hand, your saccade occurs with the bounce, rather than before it, you will be blind during the bounce. Roger Federer’s gaze arrives at the bounce area before the actual bounce transpires. They remain still there until after the ball has left the ground, at which point his gaze shifts to his projected điểm tiếp xúc. In general, elite athletes in high speed sports predict a future point of interest using visual information available during the early part of an object’s flight.

They then jump their fixation to that point of interest before the object gets there, ensuring the eyes have time to refocus on that new point before the object arrives. Aren’t We Looking Away From the Ball? The tennis giao bóng moves too quickly for you to keep the ball on your fovia for the giao bóng’s entire duration. As such, you must be content to fixate on only a few, critical points of interest, lest you attempt saccades which leave you blind during critical information reveals like the bounce.

Try to pick up the ball right before it bounces. If you predict the bounce location too early, though, you’ll lose sight of the ball for long enough that it’ll effect your vung vợt. So what’s the most lỗi tolerant way to perform our predictive saccade? If we mess up the timing a little, we still want to succeed. Try to pick up the ball right before it bounces.

If your fixation is slightly before the bounce, your eyes will be able to track it the few degrees through the bounce without requiring a saccade. If you’re a little late, most of the time you’ll still get to the bounce area before the bounce, and with your saccade completed, you’ll still see the bounce. Eye Performance Takes Practice Performing a predictive saccade to the bounce area will improve your giao bóng trả bóng, but you won’t master it overnight.

Once you tell your brain “we’re trying to jump our eyes to the bounce,” your brain will begin to self correct. Every time you get that crystal clear image of the ball coming up off the ground, your brain will make a mental note that your timing was correct. While you’re learning, you’ll often perform your saccade too early, or too late. Too early, and you’ll mis-predict the bounce location, because your brain didn’t have enough pre-bounce flight information to guess it correctly.

As a result, you won’t see the ball come off the ground clearly. Your eyes will perform a catch up saccade at some point after the bounce, and you’ll attempt your vung vợt with very minimal post bounce visual information. Too late, and you’ll saccade to the bounce area while the ball is bouncing, instead of before it bounces.

Due to saccadic masking, you’ll be blind during the bounce, and by the time your eyes re-stabilize, you have already lost the most critical visual information available. “Bounce.” If you’re having trouble, try to say “bounce” the instant the ball lands. Not a moment sooner, not a moment later. This is very difficult to get exactly right unless you perform your saccade correctly. Predict the bounce location then jump your eyes to that area, and pick up the ball on its way into the ground.

Do that, and you’ll get it right every time. If you’re having trouble, try to say “bounce” the instant the ball lands. Idiopathic shanks on the giao bóng trả bóng are probably a result of saccadic blindness – you can’t trust everything you “see.” Try to differentiate between the fake thị lực that your brain filled in after the fact, and the real thị lực that your brain was actually able to use when generating your vung vợt.

Once you succeed in jumping your eyes to the bounce area before the ball, you’ll experience a sense of control over the trả bóng, especially against heavy spin servers, that seemed impossible before. Zverev’s Brutal Injury – What Can We Learn? Nothing is more painful, frustrating, and sometimes, devastating, than injury.

We all remember the great Roger Federer shedding tears after losing the 2009 Australian Open final, only to beat Andy Roddick at Wimbledon later in the year, prompting a similar reaction from the beloved American. But neither episode was anything like what we saw from Alexander Zverev at the 2022 French Open. Those were cries of agony, not mere grief, fueled not only by the pain of his tearing ligaments, but even more so by the unrelenting frustration of being robbed of an opportunity to compete.

It was a tragedy, and I hope he recovers quickly. It also gives us an opportunity, while this specific injury holds vivid in every player’s mind, to review the basics of lỗi tolerant di chuyển in tennis. So What Happened? While moving to his right, Alexander Zverev hit a thuận tay. There are two ways to trở lại vị trí from the vị trí he was in: Slide Stutter step He chose the former, but simply missed his final step.

His foot didn’t get sufficiently far away from his giữa before contacting the ground. Friction between the ground and the edge of his sole held the foot in place, while his body weight, still moving rightward, shifted far past his giữa of thăng bằng, causing his right ankle to buckle. Alexander Zverev missed his last step, causing his foot to be far too far inside to slide safely. The result was devastating.

When the ankle buckles like this, the shoe’s sole acts as a lever to bật it; the taller the sole, the more lực is applied. lỗi Tolerant di chuyển From a lỗi tolerance standpoint, what happened to Alexander Zverev was a lỗi – a small mistake in execution. His foot caught on the ground before he wanted it to. Similar to cú đánh production, di chuyển must be lỗi tolerant. You won’t execute every step perfectly, the same way you won’t execute every shot perfectly.

We need to train in a way such that these small di chuyển faults don’t cause injury. Athleticism = lực Before we get into prevention strategies, it’s important to make something clear: Alexander Zverev is one of the most coordinated athletes on the planet, and he snapped his ankle playing tennis. I’m sure he also understands everything that follows in this article.

If you’re trying to perform at the absolute peak of human capacity, there’s going to be some risk, and if you’re 6’ 6’’ while doing so, even more. Reaching for higher performance entails reaching for higher lực production on sân. The greater the forces involved in your tennis game, necessarily, the greater the injury risk, all else being equal. When the ankle buckles, the shoe’s sole acts as a lever to bật it. Prevention #1 – Don’t Slide Severe ankle injuries are due to foot planting.

Even if your foot occasionally gets caught on the sân, if you are attempting a stutter-step theo sau, rather than a slide, the resulting sprain is typically minor. You’ll probably fall, but your full weight won’t be driven through your planted foot and into your ankle, because the goal was never to fully nạp that foot in the first place. Depending on your tennis goals, you may never need to slide.

The vị trí Alexander Zverev’s foot is in while being injured is actually an acceptable vị trí for the foot during a stutter-step theo sau. In other words, if he were attempting to have his foot merely bounce off the ground at that location, rather than stop and slide, he would have been fine, and if he’d caught the edge of his shoe unexpectedly early, he would have just tripped and fell. The stutter step theo sau is more lỗi tolerant than the slide.

It allows a greater range of faults – small execution mistakes – without injury. The price, of course, is that it’s a less time-efficient way to trở lại vị trí, putting the player in a worse vị trí defensively than a slide would. Foot Striking In a stutter-step theo sau, instead of planting the foot and using sliding friction to dissipate your năng lượng, you simply bounce off the arch of the foot as you continue on to your right.

As you shift your weight back left and stop yourself, you never allow your feet to plant, and instead always allow them to bounce back off the ground when the lực seems to demand it. Eventually, all of your rightward động năng has been dissipated, and you press back to the giữa of the sân. This method requires an athletic understanding of how to properly nạp and deload the foot.

If you’re clunking around on the sân like you have cinder blocks on your feet, one false step, and you’re going to end up like Sasha Zverev. Some players have a naturally springy step, but most don’t. Developing this ability will vastly improve both your agility and your safety on sân. Lower Soled Shoes Another trade-off you can make, if you’re a habitual ankle roller, is using lower soled shoes.

The cushioned sole of a tennis shoe helps protect the foot during heel strikes, but it also acts as a lever to bật the ankle during an ankle roll. The more cushion your shoe has, the more chủ động tấn công you can be slamming that foot into the ground when you change direction, but also the more injurious a false step becomes. If your main problem is ankle rolling, try choosing a shoe that’s lower to the ground.

This will give you a better sense of the ground as you move and will make false steps less damaging to the ankle. Prevention #2 – Slide rộng and Low A safe slide requires the final plant foot to be well outside the body’s khối tâm. This ensures that the năng lượng from the player’s đà is pushing out through the inside of the foot, rather than over the top of the foot, causing an ankle roll.

When you slide rộng, you will be more balanced: the wider your tư thế, the farther your body-weight is from tilting over either foot. Further, a wider final step increases the chance of contacting the ground with the inside of your foot first, which makes an ankle roll significantly less likely. A wider, lower slide is more lỗi tolerant. Sitting low while sliding further improves safety.

It lowers your khối tâm, decreasing any lever acting on your ankles if that khối tâm moves slightly off your giữa of thăng bằng. It also increases the activation of your lower body musculature, allowing you to improvise better in the event of an emergency. A wider, lower slide is more lỗi tolerant. The fact that you’re aiming for a rộng tư thế means that, even if your foot catches a little early, it still likely won’t be close enough to your giữa to cause a severe injury.

Even if your mistake is severe enough that your ankle does turn, the lower you are, the less leverage will be applied. Novak Djokovic steps slightly too narrowly on a hardcourt slide and “catches an edge,” but because he is sufficiently low and balanced, he is able to avoid rolling his ankle, and instead falls forward while unplanting his foot, avoiding serious injury.

During your slide, even if you “catch an edge,” and your shoe regains traction with the ground before you’re ready, you probably won’t get hurt if you’re sufficiently rộng, low, and balanced. You see this happen occasionally on the tour, and typically the player takes an awkward hop or two at the end of their slide, (or in more severe cases, falls) but is fine. Not Trading Ankle Rolls for Groin Strains A rộng, balanced slide requires a significant amount of strength and flexibility.

We aren’t trying to avoid ankle rolls, only to damage our đầu gối cartilage and strain our groin muscles every time we’re on defense. We’ll go into detail on tennis specific lower body conditioning at a later date. For now, we’ll mention the beneficial effects of the barbell back squat on slide ability. The barbell back squat is an efficient compound exercise that trains the strength, the low tư thế, and stretch phản xạ that’s employed when we slide.

tập trung on opening your hông at the bottom, and feeling that nice, explosive stretch phản xạ out of the hole. You want to feel your leg muscles reach their full stretch, and then bật back to đường bóng you up. This stretch phản xạ protects you when your muscles stretch during a slide. While you squat, ensure your weight is balanced over both feet, both front-to-back, and left-to-right, throughout the entire descent and ascent. Use less weight if you have to.

Properly balanced reps will develop your ability to maintain thăng bằng under nạp, a massive benefit that will translate to your tennis. Prevention #3 – Play Barefoot Okay, obviously this is a bit of a joke, but not entirely. When you play barefoot, your brain typically won’t giao lại you get injured. You’ll take many, many extra steps to decelerate, and you’ll never even consider sliding.

You’ll move quite gingerly, your brain acutely aware of how much lực it has to dissipate on each change of direction. If you’ve never played barefoot before, play very lightly, and certainly don’t play barefoot while keeping score or competing. It’s merely an exploratory exercise. Your body was built barefoot. When you don your tennis shoes and take to the sân to compete, understand that you’re assuming some risk.

Even an athletic freak like Alexander Zverev can take a quick misstep and bật his ankle. With training, practice, and proper kỹ thuật, this risk can be minimized. Train your strength and flexibility, slide low and rộng, and use a barefoot-mimicking, stutter-step theo sau when possible, and you’ll develop a lỗi tolerant di chuyển style that’ll keep you agile and healthy for the long run. Bad Decisions… Or Just Bad? What do Karue Sell and Scott Adams have in common?

Believe it or not, both have addressed a very common, often misunderstood trận đấu play decision: When should I take my thuận tay up the line? Scott frequently competed against a friend whom he could never beat. One day, he realized the reason: His friend would bait him into low percentage, thẳng biên attacks. It looked like there was space thẳng biên, so Scott would tấn công there, but the shots Scott would attempt were so difficult that he’d miss, and miss, and miss. So what’s the solution?

Don’t go thẳng biên, of course! Not so fast. Ex tour player Karue Sell gives us a more nuanced analysis. There are two distinctly different goals a player can have when changing direction using a thẳng biên thuận tay. One is to tấn công, but the other is merely to change the mẫu of the đánh qua lại. When attempting the second, the shot is hit higher over the lưới, and farther from the lines, making it almost as high percentage as a chéo sân thuận tay.

We’ll be using the thẳng biên thuận tay, probably the most common decision point in đánh đơn tennis, as the substrate to discuss the difference between two different fundamental kinds of misses: Bad decisions Bad execution While trying to work misses out of your game, you need to know which category you’re in, because the best ways to minimize each kind are very different. So You Missed thẳng biên… As coaches, we see this all the time.

Our students are hitting great, heavy balls chéo sân, and then, all of the sudden… a thẳng biên miss for, what seems to us, no reason. But was this really a Bad Decision miss? What if our student simply wanted to change the mẫu of the đánh qua lại, and only missed due to Bad Execution? Bad Decisions A true Bad Decision has two hallmarks: You struck the ball well It was close Bad Decisions are balls that land a few inches long, a few inches rộng, or hit the top of the lưới.

Since you’re here, I know you’ve designed your game to be lỗi tolerant, and that means that any time you strike the ball well, it should go in. Sometimes you’ll shank it off the khung vợt, and in that case, even a lỗi tolerant cú đánh will miss, but when your execution was precise enough to create solid contact near the giữa of the mặt dây, well, that needs to be good enough. If you are frequently missing close while making clean contact, you’re aiming to bad targets.

If you’re hitting into the lưới, aim higher, and accelerate 10% less to compensate. If you’re hitting long, just dial down your vận tốc by 10% (or perhaps just loosen your cầm vợt, if you notice you’re tight). If you’re missing rộng, aim farther into the sân. We talk a lot about technical lỗi tolerance, but we also need tactical lỗi tolerance. We need to select shots such that, when we mis-execute them by a little bit, they still go in. So, was our student’s thẳng biên miss a Bad Decision?

Well, how close was it, and how clean was the contact? If it landed two inches rộng, and it was well struck, then, almost certainly, had our student simply aimed a little farther into the sân, the ball would have gone in. Similarly, if their thẳng biên attempts frequently land 3-6 inches long, simply dialing down the gia tốc by 10-20% should vastly improve their miss-rate. We talk a lot about technical lỗi tolerance, but we also need tactical lỗi tolerance.

But what if the ball was hit into the back curtain? What if it was shanked onto the next sân? This miss has nothing to do with the target. No matter where they’d aimed, the di chuyển mẫu they executed wasn’t sending the ball back into the sân. It was a Bad Execution miss. Bad Execution Even for a lỗi tolerant tennis game, execution matters. If you slightly mistime, or mis-execute your cú đánh, it should still work, but sometimes, you will make bigger mistakes than that.

The more lỗi tolerant your game, the more execution faults you can cán vợt, but some faults will be so large that you’ll just miss anyway. Very rarely should you adjust your tactical game based on acute Bad Execution. Imagine you’ve just hit your thẳng biên thuận tay into the back curtain. As we’ve already discussed, this result did not show the hallmarks of a Bad Decision miss. It wasn’t a well-struck ball that landed close to its target – it was a poorly struck ball that wasn’t close at all.

So what would it look like if you tried to eliminate this miss by adjusting your decision making? If you hit it 20% slower, the shot wouldn’t have hit the back curtain, but it still would have been way out. What about 30% slower? Half speed? Just rolled the ball in? Very rarely should you adjust your tactical game based on acute Bad Execution. The level of adjustment required to make a Bad Execution miss succeed instead is large.

To eliminate these misses using shot selection requires selecting shots that are very far below the level that your good execution can cán vợt. This cedes a lot of ground. If you adjust this way, then even when you do execute well, you won’t get your usual payoff. All your shots are now slow, aimed right down the giữa, or both. More of your mishits will go in, but is the trade-off worth it? So How do I Stop Missing?

Bad Decision misses are pretty easy to work out mid-trận đấu. “On most of my thẳng biên shots, I’m hitting it well, but the ball is landing long.” Solution? Hit a little softer. The same attempts that used to go long will now fall in, and you’ll stop losing points from neutral and tấn công situations. Acute Bad Execution misses are also pretty simple. If you execute a shot poorly once or twice, accept it, breathe, relax, and move on to the next point.

Chronic Bad Execution misses, on the other hand, are much more difficult. When a certain execution mistake is happening over, and over, and over again, some adjustment will be necessary to remain competitive. “Half the time I go thẳng biên, I forget to watch the ball, and I shank it onto the next sân.” You can remind yourself to watch the ball, and if that works, great, but, chances are, you haven’t built enough of a habit of watching the ball to do it consistently.

Make a mental note to work on that in practice, later. As for the actual trận đấu, you can either: Accept your 50+% miss rate thẳng biên (probably a bad option) Bring your vung vợt vận tốc way down in hopes of cleaner contact even with sloppy thị lực Stop hitting down-the line A mix of 2 and 3 is probably best. Your issue here isn’t one of decision making. It’s that you simply cannot execute this thẳng biên shot at a trận đấu ready frequency.

The sooner you accept that uncomfortable fact, the sooner you’ll adjust to give yourself a chance in spite of it. Overlap There’s one place where Bad Decisions and Bad Execution overlap: selecting a shot you’re simply not good enough for. Here, the miss will look like Bad Execution, but really it was a Bad Decision. I see this all the time on passing shots, specifically.

A student will go for a ridiculous Rafa Nadal, full sprint, over the đầu vợt theo sau, xoáy trên cúp chuyền, and miss onto the next sân. You can call this an “execution miss,” and for a player with a good running thuận tay, it actually could be, but for most of my students, this is a Bad Decision masquerading as Bad Execution.

Yes, technically, if they’d executed the shot perfectly, it might have won the point, but in reality something like a cú lốp, or simply trying to get the ball low to the lưới player’s feet, had far more equity. Learn your own game. Everyone has different strengths and weaknesses. You might have shots that no one except you should be selecting, and yet you typically execute them well. If you miss one of those, don’t worry about it, and go back to the well next time.

On the other hand, be honest with yourself about the shots you’ve never been able to execute consistently, and relegate those to the practice sân until they improve. Coco’s Cannon You’re 5’ 9’’, 140 lbs (1.79m, 64kg), and female. How hard can you giao bóng? Answer: at least 124 mph (200 km/h), because that’s how fast the rocket Coco Gauff threw down in her third round trận đấu at Wimbledon 2022 exploded off her vợt.

Coco is one of the most explosive athletes on the planet, but even still, a giao bóng with that level of blistering speed is impossible without exceptional kỹ thuật. giao lại’s dive in. chuẩn bị Coco uses the textbook chuẩn bị vị trí I want you all to adopt. She locks her hitting arm in at 90-90, maintains a straight, stable lõi, tilts backwards, and then explodes upwards and forwards.

The 90-90 vị trí allows the arm to safely absorb the vận tốc generated by stronger muscles of the legs and thân: Coco Gauff’s final service vị trí before exploding off the ground. If the khuỷu tay is too high, the vai becomes impinged as it tries to internally rotate. This will always decrease vận tốc, and often strain the vai. If the khuỷu tay is too low, the giao bóng will typically end up fine, but won’t have quite the vận tốc as if it were higher, and thereby farther from the thân.

If the khuỷu tay is too bent, similar to the low khuỷu tay, the giao bóng will typically be fine, but lacking the vận tốc generated by the longer lever of a 90 degree khuỷu tay. If the khuỷu tay isn’t bent enough, there’s more lực applied to the inner khuỷu tay during arm gia tốc. If you’re serving pain free, that’s fine, but this is the lực that sends pitchers to the operating table for Tommy John surgery. vung vợt Initiation Coco’s giao bóng is initiated by an explosive leg đường bóng and forward twist of the lõi.

The arm hasn’t released by itself yet. Coco drives off the ground and explosively twists her torso around. As she twists, her arm is driven forward and upward. This xoay is about the axis of her cột sống, and because she is tilted upward, her arm is flung upward by this xoay. Many rec players mess up here, and pinch their vai in towards their cheek in order to “vung vợt up.” Coco, on the other hand, has her arm almost completely out to her side.

The upward nature of the vung vợt is created by the orientation of her torso, not by moving the arm up by itself. Her arm doesn’t come forward by itself, but rather as a result of the strong muscles of her thân twisting her. The twisting action starts from the ground – her đầu gối extend to initiate her motion – and continues into her thân, as her erect, stable lõi spins around. The Hand Vector Coco’s hand is thrown towards the ad side service box (yellow).

Her vai internally rotates, and the ball is struck towards the đều side (green). After the arm is flung by the thân, it rotates internally at the vai (If you see “ISR” that’s a reference to this: “internal vai xoay”). This xoay, during a tennis giao bóng, is what causes the dây to slap the ball phẳng into the service box. Here, Coco is aiming down the T. Her hand is thrown towards, roughly, the giữa of the ad side service box.

As the vai internally rotates, the vợt slaps the ball off to the left of Coco’s hand trajectory. The hand itself is not thrown towards the giao bóng’s target. The partial orthogonality between hand trajectory and ball trajectory is critical for high vận tốc serving. In order for Coco to harness her throwing cơ học, she must launch her hand to the right of her giao bóng target, because when her vai internally rotates, it’ll cause the vợt to slap the ball left of her hand’s trajectory.

theo sau A cú đánh’s theo sau provides useful information about how it was executed, even though the ball is already gone. In Coco’s theo sau, we see her right leg kick up and back – her foot almost hits her butt. Why? Because that’s what happens when a server rotates about their cột sống explosively. Look at any MLB pitcher, and you’ll see a very similar back leg kick up.

The theo sau of Coco Gauff’s giao bóng (left) and Ardolis Chapman’s 106mph fastball (right) both display a back leg kick up, a result of the explosive, balanced, torso xoay during the throw. Do not try to kick your back leg up. That won’t help. Instead, if your back leg is not kicking up, tập trung on rotating with more lực into your shot. If you rotate faster, your body will naturally decelerate that vận tốc by kicking the back leg up.

If your back leg is kicking around to the side (more like a pitcher’s) instead of up, you need to transpose your xoay. You are treating the giao bóng as a forward throwing motion, and need to throw up more. Imagine serving by throwing your vợt at the ceiling on your side of the sân, and then adjust from there.

If your back leg still isn’t kicking like Coco’s, the issue probably isn’t your lực production, but rather your lực translation – your suboptimal posture is preventing the lực from exiting your lõi and accelerating your arm. tập trung on your butt, ab, and back stability. If I freeze khung vợt you at the moment you initiate your vung vợt, I shouldn’t be able to bend you. I’m going to try to bend your thân at every góc: push back, forwards, left, right. You shouldn’t bend.

The better your posture, the more efficiently lực is transferred. The lực you generated off the ground will end up accelerating your arm more the less of it is lost bending your lõi around as you explode.

Smashing Through Plateaus

There are three critical, broad skill classes you need to master in order to be an elite tennis player. In order, they are: cú đánh production thị lực di chuyển If you’re good at these three things, you’ll be an elite tennis player, and if you’re missing any of the three, you won’t. You might be wondering, what about chiến lược? chiến thuật? Strength? These are all subordinate to the big three listed above. Take chiến thuật as an example.

Karue Sell made a fun video beating up on a 4.5 player using an intentionally handicapping chiến thuật – Karue would not hit any winners. The result? A 6-0 set, of course, because tactical prowess doesn’t come anywhere close to closing the ball striking, thị lực, and di chuyển gap between the two players. Strength and fitness are in a similar boat.

It’s great to be strong, but if you don’t know how to properly recruit that strength to generate tốc độ đầu vợt, then the extra muscle is just dead weight. Similarly, it’s great to be fit, but your fitness doesn’t matter unless your thị lực is also adept. If you’re fit enough to grind, but you miss every fourth ball due to tracking mistakes, then, guess what, your fitness barely provides an lợi thế.

The Limiting Reagent

In a chemical reaction, the “limiting reagent” is the first chemical to get fully consumed, thereby preventing further reaction. It is the thing that, were more of it added to the reaction, would spur the reaction to continue. If you want to improve in tennis, you need to tập trung on your limiting skill class, because it is your limiting skill class that’s preventing you from improving. Importantly, this means first identifying which of the skill classes is limiting you.

If you find yourself in a plateau, despite extensive practice or training, chances are you’re working on a skill class which is not your limiting skill class.

Skill Capped Bottlenecks

A very common bottleneck for aspiring players is the 4.5 USTA level (between 7-9 UTR, typically), because it’s typically the level at which players who are only good at 2 of the 3 skill classes get stuck. Elite grinders (like the YouTube (in) famous MEP) are solid 4.5s. The grinder has great thị lực, pretty solid di chuyển, and no real cú đánh production to speak of. Players with great cú đánh production and thị lực are also solid 4.5s, but they don’t move well enough to advance further.

Players with great cú đánh production who move well are in such perfect vị trí for most balls that they get away with their poor thị lực, but they lose too many points to random errors to compete at the higher levels. You’ll actually see advice on “how to get to 5.0” for each of these cases. Some will say “the difference between a 4.5 and a 5.0 is athleticism and fitness.” True, if that former 4.5 had great thị lực and strokes.

Others suggest that, “5.0 players just track the ball way better.” Also true, and a 4.5 with great di chuyển and strokes should work on thị lực to improve. Finally, the least true of the three: “5.0 players just hit harder.” cú đánh production is the most important of the three tennis skills.

There are many, many 4.5 players with the 2/3 combination of: cú đánh production + thị lực cú đánh production + di chuyển The MEP’s of the world are rare, because it is quite difficult to succeed with just di chuyển and thị lực (you’ll probably need to master secondary classes like chiến thuật as well). Rare as they are, though, they are still solid 4.5s, and like the others we discussed, they only need to master one more skill class to advance. Slap a typical 4.5 thuận tay onto MEP, and he’s a 5.0, easily.

What I’m Good at Is What Matters

Human beings like to be good at things. Human beings really like when the things they’re already good at turn out to be the important things. But this bias towards thinking things you’re good at are important interferes with tennis practices everywhere.

The ball-striker with sloppy thị lực sees the player across the lưới with mediocre kỹ thuật. “Ugh, I’m so much better than this guy, how am I losing to him?” The player with great thị lực sees the ball-striker across the lưới and thinks: “This guy can’t even make 5 shots in a row, he’s just hitting lucky winners.” The young athlete losing to the 45 year old fat 5.0 can’t understand it. “This guy can’t even move, I should be winning every point!” The answer to to each player’s confusion is simple: they are all focusing only on the skill classes that they are already good at, while dismissing the other skill classes as unimportant.

Plateaus

If the ball-striker worked on his thị lực, he’d improve tremendously. If the grinder worked on his ball-striking, he’d improve as well. (The fat 5.0 is very self aware of his di chuyển liability and just likes beating up on players who think they’re better than he is.) We don’t like to admit that the area in which we’re deficient is important.

The grinder does not respect the ball-striking of the player who can’t make 5 shots in a row, even though that player’s chuỗi động học really is better than the grinder’s. If we’re bad at something, we desperately want that thing to be unimportant.

The ball-striker does not respect the ability of the grinder to move efficiently and to track the ball cleanly into the dây 50 times in a row, even though the ball-striker, despite his cú đánh production, is likely months away from being able to do this consistently. There exists a natural human aversion to admitting we’re bad at something that really, really matters. If we’re bad at something, we desperately want that thing to be unimportant. This kind of thinking creates plateaus.

It prevents us from seeing fundamental flaws in our own game, leading to stagnation despite our practice time. And even as this mode of thinking impedes our development, it continues patting us on the back, telling us we’re doing a great job, while we take repetition after repetition working on the skill classes we’re already good at.

Misplaced Emphasis on Secondary Skill Classes

In order to cú đập through a plateau, you need to first correctly diagnose its cause, and a big pet-peeve of mine is when coaches mis-diagnose deficiencies of cú đánh production, thị lực, or di chuyển as issues of secondary skills instead – specifically as issues of chiến thuật, fitness, or effort. Here are some good examples. Telling the grinder to hit harder. This is tactical advice, but it is almost definitely misplaced.

The grinder is probably not capable of producing a lỗi tolerant, powerful thuận tay. If this player blindly tries to hit harder, it will eradicate all of their lỗi tolerance, causing their miss rate to skyrocket. As a result, they’ll quickly abandon their “powerful” cú đánh and go back to grinding. Their lack of power is not a tactical issue. It is a cú đánh production issue. This player will dramatically improve when, and only when, they learn to better coordinate their chuỗi động học.

The inverse is equally annoying. Telling the ball-striker to “go for less.” Again, another piece of bad tactical advice. The ball-striker is perfectly capable of producing the shots they are attempting… when they’re in vị trí, and when they watch the ball. Which they never are, and they never do. The issue with this player’s game is that they have glaring deficiencies in di chuyển, thị lực, or both – their shot selection decisions are a secondary factor.

This player can be very tough to work with, because they tend to think they’re much better than they really are. The player is unaware of their own thị lực/di chuyển deficiencies, and thereby also unaware that most of their ball-striking capability is next to useless during a trận đấu.

The way for this player to win a trận đấu is to tone the ball striking way, way down to trận đấu their (low) di chuyển and thị lực capabilities, but the way for them to improve is to bring their thị lực and di chuyển up to the level of their cú đánh production, not to play more conservative tennis. Telling the bad mover to get fitter, or to “stop being lazy.” Most of the time, the bad mover just doesn’t know what they’re doing.

They don’t understand how to activate the muscles in their calves, hông, lõi, back, etc in order to efficiently move around the sân. Before calling the student lazy, first actually show them how to orient their body to move explosively. This might even need to be done in the gym – some players have little to no proprioceptive understanding of what their hamstrings or glutes even do. di chuyển kỹ thuật, like cú đánh production kỹ thuật, needs to be practiced.

If a student doesn’t move well, teach them how to move well, and then have them practice moving like that over, and over, and over again, and eventually it’ll become habit just like everything else.

Triple gấp Athleticism

There’s a lot that goes into athletic di chuyển, but the basic idea is pretty simple – the more muscle you can recruit to help you move and thăng bằng, the more athletic you’re going to be. Additionally, the stronger your muscles, tendons, and ligaments are, relative to your weight, the more athletic you’re going to be. Carlos Alcaraz’s cổ tay going into gấp during his thuận tay theo sau. One of the simplest ways to maximize your muscle recruitment on sân is to move with triple gấp.

gấp is an anatomical term that just means “decreasing the góc.” A common place you’ll see it is in the sentence: cổ tay gấp occurs during the thuận tay theo sau. In other words, during the theo sau, the hand bends towards the cẳng tay – the cổ tay góc decreases. What to Bend To move athletically, we want gấp of the: hông đầu gối Ankles When the hông are bent, the posterior chain is primed.

The lower back, glutes, and hamstrings are ready to fire to forcefully straighten out that hông góc, which can đường bóng the body forward, up, left, right, or even backwards, depending on the exact situation. When the đầu gối are bent, the quadriceps are primed, ready to fire and straighten out the đầu gối góc, similarly driving the body. When the ankles are bent past their typical 90 degrees, the calves are primed.

The calves act as springs when the athlete lands with a midfoot or forefoot strike: upon landing, the ankle góc shrinks, the calves store năng lượng đàn hồi, and then they contract and giải phóng that năng lượng, driving the foot back off the ground. Roger Federer entering the athletic ready vị trí at lưới, then leveraging that vị trí to immediately pivot backwards to tấn công a cú lốp (against Rafa Nadal, 2017 Australian Open Final).

hông gấp

hông gấp is something that’s often missing in a player who is sedentary for most of their life. In the sitting vị trí, the glutes, hamstrings, and lower back are very relaxed, and as a result, many players have no athletic awareness of how to recruit these muscles. If a person’s brain doesn’t think the glutes and hamstrings are worth using, they won’t adopt a bent hông vị trí. If an athlete isn’t used to firing these muscles, it’s likely they will play with only minimal hông gấp.

The bent hông vị trí is what allows these muscles to đường bóng the body, so if a person’s brain doesn’t think the glutes and hamstrings are worth using, they won’t adopt a bent hông vị trí. Verbally cuing a player into a bent hông vị trí will often immediately improve their di chuyển, but not always. For a player who really struggles to fire these muscles, the low bar squat and the deadlift, performed properly, are great proprioceptive training tools to remedy the issue.

đầu gối and Ankle gấp

A small amount of đầu gối gấp is always healthy. It promotes quadriceps usage as you đường bóng off the ground, and it creates another degree of freedom for your body to use when absorbing the shock of landing. The farther the đầu gối go out over the toes, the more đầu gối gấp and ankle gấp we get. You might be wondering here – isn’t an excessive đầu gối-over-toes vị trí what we said to avoid in order to protect your đầu gối? Yes and no.

The đầu gối-over-toes vị trí is an extremely useful athletic vị trí, and you want to be able to utilize it, but it’s also a high stress vị trí for your đầu gối. The fastest, most reliable way to tấn công this midcourt bóng trên cao was to đường bóng forward with the đầu gối-over-toes vị trí (left). Roger then uses minimal hông gấp when jumping for the bóng trên cao (giữa), because that makes the cú đánh easier to execute.

He prepares to land with slight gấp in the đầu gối and hông (right), maximizing shock absorption. The biggest đầu gối killer is routine đầu gối gấp without accompanying hông gấp. If the đầu gối are bent, but the hông are straight, then the đầu gối and quadriceps must accommodate the entire nạp of the body, without help from the strong muscles of the lower back, glutes, and hông. This vị trí should only be used when necessary.

In Roger’s initial đường bóng to the bóng trên cao, his hông are bent: his glutes and hamstrings are playing a large roll in driving his body forward, not just his quadriceps. It is only when playing the bóng trên cao itself that he straightens out his hông góc, and uses an almost exclusively quadriceps driven motion.

đầu gối gấp with hông gấp is what maximizes lực production and safety, but to maximize athleticism – your ability to do anything and everything around the sân – your đầu gối need to be strong enough to occasionally cán vợt things all on their own.

Learn Your Own đầu gối Ability

Everyone’s đầu gối can cán vợt different amounts of stress without becoming injured. The stronger your đầu gối, the more đầu gối gấp you can use, and the more athletic you’ll be. The weaker your đầu gối, the less đầu gối gấp you can use, and the less athletic you’ll be. Roger Federer possesses world class đầu gối strength. Here’s his victory celebration from Wimbledon 2004. Not only did he get his đầu gối into this vị trí, but he fell into this vị trí and then caught himself here.

Chances are, if you tried this, your đầu gối would bật. Roger Federer falling to the ground after winning the 2004 Wimbeldon final against Andy Roddick, unintentionally showcasing his world class đầu gối ability. That’s why Roger Federer can, as a matter of routine, enter positions with his đầu gối way out over his toes, đường bóng out of those positions, and not get hurt. He has world class đầu gối. If you don’t, you need to rely more on your butt and hamstrings, and less on your đầu gối.

Sit your butt back more, bend over more at the hông (with a straight back), and use less ankle gấp. Training Posterior chain training will make you more athletic. đầu gối training will make you more athletic. Stronger calves will make you more athletic. di chuyển improvement is a dance between training and kỹ thuật. The goal of training is to enable yourself to enter more athletic positions without getting hurt, and then to empower yourself to generate more lực out of those positions.

di chuyển improvement is a dance between training and kỹ thuật. This article is an introduction to the basic cơ sinh học of athletic di chuyển, but I’d be remiss if I didn’t include some brief training recommendations as well. Most adult players only have a few hours per week to train (if that), so these are the densest ways to use that time. hông gấp and glute/hamstring strength The lowbar squat to parallel, and the deadlift.

These two exercises are the best for not only increasing the strength of these muscles, but also your proprioceptive awareness of how to fire them. tập trung on high quality, full range of motion reps, during which you can feel the proper muscles firing and fatiguing. Lift heavy, but not so heavy that your hình thức is compromised. đầu gối ability and quadriceps recruitment Loaded backwards running and the ass-to-grass squat.

A very common injury case for the đầu gối is deceleration – the đầu gối has to slow you down when you stop, and it takes a lot of nạp when doing so. Decelerating forwards is a very similar job to accelerating backwards, which is why loaded backwards di chuyển strengthens the đầu gối. The ass-to-grass squat is similar to the squat to parallel, except that proportionally more of the nạp is on the đầu gối – the đầu gối-over-toes vị trí is loaded, as is the entire range of motion into and out of it.

Ankle ability, foot striking, and calf strength Barefoot running. It’s the activity your foot was designed for, and it’s the fastest way to train it back into shape. If you’re used to running with shoes, your natural foot ability is probably very low, and you’ll have to start very slowly with this at first – not even a full mile on your first run. Ease into it, don’t ignore pain.

Running barefoot will activate and train the entire arch/ankle/Achilles tendon/calf spring that was built into your foot so your ancestors could run their prey to death, and that training will carry over into your tennis foot striking as well.
